## §0 Executive Summary and Reading Map

This artifact is a Stage 0/1 research synthesis on asynchronous programming, conducted in support of the Prologos language project's concurrent and distributed runtime efforts. It surveys the field's foundational, algebraic, practical, structured-concurrent, and frontier eras (1965–2026), with a strong leaning toward a working hypothesis that asynchrony is best understood as the operational semantics of computation in a quantale-enriched category over a propagator substrate. The artifact's purpose is to inform follow-on design tracks (notably the L2 multi-network runtime, the quantale-enriched cc(D) realisation, and the user-facing async syntax that has been deferred since S8b in 2026-03), not to commit to specific architectural decisions.

### What this artifact is

A long-form (≈75,000 words / ≈110 pages dense markdown including bibliography) research synthesis structured as eight era surveys plus a synthesis chapter, with bookend framing. Each era survey was produced by an independent research agent with its own bibliography and cross-references; the synthesis chapter argues the working hypothesis from the assembled evidence. The artifact stands alone but cross-references existing project research artifacts (notably PROCESS_CALCULI_SURVEY, RESEARCH_PARALLEL, TROPICAL_QUANTALE_RESEARCH, EFFECTFUL_PROPAGATORS_RESEARCH, and the PPN 4C tropical addendum).

### What this artifact is not

It is not a design document. It does not commit the project to any particular L2 architecture, async syntax, or concrete algebra. It does not prescribe an implementation path. It does not adjudicate among the candidate frontier hypotheses (a)/(b)/(c) listed in §8 — the user's framing prioritises (c) but supports all three.

### Reading map

The artifact has eleven top-level sections.

**§1 Frame and Working Hypothesis.** The framing of the artifact: what asynchronous programming is, why it is a fundamental rather than tactical question, and the working hypothesis the synthesis chapter argues against the assembled evidence.

**§2 Era I — Foundational Concurrency Theory (1965–1990).** Dijkstra semaphores, Hoare monitors, Mesa signal-and-continue, the CSP/CCS/π trinity, Hewitt actors, Erlang/OTP, MultiLisp/Halstead futures, dataflow I-structures, CML first-class events, and the Honda-Tokoro / Boudol asynchronous-π discovery — all framed against the *sync-rendezvous vs async-buffered dichotomy* that subsequent eras inherit. (Slice 01.)

**§3 Era II — Algebraic and Categorical Semantics of Concurrency (1985–2005).** Linear logic (Girard), proofs-as-processes (Caires-Pfenning, Wadler), game semantics (HO, AJM, Melliès asynchronous games), CCP (Saraswat-Rinard-Panangaden closure-operator semantics, soft CCP, LCC, hyperdoctrinal model), quantale theory (Mulvey, Joyal-Tierney, Rosenthal, Stubbe quantaloids), Q-enriched categories and Lawvere metric, Chu-spaces concurrency, event structures, coalgebraic concurrency, algebraic effects (Plotkin-Power 2002 / 2003). (Slices 02, 03, 04.)

**§4 Era III — The Industrial Pragmatism (1995–2015).** Erlang-Akka-Orleans actor lineage, F#/C#/JavaScript/Python/Rust/Swift/Kotlin/Go futures-and-async-await arc, Mark Miller's E and CapTP, Bob Nystrom's "What Color Is Your Function?" diagnostic, FRP and reactive-extensions, Naiad / timely / differential dataflow. (Slices 05, 06.)

**§5 Era IV — The Structured-Concurrency Turn (2015–2024).** Smith's "Notes on Structured Concurrency" diagnostic, Trio / Kotlin / Swift / Java structured-concurrency primitives, algebraic effect handlers (Eff, Frank, Koka, OCaml 5), asynchronous session types and the decidability frontier (Bravetti-Lange-Yoshida), capability-based effect systems (Effekt), CALM / Bloom / BloomL. (Slice 07.)

**§6 Era V — The Frontier (2020–2026).** Choreographic programming (Pirouette, Choral, ChoRus, λ_QC), the Spritely / OCapN revival of CapTP, post-2020 quantale-enriched concurrent semantics (Beohar-Gurke-König-Messing-Forster-Schröder-Wild quantale-coalgebraic logics; Goncharov-Hofmann-Nora-Schröder-Wild Kantorovich functors), effect-handler categorification (Ahman, Dagnino-Pasquali-Streicher fibrational tale, Xie-Johnson-Maclaurin-Paszke parallel handlers, Iris/OCaml separation logic for effects), tropical mathematics in scheduling and lambda calculus (Le Boudec network calculus, Barbarossa-Pistone tropical λ-calculus), and the explicit gap inventory. (Slices 06 §4.5/§6.2 + 08.)

**§7 Cross-cutting Quantale Deep-Dive.** Pure quantale theory recap; tropical (min-plus) quantale and its applications; other quantales (Boolean, probabilistic, relational, free, linear-logic, concurrent Kleene, divisibility, Day-convolution); quantale modules and closure operators (the Saraswat-CCP algebraic underwriting); async composition as a quantale operation; quantale-causal clocks; stratification on quantale modules. (Slice 04.)

**§8 Synthesis: A Quantale-Enriched cc(D) Substrate.** The chapter where the lean is allowed to be argued. Eight subsections develop the genealogical claim, the function-coloring relocation, the structured-concurrency-as-CKQ account, the L2 architecture sketch with quantale-causal clocks, the decidability remark, the cost-order-fairness-resource projection claim, the gap inventory, and a calibrated reading of the hypothesis against the evidence.

**§9 Sub-Questions for Follow-On Tracks.** The research program the working hypothesis implies, broken down into specific design-track candidates with prerequisites and cross-references.

**§10 Annotated Bibliography.** Organised by era and sub-field. Each citation gets a 2–4 line annotation explaining its contribution to the argument, why this artifact cites it, and how it connects to neighbouring citations or the working hypothesis.

**§11 Out-of-Scope Declarations.** What the artifact deliberately does not cover, with reasons.

### Citation conventions

Citations appear inline as `[Author Year]` or `[Author Year Short]` for disambiguation; full annotated entries are co-located with their use in each era section's *References* subsection. §10 documents the bibliography's section-by-section organisation. Cross-references between sections appear as `§X.Y` with brief context where ambiguous. Project artifacts (rules, tracking docs, principles) are referenced by their relative path (e.g., `rules/stratification.md`, `docs/tracking/2026-03-07_ARCHITECTURE_AD_IMPLEMENTATION_DESIGN.org`).

### A note on section ordering

The artifact was assembled from eight Phase-1 slices written by independent research agents, each with a specific era / sub-field mandate. Each slice was self-contained (sections plus bibliography) and cross-referenced its neighbours. The assembled artifact preserves the slice-level organisation rather than imposing strict numerical ordering on every subsection. Two minor consequences for the reader: (i) §3.6 (categorical concurrency in *-autonomous frameworks) appears before §3.3 (CCP) and §3.4–3.5 (quantale theory), reflecting that slice 02 covered §3.1, §3.2, and §3.6 in one mandate while slice 03 covered §3.3 in a separate mandate; (ii) §7 (the cross-cutting quantale deep-dive) appears alongside §3.4–3.5 because the same agent wrote both. The TOC and this reading map are the primary navigation aids; section numbering should be read as a *labelling system* rather than a strict ordering.

## §1 Frame and Working Hypothesis

### §1.1 The async question on a synchronously-clocked propagator substrate

Asynchronous programming is among the most heavily-trafficked topics in practical programming-language design — every mainstream language has an async story, every framework has an opinion, every web-scale system has a tactical answer — and one of the least theoretically settled. The Era III section of this artifact (§4) catalogues fifteen-plus distinct primitive choices made by Erlang, Akka, Orleans, F#, C#, Python, JavaScript, Rust, Swift, Kotlin, and Go, with no two languages agreeing on a single normative answer. The Era IV section (§5) catalogues five further attempts (structured concurrency, algebraic effects, async session types, capability-based effects, CALM/BloomL) at correcting Era III's pathologies, with the field's leading designers openly acknowledging — though not in retraction language — that async/await as institutionalised was a tactical mistake. The Era V frontier (§6) shows the field still actively searching for a unified algebraic framing.

The Prologos project, however, faces a more specific question. The substrate the project's compiler runs on is a *propagator network*: lattice-valued cells with monotone merge functions, BSP scheduling, CALM-monotone-within-strata. The substrate is by design *synchronous in its round structure* — every BSP round fires simultaneously, every cell merge is well-defined under join-semilattice axioms, the network's quiescent state is a least fixed point. The mantra that drives the project's design — "all-at-once, all in parallel, structurally emergent information flow ON-NETWORK" — is the operational form of this substrate's discipline.

The async question for Prologos is therefore: *what is the user-facing async model on a synchronously-clocked BSP substrate?* The substrate is structurally synchronous; the user-facing language must support asynchronous programming patterns; the relationship between the two must be principled rather than ad-hoc. Three layers must be unified: (L1) the substrate concurrency itself — Zig-kernel work-stealing, EBR, parallel BSP rounds; (L2) the multi-network runtime — vat-style isolation, cross-network message delivery, distributed propagation across nodes; and (L3) the user-facing async syntax — the `!!`/`??`/`@` operators reserved by S8a, promise pipelining, await-blocks. Each of the three has substantive prior work in the project (PAR Track 2 R1-R2 for L1 parallel-BSP; Architecture A+D for L2 effect-ordering theory with the runtime executor as a placeholder; S8a session-type-level async for L3 type-level forms). What does not yet exist is the framework that unifies them.

### §1.2 Working hypothesis

The working hypothesis the synthesis chapter argues, and that this artifact assembles evidence for and against, is the following:

> **Asynchrony is the operational semantics of computation in a quantale-enriched category over a propagator substrate. Futures, awaits, fairness, cost, and ordering are projections from a single algebraic object. Propagator networks instantiate Saraswat's cc(D) family for a particular constraint system D; the quantale-enriched extension is a frontier hypothesis with established formal antecedents.**

The hypothesis has two parts:

1. *Genealogical*: Prologos's propagator-network substrate, although developed in the AI / dataflow / constraint-propagation lineage (Sutherland → Steele → Borning → Sussman-Radul), is structurally an instance of Saraswat's *concurrent constraint programming* family cc(D), with cc's tell/ask discipline reading as monotone-cell-write/threshold-read and cc's closure-operator semantics (Saraswat-Rinard-Panangaden 1991) carrying through verbatim. The architectural moves the project has independently developed (dynamic topology, stratification, per-cell decomposition) are extensions of the cc(D) framework rather than departures from it.

2. *Algebraic*: The natural extension of the cc(D) framework to handle asynchrony is *quantale enrichment*. Bistarelli-Montanari-Rossi's c-semirings (J. ACM 1997) — formally commutative quantales when completed — give *soft CCP* (Bistarelli-Montanari-Rossi 2006), in which the constraint store carries quantitative grading rather than Boolean entailment. The project's tropical-quantale ((min, +)) cost layer on cells (PPN 4C addendum, 2026-04-26) is an instance of soft cc(Q) for Q the Lawvere quantale. Generalising to other quantales gives cost (tropical), causal order (vector-clock-style ℕ-modules), probability ([0,1] under multiplication), resource (effect quantales), and combinations (e.g., ℕ ⊗ T for round-paired-with-cost causal clocks). The hypothesis is that all of asynchrony's algebraic content — composition, ordering, fairness, cost, resource, locality — admits a unified treatment in a quantale-enriched setting.

The hypothesis is not a theorem the artifact will prove. It is a *direction for design-track work*. The artifact assembles evidence that the direction is viable and that the framework's formal antecedents are well-developed. Sections §8.1 through §8.7 argue specific consequences of the hypothesis; §8.8 calibrates how strongly each consequence is supported by the assembled evidence.

### §1.3 The artifact's scope and tone

The artifact is *neutral with a strong lean*. The era surveys (§2–§6) are written without arguing the hypothesis; each surveyor flagged hypothesis-supporting connections where they arose naturally but did not advance the case. The synthesis chapter (§8) is the place where the lean is allowed to be argued, with calibrated honesty about which claims are settled, which are strongly supported, and which remain hypothesis-level.

The tone is third-person scholarly throughout. Where the artifact takes a position, it does so explicitly and defensibly. Where the published literature is silent or contested, the artifact says so. Where the project's own design diverges from the literature it builds on, the artifact catalogues the divergence rather than eliding it.

The artifact addresses six audiences:

1. *Prologos project members* needing a thorough grounding in the async literature for follow-on design tracks.
2. *Future readers of project artifacts* who will encounter the cross-references this artifact establishes.
3. *Adversarial reviewers* (internal or external) who will challenge the working hypothesis or specific claims.
4. *Researchers in adjacent fields* (CCP, quantale theory, distributed systems, capability-secure programming) for whom the artifact is one possible point of contact with the project.
5. *Implementation engineers* who will build the L1, L2, and L3 layers and need design-precedent evidence.
6. *Track designers* who will refine the working hypothesis into specific track scopes (per §9).

The artifact does *not* address: end users of the language as a programming environment; an introductory-level audience for whom the era surveys would be too dense; readers seeking only the practical-engineering content.

### §1.4 What the artifact establishes

The artifact establishes four things, in roughly decreasing order of strength.

*First*, that propagator networks instantiate cc(D) at the per-cell-store level. This is settled by §3.3.7 and §8.1; the structural correspondence is tight, the closure-operator semantics carries through, and the cc(D) framework's published results apply.

*Second*, that the quantale-enriched extension of cc(D) — soft cc(Q) — is the natural setting for the project's tropical-quantale cost layer, with the connection passing through Bistarelli-Montanari-Rossi 1997's c-semiring framework. This is established by §3.3.6 and §8.6, with the completion-conditions caveat that the c-semiring must be completed under arbitrary suprema for the quantale identification to be exact.

*Third*, that several Era IV / Era V developments — algebraic effect handlers, structured concurrency, BloomL, capability-passing style, choreographic programming — admit recasting as projections of a quantale-enriched substrate. This is supported by §5, §6.4, and §8.6 but not demonstrated; the recasting is a research program rather than a result.

*Fourth*, that significant gaps in the published 2020–2026 literature — gap (b) "propagator network as quantale-enriched category", gap (d) "unified treatment of cost+order+fairness+resource", gap (h) "function coloring at the substrate level" — are tractable rather than speculative, and the project's design tracks are positioned to address them. This is supported by §6.6 and §8.7.

What the artifact does *not* establish, but argues is the right direction to test: that the unified treatment of cost, ordering, fairness, and resource as quantale projections of one substrate (H4 in §8.8) is achievable. This is the genuine frontier hypothesis the project's design tracks will, severally, validate or refute.

## §2 — Era I: Foundational concurrency theory (1965–1990)

The foundational era of concurrency theory begins, almost arbitrarily, in 1965, when Edsger Dijkstra circulated EWD 123 — *Cooperating sequential processes* — within the Mathematical Centre at the Technological University of Eindhoven. It ends, equally arbitrarily, around 1990, when Vijay Saraswat's thesis *Concurrent Constraint Programming Languages* [Saraswat 1989] generalizes a quarter-century of process-algebraic effort into the cc(D) family that the present project takes as one of its conceptual antecedents. The era's intellectual architecture is dominated by two operational shapes that arose in different communities and that subsequent decades have not fully reconciled: the **synchronous rendezvous** of Hoare's CSP and Milner's CCS, in which two processes meet at a precisely-timed handshake and exchange information atomically; and the **asynchronous send-without-acknowledgment** of Hewitt's actors, refined later by Honda–Tokoro [1991] and Boudol [1992] into asynchronous variants of the π-calculus. The split between these two operational shapes is what §2.6 names the *sync–rendezvous vs. async–buffered dichotomy* and treats as a load-bearing diagnostic for everything that follows: every later attempt to unify asynchrony — futures, promises, eventual sends, async/await, channels, CCP variables — can be read as an effort to either subsume one shape under the other, refuse the choice entirely, or re-express both as projections from some richer algebraic structure. The working hypothesis underlying the research synthesis (asynchrony as the operational semantics of computation in a quantale-enriched category over a propagator substrate) reads the era's struggle as exactly this last project.

### §2.1 Synchronization primitives — Dijkstra, Brinch Hansen, Hoare, and the OS-thread substrate

Dijkstra's *Cooperating sequential processes* [Dijkstra 1965/1968] is a sui generis document: simultaneously the founding paper of concurrent programming, the introduction of mutual exclusion as a concept, the introduction of the producer–consumer pattern, and the introduction of the semaphore as a synchronization primitive carrying the algebraic surface that all later primitives have built on or argued against. The paper begins with the famous concession — that one cannot reason about a concurrent program by extending Floyd-style sequential reasoning — and proposes instead an indivisibility discipline: certain operations on shared variables are *atomic with respect to other concurrent operations*, and these atomic operations are the only objects of correctness reasoning. The semaphore is then defined as a non-negative integer accessed only through two indivisible primitives, P and V (from Dutch *prolaag* and *verhoog*), which decrement and increment under the constraint that a P-operation suspends its caller if decrementing would yield a negative value. Crucially, Dijkstra observes that the primitives' semantics already commit to a scheduling policy: in the canonical reading, blocked P operations must eventually be served (no permanent starvation), but the order is not specified. The *fairness* obligation — what would later be formalized via Park's omega-regular fair-concurrency operators [Park 1981] — is born inseparably from the synchronization primitive itself.

The central technical move in EWD 123 is one that subsequent eras have not improved on at the level of *primitive*: the introduction of an algebraic surface where primitive operations are visible to a correctness proof and the operational refinements (busy-wait spinlocks, kernel-blocking semaphores, monitor-implemented condition variables) are buried beneath. This separation between an algebra-of-primitives and a substrate-of-implementation is the structural frame within which everything Era I produces will sit.

By 1972–73, Dijkstra's collaborators had begun complaining about the discipline cost. Per Brinch Hansen's *Operating System Principles* [Brinch Hansen 1973] — described in retrospect as the first comprehensive operating-systems textbook — argues that a programming language that exposes only semaphores is too low-level for systems engineering; the burden of writing the V at every exit path of every critical section, including exception paths, makes correctness fragile in the same way that explicit memory deallocation makes correctness fragile. Brinch Hansen's response is the **monitor**: a syntactic construct that bundles state and the operations on it into a unit guaranteeing mutual exclusion between operations [Brinch Hansen 1973; Hoare 1974]. The monitor's proof rule, formalized by Hoare in the canonical *Monitors: an operating system structuring concept* [Hoare 1974], reduces to: any monitor invariant that holds at entry holds at exit, modulo a controlled escape via *condition variables* on which a process may `wait` (releasing the lock) and another may `signal` (resuming a waiter).

The signal semantics, however, fragmented immediately. Hoare's original 1974 reading was *immediate-resumption*: `signal` implicitly transfers the monitor lock to the awakened waiter, who proceeds atomically with the still-valid invariant. The Mesa team at Xerox PARC, building a real production system at scale, found the cost of this discipline prohibitive [Lampson & Redell 1980]. Mesa monitors instead adopted *signal-and-continue*: the signaller retains the lock; the signalled process becomes runnable but, on resumption, must re-check the condition because the world may have shifted. The shift from `if cond.wait()` to `while not cond_predicate: cond.wait()` — a discipline now universal in POSIX `pthread_cond_wait`, Java `Object.wait`, and every imitator — is a *direct artefact* of this 1980 design choice. Lampson and Redell also introduced `cond.broadcast`, observing that signalling exactly one waiter is sometimes the wrong scheduling decision and that a fan-in alarm-clock pattern wants to wake all waiters and let predicate re-checks sort out who proceeds. The Mesa paper deserves more attention than its citation count suggests: it is the first systematic catalogue of how monitor discipline collapses under timeouts, aborts, exception propagation, nested monitor calls, and creation/destruction of monitor instances — precisely the entanglements that later async runtimes (cancellation in modern async/await; supervision trees in Erlang/OTP) had to re-solve.

The OS-thread substrate that emerged from this lineage — Mach, Solaris LWPs, Linux 2.6's NPTL, Windows native threads — is at its heart Dijkstra's primitives plus Hoare's monitors plus Lampson–Redell's broadcast and re-checking discipline plus a kernel scheduler that implements fairness empirically rather than algebraically. The architecture is robust in the engineering sense and lattice-hostile in the structural sense: every cell of state has identity tied to mutual exclusion, every read potentially blocks, and the compositional algebra of Dijkstra's original primitive is buried under decades of pragmatic accretion. The propagator-network substrate this project takes as foundational is in some sense an answer to the lattice-hostility of the OS-thread tradition: cells valued in lattices with monotone merge, propagators that fire when input cells refine, no mutual exclusion as a primitive at all.

### §2.2 The process-algebra trinity — CSP, CCS, π-calculus

The 1970s closed with two near-simultaneous attempts to give concurrency a mathematics rather than a folklore. Hoare's *Communicating Sequential Processes* [Hoare 1978] was published in *Communications of the ACM* in August 1978; Milner's *A Calculus of Communicating Systems* [Milner 1980] appeared as Lecture Notes in Computer Science volume 92 two years later. Both were responses to the perceived failure of monitor-and-semaphore discipline to scale to a general theory of concurrent computation. Both rejected shared mutable state as the primitive object of reasoning and replaced it with **communication** — explicit, named, point-to-point exchange between named processes. Both committed to **synchronous rendezvous**: the act of communication is a single atomic event jointly executed by two parties.

The original 1978 CSP paper [Hoare 1978] is a programming-language paper, not a process-algebra paper: it proposes a syntax — guarded commands extended with input commands `?` and output commands `!`, parallel composition `||`, named processes — and argues, by example, that an enormous range of concurrent programming patterns can be expressed without semaphores, monitors, or shared variables. The communication construct itself is a rendezvous: `P!v` (process P sending value v) blocks until some matching `Q?x` (process Q receiving into variable x) is jointly available; the two complete atomically and, in a fundamental simplification of the model, neither side can buffer. The original paper offers no formal denotational semantics — Hoare presents it as a programming style — and a number of the design choices, particularly the inability to express unbounded nondeterminism and the conflation of process names with channel names, generated dissatisfaction. The 1985 book *Communicating Sequential Processes* [Hoare 1985] is a different mathematical object: developed jointly with Stephen Brookes and A. W. Roscoe over the early 1980s, it gives CSP a denotational failures-divergences semantics and crystallizes an algebra of operators (prefix, choice `[]`, internal choice `|~|`, parallel `||`, hide `\`) admitting an equational theory, model-checking via FDR, and extensive refinement-based reasoning [Brookes, Hoare & Roscoe 1984].

Milner's CCS, developed at Edinburgh's LFCS in the late 1970s and presented in [Milner 1980], differs from CSP in a crucial methodological respect: it is built around **bisimulation**, not refinement, as the primary equivalence. The 1980 monograph introduces the operators that have become standard — action prefix `a.P`, summation `+`, parallel composition `|` with synchronization on complementary actions `a` and `\bar{a}`, restriction `\nu`, recursion — and presents an operational semantics via labelled transition systems. Milner's *Communication and Concurrency* [Milner 1989] reorganizes the theory and elevates bisimulation to the central notion. Bisimulation, as a coinductive equivalence, sits at the structural-thinking layer that the present project's SRE lattice lens explicitly evokes (see structural-thinking.md): two systems are bisimilar iff they can mutually simulate each other's actions step-for-step, which is precisely the kind of structural-emergent equivalence that survives translation into a propagator-cell formulation. The bisimulation method is itself attributed to David Park's [1981] *Concurrency and Automata on Infinite Sequences*, where it appears almost incidentally as a proof technique for omega-regular language equivalence, before Milner makes it the central object of process-algebraic reasoning. Davide Sangiorgi's historical survey [Sangiorgi 2009] traces in detail the slightly tangled origin of bisimulation: Milner's [1980] notion of *strong simulation*, generalized by Park into the symmetric two-way structure that became *bisimulation*, then re-imported by Milner.

The π-calculus arrives in 1989/1992 as Milner's response to a limitation he had grown to see in CCS: communication channels are static. Two processes can synchronize over a fixed channel, but a process cannot *send a channel name as a value* and thus dynamically reconfigure the communication topology. The two-part *A Calculus of Mobile Processes* by Milner, Parrow, and Walker [Milner, Parrow, Walker 1992a, 1992b] resolves this with a tiny syntactic extension whose consequences are out of all proportion: actions become `\bar{a}\langle b \rangle` (output the name `b` on channel `a`) and `a(x)` (input on `a`, binding `x`), and the resulting calculus turns out to encode the lambda calculus, encode object-oriented method dispatch, and serve as the lingua franca for the next two decades of concurrent-language semantics. Sangiorgi and Walker's textbook [Sangiorgi & Walker 2001] consolidates the theory: typed π-calculi, higher-order processes, the relationship to lambda calculus, and applications to object-oriented design.

The π-calculus matters for the present synthesis for two reasons. First, *names* are the primitive object: a process's identity is given by the names it can send and receive, and dynamic generation of fresh names (via `\nu`) is the algebraic encoding of object creation, channel creation, scope creation, even nonce generation in cryptographic protocols. Second, the original π-calculus is *synchronous* — every output blocks until matched by an input — and the question of whether an asynchronous variant is fundamentally weaker or merely a notational simplification becomes a load-bearing technical question that §2.6 below treats explicitly.

### §2.3 Actors — Hewitt, Agha, and the Erlang lineage

While Hoare and Milner were building synchronous process algebras, Carl Hewitt at MIT was building something operationally and philosophically distinct. The 1973 IJCAI paper *A Universal Modular ACTOR Formalism for Artificial Intelligence* [Hewitt, Bishop & Steiger 1973] proposes **actors** as a single uniform object: every entity is an actor, communication occurs by sending messages addressed to actors, and actor behaviour is defined by what messages it accepts and what new actors it creates upon receipt. There is no shared state, no synchronous handshake, no joint atomic event between sender and receiver. Sends are *fire-and-forget*: the sender continues immediately, the message arrives eventually (or not) at the receiver, and the receiver processes one message at a time in a loop driven by its own continuation. Hewitt's later [1977] *Viewing Control Structures as Patterns of Passing Messages* takes the radical position that all of programming-language control flow — sequential composition, conditionals, loops, exceptions, function call/return, even iteration over data structures — can be expressed as message-passing protocols among actors, with the apparent variety of control constructs reducing to *patterns of message routing*.

Two structural commitments distinguish actors from CSP/CCS at the level of operational semantics. **First**, actor sends are asynchronous in the strong sense — there is no joint event that includes both the sender's send-action and the receiver's receive-action, only a sender-local send followed by a non-deterministically-later receiver-local receive. This implies the existence of an *unbounded mailbox* at every actor (formal models permit unbounded buffering) and forces unbounded nondeterminism, which the original CSP could not express. **Second**, actor identities are first-class addresses: actors can be created dynamically, their addresses passed in messages, and the communication topology evolves as a consequence of computation. The 1973 Hewitt formalism predates the 1989 π-calculus by sixteen years on this exact point; the π-calculus's *names* are, structurally, an algebraicization of what Hewitt's actor *addresses* had already been.

Gul Agha's PhD thesis at MIT, published as *Actors: A Model of Concurrent Computation in Distributed Systems* [Agha 1986], gives the actor model its first systematic semantics. Agha distinguishes actor *behaviours* (what an actor does on receiving a message) from actor *configurations* (snapshots of a system of actors and a multiset of in-flight messages), defines a transition system over configurations, and proves a number of equational properties. Agha's treatment makes explicit a methodological commitment that Era II will absorb (see §3 — neighbouring slice on categorical semantics): an actor is a *behavior* (a function from message to (effects, next-behavior)), and the apparently stateful actor is the trace of a sequence of behaviours each of which produced its successor. This view recovers a kind of equational reasoning even without the algebraic infrastructure of CCS or CSP.

The Erlang lineage [Armstrong 2003] takes Hewitt's actors and Agha's mathematics into an industrial setting. Erlang was developed at Ericsson Computer Science Laboratory beginning in 1986 by Joe Armstrong, Robert Virding, and Mike Williams, in response to the requirement that Ericsson telephone exchanges *never go down*. The design commitments — share-nothing concurrency, asynchronous message passing as the only inter-process primitive, supervision trees as the structural pattern for fault-tolerance, "let it crash" as the discipline for handling unanticipated errors — are an engineering reading of Hewitt's actors. Armstrong's 2003 thesis *Making reliable distributed systems in the presence of software errors* [Armstrong 2003] makes the deeper claim explicit: actor-style isolation between processes is not merely a concurrency idiom but the structural prerequisite for *fault tolerance*, because state isolation means that the failure of one process cannot corrupt the state of another, and supervision means that recovery is composable. The relationship to the propagator-network substrate is suggestive: cells in a propagator network are also share-nothing in the sense that they are accessed only through monotone merge, and the failure of a propagator leaves its cell at the join of all successful writes — a CALM-monotone analogue of "let it crash."

### §2.4 Futures and dataflow — MultiLisp, Id, Lucid, I-structures

A different community, working in functional and applicative programming, arrived at the asynchrony question from a different direction. Rather than asking *how should concurrent processes communicate* — the CSP/CCS/actor question — they asked *how should an applicative computation expose the parallelism it implicitly contains*, given that pure functions have no observable order-of-evaluation dependencies. This direction begins with Friedman and Wise's [1976] *The Impact of Applicative Programming on Multiprocessing*, which introduces the term **promise** for a placeholder representing the result of a not-yet-completed parallel computation, and Hewitt and Baker's [1977] *The Incremental Garbage Collection of Processes*, which introduces the term **future** for the same notion in the context of a parallel argument-evaluation strategy that Baker and Hewitt call *call-by-future*. Promises and futures are, mathematically, the same object — a write-once cell with monotone resolution from `pending` to `value` — viewed once from the producer side (the promise is what the computation *will deliver*) and once from the consumer side (the future is what the computation *can wait on*).

Robert Halstead's *MULTILISP: A Language for Concurrent Symbolic Computation* [Halstead 1985], published in TOPLAS, is the first widely-known production language to make futures a first-class linguistic construct. MultiLisp extends Scheme with the `(future expr)` form: evaluating `(future expr)` immediately returns a future-cell that will, in parallel, run `expr` to completion and write the result; any operation that demands the value of the future blocks until the computation completes. The combination is enormously expressive: `(future (factorial 100))` is a parallel call, `(map future expressions)` is a parallel map, and the type of the future is invisible to the rest of the program (touching the future blocks if necessary; otherwise it is transparent). MultiLisp ran on the Concert shared-memory multiprocessor at MIT and demonstrated, with measured benchmarks, that *speculative parallel evaluation before results are known to be required* could yield speedups over sequential evaluation — an early data point for what would become speculative execution in modern CPUs.

The dataflow community, working largely in parallel with Hewitt's actors but much closer to Halstead's futures, generalized the future-cell to entire data structures. Jack Dennis and David Misunas [1975] introduced the dataflow architecture proper at MIT, in which an instruction fires when its operands are available rather than when a sequencer reaches it — fundamentally inverting the von Neumann fetch-execute-cycle. Wadge and Ashcroft's *Lucid* [Wadge & Ashcroft 1985], developed from 1976 onward, takes the dataflow perspective into language design: every variable is a *stream* — an infinite sequence of values indexed by time-step — and computation is expressed via stream-equations rather than sequential assignment. Arvind and Nikhil's *Id*, and its successor *Id Nouveau* at MIT's Computation Structures Group [Arvind, Nikhil & Pingali 1989], extends functional programming with **I-structures** — write-once, read-many cells with implicit synchronization where readers block until the cell is written — and **M-structures** — mutable cells with synchronized take/put discipline supporting destructive update without losing determinacy in the surrounding pure code. The TOPLAS paper *I-Structures: Data Structures for Parallel Computing* [Arvind, Nikhil & Pingali 1989] argues that I-structures resolve a long-standing tension in functional parallelism: pure functional data structures admit unlimited parallelism but force expensive copying for incremental updates; imperative data structures admit cheap update but lose determinism. I-structures preserve determinism (a cell is written exactly once; reads are insensitive to write timing) while enabling cheap incremental update. The relationship to the propagator-substrate of the present project is direct: an I-structure cell is a write-once cell over a discrete two-element lattice (`bottom \sqsubseteq value`), an M-structure cell is a more elaborate lattice with take/put admitting a quantitative-type-theoretic linear treatment (the value at the cell is consumed on take), and Lucid's stream variables are propagator-style cells over an infinite-history lattice. The dataflow community's data structures *are* lattice-valued cells under monotone merge — they did not name them as such because the lattice-theoretic framing belongs to the next era.

### §2.5 CML events — first-class synchronization values

By the late 1980s, the CSP/CCS lineage had given concurrent programming a process algebra; the actor lineage had given it asynchronous message passing; the dataflow lineage had given it write-once cells. None had given it a way for a programmer to *abstract over the choice of synchronization*. If a library exposes a channel-receive operation, and a client wants to wait on that channel *or* a timeout *or* another channel, the client must reach into the library's implementation; synchronization choice is not first-class. John Reppy's PhD work at Cornell [Reppy 1991, 1999] addresses exactly this gap.

*CML: A Higher-Order Concurrent Language* [Reppy 1991], published at PLDI '91, introduces a domain of values called **events**. An event is a *synchronous operation reified as a value*: `recvEvt(ch)` is the event-of-receiving-on-`ch`, `sendEvt(ch, v)` is the event-of-sending-`v`-on-`ch`, and so on. Crucially, events admit an algebra of combinators: `choose(e1, e2)` is the event of synchronizing-on-either-`e1`-or-`e2` (with the choice resolved at sync time by which becomes ready first), `wrap(e, f)` post-processes the value produced by `e` through function `f`, `guard(thunk)` defers event construction until sync time. The event algebra is closed under sync: an event of arbitrary structural complexity is consumed by a single `sync` call that either synchronizes on the chosen base event or blocks until one becomes available.

The architectural significance of CML's events is that they make **synchronization compositional**. A library can expose its synchronization primitives as events; clients can combine them using the event algebra without breaking the library's encapsulation; new patterns (transactional events, multi-party rendezvous, composable timeouts) can be expressed without changes to the runtime. Reppy's *Concurrent Programming in ML* book [Reppy 1999] develops the full theory and exhibits a parallel build system, a window manager, and a distributed tuple-space implementation as illustrating examples. The CML approach has been re-implemented in Haskell (as *Software Transactional Memory* with `STM` as the event-monad), in Racket (as *Concurrent ML* in the Racket runtime), in F# (as *async workflows* with composable continuations), and in Go (where `select` is the event-choose operator, though the algebra is not closed under additional combinators). The propagator-substrate analogue is suggestive: a CML event is, structurally, a *describable potential synchronization* — exactly what a propagator that watches a cell-readiness signal but has not yet fired *is*. CML's contribution to the synthesis the present project is reaching toward is the demonstration that synchronization itself can be a first-class compositional object — not just a primitive bolted into the language but a value with an algebra. This becomes load-bearing in §2.6 and beyond.

### §2.6 The sync-rendezvous vs async-buffered dichotomy — Honda–Tokoro and Boudol

The dichotomy that Era I produces — and that subsequent eras have struggled to overcome — emerges most clearly in the specific encoding question that Honda–Tokoro and Boudol both addressed in 1991–1992.

The CSP/CCS/π-calculus tradition had committed to **synchronous rendezvous**: a send and a matching receive constitute a single atomic event. Synchronous communication has a number of mathematically attractive properties: bisimulation equivalences are well-behaved, the labelled transition system is finite-branching when the process syntax is, and the equational theory of CCS-style channels has clean axiomatizations. The actor tradition, in contrast, had committed to **asynchronous send-without-acknowledgment**: send is sender-local, receive is receiver-local, no joint event exists, mailboxes are unbounded. Asynchronous communication has a number of operationally-attractive properties: non-blocking sends compose naturally with pure computation, fault-isolation is structural (the receiver's failure does not back-propagate into the sender), and distributed-system implementation is direct (the asynchrony is already what the network gives you).

The two traditions produced expressively-different calculi and the question — *are they equivalent up to encoding?* — was not idle. Kohei Honda and Mario Tokoro's *An Object Calculus for Asynchronous Communication* [Honda & Tokoro 1991], published at ECOOP '91, introduces the asynchronous π-calculus by *omitting* output-prefixing: in the asynchronous π-calculus, `\bar{a}\langle v \rangle` is itself a complete process (a "message in flight"), not a prefix that continues with `\bar{a}\langle v \rangle.P`. The expressive cost is that a sender cannot directly express "send `v` and *then* do `P`" without an explicit acknowledgment from the receiver. Gérard Boudol's INRIA technical report *Asynchrony and the Pi-Calculus* [Boudol 1992] gives an independent presentation of essentially the same calculus and proves that *output-prefixing can be encoded* within asynchronous communication using fresh-name passing: `\bar{a}\langle v \rangle.P` becomes `\nu c. (\bar{a}\langle v, c \rangle | c().P)` — send `v` along with a fresh acknowledgment channel `c`, and await an acknowledgment on `c` before continuing. The encoding works, but it imposes a coordination round-trip that the synchronous original did not require.

The deeper question — whether synchronous π and asynchronous π are *expressively equivalent in the bisimulation sense* — was settled negatively by Catuscia Palamidessi [Palamidessi 1997, 2003]: synchronous π can express *mixed-choice* (choice over inputs and outputs) which asynchronous π cannot encode while preserving reasonable equivalences. The two calculi are *not* equivalent; the synchronous tradition is strictly more expressive for problems that require joint coordination, and the asynchronous tradition is strictly more aligned with operational reality for distributed systems. **The dichotomy is real, mathematically witnessed, and not a notational artefact.**

The cost the dichotomy imposes on the broader concurrency-theory project is the recurring obligation to choose. Every later language that introduces async primitives has had to decide: are channels synchronous (Go-style `chan` with optional buffering, but blocking-by-default), asynchronous (Erlang-style mailboxes, never blocking the sender), or some hybrid (Reppy's CML events, where both kinds exist as separate event-values and the programmer composes); are futures eager or lazy; are awaits structural (try-with-resources, structured-concurrency scopes) or scheduled (await-anywhere, with no syntactic gate); is fairness a property of the scheduler or a property of the language semantics. None of these choices have a forced answer from operational concerns alone; the answer depends on what algebraic structure one expects synchronization to admit, and that algebraic structure is exactly what Era I left under-specified.

The propagator-network substrate that the present project takes as foundational reads the dichotomy as a symptom of a deeper architectural choice. A propagator is, by construction, *neither* synchronous-rendezvous *nor* asynchronous-fire-and-forget: it is *information-flow-on-network*. A propagator fires when its input cells refine; the firing is not synchronized with the cell-write that triggered it (asynchronous in that sense), but it is also not a fire-and-forget message because the propagator's output is, in turn, a monotone refinement of an output cell observable by other propagators (synchronous in *that* sense). The lattice-monotone framing — cells valued in a join-semilattice, propagators as monotone functions, fairness as a Tarski-fixpoint termination guarantee — *dissolves* the sync/async dichotomy by replacing both shapes with a third: refinement on a lattice. The working hypothesis underlying this synthesis claims that what Era I produced as two operational shapes are, properly understood, two projections from a single algebraic object — the quantale-enriched propagator network — and that the era's failure to unify them traces directly to the absence of the algebraic structure (quantales, enriched categories, and the constraint-system structure D that Saraswat's cc(D) family begins to articulate) that the synthesis chapter [working hypothesis above] takes as load-bearing.

§2.6 is the diagnostic the rest of the synthesis must absorb. Subsequent eras introduce richer constructs (futures, promises, async/await, channels, streams, eventual sends, transactional events) but *none* of them exits the dichotomy at the level of operational shape. They merely choose a side and decorate it. The exit, if there is one, lies in the algebraic re-framing that Era II — categorical and game-semantic accounts (see §3, neighboring slice) — and Era III (CCP and onward, see §3.3 neighbouring slice) attempt.

### §2.7 The bridge to Era II — concurrent logic languages

Between the foundational era and the Era II algebraic re-framings sits the concurrent logic programming community, which deserves brief mention here as a transitional moment. PARLOG (Clark and Gregory 1986), Concurrent Prolog (Shapiro 1986), and Guarded Horn Clauses (Ueda 1986) all attempt to fuse the Hoare-style guarded-command discipline with Prolog's logic-programming substrate, expressing concurrency as parallel evaluation of clauses with shared logic variables serving the role of communication channels. Logic variables, once bound, are *write-once* — exactly the I-structure discipline of [Arvind, Nikhil & Pingali 1989] — and the unification operation that binds a variable is, structurally, a *monotone refinement on a constraint lattice*. The neighbouring slice §3.3 covers Saraswat's [1989, 1993] thesis and the cc(D) family that abstracts these languages into a parametric framework; the brief mention here is sufficient to flag that, by the late 1980s, multiple research communities had independently arrived at *write-once cells with monotone refinement* as a workable substrate for concurrency — the dataflow community as I/M-structures, the logic community as logic variables, the future-and-promise community as resolved-once placeholders. The convergence is striking and is one of the threads the synthesis chapter can lean on: the propagator substrate is not an exotic recent invention but a generalization of a structure that Era I produced under at least three distinct names.

[Armstrong 2003] Armstrong, Joe. *Making Reliable Distributed Systems in the Presence of Software Errors*. PhD thesis, Royal Institute of Technology, Stockholm, 2003. <https://erlang.org/download/armstrong_thesis_2003.pdf>
> Documents the Erlang research program at Ericsson (begun 1986) and argues that share-nothing actor-style isolation is the structural prerequisite for fault tolerance — supervision trees, "let it crash", hot-code replacement. Cited as the industrial-engineering instantiation of Hewitt/Agha actors. Connects to the propagator substrate's CALM-monotone fault model: failure as join-with-bottom rather than corrupted state.

[Arvind, Nikhil & Pingali 1989] Arvind, Rishiyur S. Nikhil, and Keshav K. Pingali. "I-Structures: Data Structures for Parallel Computing." *ACM Transactions on Programming Languages and Systems* 11(4):598–632, October 1989. <https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/69558.69562>

Direct ancestor of the propagator-network's lattice-valued cells with monotone merge.
