Cited in §2.5 as the first construct that makes synchronization itself compositional; structurally the closest Era I predecessor to the propagator-substrate's notion of "describable potential synchronization."

[Reppy 1999] Reppy, John H. *Concurrent Programming in ML*. Cambridge University Press, 1999. ISBN 0-521-48089-2.
> Book-length development of CML with extended examples (parallel build system, window manager, distributed tuple-space). Cited in §2.5 as the consolidated reference for first-class events; the patterns demonstrated here are echoed in Haskell STM, Racket CML, F# async workflows, and Go's `select`.

[Sangiorgi 2009] Sangiorgi, Davide. "On the Origins of Bisimulation and Coinduction." *ACM Transactions on Programming Languages and Systems* 31(4), 2009. <https://www.cs.unibo.it/~sangio/DOC_public/history_bis_coind.pdf>
> Historical survey tracing bisimulation's origin: Milner's [1980] strong-simulation, generalized by Park into symmetric bisimulation, then re-imported by Milner into [1989]. Cited briefly in §2.2 as authoritative source on the slightly tangled bisimulation chronology.

[Sangiorgi & Walker 2001] Sangiorgi, Davide, and David Walker. *The π-Calculus: A Theory of Mobile Processes*. Cambridge University Press, 2001. ISBN 0-521-78177-9.
> Textbook consolidation of π-calculus theory: typed variants, higher-order processes, relationship to lambda-calculus, applications to OO design. Cited in §2.2 as the standard graduate-level reference; the cross-cutting *names* abstraction it develops anticipates several Era II structural moves.

[Saraswat 1989] Saraswat, Vijay A. *Concurrent Constraint Programming Languages*. PhD thesis, Carnegie-Mellon University, January 1989. Published in revised form by MIT Press, 1993, in the ACM Distinguished Dissertation Series.
> Introduces the cc(D) family parameterized by a constraint system D. The thesis abstracts logic-variable-with-unification, write-once cells, and tell/ask synchronization into a uniform framework. Mentioned briefly in §2.7 as the bridge to Era II and Era III; deferred to neighbouring slice §3.3 (Agent 3) for full treatment. Load-bearing for the synthesis chapter's working hypothesis: cc(D) is the formal antecedent the propagator-network substrate generalizes.

[Saraswat, Rinard & Panangaden 1991] Saraswat, Vijay A., Martin Rinard, and Prakash Panangaden. "Semantic Foundations of Concurrent Constraint Programming." *POPL '91: 18th ACM Symposium on Principles of Programming Languages*, pp. 333–352, January 1991. <https://people.csail.mit.edu/rinard/paper/popl91.pdf>
> Gives cc(D) a denotational semantics in terms of *closure operators* on the constraint lattice — inflationary, monotonic, idempotent. Cited briefly in §2.7 as the formal-semantics companion to [Saraswat 1989]; the closure-operator view connects directly to the propagator-network's monotone-merge cell semantics. Deferred to §3.3 for full treatment.

[Shapiro 1986] Shapiro, Ehud. "Concurrent Prolog: A Progress Report." *IEEE Computer* 19(8):44–58, August 1986.
> Concurrent Prolog: dataflow synchronization on logic variables, guarded-command nondeterminism. Mentioned in §2.7 as one of the three near-simultaneous concurrent-logic-language proposals; deferred to neighbouring slice §3.3.

[Ueda 1986] Ueda, Kazunori. "Guarded Horn Clauses." EngD thesis, University of Tokyo, 1986. <https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/Guarded-Horn-Clauses-Ueda/b0dfca5746a707bb3920fe9c23826322c2e85f3b>
> GHC: a flat concurrent logic language combining Hoare-guarded commands with Horn-clause logic. Mentioned in §2.7; deferred to §3.3.

[Wadge & Ashcroft 1985] Wadge, William W., and Edward A. Ashcroft. *Lucid, the Dataflow Programming Language*. Academic Press, 1985.
> Lucid: every variable is an infinite stream indexed by time-step; computation is expressed as stream-equations. Cited in §2.4 as the dataflow tradition's linguistic instantiation of "variables as histories" — structurally adjacent to the propagator-network's cells-as-monotone-histories.

---

# Section 3 — Era II: Algebraic and Categorical Semantics of Concurrency (1985–2005)

## §3.1 Linear Logic and the Concurrent Reading of Proofs

### 3.1.1 Girard's Linear Logic: resources, polarities, exponentials

The publication of Jean-Yves Girard's "Linear Logic" in *Theoretical Computer Science* 50 (1987) [Girard1987] is, for the purposes of this survey, the inflection point at which proof theory ceases to be a tool for the foundations of mathematics and becomes a generative grammar for concurrent processes. The technical move is the refusal of structural rules — contraction (`A ⊢ B` does not imply `A, A ⊢ B`) and weakening (`A ⊢ B` does not imply `A, C ⊢ B`) are removed from the sequent calculus and re-introduced selectively through the *exponential* modalities `!A` ("of course A", a positive connective permitting unbounded use) and `?A` ("why not A", its negative dual). The connectives split into two groups along an orthogonal axis. The *multiplicatives* `⊗` (tensor) and `⅋` (par) describe the parallel composition of two resources that must both occur exactly once; the *additives* `&` (with) and `⊕` (plus) describe a deterministic choice between alternatives, of which exactly one is used. Negation `(·)^⊥` is involutive and exchanges the two halves of each pair: `(A ⊗ B)^⊥ = A^⊥ ⅋ B^⊥`, `(A & B)^⊥ = A^⊥ ⊕ B^⊥`, `(!A)^⊥ = ?A^⊥`. As Stanford's encyclopaedic treatment notes, the connectives `⊗, ⊕, 1, 0, !` are positive while their duals `⅋, &, ⊥, ⊤, ?` are negative [SEPLinear]; this polarity is not a notational convenience but a phase distinction that game semantics will subsequently realise as the alternation between Proponent and Opponent moves.

The exponentials are governed by four rules — *contraction, weakening, dereliction, promotion* — and the asymmetry between them encodes the asymmetry between read-once and read-many resources. Promotion `⊢ Γ ⊢ A / ⊢ ?Γ ⊢ !A` is contextual: it requires every passive formula in the conclusion to be wrapped in a `?`-modality, mimicking the necessity rule of S4 modal logic. Dereliction is the cost of moving from the unbounded world `!A` back to the linear world `A`; it is the local act of "spending" one copy of an indefinitely available resource [LLBook2024]. The reading proposed by Girard is computational from the start: a proof of `A ⊸ B` (linear implication, defined as `A^⊥ ⅋ B`) is a function that consumes exactly one resource of type `A` and produces exactly one of type `B`. The classical lambda calculus, by contrast, lives only in the `!`-fragment, where `A ⇒ B` decomposes into `!A ⊸ B` — every classical function may consult its argument arbitrarily many times.

The architectural relevance of this decomposition for a propagator-network substrate is straightforward to state. A linear cell is a cell that, in the working hypothesis of this survey, holds a *consumed-once* token; its merge function is a partial operation that either accepts a unique write or detects a use-after-consume conflict. A cell of type `!A` is the propagator-network analogue of a piece of paper on a copying machine, in Girard's image: its content is a potentiality, copyable on demand. The QTT multiplicities `m0, m1, mw` of the Prologos elaborator (see project glossary) are the syntactic shadow of Girard's exponentials lifted to a dependent type theory; the coincidence between linear logic's resource-counting and propagator networks' lattice-monotone token semantics is the substrate on which the synthesis chapter will build.

### 3.1.2 Geometry of Interaction: cut-elimination as dynamics

Almost immediately after introducing linear logic, Girard pursued the *Geometry of Interaction* (GoI) programme, beginning with "Geometry of Interaction I: Interpretation of System F" (Logic Colloquium 1988, published 1989) [GirardGoI1] and "Geometry of Interaction II: Deadlock-Free Algorithms" (COLOG-88, 1988) [GirardGoI2]; the programme continued through "GoI III" and beyond into the 1990s [GoIWiki]. The motivating question is not *what* a proof denotes (its static meaning, captured by a denotational model) but *how* a proof reduces under cut elimination — its *dynamics*. Girard's answer is that cut elimination can be encoded as a single fixed-point equation, the *execution formula*, on operators in a Banach space `B(H)` of bounded operators on the Hilbert space `H = ℓ²` of square-summable infinite sequences [GoIWiki]. A proof becomes a partial isometry; cut elimination becomes an algebraic computation on its restriction to a feedback subspace. The first major application of GoI was a clean analysis of Lamping's algorithm for optimal lambda-calculus reduction; through the 1990s GoI became the technical bridge between proof normalisation and abstract machines, with strong influence on game semantics for both linear logic and PCF [GoIWiki].

The relevance of GoI to the working hypothesis is again architectural. GoI replaces a sequent (a static syntactic object) by an operator that *interacts* with another operator at a designated interface — the cut formula — and the interaction is computed by the execution formula, which iterates the partial isometry until quiescence. This is recognisably the BSP loop of a propagator network: a proof is a propagator, a cut is a shared cell, and the execution formula is the fixed-point of monotone information flow at the interface. Abramsky and Jagadeesan's "New Foundations for the Geometry of Interaction" (*Information and Computation* 111, 1994) [AbramskyJagadeesan1994] reformulated GoI in terms of *traced monoidal categories* and ultimately *linear combinatory algebras* [AbramskyGoI2002], replacing operator-theoretic specifics with a purely categorical interface and clarifying that the trace operator (the categorical feedback) is what does the work. The conceptual yield, important for the synthesis, is that a category with an iteration operator on its hom-sets is an axiomatic propagator substrate; GoI provides the proof-theoretic vocabulary in which feedback-and-fixpoint becomes proof-and-cut.

### 3.1.3 Computational interpretations and the "proofs as processes" paradigm

Abramsky's "Computational Interpretations of Linear Logic" (*Theoretical Computer Science* 111, 1993) [Abramsky1993] is the canonical articulation of how linear-logic proofs become process terms. For the intuitionistic fragment, Abramsky derives a refinement of the lambda calculus in which order of evaluation and storage allocation become explicit; the Curry–Howard isomorphism is preserved, with proofs corresponding to programs and cut elimination to computation. For the classical fragment, the picture pivots: a proof of a multi-conclusion sequent `⊢ A₁, ..., Aₙ` corresponds to a *concurrent process* with `n` ports, and cut elimination becomes communication between ports — a process-calculus reduction in the style of Berry and Boudol's chemical abstract machine. This is the genesis of the "proofs as processes" slogan, which Bellin and Scott then formalised in detail.

Bellin and Scott's "On the π-calculus and Linear Logic" (*Theoretical Computer Science* 135, 1994) [BellinScott1994] gives a translation from classical linear logic (CLL) into Milner's synchronous π-calculus such that cut elimination converts proofs to proofs if and only if the translated π-terms reduce to one another. The translation is at the level of proof-nets — Girard's parallel-syntax presentation of CLL — and the soundness/completeness theorems are obtained for the multiplicative fragment with explicit information-flow analysis through proof-net cuts. The cost paid is the introduction of structural equivalences in the π-calculus (such as `x(y).x(z).P ≡ x(z).x(y).P`, permuting two reads on the same channel) which reflect the absence of sequencing in proof nets. Bellin and Scott did not extend the result cleanly to additives, and the axiom-restriction needed to obtain the tight correspondence (atomic-axiom restriction, or omitting the axiom rule entirely) became a recurring technical point in subsequent attempts.

### 3.1.4 Session types as linear propositions: Caires–Pfenning and Wadler

The Caires–Pfenning paper "Session Types as Intuitionistic Linear Propositions" (*CONCUR* 2010) [CairesPfenning2010] is the breakthrough that gave the proofs-as-processes programme a clean type-theoretic shape and brought linear logic into mainstream programming-languages research as the foundation of session types. Caires and Pfenning give a type system for a session-typed π-calculus that is *exactly* the sequent calculus of dual intuitionistic linear logic: the multiplicatives `⊗` and `⊸` correspond to the input and output of a channel within a session, the additives correspond to internal and external choice (`⊕` for offering a choice, `&` for selecting), and the exponentials provide the bridge between linear (single-use) and shared (replicated) channels. Type preservation and progress correspond to *session fidelity* (each session ending in the protocol it advertised) and *deadlock freedom*; the latter is automatic from the cut-elimination property of the underlying logic. Crucially, Caires and Pfenning establish a tight operational correspondence between π-calculus reduction and cut-elimination steps, yielding the first purely logical account of all major features of session types — including shared channels — in a single uniform framework.

Wadler's "Propositions as Sessions" (*ICFP* 2012; journal version *JFP* 24, 2014) [Wadler2012, Wadler2014] complements Caires and Pfenning by giving a similar treatment based on *classical* linear logic and by naming two languages — `CP` (a classical-LL session calculus) and `GV` (a linear functional language with session types) — together with a translation `GV → CP`. Wadler's deadlock-freedom theorem is striking in its directness: deadlock-freedom is a *consequence of the Curry–Howard correspondence* with linear logic; if the proof exists, the session does not deadlock. The Wadler treatment also formalises, for the first time, the connection between the standard presentations of session types in the operational-semantics tradition (Honda, Vasconcelos, Kubo) and the proof-theoretic setting [HondaVasconcelosKubo1998].

Pfenning and Griffith's "Polarized Substructural Session Types" (*FoSSaCS* 2015) [PfenningGriffith2015] refines the picture by introducing *polarities* into the session-type system, distinguishing positive (sender-sensitive) and negative (receiver-sensitive) types and recovering an explicit asynchrony in the operational semantics. Their language SILL integrates functional and message-passing concurrent programming under a single type discipline that guarantees both global progress (deadlock-freedom) and session fidelity (type preservation). The polarised account is technically important because it makes explicit that the asymmetry between a sending and a receiving end of a session — the operational fact that one side blocks until the other is ready — is the *same* asymmetry that linear logic encodes between positive and negative connectives; asynchrony, on this reading, is a polarity phenomenon, not a separate semantic feature requiring its own machinery. This is a load-bearing observation for the synthesis chapter: when the chapter makes the claim that asynchrony is a projection from a quantale-enriched algebraic object, the polarised-session-type tradition is one of the prior projections that empirically supports the claim.

### 3.1.5 *-autonomous categories as denotational substrate

The denotational counterpart of these proof-theoretic developments is the theory of *-autonomous categories. Barr's monograph "*-Autonomous categories and linear logic" (*Mathematical Structures in Computer Science* 1, 1991) [Barr1991] shows that *-autonomous categories — symmetric monoidal closed categories `(C, ⊗, I, ⊸)` equipped with a dualising object `⊥` such that the canonical map `A → (A ⊸ ⊥) ⊸ ⊥` is an isomorphism — are sound and complete models for the multiplicative fragment of classical linear logic. The dualising object provides the *involutive* negation `A^⊥ ≜ A ⊸ ⊥` that classical (rather than intuitionistic) linear logic requires; the par connective is recovered as `A ⅋ B ≜ (A^⊥ ⊗ B^⊥)^⊥`. Barr's extension to the *non-symmetric* case ("Nonsymmetric *-autonomous categories", *TCS* 139, 1995) [Barr1995] handles the cyclic and non-commutative variants relevant for asynchronous and partially-ordered process algebras.

Cockett and Seely's "Proof theory for full intuitionistic linear logic, bilinear logic, and MIX categories" (*Theory and Applications of Categories* 3, 1997) [CockettSeely1997] handles the *full* intuitionistic fragment FILL (Hyland–de Paiva) and Lambek's bilinear logic BILL by working in monoidal categories with two interacting tensors; the essential difference between FILL and BILL turns on whether a certain tensorial strength is required to be an isomorphism. The MIX rule — admitting `Γ ⊢ Δ` and `Γ' ⊢ Δ'` to combine to `Γ, Γ' ⊢ Δ, Δ'` — is shown to correspond to a *natural transformation* `A ⊗ B → A ⅋ B` whose categorical properties exactly characterise mix categories. Hyland and Schalk's "Glueing and orthogonality for models of linear logic" (*TCS* 294, 2003) [HylandSchalk2003] gives a uniform construction technique — *double glueing with orthogonality* — that produces models of all the structure of linear logic, including the exponentials, by abstracting the core combinatorial ingredient from earlier game-semantic and coherence-space constructions. The double-glueing technique is the technical workhorse for several subsequent fully-abstract game-semantic models, and has the right shape for transport to the propagator setting: a glued object is an object decorated with a predicate (the orthogonality), and the operational reading of the orthogonality predicate is precisely a "consistency check" of the kind a propagator-network's monotone merge function must enforce.

The Mellies survey "Categorical Semantics of Linear Logic" (*Panoramas et Synthèses* 27, Société Mathématique de France, 2009) [Mellies2009] is the canonical reference for the categorical models accumulated during this era. It catalogues *Seely categories*, *linear-non-linear adjunctions*, *new-Seely categories*, and the various refinements introduced over nearly twenty years before a stable consensus emerged. The survey is also where the connection between linear logic models and game semantics is most carefully drawn — making it the natural pivot to §3.2.

## §3.2 Game Semantics: Interaction as Computation

### 3.2.1 Strategies and arenas: the two routes to PCF

The game-semantic resolution of the long-standing full abstraction problem for PCF was achieved independently and contemporaneously by two research lines that converged on essentially the same answer. Hyland and Ong's "On Full Abstraction for PCF: I, II, and III" (*Information and Computation* 163, 2000) [HylandOng2000] and Abramsky, Jagadeesan, and Malacaria's "Full Abstraction for PCF" (*Information and Computation* 163, 2000) [AJM2000] both construct order-extensional fully abstract models for Scott's prototypical higher-order functional language PCF. (Both papers circulated as drafts from 1994; their joint publication in the same volume of *Information and Computation* in 2000 reflects the parallel nature of the discovery.)

The shared structure of the two models is the following. Types are interpreted as *games* (combinatorial structures called *arenas* in Hyland–Ong, with a slightly different presentation in AJM). A play is a sequence of moves; moves are labelled with one of two polarities, *Player* (P) or *Opponent* (O), corresponding to the proof/refutation polarity of the underlying linear-logic positive/negative split. Programs are interpreted as *strategies* — functions that, given a play history, prescribe the next P-move (or refuse to play, allowing the dialogue to terminate). Cut elimination becomes *strategy composition*, defined by hiding the moves on a shared interface. The model is fully abstract when the equality of strategies coincides with observational equivalence in the source language.

The two models differ in how they restrict strategies to capture exactly the PCF-definable behaviours. Hyland and Ong require strategies to be *innocent*: an innocent strategy plays its next move based only on the current *P-view* (the player's restricted view of the play history obtained by following pointers backward through the play tree), not on the full play. AJM use *history-free* strategies in conjunction with a quotient by the intrinsic preorder [AJM2000]. Both restrictions exclude state-dependent strategies, recovering the purely functional character of PCF; both are subsequently relaxed (innocence weakened, history-freedom replaced) to model state, control operators, references, and concurrency. Abramsky, Honda, and McCusker's "A Fully Abstract Game Semantics for General References" (*LICS* 1998) [AbramskyHondaMcCusker1998] — winner of the LICS Test-of-Time Award — shows that dropping the innocence/history-free constraints produces a fully abstract model for ML-style higher-order references; the lesson is that game semantics is *modular*: more permissive strategy classes correspond to more expressive computational features [AbramskyPubsTheme].

For the working hypothesis of this survey, three observations are load-bearing.

First, the *positions* of a game (the set of legal play histories of a given length) form a partially ordered set under the prefix relation: this is structurally the data of an *event structure* with conflict (different choices at the same position) and causality (later moves depending on earlier ones). The fully abstract models of HO and AJM are thus computing on a poset that is naturally a propagator-network state space; positions are *cells*, and moves that extend a position are *propagator firings* that advance the state monotonically.

Second, *strategy composition* — defining a strategy on `A ⊸ C` from strategies on `A ⊸ B` and `B ⊸ C` by hiding the `B`-interface and synchronising on it — is structurally a *cell-mediated information flow*: the shared `B`-interface is a cell, and the two strategies are propagators that read and write to that cell, with the resulting composite strategy being the network's quiescent state.

Third, the *innocence* constraint — that a strategy depends only on its P-view — is the game-semantic counterpart of a propagator's *locality*: a propagator fires based only on the values of its declared input cells, not on the entire global state. The HO innocence theorem says that PCF-definable strategies are exactly the locally-determined ones; this is a direct precursor of the CALM theorem (consistency-as-logical-monotonicity), which says that monotone computations on a propagator network are coordination-free.

### 3.2.2 Asynchronous games: positions over Mazurkiewicz traces

Mellies' "Asynchronous Games" series — published across LICS 2003 [MelliesAG1] (preprint "Asynchronous Games 1: Uniformity by Group Invariance"), CONCUR 2004 ("Asynchronous Games 2: The True Concurrency of Innocence", LNCS 3170) [MelliesAG2], 2005 ("Asynchronous Games 3: An Innocent Model of Linear Logic", *ENTCS* 122) [MelliesAG3], LICS 2005 ("Asynchronous Games 4: A Fully Complete Model of Propositional Linear Logic") [MelliesAG4] — is the deepest sustained attempt to lift game semantics from its sequential origin to a properly *concurrent* model in which positions, moves, and innocence are all generalised to handle true parallelism rather than alternation.

The technical pivot is to play games not on totally-ordered sequences of moves but on *Mazurkiewicz traces* — equivalence classes of move sequences modulo the commutation of independent moves. An *asynchronous graph* `G = (G, ◇)` is a directed graph `G` together with a symmetric *tiling relation* `◇` on pairs of length-two paths with the same source and same target [MelliesMimram2007]; the tiling encodes "these two moves are independent and can be permuted." An asynchronous graph satisfies the *cube property* when every hexagonal diagram induced by two coinitial-cofinal paths can be tiled — a higher-dimensional confluence condition. Asynchronous games are then played on asynchronous transition systems (asynchronous graphs with polarity labels on each transition), and the *innocence* condition of Hyland–Ong is reformulated diagrammatically as a *positional* condition on strategies: an innocent strategy in the asynchronous setting is one that depends only on the position (the equivalence class) reached, not on the particular path taken [MelliesPositional].

The yields of this lifting, codified across the four papers, are substantial.

(i) In AG1, *uniformity* — the property of innocence — is recharacterised as *bi-invariance* under two interacting group actions (one for Player permutations of P-moves, one for Opponent permutations of O-moves); a strategy is innocent iff for every Opponent permutation, there is a Player permutation under which the strategy is invariant [MelliesAG1]. This is a striking algebraic reformulation of innocence as a *symmetry property*, not a combinatorial one.

(ii) In AG2, the true-concurrency content of innocent strategies is extracted: innocent strategies are not merely partial functions on plays, they are *positional strategies regulated by forward and backward interactive confluence properties* [MelliesAG2]. Innocence becomes a confluence theorem on asynchronous transition systems.

(iii) In AG3, Mellies builds an innocent model of linear logic — every linear-logic formula is interpreted as an asynchronous game, and proofs as innocent strategies — providing the first concurrent game-semantic interpretation of all the connectives, including additives.

(iv) In AG4, the model is shown to be *fully complete* for propositional linear logic: every innocent winning strategy in the model is the interpretation of a (unique) linear-logic proof [MelliesAG4]. Full completeness — the converse of soundness — is the strongest possible categorical-semantic property and historically the hardest to achieve for linear logic (the precursor was Abramsky–Mellies, "Concurrent Games and Full Completeness", LICS 1999 [AbramskyMellies1999], which obtained MALL full-completeness in a different categorical framework).

The relevance to the working hypothesis is direct enough that we will surface it explicitly here and let the synthesis chapter re-purpose it. *Positions in an asynchronous game are propagator-network cells*: they are the lattice elements that a play accumulates as moves are fired. *Moves are propagator firings*: they extend a position monotonically, and the cube property — the higher-dimensional confluence of the tiling — is precisely the *CALM condition* that monotone propagator firings can be permuted without changing the final state. *Innocent strategies are the locally-determined propagators* — those whose firing depends only on the position (the cell value), not on the path by which the position was reached. The synthesis chapter will be able to lean on this structural identification when arguing that asynchronous game semantics provides a *prior denotational substrate* for propagator networks, with the Hasse diagram of positions playing the role of the parallel-decomposition skeleton.

Mellies' subsequent work continues to refine this picture. "Categorical Combinatorics for Innocent Strategies" with Harmer and Hyland (*LICS* 2007) [HarmerHylandMellies2007] gives the categorical algebra: innocent strategies arise as the maps in a two-sided Kleisli category for an interacting comonad–monad pair on a more primitive category of games, with a distributive law mediating the two; the combinatorial notions of *view* and *legal play* are derived structurally rather than imposed combinatorially. The Mellies–Mimram series "Asynchronous Games: Innocence Without Alternation" (*CONCUR* 2007) [MelliesMimram2007] and the journal version "Asynchronous Games vs. Concurrent Games" pushed the model to drop alternation entirely, giving the first fully concurrent (non-alternating) innocent strategy model; the technical bridge is the relationship between asynchronous games and the *concurrent strategies on event structures* developed by Rideau and Winskel (see §3.6 below).

The connection to *polynomial functors* — flagged in the prompt as a load-bearing thread for the synthesis — develops in Mellies' subsequent *tensorial logic* programme [MelliesTensorial], where dialogue categories (symmetric monoidal categories with a notion of tensorial negation that is not required to be involutive) provide a unified algebra in which innocent strategies and linear-logic proofs both live. Tensorial logic relaxes the assumption of involutive negation and makes explicit that game-semantic models live in *non-*-autonomous* monoidal categories with negation; the polynomial-functor connection enters via the description of strategies as polynomial decompositions of dialogues. This thread is the technical pivot between Era II (categorical concurrency 1985–2005) and the post-2010 quantale-enriched-game frontier; we hand this off to §6.3 (Mellies' modern polynomial-functor work) and only flag here that the seeds are visible in the AG3–AG4 papers and become explicit in the tensorial-logic programme from 2010 onwards.

## §3.6 Concurrency in *-Autonomous, Monoidal, and Coalgebraic Frameworks

The categorical-semantics tradition in concurrency theory, distinct from but interweaving with the linear-logic and game-semantics threads, develops its own canonical references between 1985 and 2005. The unifying ambition is to find a single category in which all the major models for concurrency — transition systems, synchronisation trees, Petri nets, event structures, asynchronous transition systems, traces, pomsets — can be objects, with morphisms that are simulations or behavioural maps, so that comparisons between models become functorial relationships and bisimulation becomes a categorical notion derived structurally rather than per-model.

### 3.6.1 Models for concurrency: the Winskel–Nielsen handbook chapter

Winskel and Nielsen's "Models for Concurrency" (*Handbook of Logic in Computer Science* 4, Oxford University Press, 1995) [WinskelNielsen1995] is the canonical survey of this programme. The chapter (148 pages) is organised around a hierarchy of concurrent models distinguished by two binary axes — interleaving vs. non-interleaving (whether parallelism is reduced to nondeterministic interleaving or represented explicitly via causal independence) and behavioural vs. system (whether the model gives only the externally-observable behaviour or the internal structure as well). The major models are positioned within this hierarchy:

- **Interleaving behavioural**: synchronisation trees, languages over an alphabet of actions.
- **Interleaving system**: labelled transition systems (LTSs).
- **Non-interleaving behavioural**: Mazurkiewicz traces, pomsets (Pratt's partially-ordered multisets [Pratt1986]).
- **Non-interleaving system**: Petri nets, asynchronous transition systems (transition systems with an explicit independence relation), event structures.

The unification is achieved by casting each model as a category with a faithful functor capturing its structure-preserving maps, and by relating models via *adjunctions* between these categories. The Petri-net-to-event-structure functor, the transition-system-to-synchronisation-tree functor, and the asynchronous-transition-system-to-Mazurkiewicz-trace functor all sit in the resulting bicategory of models, and the entire picture is one of a *hierarchy of categories of computational models*, where moving up the hierarchy (towards more abstract behavioural models) corresponds to a left adjoint and moving down (towards more structured system models) to a right adjoint.

For the propagator-network substrate, this categorical hierarchy is structurally analogous to the BSP-LE strata system: each stratum is a category of propagator networks at a given abstraction level, with Galois connections between strata corresponding to the adjunctions between concurrency models. The "non-interleaving system" level — Petri nets and event structures — is the closest correspondent to the propagator-network level, since both represent computation as an explicit causal partial order over discrete state-changing events.

### 3.6.2 Event structures: causality, conflict, and the propagator analogue

Winskel's "Event Structures" (*Petri Nets: Central Models and Their Properties*, LNCS 255, 1987) [Winskel1987] formalises *event structures* as the canonical non-interleaving system model. An event structure is a triple `(E, ≤, #)` where `E` is a set of events, `≤` is a partial order encoding causal dependence (`e ≤ e'` means `e` must occur before `e'`), and `#` is a symmetric irreflexive *conflict* relation encoding that two events cannot both occur in any single run of the system. The configurations of an event structure — its possible states — are subsets `X ⊆ E` that are *downward-closed* under `≤` (causally complete) and *conflict-free* (`e # e'` implies not both in `X`). These configurations form a *prime algebraic domain* under inclusion; equivalently, they form a *coherence space* in the sense of Girard, providing a direct semantic bridge to linear logic.

The structural identification with propagator networks is again immediate. An event-structure event `e` with `e ≤ e'` is a propagator that must fire before another propagator. The conflict relation `#` is the negative side of the propagator-cell merge: when two propagator firings would write incompatible values to a shared cell, the cell's lattice merge produces a *contradiction* (a conflict). Configurations are *consistent monotone histories* — exactly the notion of a propagator network's quiescent state in a single worldview. The prime-algebraic-domain structure is the lattice structure of the cell. The connection between event structures and concurrent constraint programming was made explicit in Saraswat, Rinard, and Panangaden's "Semantic Foundations of Concurrent Constraint Programming" (POPL 1991, recipient of the ALP Most Influential Paper Award) [SaraswatRinardPanangaden1991]; we defer the CCP-specific treatment to §3.3 (Agent 3) and only note here that event structures are the operational substrate that CCP closure-operator semantics builds on.

Winskel's continuing programme — culminating, after the period covered here, in "Concurrent Strategies" with Rideau (LICS 2011) and "Games and Strategies as Event Structures" (*LMCS* 2017) [RideauWinskel2011, WinskelGSE] — generalises both the Hyland–Ong arena games and the Mellies asynchronous games to event-structure-valued strategies, giving the most general categorical setting yet known for game semantics with full concurrency. The key construction is a bicategory of event-structure games and *concurrent pre-strategies* (event-structure maps respecting receptivity and innocence), with a *copycat* strategy as the identity and a composition by hiding shared events. A pre-strategy is a strategy when its composition with copycat is itself; this characterisation, together with subsequent presentations in terms of *distributive laws* between comonads and monads on the underlying categories, places event-structure game semantics on the same categorical-combinatorics footing as Harmer–Hyland–Mellies' innocent strategies on arena games. The synthesis chapter will note that the propagator-network setting is structurally an event-structure game with one Player (the elaborator) and one Opponent (the user/source program), with strategies being the elaboration discipline itself.

### 3.6.3 Bisimulation from open maps: a structural notion of behavioural equivalence

Joyal, Nielsen, and Winskel's "Bisimulation from Open Maps" (*Information and Computation* 127, 1996) [JoyalNielsenWinskel1996] (preprint BRICS RS-94-7, 1994) gives a uniform categorical definition of bisimulation across the categories of concurrency models. The construction starts from a subcategory `P ↪ M` of *path objects* in a model category `M` (paths in a transition system, causally-ordered subconfigurations in an event structure). A morphism `f: A → B` in `M` is *P-open* if every commutative square

```
P ⟶ A
↓     ↓ f
P' ⟶ B
```

with the left-hand morphism a path-extension can be filled by a diagonal `P' → A` making the upper triangle commute on the nose and the lower triangle commute up to the path-extension. Two objects `A, B` are *P-bisimilar* if there is a span `A ← S → B` of P-open morphisms. The remarkable result is that for `M = ` labelled transition systems and `P = ` finite paths, P-bisimulation specialises *exactly* to Milner's strong bisimulation; for `M = ` synchronisation trees, transition systems with independence (an abstraction of Petri nets), and labelled event structures, the same uniform definition produces the appropriate model-specific bisimulations. The categorical apparatus thus generates, rather than merely catalogues, the appropriate equivalence relation for each model.

For the propagator-network substrate, open-map bisimulation is the natural template for *behavioural equivalence between propagator networks*: two networks are bisimilar when there is an open map between their configuration spaces — that is, when path-completion in one is matched by path-completion in the other. The technical importance of open maps for the synthesis is that they are *information-flow-preserving*: P-open morphisms preserve the lifting of paths, which is structurally the same as preserving the monotone information flow through cells. The open-map approach extends naturally to *timed bisimulation* [TimedBisim], probabilistic bisimulation, and bisimulation for higher-dimensional automata [HDABisim], all of which are covered in subsequent papers in the same Joyal–Nielsen–Winskel framework.

### 3.6.4 Chu spaces and event-state duality: Pratt's programme

Vaughan Pratt's *Chu spaces* programme [Pratt1999, Pratt2002] provides a third strand of *-autonomous concurrency theory, distinct from but technically aligned with the Barr/Cockett–Seely tradition. A Chu space over a set `K` is a triple `(A, X, ⊨)` where `A` is a set of points, `X` is a set of states, and `⊨: A × X → K` is a satisfaction relation valued in `K`. The category of Chu spaces over a fixed `K`, with morphisms being adjoint pairs `(f, g)` such that `f(a) ⊨ x ↔ a ⊨ g(x)`, is a *-autonomous category — providing one of the cleanest concrete realisations of the algebra of classical linear logic. The key insight, made operational in Pratt's "Chu Spaces: A Model of Concurrency" (1995 Stanford notes; "Chu Spaces" course at Coimbra summer school 1999) [PrattChuStanford], is that Chu spaces formalise an *event-state duality*: the points are events (the things that happen), the states are configurations (the things that hold), and the satisfaction relation `⊨` records which events have occurred in which states.

The duality has direct concurrent-semantic content. Pratt's "Event-State Duality: The Enriched Case" (*CONCUR* 2002) [Pratt2002] extends the construction from `K = {0, 1}` (classical Chu spaces) to general enriched bases — Chu spaces over a closed monoidal category `V`. With `V = ` a quantale (a complete lattice with a monoidal multiplication distributing over joins; see §3.4 / Agent 4), the Chu construction yields *V-enriched Chu spaces* in which the satisfaction relation is quantale-valued and records, for instance, the *cost* or *time* at which an event occurs in a state. This is the precise technical bridge between the *-autonomous-categorical concurrency tradition and the quantale-enriched programme that the synthesis chapter argues is the algebraic kernel of asynchrony: Chu spaces over a quantale unify event structures, higher-dimensional automata, and timed/probabilistic transition systems within a single *-autonomous categorical framework. For propagator networks, the V-enriched Chu construction provides the abstract recipe: a propagator-network configuration is an enriched Chu space whose satisfaction relation `⊨: A × X → V` records *cost* or *delay* (Prologos's tropical-quantale (`min`, `+`) cells) or *information content* (Prologos's lattice-valued cells in general).

The connection to linear-logic semantics had been made by Barr [Barr1991, Barr1995] and by Pratt's students: V. Gupta's PhD thesis "Chu Spaces: A Model of Concurrency" (Stanford, 1994) [GuptaThesis] systematically used Chu spaces to model various concurrency primitives — synchronisation, conflict, choice, asynchronous transitions — within the unified *-autonomous setting. The interaction-categories programme of Abramsky, Gay, and Nagarajan ("Interaction Categories and the Foundations of Typed Concurrent Programming", Marktoberdorf 1994 / 1996) [AbramskyGayNagarajan1996] provides the complementary foundation: a category whose objects are session-typed interaction protocols and whose morphisms are processes implementing them, with composition being parallel composition with hiding — structurally a *-autonomous category in which the multiplicatives are parallel composition and the dual.

### 3.6.5 Coalgebra: behaviour as final-coalgebra structure

The coalgebraic strand of categorical concurrency theory, less directly related to the linear-logic and game-semantics threads but increasingly influential through the late 1990s, provides a complementary foundation. Rutten's "Universal Coalgebra: A Theory of Systems" (*Theoretical Computer Science* 249, 2000) [Rutten2000] is the canonical survey. The starting point is dual to the algebra side of universal algebra: where an algebra for a signature `Σ` is a set `A` together with operations `Σ-A → A`, a *coalgebra* for an endofunctor `F: C → C` on a category `C` is an object `A` together with a structure map `α: A → F(A)` describing the *behaviour* of states. For `F(X) = P(Act × X)` (the powerset of actions paired with successor states), `F`-coalgebras are exactly labelled transition systems; for `F(X) = X^{Act}` (functions from actions to successors), `F`-coalgebras are deterministic automata.

The coalgebraic framework gives, for each behaviour functor `F`, a canonical notion of behavioural equivalence — *F-bisimulation* — derived structurally from the functor: a span `A ← R → B` is an F-bisimulation when there is a coalgebra structure on `R` making the projections coalgebra homomorphisms. Crucially, F-bisimulation specialises to Milner's strong bisimulation for transition-system functors, to language equivalence for automaton functors, and to appropriate notions for probabilistic, weighted, and timed systems. The connection to Joyal–Nielsen–Winskel open-map bisimulation [JoyalNielsenWinskel1996] is that F-bisimulations and P-bisimulations coincide in the categories where both are defined; both are *structural* derivations of the appropriate equivalence rather than per-model definitions [RelatingCoalgebraic].

The propagator-network relevance of coalgebra is that the network's BSP scheduler is naturally a coalgebraic step: each round, the scheduler reads the current cell values (the coalgebra state) and produces the next round's worklist (the next state in `F(A)`). The fixpoint of the BSP loop is the final coalgebra of the appropriate behaviour functor. This positions propagator-network semantics squarely within the coalgebraic concurrency tradition, with the additional structure that the cells carry monotone lattice values rather than unstructured states.

### 3.6.6 Algebraic effects: the transition into Era IV

The closing development of Era II, occurring at its very end, is Plotkin and Power's algebraic-effects programme. "Notions of Computation Determine Monads" (*FoSSaCS* 2002) [PlotkinPower2002] revisits Moggi's monadic semantics of computational effects [Moggi1991] and shows that, under mild conditions, the notion of computation generated by a set of *operations* (with arities and equations) determines a unique monad. State, exceptions, nondeterminism, and I/O all arise this way; only continuations resist the algebraic treatment and require the more general framework of "algebraic operations and generic effects" developed in [PlotkinPower2003]: "Algebraic Operations and Generic Effects" (*Applied Categorical Structures* 11, 2003) shows that, in a sufficiently structured base category (a complete and cocomplete symmetric monoidal closed `V` with a strong `V`-monad `T`), generic effects and algebraic operations are equivalent, and both extend the standard monadic semantics of the computational lambda calculus uniformly.

The algebraic-effects framework is the immediate technical predecessor of the *handlers of algebraic effects* programme (Plotkin–Pretnar 2009 [PlotkinPretnar2009]) that defines Era IV's practical landscape — the languages Eff, Koka, and Frank, and the post-2015 effect-system tradition that §5 (Agent 7) covers. We flag here only the structural fact that algebraic operations *correspond to free coalgebra structure* over the base monad's algebra, providing a direct technical bridge from the algebraic-effects programme to the coalgebraic-concurrency programme of §3.6.5. The bridge becomes load-bearing when the synthesis chapter argues that asynchronous effects (futures, awaits, fairness) admit an algebraic-operations presentation whose corresponding monad is *quantale-enriched*; the post-2020 work that §6.3 covers takes this synthesis as one of its starting points.

### 3.6.7 Summary structural alignment with propagator networks

The collective yield of §3.1, §3.2, and §3.6 — surveyed across roughly two decades of categorical-semantics development — can be condensed into the following structural alignment with the propagator-network substrate that the synthesis chapter will exploit.

A *cell* is a position in an asynchronous game, equivalently a configuration in an event structure, equivalently an enriched Chu space's state, equivalently a coalgebra state. A *propagator firing* is a move in a game, an event in an event structure, a transition in a Chu-space morphism, a coalgebraic step. A *propagator network* is the underlying directed graph of a strategy on event structures with conflict, equivalently an asynchronous transition system, equivalently a *-autonomous category morphism. The *monotone merge function* of a cell is the orthogonality predicate of a glued model, the conflict relation `#` of an event structure, the satisfaction relation `⊨` of a Chu space. *CALM-monotone parallel firing* is the cube property of an asynchronous game, the confluence of a path category, the bisimulation-stability of an open map. *BSP rounds* are coalgebraic unfoldings; *quiescence* is the final coalgebra; *fixpoints* are GoI's execution formula instantiated on the appropriate operator algebra. The *strata* of the BSP-LE stratification system are the categorical-hierarchy levels of Winskel–Nielsen; the *Galois connections* between strata are the adjunctions between model categories.

The 1985–2005 categorical-semantics literature, in other words, has already provided — in fragments distributed across linear logic, game semantics, Chu spaces, event structures, and coalgebra — the *complete* algebraic and categorical vocabulary that propagator-network substrates need. The synthesis chapter's task is to integrate these fragments under a single quantale-enriched scheme; this section's task has been to map the territory.

[Girard1987] Girard, J.-Y. "Linear Logic." *Theoretical Computer Science* 50(1), 1987, pp. 1–102. <https://doi.org/10.1016/0304-3975(87)90045-4>
> The foundational paper introducing linear logic — multiplicatives `⊗`/`⅋`, additives `&`/`⊕`, exponentials `!`/`?`, involutive negation. Cited as the origin of resource-conscious proof theory and the polarity decomposition that game semantics later realises as Player/Opponent alternation.

[SEPLinear] Di Cosmo, R., Miller, D. "Linear Logic." *Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy*, Fall 2023 Edition. <https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/logic-linear/>
> Survey article giving the canonical statement of linear-logic syntax, polarities, and exponential rules. Cited for the standard polarity classification (positive: `⊗ ⊕ 1 0 !`; negative: `⅋ & ⊥ ⊤ ?`).

[LLBook2024] De, A., Grellois, C. "Linear Logic" course notes / handbook. IRIF / CNRS, 2024. <https://ll-handbook.pages.math.cnrs.fr/book/ll-handbook-public.pdf>
> Modern handbook treatment of linear logic with detailed coverage of the four exponential rules (contraction, weakening, dereliction, promotion). Cited for the technical statement that promotion is contextual and invertible while dereliction is not.

[GirardGoI1] Girard, J.-Y. "Geometry of Interaction I: Interpretation of System F." In: *Logic Colloquium '88* (Ferro et al., eds.), North-Holland, 1989, pp. 221–260.
> First paper of the GoI programme; introduces the *execution formula* as a fixpoint encoding of cut elimination on operators in `B(H) = B(ℓ²)`. Cited as the dynamics-of-cut-elimination origin point that subsequent traced-monoidal-category reformulations build on.

[GirardGoI2] Girard, J.-Y. "Geometry of Interaction II: Deadlock-Free Algorithms." In: *COLOG-88*, LNCS 417, Springer, 1988, pp. 76–93.
> Second GoI paper; addresses the cut-elimination-as-process-interaction reading via the execution formula's iteration structure. Cited as the bridge between proof normalisation and process semantics.

[GoIWiki] "Geometry of Interaction." *Wikipedia*, current revision. <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geometry_of_interaction>
> Survey of the GoI programme through the 1990s, including connections to Lamping's optimal reduction algorithm. Cited for the technical history; paywalls on the original GoI papers make this a useful secondary source.

[AbramskyJagadeesan1994] Abramsky, S., Jagadeesan, R. "New Foundations for the Geometry of Interaction." *Information and Computation* 111(1), 1994, pp. 53–119. <https://doi.org/10.1006/inco.1994.1041>
> Reformulates Girard's GoI in terms of *traced monoidal categories*, replacing operator-algebra specifics with a categorical interface. Cited as the categorical-axiomatic GoI that provides the propagator-substrate-relevant abstraction (trace = feedback = fixpoint).

[AbramskyGoI2002] Abramsky, S. (with Haghverdi, Scott). "Geometry of Interaction and Linear Combinatory Algebras." *Mathematical Structures in Computer Science* 12, 2002. <https://www.cs.ox.ac.uk/people/samson.abramsky/pubsthematic.html>

[Abramsky1993] Abramsky, S. "Computational Interpretations of Linear Logic." *Theoretical Computer Science* 111(1–2), 1993, pp. 3–57. <https://doi.org/10.1016/0304-3975(93)90181-R>

[BellinScott1994] Bellin, G., Scott, P. J. "On the π-calculus and Linear Logic." *Theoretical Computer Science* 135(1), 1994, pp. 11–65. <https://doi.org/10.1016/0304-3975(94)00104-9>

[CairesPfenning2010] Caires, L., Pfenning, F. "Session Types as Intuitionistic Linear Propositions." In: *CONCUR 2010*, LNCS 6269, Springer, 2010, pp. 222–236. <https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-15375-4_16>
