[TimedBisim] Nielsen, M., Hune, T. "Timed Bisimulation and Open Maps." LNCS 1450, Springer, 1998. <https://doi.org/10.1007/BFb0055787>
> Open-map bisimulation extended to timed transition systems. Cited as evidence that the open-map approach handles real-time concurrency.

[HDABisim] Goubault, E., Mimram, S. "A Category of Higher-Dimensional Automata." LNCS 3441, 2005. <https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-540-31982-5_12>
> Open-map bisimulation extended to higher-dimensional automata. Cited for completeness; HDAs are the geometric counterpart to event structures.

[RideauWinskel2011] Rideau, S., Winskel, G. "Concurrent Strategies." In: *LICS 2011*, IEEE, 2011, pp. 409–418. <https://doi.org/10.1109/LICS.2011.13>
> Generalises Hyland–Ong arena games and Mellies asynchronous games to event-structure-valued strategies, with a bicategory of pre-strategies and copycat as identity. Post-Era-II but the natural completion of the Era II event-structure programme.

[WinskelGSE] Castellan, S., Clairambault, P., Winskel, G. "Games and Strategies as Event Structures." *Logical Methods in Computer Science* 13(3), 2017. <https://doi.org/10.23638/LMCS-13(3:35)2017>
> Definitive treatment of concurrent strategies as event structures; characterises strategies via composition with copycat and gives distributive-law presentation. Post-Era-II but completes the event-structure-game programme.

[Pratt1999] Pratt, V. "Chu Spaces." Course notes, School in Category Theory and Applications, Coimbra Summer School, 1999. <http://chu.stanford.edu/guide.html>
> Pedagogical and reference treatment of Chu spaces over a fixed `K`. Cited as the canonical reference for the Chu-spaces concurrency programme.

[Pratt2002] Pratt, V. "Event-State Duality: The Enriched Case." In: *CONCUR 2002*, LNCS 2421, Springer, 2002, pp. 41–67. <https://doi.org/10.1007/3-540-45694-5_3>
> V-enriched Chu spaces over a closed monoidal category — when V is a quantale, satisfaction relations are quantale-valued (cost, time, probability). Cited as the technical bridge between *-autonomous concurrency and quantale-enriched algebra.

[PrattChuStanford] Pratt, V. and students (Gupta et al.). "Chu Construction" Stanford notes. <http://boole.stanford.edu/pub/ph94.pdf>
> Stanford-internal notes treating Chu spaces with quantum aspects. Cited for completeness of the Chu-programme history.

[GuptaThesis] Gupta, V. "Chu Spaces: A Model of Concurrency." PhD thesis, Stanford University, 1994.
> Systematic application of Chu spaces to concurrency primitives (synchronisation, conflict, choice, asynchrony). Cited as the comprehensive Chu-spaces-for-concurrency reference.

[AbramskyGayNagarajan1996] Abramsky, S., Gay, S., Nagarajan, R. "Interaction Categories and the Foundations of Typed Concurrent Programming." In: *Proceedings of the 1994 Marktoberdorf Summer School on Deductive Program Design*, NATO ASI Series, Springer, 1996, pp. 35–113.
> Interaction categories: a *-autonomous category whose objects are session-typed protocols and morphisms are processes implementing them. Cited as the *-autonomous foundation for typed concurrent programming, complementary to Pratt's Chu spaces.

[Rutten2000] Rutten, J. J. M. M. "Universal Coalgebra: A Theory of Systems." *Theoretical Computer Science* 249(1), 2000, pp. 3–80. <https://doi.org/10.1016/S0304-3975(00)00056-6>
> Canonical survey of coalgebraic semantics of state-based systems. F-coalgebras for endofunctor F: transition systems for `F = P(Act × _)`, automata for `F = (_)^Act`, and so on. Cited as the coalgebraic-concurrency reference.

[RelatingCoalgebraic] Staton, S. "Relating Coalgebraic Notions of Bisimulation." LNCS 5728, 2009. <https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-03741-2_14>
> Establishes the equivalence of F-bisimulation and open-map bisimulation in the categories where both are defined. Cited as the technical bridge between the Joyal–Nielsen–Winskel and the Rutten coalgebraic strands.

[PlotkinPower2002] Plotkin, G., Power, J. "Notions of Computation Determine Monads." In: *FoSSaCS 2002*, LNCS 2303, Springer, 2002, pp. 342–356. <https://doi.org/10.1007/3-540-45931-6_24>
> Algebraic-operations-and-equations presentations of computational effects (state, exceptions, nondeterminism, I/O) generate the corresponding monads. Continuations resist this treatment. Cited as the algebraic-effects origin point.

[PlotkinPower2003] Plotkin, G., Power, J. "Algebraic Operations and Generic Effects." *Applied Categorical Structures* 11(1), 2003, pp. 69–94. <https://doi.org/10.1023/A:1023064908962>
> Generalises [PlotkinPower2002] to enriched-monad bases; shows generic effects ≡ algebraic operations. Cited as the foundation for handlers of algebraic effects (transition into Era IV).

[PlotkinPretnar2009] Plotkin, G., Pretnar, M. "Handlers of Algebraic Effects." In: *ESOP 2009*, LNCS 5502, Springer, 2009, pp. 80–94. <https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-00590-9_7>
> Defines effect handlers as deconstructors for algebraic-effect operations. Cited as the immediate predecessor of Era IV practical effect-system languages (Eff, Koka, Frank); defer to §5 (Agent 7).

[Moggi1991] Moggi, E. "Notions of Computation and Monads." *Information and Computation* 93(1), 1991, pp. 55–92. <https://doi.org/10.1016/0890-5401(91)90052-4>
> Foundational monadic semantics of computational effects. Cited as the predecessor that Plotkin–Power 2002 algebraicises.

---

## §3.3 Concurrent Constraint Programming (CCP) and the Propagator Genealogy

### §3.3.1 Origins and motivation

The concurrent constraint programming (CCP) family of languages emerged at the end of the 1980s out of two prior research programs that had developed in parallel and largely in mutual isolation. The first was concurrent logic programming (CLP), whose representatives were the family of "committed-choice" languages — Concurrent Prolog [Shapiro 1986], PARLOG [Clark-Gregory 1986], Guarded Horn Clauses (GHC) [Ueda 1986], KL1 [Chikayama 1988], and the various "flat" subset versions of these — designed to provide a declarative reading of concurrent computation built on logical clauses with synchronization operators (commit guards, wait guards, cut guards). The second was constraint logic programming (CLP), instantiated by Prolog II / Prolog III [Colmerauer 1990], CLP(R) [Jaffar-Lassez 1987], and CHIP [Dincbas et al. 1988], which had replaced unification-over-Herbrand with a parametric notion of constraint solving over an arbitrary domain D.

The synthesis was effected by Vijay A. Saraswat in his 1989 Carnegie Mellon doctoral dissertation [Saraswat 1989a]. The thesis (technical report CMU-CS-89-108, completed in January 1989 under the supervision of Dana Scott) was subsequently revised and expanded for publication in the ACM Doctoral Dissertation Award series by MIT Press in 1993 [Saraswat 1993]. The 1989 thesis received the ACM Doctoral Dissertation Award; the 1991 POPL paper that distilled its semantic core, "Semantic Foundations of Concurrent Constraint Programming" [Saraswat-Rinard-Panangaden 1991], received the Association for Logic Programming's Most Influential Paper in Twenty Years Award in 2004. These two facts — the dissertation award and the twenty-year retrospective award — establish CCP as a mature, recognised body of theoretical work whose architectural primitives are not folklore but the documented product of a deliberate synthesis program with a published mathematical foundation.

A pre-CCP technical antecedent that Saraswat's POPL 1991 paper repeatedly cites is M. J. Maher's "Logic Semantics for a Class of Committed-Choice Programs" [Maher 1987], presented at ICLP'87 in Melbourne. Maher's contribution was the ALPS class of committed-choice languages: a framework that recast the synchronization primitives of GHC and Concurrent Prolog as logical entailment checks against a "constraint store." This recasting is what Saraswat generalises and rebuilds on Scott's information-systems-style domains; the lineage from ALPS to CCP is direct, and Saraswat in [Saraswat-Rinard-Panangaden 1991] writes explicitly that the "notion of determinacy was studied in the set-up of logic programming languages in [Maher 1987], but no characterizations of the kind we provide here were given." Maher gave a logical reading; Saraswat-Rinard-Panangaden converted it into a denotational model on a lattice.

Saraswat himself frames the move from concurrent logic to concurrent constraint as a deliberate paradigm shift away from the von Neumann conception of "store as valuation" toward "store as constraint." This wording — emphatic in the POPL paper's introduction — is load-bearing for the genealogical claim of §3.3.7 below: a constraint store is not a memory of values that can be overwritten but a partial-information structure that can only be refined by adding consistent constraints. This is precisely the I-structures discipline of [Arvind-Nikhil-Pingali 1989] (Id Nouveau, MIT-style dataflow), the LVar discipline of [Kuper-Newton 2013], the propagator-cell discipline of [Sussman-Radul 2009], and the BloomL lattice discipline of [Conway et al. 2012]. All four reinvent the same primitive in different intellectual cultures. Saraswat's CCP is the concurrent-logic / constraint-programming branch; the others come out of dataflow, deterministic parallelism, AI, and distributed databases respectively. They have largely not cited each other.

### §3.3.2 The cc(D) family

Saraswat's framework is parametric over a constraint system D. A "constraint system" is a structure ⟨D, ⊢⟩ where D is a countable set of primitive tokens (vocabulary of assertions) and ⊢ ⊆ ℘_f D × D is an entailment relation between finite token-sets and tokens, satisfying

- C1 (reflexivity): u ⊢ P whenever P ∈ u
- C2 (transitivity / cut): if u ⊢ Q whenever u ⊢ P for all P ∈ v, and v ⊢ Q, then u ⊢ Q

[Saraswat-Rinard-Panangaden 1991, Definition 2.1]. Extending ⊢ to a relation on finite token-sets by u ⊢ v iff u ⊢ P for all P ∈ v, the elements of the constraint system are equivalence classes of finite token-sets under the resulting equivalence ≈ (the bidirectional entailment closure). The compactness of ⊢ ensures that the resulting elements form an algebraic complete lattice (|D|, ⊆) with bottom element **true** = [∅] (entailing nothing not already entailed) and top element **false** = ⊓D (the inconsistent state). This lattice is the constraint system D's value space.

The key Saraswat insight, on which the entire CCP enterprise rests, is that D is chosen *parametrically* — each instance of CCP, written cc(D), is obtained by plugging in a particular constraint system. Examples in [Saraswat-Rinard-Panangaden 1991, §2]:

- **Herbrand**: equality on first-order terms with function symbols. The Herbrand cc(Herbrand) language is what concurrent logic programming languages effectively use; the entailment is unification.
- **Kahn**: streams over a finite-data domain B. The Kahn constraint system D(B) gives cc(B) which is precisely Kahn dataflow [Kahn 1974]. Saraswat-Rinard-Panangaden devote space to this in their Example 2.2 to make the claim concrete: dataflow languages are an instance of CCP at a particular choice of constraint system.
- **Rational intervals**: tokens of the form X ∈ [x, y] with x, y rational, entailment by interval inclusion. The lattice is algebraic (and finitary if intersections of rational intervals are taken as the elements). This gives a constraint system over which arithmetic refinement programs run.
- **Finite domains**: tokens of the form X ∈ S for finite S; the basis for cc(FD) [Van Hentenryck 1989], CHIP, and most propagation-based finite-domain solvers.

The two basic primitives of cc(D), introduced by Saraswat as the "ask-and-tell" primitives, are:

- **tell c**: an agent unconditionally augments the current store σ with the constraint c. The result store is σ ⊔ c, i.e., the lattice join. Operationally, tell c is a single transition `c —(d, c⊔d)→ true` whenever c ≠ true (Definition 4 in [SRP91]). Telling a constraint never blocks (in the basic eventual-tell variant); it is purely a monotone augmentation. The operation is idempotent (tell c twice = tell c once), commutative (order-independent), and associative.

- **ask c → P**: an agent blocks until the store entails c. When the store σ satisfies σ ⊒ c (i.e., σ ⊢ c when read at the token level), the ask is "enabled" and the body P begins to execute. The semantic reading is `c → P = λx. if x ⊒ c then P x else x`: the agent watches the store, fires its body when a sufficient condition becomes entailed, and is otherwise quiescent.

Composition operators in [Saraswat-Rinard-Panangaden 1991, Table 1, "Determinate Ask-and-Tell Languages"]:

- **Parallel composition (A ∧ B)**: both A and B run concurrently on the same store. Both their tells contribute; both their asks are checked against the cumulative store. There is no notion of who fired first; only the resulting fixpoint matters.
- **Hiding / existential quantification (∃X. A)**: A runs in an extended namespace where X is local; the externally-visible store is the projection along the cylindrification axis associated with X. The treatment uses cylindric algebra [Henkin-Monk-Tarski 1971] to axiomatise ∃X as a continuous lattice operator with E1–E4 axioms (extensivity, monotonicity, idempotence on join with bound variable, and commutation of ∃X∃Y).
- **Recursion / procedure call (p(X))**: the standard fixed-point treatment, with declaration body invoked under a renaming substitution.
- **Sum / nondeterministic choice**: in determinate cc the sum operator is excluded; in indeterminate cc, the sum Σ_i ask c_i → A_i selects one branch nondeterministically among those whose guards are entailed.

The crucial design choice — the one that distinguishes cc from earlier process algebras — is that the parallel-composition operator does NOT carry control flow. cc has no synchronous handshake (à la CCS, CSP, π-calculus), no ordered message-passing, no scheduler-determined interleaving. All communication is by shared store, asynchronous in the sense that telling does not require any acknowledgement, and synchronization is only by ask blocking on entailment. This is what makes cc compositionally simple compared with CCS-derivative languages: branching structure is unnecessary, and the denotational semantics can collapse to a flat sequence of tell/ask interactions ([de Boer-Palamidessi 1990], [de Boer-Kok-Palamidessi-Rutten 1991]).

**Determinate vs indeterminate cc**: a determinate cc program has no `Σ` choice operator; equivalently, each ask has at most one continuation, so on any input the program output is unique up to the lattice join (it is a function from input store to output store). Indeterminate cc admits the choice operator and produces a set of possible output stores. The semantic theory of determinate cc is markedly simpler: the denotation of a process IS a closure operator (§3.3.3 below), and the process's input-output behaviour is fully characterized by its set of *resting points* — those stores σ with f(σ) = σ where the agent halts without producing further information [SRP91, §3].

Note carefully: both ask and tell are monotone in the store. Telling can only grow the store; asking blocks until growth crosses a threshold. This is the CALM-monotone form that the CCP literature recognized in 1989–1991 — twenty years before Hellerstein's CALM theorem [Hellerstein 2010, Alvaro et al. 2011] formalised the corresponding "monotone = coordination-free" claim for distributed computation. The connection is direct (see §3.3.7 below and Agent 7's BloomL discussion at §5.5).

### §3.3.3 Semantic foundations

The defining semantic move of Saraswat-Rinard-Panangaden 1991 is the identification

> **A determinate cc agent is denoted by a closure operator on the constraint system's lattice.**

A *closure operator* on a complete lattice (L, ⊑) is a function f : L → L satisfying

1. **Extensivity**: ∀c. c ⊑ f(c) — the agent never erases information; output dominates input.
2. **Idempotence**: ∀c. f(f(c)) = f(c) — once the agent has reached resting on input c, re-running on its own output produces no new information.
3. **Monotonicity**: ∀c, d. c ⊑ d ⇒ f(c) ⊑ f(d) — more input information yields at least as much output information.

These three properties are the characterizing axioms of closure operators in lattice theory [Tarski 1956; Birkhoff 1948], also called *consequence operators* in the algebraic logic tradition. The point of the SRP semantics is that the closure-operator denotation is determined uniquely by its set of fixed points (its "resting points"), and that the resting-point set provides a fully abstract model: two cc agents have the same resting-point set if and only if they are observationally equivalent [SRP91, §3, pp. 339–340].

The basic equation, in the SRP development, is:

  ⟦tell c⟧(d) = c ⊔ d                  (the closure operator that adds c to its input)
  ⟦c → A⟧(d) = if d ⊒ c then ⟦A⟧(d) else d       (the operator that fires only above threshold)
  ⟦A ∧ B⟧(d) = ⟦A⟧(d) ⊓ ⟦B⟧(d) — i.e., parallel composition is the **intersection of fixed-point sets** of the two component closure operators, equivalently the join of their actions until joint quiescence.

This last equation is load-bearing: the parallel composition of two agents IS their **set-intersection** at the level of fixed points, equivalently the supremum (least closure operator above both) at the level of operators. The ordering on closure operators is the divergence order: f ≤ g iff f ⊆ g ∪ d_f (the divergences of f contain those of g) [SRP91, Definition 3.1]. The lattice of closure operators thus inherits a complete lattice structure from the underlying constraint system, and the cc combinators ∧ (parallel), ∃X (hiding), c → · (ask-prefix), and recursion (least fixpoint) are all continuous operators on this lattice. The parallel composition combinator is symmetric, associative, and has identity element **true** (the trivial closure that does nothing); these laws (L5–L7 in [SRP91]) are read off from the closure-operator algebra.

The final piece of the SRP semantics is the treatment of hiding ∃X. Existential quantification is, at the level of the constraint system, a *cylindrification* operator drawn from cylindric algebra [Henkin-Monk-Tarski 1971]. The diagonal elements d_XY = "X = Y" allow simulation of parameter passing via substitution-as-constraint, an idea that comes from Lambek-Scott [Lambek-Scott 1986] and is, the authors note, ultimately a form of coherent logic structure suggested by categorical logic. The relevant axioms are E1–E4 on ∃X and D1–D3 on the diagonal elements [SRP91, Definitions 2.3–2.5]; hiding lifts to a *kernel* (dual closure) operator on the closure-operator lattice, and in the operational semantics is realised by the rule for ∃X(d, A): A runs against an internal store d, and information about X is hidden from outside via cylindrification.

Indeterminate cc requires more machinery. The denotation is no longer a single closure operator but a set of "bounded trace operators" [SRP91, §3.4–§3.5]; equivalently, the agent is identified with the set of *paths* (sequences of ask/tell interactions) it can engage in, modulo natural closure conditions. The process algebra of indeterminate cc was developed independently by [de Boer-Kok-Palamidessi-Rutten 1991] under the title "The Failure of Failures in a Paradigm for Asynchronous Communication" (CONCUR'91, LNCS 527, pp. 111–126), where they observe that the asynchronous nature of cc communication eliminates the need for the branching-time / failures-divergences structure of CSP-style process algebras: a flat sequence of constraints is sufficient. This is a fundamental observation about the separability of asynchronous communication semantics, and is what licenses the closure-operator framework to scale up to non-determinate cc with only mild additional structure. The full development was given in [de Boer-Kok-Palamidessi-Rutten 1991], with the journal version in [de Boer-Palamidessi 1995] (Inf. Comp. and TCS papers).

A still later categorical-logical development, "A Logical View of Concurrent Constraint Programming" by Mendler, Panangaden, Scott, and Seely [MPSS 1995, Nordic J. Computing 2:181–220], lifts the SRP closure-operator semantics to a hyperdoctrine in the sense of Lawvere. The combinators of determinate cc become logical connectives: parallel composition ∧ becomes conjunction in the indexed logic; existential quantification ∃X becomes (literal) existential quantification along the cylindrification fibre; the closure-operator lattice provides the propositional structure. The earlier conference version [Panangaden-Saraswat-Scott-Seely 1993, "A Hyperdoctrinal View of Concurrent Constraint Programming," REX Workshop, LNCS 666] established that the denotational semantics of determinate ccp forms a categorical structure called a hyperdoctrine, which is the categorical basis for first-order logic. The combinators of determinate ccp can therefore be viewed as logical connectives. This is one of the strongest formal expressions of the slogan "logic IS computation" that the field produced — and it generalises directly to the propagator-network setting, where a propagator firing on a cell is just the closure-operator semantics applied to a particular concrete realisation.

### §3.3.4 Concrete instances of the cc family

The cc(D) parametric framework spawned a substantial family of concrete languages.

**Janus** [Saraswat-Kahn-Levy 1990, "Janus: A Step Towards Distributed Constraint Programming," NACLP'90]: an early cc instance focused on distributed execution. Janus is a constraint-based language with declarative syntax built on guarded clauses (procedures defined with asks for reading constraints and tells for adding them) and was designed to illustrate that the actor model could be embedded as a special case of CCP — a fact made explicit in [Kahn-Saraswat 1990, "Actors as a Special Case of Concurrent Constraint Programming"]. The actor pattern is reinterpreted as a particular discipline on cc tells (each actor "owns" its message queue cell), suggesting that asynchronous message passing and CCP are structurally equivalent when the underlying constraint system is chosen appropriately.

**AKL — Andorra Kernel Language** [Janson-Haridi 1991, "Programming Paradigms of the Andorra Kernel Language," ILPS'91]: developed at SICS by Janson and Haridi, AKL is a synthesis of Prolog (don't-know nondeterminism), GHC (don't-care nondeterminism via committed choice), and CCP. AKL aims to unify search-based logic programming with concurrent logic programming under a single computation model — the Andorra Principle — that reduces deterministic goals before nondeterministic ones. AKL is a CC language in the sense that its primitives are ask/tell; its distinguishing innovation is the concurrent treatment of don't-know and don't-care nondeterminism in one calculus.

**Oz / Mozart** [Smolka 1995, "The Oz Programming Model," in Computer Science Today, LNCS 1000, pp. 324–343; subsequently the Mozart Programming System v1 (1996), v2, v3 by Henz, Smolka, Würtz, Van Roy, Haridi]: Oz is the most fully realised CCP language, evolving over the 1990s into a multi-paradigm system with logic, functional, object-oriented, distributed, and constraint programming as facets of a uniform CCP-based core. The 1995 Smolka paper argues that the Oz Programming Model subsumes higher-order functional and object-oriented programming as facets of a general concurrent constraint model; Oz's *ports* (asynchronous message channels) are explicitly cc-cells with FIFO disciplines, and Oz's *spaces* are encapsulated cc sub-stores for search.

**cc(FD)** [Van Hentenryck-Saraswat-Deville 1991/1995, "Design, Implementation and Evaluation of the Constraint Language cc(FD)," LNCS 910, pp. 293–316]: the finite-domain instance of cc, with constraint propagation tightly integrated. cc(FD) became a major route by which CCP ideas reached the constraint-programming community proper (CHIP-descendant solvers). It is also the canonical setting in which CCP propagators map most directly onto traditional finite-domain propagation algorithms (AC-3, GAC), which are by inspection closure operators in the SRP sense.

**Gödel** [Hill-Lloyd 1994] and **CHIP** [Dincbas et al. 1988] are CLP-tradition siblings rather than CCP per se, but the lines blurred during the 1990s; Gödel's pruning-by-delay mechanism is structurally a cc ask, and CHIP's domain-narrowing constraints are tells.

**LCC — Linear Concurrent Constraint** [Saraswat-Lincoln 1992, "Higher-Order, Linear, Concurrent Constraint Programming," Xerox PARC technical report; Fages-Ruet-Soliman 2001, "Linear Concurrent Constraint Programming: Operational and Phase Semantics," Inf. Comp. 165(1):14–41]: replaces the classical (intuitionistic) constraint system with a linear constraint system based on Girard's linear logic. Constraints can now be *consumed* by tells, not only added — restoring a notion of "state change" within the cc paradigm and connecting CCP to substructural logics. The Fages-Ruet-Soliman paper is the canonical operational+phase-semantics treatment, and it characterises LCC computations in terms of provability in intuitionistic linear logic. This direction is significant because it weakens the monotone-store discipline in a controlled way; the linear store still yields a categorical model (a *-autonomous category, see Agent 2 §3.6), but it admits non-monotonicity of a particular structured form. See §3.3.5 below for further LCC developments.

The cc family has continued to expand. Subsequent variants include hybrid cc [Gupta-Jagadeesan-Saraswat 1996, "Hybrid cc, Hybrid Automata and Program Verification," Hybrid Systems III, LNCS 1066], default cc [Saraswat-Jagadeesan-Gupta 1995, "Default Timed Concurrent Constraint Programming," POPL'95, pp. 272–285] for strong preemption, the spatial / epistemic CCP work of Knight-Palamidessi-Panangaden-Valencia [CONCUR 2012] which adds modal layers to the constraint system, and the subexponential CCP of Olarte-Pimentel-Nigam [TCS 2015]. A comprehensive 2013 survey is [Olarte-Rueda-Valencia 2013, "Models and Emerging Trends of Concurrent Constraint Programming," Constraints 18(4):535–578], which catalogues these descendants and their relationships.

### §3.3.5 Variants and extensions

This section foregrounds five extensions of the basic cc paradigm that bear on the working hypothesis (propagator networks instantiate cc(D); the quantale-enriched extension is a frontier hypothesis with established formal antecedents).

**Linear CCP (LCC)**: as introduced above, LCC replaces the classical entailment-based constraint system with one founded on intuitionistic linear logic. Tokens become resources; a tell of c may consume an existing token c' if the underlying linear structure permits. The Fages-Ruet-Soliman 2001 paper [Inf. Comp. 165(1):14–41] is the comprehensive treatment; it gives both an operational SOS semantics for LCC and a phase semantics in linear logic, proving that LCC stores (and successes in the indeterminate fragment) are characterised in intuitionistic linear logic. Fages-Ruet-Soliman also relate LCC to Constraint Handling Rules (CHR), showing that CHR can be understood as a sub-language of LCC with a particular discipline; this is significant for connecting the propagator-style constraint solving of CHR back to CCP. Earlier development: [Saraswat-Lincoln 1992] introduced higher-order LCC; [Bouali-Pinchinat-Sutre 1996, "Concurrent Constraint Programming and Non-Commutative Linear Logic," CSL'96, LNCS 1224] explored the non-commutative extension. From the perspective of the working hypothesis, LCC is the established CCP route into the substructural / quantale-enriched space — its constraint system is a linear-logic phase space, which is structurally similar (though not identical) to a quantale-enriched constraint system in the sense of [Bistarelli-Montanari-Rossi 1997].

**Soft CCP** [Bistarelli-Montanari-Rossi 1997, "Semiring-Based Constraint Satisfaction and Optimization," J. ACM 44(2):201–236; Bistarelli-Montanari-Rossi 2006, "Soft Concurrent Constraint Programming," ACM TOCL 7(3):563–589]: replaces the {0, 1}-valued (consistent / inconsistent) constraint store with an algebraic structure called a *c-semiring* (a tuple ⟨A, +, ×, 0, 1⟩ with the + operation idempotent and ×-distributive). The c-semiring's idempotent + induces a partial order ≤ on A under which A is a complete lattice (the lub IS the + operation); × is a monoid operation that acts as "constraint combination." This is precisely the quantale-enriched setting, although Bistarelli-Montanari-Rossi's exposition predates explicit identification of the structure as a quantale. **A c-semiring with the standard idempotent + and well-behaved × is a commutative quantale** (the join-semilattice with monoid structure); this identification is recognised in the soft-constraints literature, e.g., in the Soft Concurrent Constraint Programming book [Bistarelli 2004, LNCS 2962]. Soft CCP gives a quantale-enriched cc framework: the store is no longer a plain lattice but a quantale-valued mapping, tells contribute via the quantale's × operation, and asks check for threshold values in the quantale order. The relevance to the working hypothesis is direct: this is the canonical quantale-CCP connection in the literature, established in 1997 (J. ACM) and now nearly thirty years old. The propagator-quantale frontier hypothesis is therefore not novel as a structural connection — it is novel only in being explicitly framed as a *propagator-network* instantiation, where the lattice decomposition (per the Hyperlattice Conjecture) carries operational weight.

**Probabilistic CCP** [Di Pierro-Wiklicky 1998, "An Operational Semantics for Probabilistic Concurrent Constraint Programming," IEEE COMPSAC'98]: replaces the determinate / indeterminate choice with a probabilistic choice, weighting the alternatives in `Σ p_i :: ask c_i → A_i` by probabilities. The denotational semantics moves from closure operators on a lattice to *linear operators on a probabilistic powerdomain* [Jones-Plotkin 1989]. The probabilistic cc model is a lattice-of-distributions structure, mathematically dense but operationally more remote from the propagator picture; it does, however, suggest the *probabilistic quantale* framework that has appeared in some recent (post-2020) work [Goubault-Larrecq, Jia-Tang etc.]. The 1997 Saraswat-Gupta-Jagadeesan POPL paper "Probabilistic Concurrent Constraint Programming" [POPL'97, pp. 121–134] gave the first comprehensive treatment.

**Temporal / Reactive CCP** [Saraswat-Jagadeesan-Gupta 1994, "Programming in Timed Concurrent Constraint Languages," NATO ASI series, vol F131; Saraswat-Jagadeesan-Gupta 1995, "Default Timed Concurrent Constraint Programming," POPL'95]: adds a notion of discrete time (round-by-round). Each time tick, the store is reset and the program executes for one cc round, then the residual continuation moves to the next time interval. This is the *tcc* (timed cc) family. A nondeterministic extension *ntcc* [Nielsen-Palamidessi-Valencia 2002, "Temporal Concurrent Constraint Programming: Denotation, Logic, and Applications," Nordic J. Computing 9:145–188] adds non-determinism. A different timed variant *tccp* [de Boer-Gabbrielli-Marchiori-Palamidessi 1997, "Proving Concurrent Constraint Programs Correct," ACM TOPLAS 19(5):685–725] gives a denotational model for timed cc with full abstraction. Default cc adds strong preemption (immediate response to absence of a constraint, ESTEREL-style synchronous-language semantics). These extensions matter for the working hypothesis because they show the cc paradigm scales naturally to time-stratified computation — exactly the setting Prologos's BSP scheduler operates in (one round of monotone propagation, then a stratum boundary).

**Spatial / epistemic CCP** [Knight-Palamidessi-Panangaden-Valencia 2012, "Spatial and Epistemic Modalities in Constraint-Based Process Calculi," CONCUR 2012, LNCS 7454]: introduces modal operators on the constraint store to express "agent i's local information" and "agent i believes c." Each modality is a closure operator with additional structure (e.g., an idempotent, monotone projection for the locality modality). This produces the *n-agent spatial constraint system*, which is a lattice-of-modal-views layered over the basic cc constraint system. The structural relevance: spatial cc is a multi-quantale system (multiple modalities, each interacting with the others by a Galois connection), and is a direct stepping-stone toward the kind of quantale-enriched architecture that Prologos's stratification system would make operational on the propagator base.

In summary across these extensions: CCP has consistently provided the conceptual scaffold for elaborating the constraint store with richer algebraic structure (linear, semiring/quantale, probabilistic, temporal, modal), each elaboration producing a corresponding extension of the closure-operator semantics. **The CCP literature has therefore produced — under the heading of "soft CCP" — a fully-developed quantale-enriched CCP framework, consistent with the working hypothesis's frontier claim; what the propagator-network literature contributes is the operational realisation as a parallel-network architecture rather than a process-calculus.**

### §3.3.6 The CCP-quantale connection (load-bearing for the synthesis)

The CCP-quantale connection is the load-bearing piece for §3.3 because it provides the formal antecedent for the working hypothesis's frontier claim that "the quantale-enriched extension [of propagator networks instantiating cc(D)] is a frontier hypothesis with established formal antecedents." Here we trace the connection in detail.

A *quantale* (Q, ⊔, ·, 0, 1) is a complete lattice (Q, ⊔) equipped with an associative binary operation · (the "tensor" or "combination") that distributes over arbitrary joins on both sides:

  q · ⊔_i p_i = ⊔_i (q · p_i)
  (⊔_i p_i) · q = ⊔_i (p_i · q)

The unit 1 is the identity of ·; 0 is the bottom of the lattice. A *commutative* quantale further requires p · q = q · p. A quantale is *idempotent* if p · p = p for all p (in which case · ⊑ ⊔ and the structure collapses toward an inflationary lattice); generic quantales need not be idempotent. The classical reference is [Rosenthal 1990, *Quantales and Their Applications*, Pitman Research Notes 234]. For background on quantales and their categorical place (frames vs locales vs quantales), see Agent 4's slice §3.4.

The **c-semiring** of [Bistarelli-Montanari-Rossi 1997] is

  ⟨A, +, ×, 0, 1⟩

with + idempotent (so ⟨A, +⟩ is a join-semilattice with bottom 0 and top 1), × distributive over +, and additional axioms making + closed under arbitrary joins (giving a complete lattice). When × is associative and unital (with 1), the structure ⟨A, +_∞, ×, 0, 1⟩ — where +_∞ is the arbitrary-supremum extension of + — is **isomorphic to a commutative quantale** with ⊔ = +_∞ and · = ×. The c-semiring framework is therefore a quantale framework; the J. ACM 1997 paper introduced quantale-valued constraints into the constraint-programming literature without using the word "quantale," and the connection was made explicit in [Bistarelli 2004, *Semirings for Soft Constraint Solving and Programming*, LNCS 2962] and in [Bistarelli-Gadducci 2006, "Enhancing Constraints Manipulation in Semiring-Based Formalisms," ECAI 2006, p. 561].

In this framework:

- The **constraint store** is no longer a single element of a Boolean / Heyting lattice (consistent or not) but an element of the c-semiring / quantale Q. Constraints carry numerical or qualitative *preference* values (fuzzy degrees, weights, costs).
- A **tell c with weight w** produces store update σ ↦ σ × c (where × is the quantale tensor); the resulting store carries the combined weight.
- An **ask** checks whether σ is "above threshold" in the quantale order (σ ≥ c_threshold).
- **Composition** of constraints uses the quantale tensor; **consistency of the store** is a non-zero quantale value.

Soft CCP [Bistarelli-Montanari-Rossi 2006, ACM TOCL 7(3):563–589] extends cc(D) to soft cc(C) where C is a c-semiring (equivalently a quantale). The closure-operator semantics generalises: each agent denotes a closure operator on the quantale-lattice (extensive, idempotent, monotone), the parallel composition is intersection-of-fixed-points in this enriched lattice, and the resting points are quantale-valued. The semantic theory is a clean elaboration of the Saraswat-Rinard-Panangaden 1991 framework, with the lattice now carrying additional algebraic structure.

Subsequent work has extended the soft CCP framework along several lines:

- **Quantitative observables and averages** [Di Pierro-Hankin-Wiklicky 2002, "Quantitative Observables and Averages in Probabilistic Constraint Programming," LNCS 2333:212–230]: probabilistic + quantitative observables in the soft CCP framework.
- **Soft CCP with local variables** [Bistarelli-Gadducci-Lluch-Lafuente 2022, "Soft Concurrent Constraint Programming with Local Variables," LNCS 13290]: extends soft CCP with cylindrification, completing the parallel with classical CCP.
- **Local spaces in soft CCP for security** [2024 LNCS paper]: extends soft CCP with notions of locality / spaces relevant to security models.
- **Algebraic structures from CCP for distributed information** [Guzman et al., arXiv:2010.10667]: lifts constraint systems to a multi-agent setting; the algebraic structure obtained is a quantale-valued lattice with locality modalities.

A more recent and structurally important development is the **quantaloidal approach to constraint satisfaction** [Fujii-Iwamasa-Kimura 2021, "Quantaloidal Approach to Constraint Satisfaction," ACT 2021, EPTCS 372:289–305, arXiv:2107.01778]. Fujii-Iwamasa-Kimura observe that fundamental CSP concepts — solution sets, polymorphisms, complexity dichotomies — can be formulated inside the 2-category PFinSet (finite sets and sets-of-functions between them), which is itself a quantaloid (a category enriched over Sup, the category of sup-lattices). They propose generalisations of CSP across broader quantaloid classes, with corresponding generalised polymorphism notions. While Fujii-Iwamasa-Kimura's work is not directly about CCP, it establishes the *category-theoretic side* of the quantale-CSP connection at a level of generality that subsumes both classical CSP and soft CCP, providing a unified categorical framework.

Even earlier, [Mendler-Panangaden-Scott-Seely 1995, "A Logical View of Concurrent Constraint Programming"] provided a *hyperdoctrine* model of (determinate) cc — a categorical structure indexed over a base category, with each fibre carrying the closure-operator lattice of cc agents. This is the categorical generalisation of cc closer to the SRP closure-operator model; the quantaloidal approach is the analogous generalisation for CSP. Connecting the two — hyperdoctrines for cc, quantaloids for CSP — gives a unified categorical picture that is, to the best of the present survey's knowledge, not yet fully systematised in a single published source.

The closure-operator semantics has also been recast by [Gabbrielli-Levi 1991, "Modeling Answer Constraints in CLP and CCP," and subsequent papers], by [Falaschi-Gabbrielli-Marriott-Palamidessi 1997, "Constraint Logic Programming with Dynamic Scheduling: A Semantics Based on Closure Operators," Inf. Comp. 137(1):41–67], and by [Falaschi-Gabbrielli-Lai-Levi 1995, "An Abstract Semantics for Concurrent Constraint Programming," LNCS 982] — all of which retain the closure-operator framework while elaborating the lattice structure for various analyses (abstract interpretation, suspension freedom, deadlock detection). These are not directly quantale-enriched, but they establish that the closure-operator framework is robust across many enriched-lattice settings, making the quantale generalisation a continuous extension rather than a structural rupture.

The 2010 survey [Gabbrielli-Palamidessi-Valencia 2010, "Concurrent and Reactive Constraint Programming," in *A 25-Year Perspective on Logic Programming*, LNCS 6125, pp. 231–253] consolidates this: "A different approach was developed in [Saraswat-Rinard-Panangaden 1991]. The basic idea consists in denoting processes as Scott's closure operators, which have the nice property of being representable by the set of their fixpoints. The operators of the language can then be described as operations on those sets. In particular, parallelism can be modeled simply by intersection." This is the canonical statement of the closure-operator + parallel-as-intersection structure; the soft-CCP elaboration generalises the underlying lattice to a quantale-enriched structure and the closure operator to a quantale-monotone operator.

Taken together: **the quantale-CCP connection is established literature, with foundational papers from 1997 (J. ACM), book treatments from 2004 and 2006 (LNCS, ACM TOCL), and continuing development through 2024.** The frontier claim of the working hypothesis is therefore not "is the connection there?" — it is — but rather "can the propagator-network-as-cc(D) realisation be elaborated to a propagator-network-as-soft-cc(Q) realisation, with Q a quantale appropriate to the project's tropical-quantale cost lattice?" This is a genuine open research question whose formal antecedents (closure operators on quantale-valued lattices) are well-developed.

### §3.3.7 The propagator-CCP isomorphism (key claim)

The genealogical claim of §3.3 is that *propagator networks (Sussman-Radul 2008/2009) instantiate cc(D) for D = an arbitrary lattice with monotone merge, in a different intellectual lineage (AI / dataflow) and a decade later than the original CCP development (Saraswat 1989)*. We now make this isomorphism explicit, stage by stage.

**The primitive correspondence**:

| CCP (cc(D), Saraswat 1989/1991) | Propagator networks (Sussman-Radul 2008/2009) |
|---|---|
| Constraint system D = ⟨D, ⊢⟩, complete algebraic lattice | Lattice with bottom value, monotone merge function |
| Constraint store σ ∈ |D| | Cell value v ∈ Lattice |
| `tell c` (monotone augmentation: σ ↦ σ ⊔ c) | `add-content cell value` (cell ← merge(cell, value)) |
| `ask c → A` (block until σ ⊒ c, then run A) | Threshold reads on cell content; propagator fires when input changes |
| Agent A as closure operator f_A : |D| → |D| | Propagator P as fire function: (cell-reads) → (cell-writes) |
| Parallel composition A ∧ B (intersection of fixed-points) | Co-installed propagators on a network (joint fixpoint) |
| Quiescence / resting point (f(σ) = σ) | Network quiescence (no propagator can fire; BSP scheduler exits) |
| Hiding ∃X. A (cylindrification) | Compound cell with components keyed by X (project away) |
| cc(Herbrand) (unification) | Propagator network with structural-unification cells |
| cc(Kahn) (streams) | Propagator network with stream cells |
| cc(FD) (finite domains) | Propagator network with finite-domain cells (AC-3 etc.) |

The correspondence is structurally tight at every line. In particular:

(a) **Tell = monotone cell-write**. A cc tell adds a constraint c to the store, computing σ ⊔ c. A propagator network's `add-content` writes a new value v into a cell, computing the merge of the existing cell value with v. The merge function on the cell IS the join (⊔) of the cc constraint system; it is monotone, idempotent, commutative, associative — the same algebraic shape, in both cases.

(b) **Ask = threshold-read**. A cc ask blocks until the store σ entails c. A propagator's fire condition is: the cell's value reaches a "threshold" sufficient for the propagator to compute meaningful output. In the LVars formulation [Kuper-Newton 2013] the threshold is made explicit as a *threshold set*; in propagator networks it is implicit in the propagator's fire function ("if input is sufficiently refined, do work; else no-op"). The mathematical structure is identical: a continuous (Scott-continuous, in fact) monotone observation on the lattice, blocking until that observation crosses a fixed threshold.

(c) **Constraint store = lattice-valued cell**. The cc store is a single lattice element σ ∈ |D| at any moment. A propagator network has many cells, each holding a lattice element. The two architectures differ in *cardinality and locality* (cc has a single global store; propagator networks have many local cells), but the per-cell structure is identical to the cc per-store structure. This is a generalization, not a fundamental difference — see §3.3.8 below for divergence points.

(d) **Closure operator = propagator at fixpoint**. A determinate cc agent is denoted by a closure operator on the lattice. A propagator's fire-function, considered as a transformation cell-state → cell-state at fixpoint, is *exactly* a closure operator: extensive (cells only grow under merge, given monotone merge), idempotent (re-firing on the same input does nothing — the propagator quiesces), and monotone (more input information yields at least as much output information). Each propagator IS a closure operator on the lattice carrier of its cells. The whole network IS the parallel composition of these closure operators — i.e., the lattice meet (intersection of fixed-points) of the per-propagator closures, equivalently the supremum of their actions. This is *exactly* the SRP 1991 parallel-as-intersection equation.

(e) **Quiescence = least fixed point**. A cc program reaches its resting point when no further information can be extracted. A propagator network reaches quiescence when no propagator can fire. Both are *least fixed points*: the smallest value σ (resp. cell-state vector) closed under all closure operators in the system. Both are reached by repeated joins until stable. Both are independent of the order in which the closure operators are applied (Knaster-Tarski / Kleene fixed-point theorem; for CCP this is the "diamond property" / confluence; for propagator networks this is CALM-monotonicity / determinism).

(f) **Hiding = compound-cell decomposition**. CCP's existential ∃X is realised via cylindrification, projecting away information about X from the externally visible store. Propagator networks realise this via *compound cells with component-indexed access* — the local information about X lives at a particular component of a compound cell, and propagators that should not see X's information are arranged to read other components. The cylindric-algebra axioms (E1–E4 in [SRP91]) translate to the requirements of component-indexed compound cells in the propagator setting (paths declared via #:component-paths, projection by component-key).

The correspondences (a)–(f) are *structural*, not metaphorical. A propagator network IS a cc program where the choice of "constraint system D" has been generalised to "any lattice with monotone merge" and the global store has been decomposed into many local cells. The closure-operator semantics of SRP 1991 carries through unchanged.

### §3.3.8 Where propagator networks diverge from CCP

The isomorphism is not perfect. Three architectural divergences are worth foregrounding because they correspond to research areas where propagator networks go beyond the classical cc(D) framework:

**(D1) Dynamic topology**. CCP has a fixed network of agents (the program is statically specified). Propagator networks support *cell creation* and *propagator installation* mid-computation, in response to information already on the network. This is a non-trivial generalization: it makes the system non-conservative (the lattice can grow new dimensions), and it requires a notion of *topology stratum* — a distinct stratum where structural mutation is processed between rounds of value computation. Saraswat's classical cc has no such distinction; cc with mobile / higher-order extensions [Saraswat-Lincoln 1992; Cardelli-Mendler 1996] introduces some flexibility but not the full propagator-style dynamic-topology discipline. Prologos's topology stratum is a CCP innovation that the cc literature does not directly cover.

**(D2) Strata**. CCP has, in its basic form, no stratification beyond the closure-operator Galois connection (which is uniformly monotone). Stratified extensions — temporal cc (clock ticks), default cc (preemption), spatial cc (modal layering) — exist but are explicitly designed for specific stratification needs. Propagator networks support a general stratification mechanism (S0 monotone, S(-1) retraction, S1 NAF, topology) where each stratum operates on its own request-accumulator cell and a handler processes pending requests between rounds (see Prologos's `register-stratum-handler!` mechanism, .claude/rules/stratification.md). This generality is a propagator-network architectural contribution; the cc literature has stratified instances but not the abstracted stratification primitive.

**(D3) Cell-locality vs flat shared store**. Classical CCP has a single global constraint store. Propagator networks have many cells, each with its own merge function and its own fire-graph. This is more modular, easier to reason about in pieces, and supports per-cell SRE structural decomposition. The cc literature's spatial extensions [Knight-Palamidessi-Panangaden-Valencia 2012] move toward this by adding agent-local sub-stores, but the shift is smaller than in propagator networks proper. Decomposition of the global store into many local cells is what allows the project's structural-emergence mantra ("structurally emergent information flow ON-NETWORK") to be realised: the shape of computation falls out of the lattice topology of the cell graph, not from a central scheduler choosing what to fire.

These divergences (D1–D3) are not refutations of the isomorphism — they are extensions of it. The structural identity at the ask/tell/store/closure-operator level holds; the propagator framework adds dynamic topology, generalised stratification, and per-cell modularity on top.

### §3.3.9 Genealogy: AI / dataflow lineage vs concurrent-logic lineage

The substantive genealogical observation is that the two lineages — propagator networks coming out of AI / dataflow, and CCP coming out of concurrent-logic / constraint-programming — *built the same primitive independently and a decade apart*, with very limited cross-citation. The propagator lineage runs:

- [Sutherland 1963] Sketchpad — first interactive constraint propagation (CAD)
- [Steele 1980] *The Definition and Implementation of a Computer Programming Language Based on Constraints*, MIT EECS PhD thesis — first elaborated propagator-style language
- [Borning 1981] ThingLab — constraint propagation in object-oriented graphics
- [McAllester 1989] truth maintenance and dependency networks
- [Sussman-Radul 2008/2009] *The Art of the Propagator*, MIT-CSAIL-TR-2009-002 — modern formulation

The CCP lineage runs (in parallel, from a different community):

- [Maher 1987] ALPS — committed-choice languages with logical entailment as synchronization
- [Saraswat 1989] CMU PhD — synthesis of CLP and committed-choice → cc(D)
- [Saraswat-Rinard 1990] POPL'90 *Concurrent Constraint Programming* — full operational semantics
- [Saraswat-Rinard-Panangaden 1991] POPL'91 *Semantic Foundations of Concurrent Constraint Programming* — closure-operator denotational semantics
- [de Boer-Kok-Palamidessi-Rutten 1991] CONCUR'91 *The Failure of Failures* — failures-divergence semantics for indeterminate cc
- [Saraswat 1993] MIT Press book — ACM Doctoral Dissertation
- [Mendler-Panangaden-Scott-Seely 1995] Nordic J. Computing — categorical-logical hyperdoctrine model

A third lineage, deterministic-parallel languages, runs:

- [Tesler-Enea 1968] AFIPS — early single-assignment proposals
- [Kahn 1974] *The Semantics of a Simple Language for Parallel Programming* — KPN dataflow
- [Arvind-Nikhil-Pingali 1989] *I-Structures* — single-assignment data structures for parallelism
- [Reppy 1999] CML — Concurrent ML
- [Marlow et al. 2011] monad-par for Haskell
- [Kuper-Newton 2013] LVars FHPC — lattice-based generalisation

The LVars paper [Kuper-Newton 2013] is significant because it explicitly identifies the lattice generalisation of single-assignment as the unifying framework. Its bibliography cites I-structures, Kahn networks, Concurrent Collections, and Bloom/Bloom^L — but **does NOT directly cite Saraswat's CCP work or the SRP 1991 closure-operator semantics**. The CCP literature is reached only at one remove: through Concurrent Collections (Budimlić et al. 2010), which themselves cite CCP indirectly via Saraswat's work at IBM in the 2000s on Concurrent Collections (CnC). This is a remarkable historical fact: the deterministic-parallelism community of the 2010s reinvented the closure-operator-on-a-lattice primitive without re-engaging the 1989–1995 cc(D) literature, despite that literature having developed exactly the relevant theory in considerable depth.

A fourth lineage, distributed databases, runs:

- [Hellerstein 2010] CALM theorem — monotone = coordination-free
- [Alvaro-Conway-Hellerstein-Marczak 2011] Bloom — monotonicity check for distributed programs
- [Conway-Marczak-Alvaro-Hellerstein-Maier 2012] BloomL SOCC — lattice generalisation of Bloom

Bloom and BloomL took the same step the LVars work did, but for distributed-database queries; again with limited engagement with CCP, although Conway's group did reach out to Hellerstein's CCP-aware students at Berkeley.

The picture: **four independent communities** (AI / dataflow / propagators; concurrent-logic-and-constraint / CCP; deterministic-parallel languages / I-structures / LVars; distributed databases / Bloom) **converged on the same mathematical primitive** (lattice-valued shared state with monotone update and threshold reads) **at four different times across roughly 25 years** (Maher 1987 for ALPS, Saraswat 1989 for cc(D), Arvind 1989 for I-structures, Kuper 2013 for LVars, Hellerstein 2010 for CALM, Alvaro 2012 for BloomL, Sussman-Radul 2008/2009 for propagators with explicit lattice merge). The convergence is structural, not coincidental — there is essentially one good answer to "how should one design a deterministic monotone shared-memory primitive?", and it is a lattice-valued cell with monotone merge and threshold reads.

CCP, having been first (1989) and most fully developed (the SRP 1991 closure-operator semantics + the Mendler-Panangaden-Scott-Seely 1995 hyperdoctrine + the Bistarelli-Montanari-Rossi 1997 quantale-enriched soft CCP + the Olarte-Rueda-Valencia 2013 survey + ongoing work) is the most substantial of these literatures. Yet it is the least cited by the others, in part because it lives in a different sub-community (logic / theoretical concurrency) and in part because its language (closure operators, cylindric algebras, hyperdoctrines) is unfamiliar to the AI and distributed-systems readers.

The genealogical claim is therefore: *propagator networks reinvent cc(D) for an arbitrary lattice and a decomposed (per-cell) store, in the AI / dataflow tradition. The closure-operator semantics of SRP 1991 carries through almost verbatim. The soft-CCP / quantale-enriched extension provides the formal antecedents for the project's tropical-quantale cost layer. The literature to engage with — for both the foundations and the future research direction — is the CCP corpus, not the AI dataflow corpus alone.*

### §3.3.10 Implications for Prologos

The CCP literature is directly applicable to the Prologos project's design questions in three ways.

**(I1) Foundations**: every propagator-design decision can be tested against the SRP 1991 closure-operator semantics. A propagator that is not extensive, not monotone, or not idempotent is, in CCP terms, *not a cc agent* — and the project's mantra ("structurally emergent information flow ON-NETWORK") expresses exactly the discipline that SRP 1991 codifies. Every Prologos cell, every merge function, every propagator fire-function should be a closure operator on the underlying lattice. When the project audits a design and finds that a parameter is not a cell, or that a fire function is not monotone, this is a CCP violation as much as a propagator violation.

**(I2) Stratification**: the cc family's temporal / default / spatial extensions provide the design vocabulary for Prologos's stratification system. The temporal cc round structure (one cc fixpoint per time tick, then residual moves to next tick) maps directly onto the BSP outer loop. The S(-1) retraction stratum maps onto LCC-style consumption (substructural contraction). The S1 NAF stratum maps onto cc with negation-as-failure (Sammet's earlier work). Each stratum is a propagator-as-closure-operator at a different level, with the BSP scheduler orchestrating the cross-stratum fixpoint.

**(I3) Quantale roadmap**: the soft-CCP / Bistarelli-Montanari-Rossi quantale framework provides the formal antecedents for Prologos's tropical-quantale cost lattice. The PPN 4C addendum's introduction of (min, +) cost on cells is structurally identical to a soft-CCP store with the (min, +) c-semiring (the tropical c-semiring [Bistarelli 2004 §3.3]). The Prologos work therefore inherits soft-CCP's closure-operator semantics, soft-CCP's parallel-as-intersection equation, and soft-CCP's resting-point characterisation — without further development of the soft-CCP story, the propagator-network realisation already is on solid quantale-CCP foundations. The frontier work (and a candidate research output for the project) is the explicit identification of the propagator-network-as-soft-cc(Q) instance, with its category-theoretic underpinnings drawn from [Mendler-Panangaden-Scott-Seely 1995] and [Fujii-Iwamasa-Kimura 2021].

The strongest single takeaway: **what the project calls its design mantra — "all-at-once, all in parallel, structurally emergent information flow ON-NETWORK" — IS, in CCP terms, the discipline of cc(D) with tell-as-only-primitive, parallel-as-intersection, and store-as-shared-lattice. The mantra is the operational form of CCP's closure-operator semantics, applied to a particular concrete realisation (a propagator network with per-cell decomposition).**
