> Extends Saraswat's cc(D) to soft cc(C) for C a c-semiring (= quantale). Closure-operator semantics generalises to quantale-monotone operators on quantale-valued lattices. The canonical soft-CCP paper.

[de Boer-Kok-Palamidessi-Rutten 1991] F. S. de Boer, J. N. Kok, C. Palamidessi, J. J. M. M. Rutten. "The Failure of Failures in a Paradigm for Asynchronous Communication." CONCUR'91, LNCS 527:111–126.
> Establishes that the asynchronous, monotone-store nature of cc eliminates the need for branching-time / failures-divergences semantics. Linear-sequence semantics suffices. Foundational for the cc operational theory.

[de Boer-Palamidessi 1990] F. S. de Boer, C. Palamidessi. "On the Asynchronous Nature of Communication in Concurrent Logic Languages: A Fully Abstract Model Based on Sequences." CONCUR'90, LNCS 458:99–114.
> Earlier statement of the same observation; complements [SRP91]'s closure-operator semantics with a sequence-based fully-abstract model.

[de Boer-Gabbrielli-Marchiori-Palamidessi 1997] F. S. de Boer, M. Gabbrielli, E. Marchiori, C. Palamidessi. "Proving Concurrent Constraint Programs Correct." ACM TOPLAS 19(5):685–725.
> Compositional verification methodology for cc programs based on the closure-operator semantics. Demonstrates the practical leverage of SRP 1991's framework.

[Conway et al. 2012] N. Conway, W. R. Marczak, P. Alvaro, J. M. Hellerstein, D. Maier. "Logic and Lattices for Distributed Programming." SOCC 2012. <https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/2391229.2391230>
> BloomL — extends Bloom with arbitrary lattices for distributed programming. Cited by Kuper-Newton 2013 as a closely-related lattice-aware system. The CCP precedent (SRP 1991 closure-operator semantics) is not directly cited; this is one of the convergent rediscoveries discussed in §3.3.9.

[Falaschi-Gabbrielli-Lai-Levi 1995] M. Falaschi, M. Gabbrielli, K. Lai, G. Levi. "An Abstract Semantics for Concurrent Constraint Programming." LNCS 982 (CCP'95).
> Abstract-interpretation framework for cc, retaining the closure-operator structure while elaborating the lattice for static analysis. Important for showing the closure-operator framework is robust.

[Falaschi-Gabbrielli-Marriott-Palamidessi 1997] M. Falaschi, M. Gabbrielli, K. Marriott, C. Palamidessi. "Constraint Logic Programming with Dynamic Scheduling: A Semantics Based on Closure Operators." Inf. Comp. 137(1):41–67.
> Application of the closure-operator semantics to CLP-with-delay; demonstrates that the SRP 1991 framework extends naturally beyond cc proper to the broader family of CLP languages.

[Fages-Ruet-Soliman 2001] F. Fages, P. Ruet, S. Soliman. "Linear Concurrent Constraint Programming: Operational and Phase Semantics." Inf. Comp. 165(1):14–41. <https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0890540100930025>
> Comprehensive operational + phase semantics for LCC. Characterises LCC stores in intuitionistic linear logic. The canonical LCC paper after Saraswat-Lincoln 1992.

[Fujii-Iwamasa-Kimura 2021] S. Fujii, Y. Iwamasa, K. Kimura. "Quantaloidal Approach to Constraint Satisfaction." ACT 2021, EPTCS 372:289–305, arXiv:2107.01778. <https://arxiv.org/abs/2107.01778>
> Categorical-categorical (2-categorical) approach to CSP, with quantaloid structure as the unifying framework. Generalises classical CSP, valued CSP, and (implicitly) soft CCP. Establishes quantaloids as the appropriate categorical setting for constraint-style computation.

[Gabbrielli-Levi 1991] M. Gabbrielli, G. Levi. "Modeling Answer Constraints in CLP and CCP." ICLP'91 / TCS series.
> Tree-structured semantics for CCP with bisimulation. Predates the closure-operator approach but later subsumed by it.

[Gabbrielli-Palamidessi-Valencia 2010] M. Gabbrielli, C. Palamidessi, F. D. Valencia. "Concurrent and Reactive Constraint Programming." In *A 25-Year Perspective on Logic Programming: Achievements of the Italian Association for Logic Programming, GULP*, A. Dovier, E. Pontelli (eds.), LNCS 6125:231–253. Springer.
> Italian-school comprehensive review of cc / reactive cc. Strong overview of the closure-operator semantics, the cylindric algebra treatment, and the temporal / reactive extensions (tcc, ntcc, tccp). Heavily cited as the structural source for §3.3.

[Gupta-Jagadeesan-Saraswat 1996] V. Gupta, R. Jagadeesan, V. Saraswat. "Hybrid cc, Hybrid Automata and Program Verification." Hybrid Systems III, LNCS 1066, Springer.
> Hybrid cc — extension to continuous time and continuous-valued constraints. Bridges cc to the synchronous-language family (ESTEREL, Lustre).

[Hellerstein 2010] J. M. Hellerstein. "The Declarative Imperative: Experiences and Conjectures in Distributed Logic." SIGMOD Record 39(1):5–19.
> The CALM theorem statement: monotonicity of a logic program implies coordination-free distributed execution. Direct relevance to CCP's monotone-store discipline.

[Henkin-Monk-Tarski 1971] L. Henkin, J. D. Monk, A. Tarski. *Cylindric Algebras Part I.* North-Holland, Studies in Logic 64.
> Foundational reference for cylindric algebras. Cited by [SRP91] for the axiomatic treatment of ∃X (cylindrification operators E1–E4 and diagonal elements D1–D3).

[Janson-Haridi 1991] S. Janson, S. Haridi. "Programming Paradigms of the Andorra Kernel Language." Logic Programming, Proc. 1991 ILPS, MIT Press, pp. 167–186.
> AKL — synthesis of Prolog (don't-know nondeterminism), GHC (committed choice), and CCP. Andorra Principle: deterministic goals before nondeterministic goals.

[Jones-Plotkin 1989] C. Jones, G. D. Plotkin. "A Probabilistic Powerdomain of Evaluations." LICS'89:186–195.
> Foundational probabilistic-powerdomain theory; relevant for probabilistic CCP semantics.

[Kahn 1974] G. Kahn. "The Semantics of a Simple Language for Parallel Programming." In Information Processing 74, North-Holland, pp. 471–475.
> Kahn process networks (KPN). Cited by [SRP91] as a particular instance of cc — the constraint system for streams gives cc(B) which is precisely KPN. Foundational for dataflow languages and (by [SRP91]) for the cc family.

[Kahn-Saraswat 1990] K. Kahn, V. Saraswat. "Actors as a Special Case of Concurrent Constraint Programming." OOPSLA/ECOOP'90.
> Demonstrates that the actor model can be embedded within cc(D) for an appropriate constraint system. Foundational for the propagator-as-cc isomorphism — propagator networks differ from CCP largely in cardinality and locality, not in primitive semantics.

[Knight-Palamidessi-Panangaden-Valencia 2012] S. Knight, C. Palamidessi, P. Panangaden, F. D. Valencia. "Spatial and Epistemic Modalities in Constraint-Based Process Calculi." CONCUR 2012, LNCS 7454.
> Adds spatial / epistemic modalities to cc constraint systems. n-agent spatial constraint system — lattice-of-modal-views.

[Kuper-Newton 2013] L. Kuper, R. R. Newton. "LVars: Lattice-based Data Structures for Deterministic Parallelism." FHPC 2013, ACM. <https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/2502323.2502326>
> LVars — generalises single-assignment to monotone-merge-on-arbitrary-lattice. Bibliography includes I-structures, Kahn networks, BloomL, Concurrent Collections, Bloom; **does NOT directly cite Saraswat 1989/1991 or the SRP closure-operator semantics**. Noteworthy for the genealogical claim of §3.3.9 — it is the rediscovery of cc(arbitrary lattice) in the deterministic-parallelism community.

[Lambek-Scott 1986] J. Lambek, P. J. Scott. *Introduction to Higher Order Categorical Logic.* Cambridge University Press, Cambridge Studies in Adv. Math. 7.
> Categorical logic, including the treatment of variables and variable-free calculi via cylindrification (Lambek's cartesian closed categories with explicit variable handling). Cited by [SRP91] for the categorical interpretation of the diagonal-element axiomatisation.

[Maher 1987] M. J. Maher. "Logic Semantics for a Class of Committed-Choice Programs." Logic Programming, Proc. 1987 ICLP (Melbourne), MIT Press, pp. 858–876.
> ALPS — the immediate antecedent of cc(D). Recasts the synchronization primitives of GHC, Concurrent Prolog, PARLOG as logical entailment checks against a constraint store. Cited by [SRP91] as the precursor in the concurrent-logic-to-CCP transition.

[Mendler-Panangaden-Scott-Seely 1995] N. P. Mendler, P. Panangaden, P. J. Scott, R. A. G. Seely. "A Logical View of Concurrent Constraint Programming." Nordic Journal of Computing 2(2):181–220. <https://www.math.mcgill.ca/rags/ccp/mpss.abstract.html>
> Categorical-logical model of (determinate) cc as a hyperdoctrine. Combinators of cc become logical connectives. The strongest formal expression of the "logic IS computation" slogan in the cc literature; foundational for connecting cc to first-order categorical logic.

[Nielsen-Palamidessi-Valencia 2002] M. Nielsen, C. Palamidessi, F. D. Valencia. "Temporal Concurrent Constraint Programming: Denotation, Logic and Applications." Nordic Journal of Computing 9:145–188.
> ntcc — non-deterministic timed cc. Provides denotational semantics + a temporal logic for cc, with applications to reactive system specification.

[Olarte-Rueda-Valencia 2013] C. Olarte, C. Rueda, F. D. Valencia. "Models and Emerging Trends of Concurrent Constraint Programming." Constraints 18(4):535–578. <https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10601-013-9145-3>
> Comprehensive survey of CCP variants and their semantics. Catalogues the closure-operator framework, the cylindric-algebra treatment, the temporal / spatial / soft / linear extensions. Authoritative reference for §3.3.4–§3.3.5.

[Panangaden-Saraswat-Scott-Seely 1993] P. Panangaden, V. Saraswat, P. J. Scott, R. A. G. Seely. "A Hyperdoctrinal View of Concurrent Constraint Programming." REX Workshop on Semantics: Foundations and Applications, LNCS 666, Springer.
> Earlier conference version of [Mendler-Panangaden-Scott-Seely 1995]. Establishes that the denotational semantics of determinate ccp forms a hyperdoctrine.

[Radul 2009] A. Radul. *Propagation Networks: A Flexible and Expressive Substrate for Computation.* MIT EECS PhD thesis. <https://dspace.mit.edu/handle/1721.1/54635>
> Comprehensive treatment of propagator networks. Lineage explicitly traced to Steele 1980 (constraint propagation), Borning 1981 (ThingLab), McAllester 1989 (TMS); does not engage CCP literature.

[Rosenthal 1990] K. I. Rosenthal. *Quantales and Their Applications.* Pitman Research Notes in Math. 234, Longman.
> Foundational quantale-theory reference. Cited as the quantale-theory backbone for Agent 4's slice §3.4 and for the soft-CCP / quantale connection here.

[Saraswat 1989a] V. A. Saraswat. *Concurrent Constraint Programming Languages.* PhD thesis, Carnegie Mellon University, CMU-CS-89-108, January 1989. Advisor: Dana Scott.
> The foundational cc(D) thesis. Awarded the ACM Doctoral Dissertation Award; subsequently the basis of the 1993 MIT Press monograph.

[Saraswat 1993] V. A. Saraswat. *Concurrent Constraint Programming.* MIT Press, ACM Doctoral Dissertation Award series. ISBN 978-0-262-19297-2 (also 0-262-52799-5 paperback). <https://mitpress.mit.edu/9780262192972/>
> Book version of the 1989 PhD with extensive elaborations. Authoritative reference for the cc(D) framework.

[Saraswat-Jagadeesan-Gupta 1995] V. A. Saraswat, R. Jagadeesan, V. Gupta. "Default Timed Concurrent Constraint Programming." POPL'95, pp. 272–285. <https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/199448.199513>
> Default cc — strong preemption (immediate response to absence of constraint). Synchronous-language semantics (ESTEREL-style). Full version: "Timed Default Concurrent Constraint Programming," J. Symbolic Computation 22(5–6):475–520, 1996.

[Saraswat-Kahn-Levy 1990] V. A. Saraswat, K. M. Kahn, J. Levy. "Janus: A Step Towards Distributed Constraint Programming." Proc. 1990 N. American Conf. on Logic Programming.
> Janus — first cc instance designed for distributed execution. Demonstrates the actor-CCP correspondence.

[Saraswat-Lincoln 1992] V. A. Saraswat, P. Lincoln. "Higher-Order, Linear, Concurrent Constraint Programming." Xerox PARC Technical Report (July 1992).
> Higher-order extension of cc combined with linear-logic constraint systems. Antecedent of [Fages-Ruet-Soliman 2001].

[Saraswat-Rinard 1990] V. A. Saraswat, M. Rinard. "Concurrent Constraint Programming." POPL'90, pp. 232–245. ACM Press. <https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/96709.96733>
> The first POPL paper on cc — operational semantics for the cc framework. Predates [SRP91]'s closure-operator denotational treatment.

[Saraswat-Rinard-Panangaden 1991] V. A. Saraswat, M. Rinard, P. Panangaden. "Semantic Foundations of Concurrent Constraint Programming." POPL'91, pp. 333–352. ACM Press. <https://people.csail.mit.edu/rinard/paper/popl91.pdf>
> The closure-operator denotational semantics of cc. Constraint system as Scott information system; agent as closure operator; parallel composition as intersection-of-fixed-points; full abstraction; cylindric algebra treatment of ∃X. **Most influential single paper in cc / CCP**; received the ALP Most Influential Paper in 20 Years award (2004). Heavy citation backbone for the entire §3.3 development.

[Smolka 1995] G. Smolka. "The Oz Programming Model." In *Computer Science Today: Recent Trends and Developments*, J. van Leeuwen (ed.), LNCS 1000:324–343. Springer.
> Foundational paper for Oz / Mozart. CCP-based computational model; ports as cc-cells; spaces as encapsulated sub-stores.

[Sussman-Radul 2009] G. J. Sussman, A. Radul. "The Art of the Propagator." MIT-CSAIL-TR-2009-002, January 2009. <https://dspace.mit.edu/handle/1721.1/44215>
> Modern formulation of the propagator-network programming model: cells with monotone merge, propagators as message-passing computational elements. Lineage traced to Steele 1980 / Borning 1981; CCP not cited. Foundational reference for the propagator side of the §3.3.7 isomorphism.

[Tarski 1956] A. Tarski. *Logic, Semantics, Metamathematics.* Oxford Univ. Press. (Especially the chapters on consequence operators.)
> Foundational reference for closure-operator / consequence-operator theory.

[Van Hentenryck-Saraswat-Deville 1991/1995] P. Van Hentenryck, V. A. Saraswat, Y. Deville. "Design, Implementation and Evaluation of the Constraint Language cc(FD)." LNCS 910:293–316.
> cc(FD) — finite-domain instance of cc. Bridge between CCP and finite-domain CSP / propagation algorithms.

---

## §3.4 Quantale theory primer

### §3.4.1 Definition and basic algebra

A *quantale* is a complete lattice $Q$ together with an associative binary operation $\otimes : Q \times Q \to Q$ that distributes over arbitrary joins on both sides. Concretely, for every family $\{a_i\}_{i \in I}$ in $Q$ and every $b \in Q$,
$$\big(\bigvee_i a_i\big) \otimes b = \bigvee_i (a_i \otimes b), \qquad b \otimes \big(\bigvee_i a_i\big) = \bigvee_i (b \otimes a_i).$$
The bilateral distributivity over arbitrary (not merely finite) joins is the core algebraic content. By the adjoint functor theorem, the bilateral distributivity is equivalent to the existence, for each $a \in Q$, of right adjoints to $a \otimes (-)$ and $(-) \otimes a$. These adjoints are the *residuations* (often denoted $a \backslash b$ and $b / a$) and they act as internal homs: the inequalities $a \otimes b \le c$, $b \le a \backslash c$, and $a \le c / b$ are equivalent. A quantale is therefore at once an order-theoretic, an algebraic, and a closed-monoidal object: it is a closed monoidal sup-lattice, equivalently a monoid object in the symmetric monoidal category $\mathbf{Sup}$ of sup-lattices and join-preserving maps [Joyal-Tierney 1984; Rosenthal 1990; Eklund et al. 2018].

A quantale is *unital* if $\otimes$ has a two-sided identity $1 \in Q$. It is *commutative* if $a \otimes b = b \otimes a$. A unital quantale is *integral* (equivalently, *affine*) when $1$ coincides with the top element $\top$. It is *idempotent* if $a \otimes a = a$ for all $a$. The simultaneous presence of integrality and idempotence forces $\otimes$ to coincide with binary meet $\wedge$, in which case the quantale collapses to a *frame* (the order-theoretic dual of a *locale*) [nLab quantale]. Frames are thus a degenerate edge of the quantale design space, and quantales are the natural noncommutative generalisation of locales — a viewpoint emphasised throughout Joyal-Tierney's *Extension of the Galois Theory of Grothendieck* [Joyal-Tierney 1984], which constructs the entire theory of locales as commutative monoid objects in $\mathbf{Sup}$ and quantales as the noncommutative variant.

A quantale is *involutive* (also *star-quantale*) if it carries an order-preserving involution $(\cdot)^* : Q \to Q$ satisfying $(a \otimes b)^* = b^* \otimes a^*$ and $1^* = 1$. Involution is the algebraic shadow of time-reversal, of relation-transpose, and of operator-adjoint; it is what allows quantales to model both quantum logic (where it lifts to a Hilbert-space adjoint) and bidirectional process semantics. The involutive case is the structure Mulvey originally singled out under the symbol "&" [Mulvey 1986].

### §3.4.2 Mulvey's "&": a brief history

The term *quantale* was coined by Christopher J. Mulvey in a four-page note titled simply "&" in the *Supplement to the Rendiconti del Circolo Matematico di Palermo*, Series II, no. 12 [Mulvey 1986]. The portmanteau *quantum + locale* was deliberate: Mulvey's motivation came from the search for a noncommutative generalisation of the locale-theoretic spectrum that could accommodate the Gelfand–Naimark spectrum of a (noncommutative) C*-algebra. The "&" symbol stood for the noncommutative product on the involutive complete lattice that arises canonically from the algebra structure on the lattice of closed two-sided ideals of a C*-algebra.

Mulvey's intuition was that the abandonment of commutativity in algebraic geometry — passing from commutative rings to noncommutative C*-algebras — must be matched in the lattice-theoretic spectrum by the abandonment of meet-idempotence. The frame (idempotent affine commutative quantale) corresponds to the commutative (and so spatial) case; the general quantale corresponds to the noncommutative case where the operator $\otimes$ is genuinely a tensor product rather than a meet. This program was developed in subsequent papers with Pelletier [Mulvey-Pelletier 1992; Mulvey-Pelletier 2001], where the *Gelfand quantale* of a C*-algebra and its *points* are constructed, with points appearing as algebraically-irreducible representations on atomic orthocomplemented sup-lattices.

The pre-quantale history is itself instructive. Joyal and Tierney's 1984 *Memoirs* monograph [Joyal-Tierney 1984] had already developed, in service of a generalised Galois theory for Grothendieck topoi, the full theory of $\mathbf{Sup}$, locales, and "rings" in $\mathbf{Sup}$ (i.e. quantales avant la lettre). The text covers sup-lattices, rings/modules/descent in $\mathbf{Sup}$, locales, spaces, open maps, and base change, with localic groupoids appearing as the representing objects for arbitrary topoi. The conceptual realisation that Mulvey's "quantal" structure was the same algebraic object as Joyal-Tierney's "ring in $\mathbf{Sup}$" gave the theory two distinct lineages — algebraic-geometric (via spectra) and categorical-logical (via topoi) — that have remained intertwined.

Rosenthal's 1990 *Quantales and Their Applications* [Rosenthal 1990] is the first book-length treatment. It systematically develops sup-lattices and frames, the basic theory of quantales and their morphisms, the connection between quantales and ideal theory in commutative rings, idempotent right-sided quantales as carriers for closed-ideal lattices of C*-algebras, and the role of quantales in Girard's linear logic. The book remains the standard introduction.

Modern treatment is offered by Eklund, Gutiérrez García, Höhle, and Kortelainen's 2018 *Semigroups in Complete Lattices: Quantales, Modules and Related Topics* [Eklund et al. 2018], which recasts the subject within categorical algebra and develops applications to C*-algebras, MV-algebras, fuzzy sets, and automata. Hofmann, Seal, and Tholen's 2014 *Monoidal Topology* [Hofmann-Seal-Tholen 2014] provides the most thorough exposition of (T,V)-algebras over quantales, fusing Barr's relational presentation of topology with Lawvere's enrichment-theoretic view of metrics under a single quantale-parametric framework.

### §3.4.3 *-autonomy and linear logic

A commutative unital quantale carries the structure of a symmetric monoidal closed (0,1)-category. When the quantale possesses a *dualising object* $\bot$ — an element such that the double-negation map $a \mapsto (a \to \bot) \to \bot$ is the identity — it becomes a *-autonomous category in the sense of Barr [Barr 1991], and provides a model for the multiplicative fragment of classical linear logic [Yetter 1990; Rosenthal 1990 ch. 8]. Yetter's 1990 *Journal of Symbolic Logic* paper "Quantales and (noncommutative) linear logic" [Yetter 1990] makes the connection explicit: commutative quantales with a dualising element model commutative MILL/MLL, while noncommutative quantales (Mulvey's original "&") model Girard's *cyclic linear logic* — a noncommutative linear logic in which the exchange rule is replaced by a cyclic permutation rule. The semantics of linear logic obtained from Girard's *phase spaces* is then Day convolution for posets [nLab phase semantics; Day 1970].

This connection is load-bearing for the working hypothesis of the present synthesis. Quantales are the algebraic shadow of linear logic; *-autonomy is what gives a quantale its negation, its De Morgan duality between $\otimes$ and its dual $\parr$, and therefore its capacity to model bidirectional information flow. A propagator network whose lattice $Q$ is *-autonomous can express both forward and backward information flow as instances of the same algebraic operation, at different polarities. The relevance to async — where futures and continuations are *the same object* with reversed time-arrow — is direct (see §7.5).

### §3.4.4 Operations summary

For a quantale $Q$ the canonical operations are:

- **Joins** $\bigvee : \mathcal{P}(Q) \to Q$ — arbitrary suprema, the lattice operation
- **Tensor** $\otimes : Q \times Q \to Q$ — the monoidal operation, distributing bilaterally over joins
- **Residuations** $\backslash, /$ — right adjoints to $\otimes$ in either argument
- **Meets** (derived) $\bigwedge : \mathcal{P}(Q) \to Q$ — by completeness
- **Optional**: unit $1$, involution $(\cdot)^*$, dualising object $\bot$

The bilateral distributivity makes $\otimes$ a *positive*, join-preserving operation in both arguments. Meets are derived but generally do not interact distributively with $\otimes$; that is what distinguishes a quantale from a frame (where $\otimes = \wedge$ and so trivially distributes over meet).

## §3.5 Q-enriched categories and Lawvere metric

### §3.5.1 The Lawvere insight

In a 1973 paper now considered foundational, F. William Lawvere observed that the category of metric spaces and short maps could be recovered as a special case of categorical enrichment [Lawvere 1973/2002]. Specifically, take the closed monoidal poset
$$\mathcal{P}_{+} = ([0, \infty], \ge, +, 0)$$
— the non-negative extended reals ordered by $\ge$ (so that smaller distances are higher in the order), with $\otimes := +$ (numerical addition) as monoidal product and $0$ as monoidal unit. A category enriched over $\mathcal{P}_{+}$ is precisely a (generalised) metric space: a set $X$ with a function $d : X \times X \to [0, \infty]$ satisfying

- $d(x, x) \ge 0$ — reflexivity, equivalently $0 \ge d(x, x)$ — i.e. the *identity arrow* $0 \to d(x,x)$ exists in $([0,\infty], \ge)$
- $d(y, z) + d(x, y) \ge d(x, z)$ — triangle inequality, equivalently the categorical *composition* $d(y, z) \otimes d(x, y) \to d(x, z)$

Lawvere's metric is *generalised* in three respects: it admits $\infty$ (corresponding to disconnected components), drops symmetry $d(x,y) = d(y,x)$ (which would correspond to $\mathcal{P}_{+}$-enriched *involution*), and drops separation $d(x,y) = 0 \implies x = y$ (which would correspond to *Cauchy completeness*). What remains — reflexivity and triangle — is exactly what categorical structure demands.

The paper's larger thesis is that *truth values for generalised logic can be taken in any closed category*, and the resulting category-theoretic notions (limits, colimits, adjunctions) acquire interpretations as generalised quantifiers, formal series, and metric completions. The triangle inequality and the cut rule of logic are revealed to be the same structural law — composition in a $V$-enriched category — at different choices of $V$ [Lawvere 1973/2002 §1, §2; Baez 2014].

The Lawvere quantale $\mathcal{P}_{+}$ is itself a quantale (commutative, unital with $0$, integral since $0$ is also the top in the reversed order). The reversal of order is the standard convention: distances are *costs*, and "lower cost" is "higher in the lattice" because the natural lattice operation on costs is *take the better one*, i.e. take the minimum, which under reverse order is the join.

### §3.5.2 Q-categories: hom-objects in a quantale

For a unital quantale $Q$, a *Q-enriched category* (or *Q-category*) consists of:

- A class of objects $\mathrm{ob}(\mathcal{C})$
- For each pair $(x, y)$, a hom-object $\mathcal{C}(x, y) \in Q$
- *Identities*: $1 \le \mathcal{C}(x, x)$
- *Composition*: $\mathcal{C}(y, z) \otimes \mathcal{C}(x, y) \le \mathcal{C}(x, z)$

A *Q-functor* $F : \mathcal{C} \to \mathcal{D}$ is a function on objects with $\mathcal{C}(x, y) \le \mathcal{D}(Fx, Fy)$. A *Q-distributor* (or *bimodule*, or *profunctor*) $\Phi : \mathcal{C} \nrightarrow \mathcal{D}$ is a $Q$-valued matrix $\Phi(d, c) \in Q$ that is functorial on both sides.

The interpretation flexes by choice of $Q$. With $Q = \mathbf{2}$ (the two-element Boolean quantale, $\otimes = \wedge$), $Q$-categories are preorders. With $Q = \mathcal{P}_{+}$, they are Lawvere generalised metric spaces. With $Q = [0,1]$ under multiplication, they are *probabilistic* spaces. With $Q = \mathcal{P}(M^*)$ the powerset of a free monoid, they are weighted automata. The same algebra of categorical structure — composition, adjunction, weighted (co)limits, Cauchy completion — instantiates in all of them.

Kelly's *Basic Concepts of Enriched Category Theory* [Kelly 1982/2005] is the standard treatise covering enrichment over a closed symmetric monoidal $V$. It develops $V$-categories, weighted (co)limits, Yoneda, Kan extensions, and the doctrine of essentially algebraic structures. The 1982 Cambridge lecture notes were reissued in 2005 as TAC Reprint 10, a freely-available version that has become a working reference for the enriched-category community.

### §3.5.3 Cauchy completion and weighted (co)limits

A profunctor $\Phi : \mathcal{C} \nrightarrow \mathcal{D}$ admits a *right adjoint* if there is $\Psi : \mathcal{D} \nrightarrow \mathcal{C}$ with $\Psi \circ \Phi \le \mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}$ and $\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{D}} \le \Phi \circ \Psi$. A $Q$-category is *Cauchy complete* when every left-adjoint profunctor into it is *representable* (factors through a hom). Cauchy completion is the closure under *absolute (co)limits* — those preserved by every $Q$-functor.

For metric spaces ($Q = \mathcal{P}_{+}$), Cauchy completeness coincides with the classical metric notion: a space is Cauchy complete iff every Cauchy sequence converges. This is Lawvere's celebrated re-discovery: the metric notion is captured exactly by the categorical absolute-limit notion, with no metric-specific argument [Lawvere 1973/2002 §6; Stewart 2023].

The general theory of $Q$-cocompletions and weighted colimits was substantially extended by Stubbe in a series of papers [Stubbe 2005a; Stubbe 2005b; Stubbe 2006; Stubbe 2013] to the setting of *quantaloids* — many-object generalisations of quantales (a quantaloid is a category enriched in $\mathbf{Sup}$, equivalently a "many-object quantale" where the hom-sets are sup-lattices). Stubbe systematically treats $Q$-categories, $Q$-functors, $Q$-distributors, adjoint $Q$-functors, weighted (co)limits, presheaf categories, free cocompletion, Cauchy completion, and Morita equivalence. The 2005 *TAC* paper "Categorical structures enriched in a quantaloid: categories, distributors and functors" [Stubbe 2005a] sets up the foundations; "Tensored and cotensored categories" [Stubbe 2006] characterises cocomplete $Q$-categories as the tensored-and-conically-cocomplete ones, or equivalently the tensored-cotensored-and-order-cocomplete ones.

### §3.5.4 Q-modules and sup-lattice-valued actions

For a quantale $Q$, a *(left) Q-module* is a sup-lattice $M$ with a sup-preserving action $\cdot : Q \times M \to M$ satisfying $(a \otimes b) \cdot m = a \cdot (b \cdot m)$ and (in the unital case) $1 \cdot m = m$. The action distributes over joins in both arguments: $\big(\bigvee_i a_i\big) \cdot m = \bigvee_i (a_i \cdot m)$ and $a \cdot \big(\bigvee_j m_j\big) = \bigvee_j (a \cdot m_j)$ [Galatos-Tsinakis 2005; Russo 2010; Eklund et al. 2018 ch. 3].

The category $Q\text{-}\mathbf{Mod}$ has objects the $Q$-modules and morphisms the sup-preserving action-preserving maps. It is a complete and cocomplete category. *Free $Q$-modules* are generated by sets — the free $Q$-module on a set $X$ is $Q^X$ with the diagonal action — and the *Q-module transforms* (homomorphisms between free modules) play the same role as matrices in linear algebra over a ring. Russo's 2010 paper [Russo 2010] develops Q-module transforms as universal operators and applies them to fuzzy image compression and mathematical morphology.

A *closure operator* on a $Q$-module $M$ is an order-preserving, inflationary, idempotent map $j : M \to M$. A closure operator that additionally preserves the action — $j(a \cdot m) = a \cdot j(m)$ — corresponds to a *quotient* of $M$. On a quantale itself, the analogous notion is a *nucleus*: a closure operator $j$ with $j(a) \otimes j(b) \le j(a \otimes b)$. Nuclei on a quantale $Q$ correspond bijectively to quantale congruences and so to quantale quotients [Rosenthal 1990 ch. 3; nLab quantale]. The set of nuclei on $Q$ is itself a quantale under pointwise order; this is the foundation of the quantic-nuclei calculus [On quantale of quantic nuclei 2019].

### §3.5.5 Approach spaces and the topology–metric–order triad

Lowen's *Approach Spaces* [Lowen 1997] introduced a topological structure that simultaneously generalises topological spaces and metric spaces. An approach space is a set $X$ with a *distance function* $\delta : X \times \mathcal{P}(X) \to [0, \infty]$ measuring the distance from a point to a set, satisfying axioms that reduce to the topological case when $\delta$ is two-valued and to the metric case when $\delta(x, A) = \inf_{a \in A} d(x, a)$. The category $\mathbf{App}$ of approach spaces is a topological category over $\mathbf{Set}$ that contains both $\mathbf{Top}$ and $\mathbf{Met}$ as full subcategories.

The categorical realisation due to Clementino-Hofmann-Tholen and developed in *Monoidal Topology* [Hofmann-Seal-Tholen 2014] is that approach spaces are $(T, V)$-algebras for $V = \mathcal{P}_{+}$ (the Lawvere quantale) and $T$ the ultrafilter monad. Replacing the quantale gives different geometric/probabilistic structures on the same monad scaffolding, while replacing the monad gives different relational structures (preorders, metric spaces, Cauchy spaces) over the same quantale of values. The book exhibits this $(T, V)$-parametric framework as a unifying topology-metric-order triad with quantales as the *cost* parameter and monads as the *shape* parameter.

## §7 Cross-cutting deep-dive

### §7.1 Quantale theory recap

Refer to §3.4 for definitions. The salient operational facts are:

- **Bilateral distributivity** of $\otimes$ over arbitrary joins guarantees the existence of internal homs (residuations) by the adjoint functor theorem, making every quantale automatically a closed monoidal sup-lattice.
- **Joins are unconditional** (over arbitrary sets), making quantales *complete* in a way that bare semirings are not. This is decisive for fixpoint computations: arbitrary joins of monotone trajectories converge.
- **Meets are derived but generally non-distributive** with respect to $\otimes$. The simultaneous distributivity $(a \wedge b) \otimes c = (a \otimes c) \wedge (b \otimes c)$ holds only when the quantale is a frame.
- **Modules and quantaloids** are the natural many-object/multi-typed generalisations. Quantaloids ([Stubbe 2005a]) are categories enriched in $\mathbf{Sup}$; they generalise quantales the way ringoids generalise rings.

### §7.2 Tropical (min-plus) quantale

#### §7.2.1 Definition

The *tropical quantale*, also called the *Lawvere quantale* or *min-plus algebra*, is
$$\mathcal{T} = ([0, \infty], \ge_{\text{op}}, +, 0)$$
or equivalently $([0, \infty], \min, +, 0, \infty)$ when written as a semiring (joins are minima under $\le$ which become joins under reverse order $\ge$, addition is the monoidal product, $0$ is the multiplicative unit, $\infty$ is the additive zero / lattice bottom). The dual *max-plus* quantale $([{-}\infty, \infty], \max, +, {-}\infty, 0)$ is structurally identical under sign inversion. Both are completed forms (allowing $\pm\infty$) of the tropical semirings $(\mathbb{R}, \min, +)$ and $(\mathbb{R}, \max, +)$ studied in tropical geometry [Maclagan-Sturmfels 2015; Itenberg-Mikhalkin-Shustin 2007].

The tropical quantale is *commutative*, *unital* (identity $0$), and *integral* (identity coincides with the top of the order, which is $0$ under the cost interpretation $a \le b$ iff $a \ge_{\mathbb{R}} b$). It is *not* idempotent: $a \otimes a = a + a = 2a \ne a$ in general. The *residuation* is $a \backslash b = b - a$ (truncated at $\infty$ if $b < a$), reflecting the slack remaining after committing $a$ units of cost toward a budget of $b$.

#### §7.2.2 Tropical mathematics: scope and history

Tropical mathematics arose independently in several settings. Cuninghame-Green's 1979 *Minimax Algebra* [Cuninghame-Green 1979] systematically develops the algebra $(\mathbb{R}, \max, +)$ as a linear-algebra-style framework for scheduling, network optimisation, and optimal control, with eigenvector theory, matrix powers, and a complete Perron–Frobenius analogue. The book is the canonical early reference and contains a substantial treatment of the spectral theory now standard in max-plus algebra. The same algebra was rediscovered or independently developed by Vorobyev, Romanovskii, Gondran, and others in the Soviet and French operations-research traditions.

Tropical *geometry* — the study of varieties cut out by tropical polynomials, where the "polynomials" are piecewise-linear concave functions — is the geometric realisation of this algebra. Maclagan and Sturmfels' 2015 *Introduction to Tropical Geometry* [Maclagan-Sturmfels 2015] is the modern textbook and develops algebraic varieties, tropical curves, the Newton-polytope approach, and the connection to non-archimedean geometry via Berkovich spaces. Itenberg-Mikhalkin-Shustin's *Tropical Algebraic Geometry* [Itenberg-Mikhalkin-Shustin 2007/2009] grew out of an Oberwolfach Seminar and emphasises the Viro patchworking construction connecting real algebraic geometry to tropical geometry, and the Mikhalkin correspondence theorem relating tropical curve counts to Gromov-Witten invariants.

For the present synthesis, the relevant slice of tropical algebra is its application to *deterministic queueing*, *scheduling*, and *network calculus*. Le Boudec and Thiran's *Network Calculus* [Le Boudec-Thiran 2001] is the canonical reference for min-plus algebra applied to the analysis of communication networks, building on the foundational work of Cruz [Cruz 1991]. The book systematically treats arrival curves, service curves, and end-to-end delay bounds via min-plus convolution
$$(f \otimes g)(t) = \inf_{u} \{f(t - u) + g(u)\}$$
which is the min-plus analogue of ordinary linear convolution and computes (the tightest upper bound on) the cumulative output of a server fed an input characterised by arrival curve $f$ when the server provides service curve $g$. Min-plus deconvolution and sub-additive closure are the core operational tools for computing worst-case delays. The theory was developed independently by Cruz and Chang in the early 1990s [Cruz 1991], with the network calculus framework crystallising through the 2001 book.

#### §7.2.3 Tropical algebra as a quantale: technical remarks

The completed tropical semiring $(\mathbb{R} \cup \{\infty\}, \min, +)$ is a commutative integral quantale. The completion is necessary for the quantale axioms — bilateral distributivity over *arbitrary* joins requires the lattice to be complete, and arbitrary infima of non-negative reals can equal zero in the limit but require a top element to bound joins of unbounded sets above. In the cost interpretation, $\infty$ is *unbounded cost* (= $\bot$ in the lattice, $0_{\otimes}$ in the semiring), and $0$ is *no cost* (= $\top$, $1_{\otimes}$).

The Lawvere quantale's role as the parameter for *generalised metric spaces* (§3.5) is the categorical realisation. A space $(X, d)$ is a $\mathcal{T}$-enriched category iff $d$ satisfies $d(x, x) \le 0$ (reflexivity, since $0$ is the unit) and $d(x, y) + d(y, z) \ge d(x, z)$ (triangle, since composition is $\otimes$ which is $+$).

#### §7.2.4 Recent: propositional logic for the Lawvere quantale

A recent line of work [Bacci-Mardare-Panangaden-Plotkin 2023] develops a *propositional logic for the Lawvere quantale* — a many-valued logic whose truth values are non-negative reals, capturing quantitative reasoning about distances, costs, and approximations. This connects to the *quantitative algebraic theories* line of work and to quantale-valued modal logics for coalgebras [Pavlovic-Mislove-Worrell 2011; Wild-Schröder 2024]. For the Prologos roadmap, the Lawvere quantale is therefore not only a cost-tracking algebra but a *propositional logic* with its own deduction system — which is directly relevant to the propagator stratum analysis (see §7.5).

#### §7.2.5 Project anchor: tropical-quantale on Prologos cells

Prologos already deploys a tropical-quantale cost lattice on cells under the PPN 4C tropical addendum (2026-04-26). The min-plus structure provides the merge function — joining two cost-tagged values takes the minimum cost — and the tensor product provides the cost-composition rule when chaining propagators in series. Bilateral distributivity is the algebraic underwriting for parallel joins of cost-bounded propagator paths: the join of two execution branches' costs is the min of the per-branch costs, and the cost of the parallel execution is the tensor (sum) of the per-branch resource consumption — both operations distribute over joins, so the propagator network is internally consistent with respect to cost. This is the most direct project-relevant instance of the quantale-foundations framework.

### §7.3 Other quantales

The design space of quantales is broad. The following examples are landmark.

**Boolean quantale** $\mathbf{2} = (\{\bot, \top\}, \le, \wedge, \top)$. The two-element lattice with binary meet as tensor and $\top$ as unit. This is the degenerate frame; $\mathbf{2}$-enriched categories are preorders. As a logic, it is propositional truth: classical propositional logic.

**Probabilistic quantale** $([0, 1], \le, \cdot, 1)$ with multiplication as tensor. Used to model probabilistic transition systems and probabilistic metric spaces. Heckmann and Mislove's powerdomain work [Heckmann 1994; Mislove 2000] developed the categorical foundations for probabilistic semantics; the connection to quantale modules has been a recurring theme.

**Powerset (relation) quantale** $\mathcal{R}(M) = (\mathcal{P}(M \times M), \cup, \circ, \mathrm{id}_M)$ for a set $M$. The lattice of binary relations on $M$ with relational composition as tensor and the identity relation as unit. This quantale is involutive (with $R^* = R^{-1}$), and was the example through which Mulvey-Pelletier developed the *quantisation of the calculus of relations* [Mulvey-Pelletier 1992]. It is the canonical noncommutative quantale.

**Free quantale on a set $X$**: the powerset of the free semigroup (resp. free monoid for unital case) over $X$, with set-union as join and the product extended to subsets pointwise: $A \otimes B = \{a \cdot b : a \in A, b \in B\}$ [nLab quantale]. This is the *string quantale*, central in formal language theory and weighted automata.

**Linear logic quantale** — any commutative quantale with a dualising element provides a *-autonomous structure modelling MILL/MLL [Yetter 1990; Rosenthal 1990 ch. 8]. The phase-space construction of Girard is a particular case.

**Concurrent Kleene quantales** — a *double quantale* with two tensor products linked by an *exchange law* $(a \otimes_{\|} b) \otimes_{;} (c \otimes_{\|} d) \le (a \otimes_{;} c) \otimes_{\|} (b \otimes_{;} d)$, modelling the interaction between sequential and parallel composition [Hoare-Möller-Struth-Wehrman 2009; Laurence-Struth 2018]. The exchange law is the algebraic content of "concurrent execution refines (or is refined by) any sequential ordering of independent steps." Convolution-algebra realisations of concurrent quantales have been developed [Cranch-Doherty-Struth 2017].

**Idempotent right-sided quantale of closed ideals of a C*-algebra** — Mulvey's original example, an idempotent (since $I \otimes I = I$ for ideals) right-sided (one-sided multiplication) quantale [Mulvey 1986; Rosenthal 1990 ch. 7].

**Divisibility quantale** — positive naturals ordered by divisibility, with multiplication as $\otimes$ and lcm as join. A simple commutative example with non-trivial residuation (division).

**Day convolution quantales** — for any monoidal preorder $M$, the down-closed subsets $\mathrm{Dn}(M)$ form a quantale via the convolution product $A \otimes B = \mathrm{down}\{a \cdot b : a \in A, b \in B\}$ [Day 1970; nLab Day convolution]. This is the categorified-Stone-duality realisation of phase-space semantics in linear logic.

### §7.4 Quantale modules and closure operators

#### §7.4.1 Module action and the Galois connection

A $Q$-module action $\cdot : Q \times M \to M$ defines, for each $a \in Q$, an operator $a \cdot (-) : M \to M$ that preserves joins (by the action's bilateral distributivity). Therefore each such operator has a right adjoint $a \to (-) : M \to M$ — the *residual* of the action. The pair $(a \cdot (-), a \to (-))$ is a *Galois connection* between $M$ and itself.

Conversely, any Galois connection $\ell \dashv r$ between sup-lattices $L$ and $M$ gives rise to a *closure operator* $r \circ \ell$ on $L$ (and an *interior operator* $\ell \circ r$ on $M$). The fixpoints of the closure operator form the *closed elements*, which inherit a sup-lattice structure where joins are computed via the closure: $\bigvee^{\text{cl}}_i a_i = (r \circ \ell)\big(\bigvee_i a_i\big)$ [Joyal-Tierney 1984; Erné et al. 1993].

This is the algebraic content of *Saraswat's closure-operator semantics for CCP* [Saraswat-Rinard-Panangaden 1991] (covered in §3.3 by Agent 3 — see cross-reference). A CCP store is an element of a constraint system (a complete lattice), agents are bounded closure operators on the store, and parallel composition of agents is the *meet* of closure operators (which is the closure operator whose fixpoints are the intersection of the components' fixpoints). The quantale-module realisation lifts this from a constraint *lattice* to a constraint *quantale*: agents become $Q$-module endomorphisms with closure properties, and parallel composition becomes the natural meet on closure operators of the module. The semiring-CCP and soft-CCP extensions [Bistarelli-Montanari-Rossi 1997; Olarte-Pimentel-Rueda 2014] generalise the constraint store to a *c-semiring* (a particular kind of commutative quantale) — see §3.3 in Agent 3's slice for the application; the framework laid out here is the algebraic side of that bridge.

#### §7.4.2 Nuclei

On a quantale $Q$ itself, a *nucleus* is a closure operator $j : Q \to Q$ satisfying additionally $j(a) \otimes j(b) \le j(a \otimes b)$ — that is, the closure is *lax-monoidal*. Nuclei correspond bijectively to *quantale congruences*, and the closed elements (fixpoints of the nucleus) form a quotient quantale [Rosenthal 1990 ch. 3]. On a frame, nuclei correspond to sublocales (the locale-theoretic generalisation of a subspace).

The set $N(Q)$ of nuclei on $Q$ is itself a quantale under pointwise order [On quantale of quantic nuclei 2019]. The framework of "iterated nuclei" thus admits a lifting where a propagator network can carry not only its own quantale-valued state but also the lattice of *closure operators* that determine which states are *committed* — a topic with strong relevance to ATMS-style worldview management.

#### §7.4.3 Bonsangue-Gadducci-style quantale-module concurrency

Bonsangue and collaborators have developed quantale-module models for concurrency, including *metric predicate transformers* for refinement [Bonsangue-Kok 1995]. The intuition is that a program is a $Q$-module endomorphism (mapping initial-state predicates in a $Q$-module to final-state predicates), refinement is module-morphism inclusion, and parallel/sequential composition are different module operations. Gadducci's recent work extends these ideas to quantum-process calculi and convolution-based concurrency [Cranch-Doherty-Struth 2017]. Module-theoretic refinement is decisive for the project goal of reasoning about propagator-network refinement in a quantale-uniform way: the propagator network IS a $Q$-module action on a state lattice, and refinements of propagator behaviour are $Q$-module morphisms.

### §7.5 Async composition as quantale operation

This section is the bridge between the quantale-foundations slice and the working hypothesis. The synthesis §8.4 leans on it; we therefore develop it with care, marking the speculative content explicitly.

#### §7.5.1 Sequential vs parallel composition

In a quantale, $\otimes$ is *one* monoidal product. To distinguish sequential ("then") from parallel ("with") composition, one needs *two* monoidal products on the same lattice, related by an inequational interaction. This is exactly the *concurrent Kleene quantale* / *double quantale* structure of Hoare-Möller-Struth-Wehrman [Hoare-Möller-Struth-Wehrman 2009]:
$$(a \otimes_{\|} b) \otimes_{;} (c \otimes_{\|} d) \le (a \otimes_{;} c) \otimes_{\|} (b \otimes_{;} d)$$
The inequality direction reflects the asymmetric refinement: any sequential interleaving of two pairs of independent actions refines the genuinely-concurrent execution, but not conversely. Both products distribute over the lattice joins of the underlying quantale.

The interpretation for an asynchronous execution model:

- **$\otimes_{\|}$**: parallel composition of futures, of independent propagator firings, of speculative branches not yet reconciled
- **$\otimes_{;}$**: sequential composition along the dataflow direction, "this future depends on that one"
- **$\bigvee$**: nondeterministic choice / disjunction / merge of speculative branches at their reconciliation point
- **$\bigwedge$**: synchronisation / conjunction / intersection of constraints

The double-quantale structure is the algebraic statement that *async composition is a quantale operation* — specifically, async is the $\otimes_{\|}$ operation, while the dataflow ordering that emerges from cell-dependency is the $\otimes_{;}$ operation, and the exchange law is the formal expression of what it means for the system to be "all-at-once, all-in-parallel" while still respecting per-cell dataflow depth.

This realises the working hypothesis at a precise algebraic level: the propagator network is a model of a concurrent Kleene quantale, where the lattice is the cell value-state space, $\otimes_{\|}$ is the BSP-round simultaneous-firing operation, and $\otimes_{;}$ is the structural dataflow ordering. Stratification (S0 / S1 / S(-1)) gives a *stack* of concurrent Kleene quantales linked by morphisms — but this part is open (see §7.5.4).

#### §7.5.2 Causal composition and Q-enriched composition

When the underlying quantale is *not* the Boolean quantale (i.e. composition is genuinely Q-valued, not just yes/no), the composition law $\mathcal{C}(y, z) \otimes \mathcal{C}(x, y) \le \mathcal{C}(x, z)$ acquires *quantitative* content. For $Q = \mathcal{T}$ (Lawvere quantale), this is the triangle inequality on costs/distances. For $Q$ a probabilistic quantale, this is the multiplicative inequality of conditional probabilities. For $Q$ a Boolean quantale of relations, this is binary causal-precedence.

Async composition along a propagator chain therefore acquires a $Q$-valued *causal cost*, which is the $\otimes$-product of the per-step costs, bounded above by the direct-cost endpoint. The propagator network's emergence of dataflow depth as a partial order is the order on the underlying $Q$-categorical hom-objects.

#### §7.5.3 Quantale-causal clocks: vector clocks generalised

Lamport's happens-before relation is a partial order; its vector-clock realisation [Mattern 1988; Fidge 1988] embeds events in the lattice $\mathbb{N}^P$ where $P$ is the set of processes [vector clock; Lamport 1978]. The vector clock is *exactly* the canonical embedding of an event into the free $\mathbb{N}$-quantale-module on the process set: the join (component-wise max) on $\mathbb{N}^P$ is the lattice operation, and the local-clock-tick action of a process is the $\mathbb{N}$-action.

Lifting this to a general quantale $Q$, a *Q-valued vector clock* would assign to each event an element of the free $Q$-module $Q^P$. The merge of clocks on message receipt is the $Q$-module join. The local action of an event is the $Q$-tensoring on the process's component. The induced order on events is the $Q$-categorical order: $e_1 \le e_2$ iff $e_1$'s clock ≤ $e_2$'s clock in $Q^P$.

The choice of $Q$ determines what the "clock" measures:
- $Q = \mathbb{N}$ (or $\mathbf{2}$): classical vector clock — counts events / records causal precedence
- $Q = \mathcal{T}$ (Lawvere): cost-aware clock — records minimum-cost causal path
- $Q = [0,1]$ (probabilistic): probabilistic clock — records probability mass of causal influence
- $Q$ involutive: bidirectional clock — distinguishes causally-from from causally-to

For Prologos, where async results emerge from BSP rounds plus tropical-cost cells, the natural ambient is a $\mathcal{T}$-valued clock structure on cells, with the Hasse diagram of causal precedence weighted by tropical cost. *Speculative working hypothesis*: the propagator network's $(t, c)$-state — round-number $t$ paired with tropical cost $c$ — is exactly the canonical $(\mathbb{N} \times \mathcal{T})$-valued vector clock for the BSP-LE execution model. This unifies the BSP scheduler (round-counting) with the tropical-cost addendum (cost-tracking) as a single $Q = \mathbb{N} \otimes \mathcal{T}$ quantale-clock structure.

#### §7.5.4 Stratification on quantale modules — open

How does the quantale carry stratification (S0 monotone, S1 NAF, S(-1) retraction)? Two candidate algebraic structures:

(a) *Stratum as a quantale-module retract.* Each stratum $S_k$ is a $Q$-module $M_k$ with a closure operator $j_k : M_{k-1} \to M_{k-1}$ whose closed elements are the values that survive to stratum $k$. Retraction is the closure-operator action, and CALM-monotone fixed-point computation at each stratum is the iteration of $j_k$ to its least fixpoint.

(b) *Stratum as an iterated Q-quantaloid.* The strata stack into a quantaloid (multi-typed quantale) where the $k$-th type is the values at stratum $k$, hom-objects $\mathrm{hom}(M_k, M_{k'})$ for $k' \le k$ are the Galois connections that propagate values upward, and the bridge propagators of the project's stratification framework are quantaloid morphisms.

Both are speculative. They share the property that the single-quantale algebra of §3.4 lifts to a *parametric* (multi-typed, quantaloid) algebra at the cost of a more complex monoidal structure, with the strata acting as the parameter axis. This is consistent with Stubbe's quantaloid program [Stubbe 2005a-2013] — quantaloids are the natural many-typed generalisation of quantales, and stratified semantics is exactly a many-typed phenomenon. *Cross-reference request*: §6.3 (Agent 8) on recent quantale-concurrent work likely contains the most current frontier on this question; we defer the resolution to that slice.

#### §7.5.5 Synthesis: the quantale-substrate hypothesis

Reading §3.4 through §7.5 together gives the core *algebraic* form of the working hypothesis:

1. Async is *a* quantale operation (a tensor $\otimes_{\|}$ in a concurrent Kleene quantale).
2. Sequential composition is *another* quantale operation ($\otimes_{;}$) on the same underlying lattice.
3. Cost / distance / probability / approximation are *Q-enriched* hom-objects, where $Q$ varies by application.
4. Causal clocks are *Q-module valuations* — the vector clock is the $Q = \mathbb{N}$ instance.
5. Stratification is *Q-quantaloid layering* (speculative).
6. Linear-logic / *-autonomy gives bidirectional information flow and the algebraic shadow of futures-and-continuations duality.
7. The propagator-network primitive of "monotone merge over a complete lattice" is the *underlying lattice* of a quantale; making the lattice into a quantale by adding $\otimes_{\|}$ and $\otimes_{;}$ is the algebraic content of putting async on the substrate.

The Lawvere/Mulvey/Joyal-Tierney/Rosenthal/Stubbe lineage is therefore *not* a tangential mathematical curiosity but the precise algebraic vocabulary in which the working hypothesis can be stated, falsified, and developed. Where the synthesis §8.4 calls for "distributed propagator with quantale-causal clocks", this section provides the underwriting: the quantale is a free or generated $\mathcal{T}$-module (or richer $Q$); the clock is a $Q^P$-valuation; the Hasse diagram of causal precedence is the order on the $Q$-categorical hom-objects; and the BSP scheduler's round structure is the dataflow $\otimes_{;}$ component of a concurrent Kleene quantale.
