[Eklund-Gutiérrez-García-Höhle-Kortelainen 2018] Eklund, P., Gutiérrez García, J., Höhle, U., Kortelainen, J. *Semigroups in Complete Lattices: Quantales, Modules and Related Topics.* Developments in Mathematics 54, Springer, 2018. <https://link.springer.com/book/10.1007/978-3-319-78948-4>
> Modern textbook treatment of quantales recasting them within categorical algebra. Covers sup-lattices, quantales, $Q$-modules, MV-algebras, fuzzy sets, automata. Replaces Rosenthal as the standard reference for the post-2000 perspective.

[Erné-Koslowski-Melton-Strecker 1993] Erné, M., Koslowski, J., Melton, A., Strecker, G.E. "A Primer on Galois Connections." *Annals of NY Academy of Sciences*, 1993.
> Survey of Galois connections in order theory. Underwrites the closure-operator material in §7.4.

[Fidge 1988] Fidge, C.J. "Timestamps in message-passing systems that preserve the partial ordering." *Australian Computer Science Communications*, 1988.
> Together with Mattern 1988, the original vector-clock construction. Process-indexed integer vectors with component-wise max as the merge operation form the canonical $\mathbb{N}$-valued causal clock.

[Galatos-Tsinakis 2005] Galatos, N., Tsinakis, C. "Generalized MV-algebras." *Journal of Algebra*, 2005.
> Develops modules over (residuated) quantales in the context of substructural logics and MV-algebras. Background for the $Q$-module material in §7.4.

[Heckmann 1994] Heckmann, R. "Probabilistic Power Domains, Information Systems, and Locales." *Mathematical Foundations of Programming Semantics* (MFPS 1993), LNCS 802, 1994. <https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/3-540-58027-1_20>
> Three equivalent definitions of the probabilistic powerdomain (dcpos, information systems, locales) coinciding on continuous domains. Foundational for probabilistic-quantale semantics referenced in §7.3.

[Hoare-Möller-Struth-Wehrman 2009] Hoare, C.A.R., Möller, B., Struth, G., Wehrman, I. "Concurrent Kleene Algebra." *CONCUR 2009*, LNCS 5710, 2009. <https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-642-04081-8_27>
> The double-quantale / concurrent Kleene algebra paper. Two products linked by the exchange law $(a\|b);(c\|d) \le (a;c)\|(b;d)$. Validates Hoare triples and Jones rely/guarantee. The algebraic shadow of "concurrent execution refines any sequential interleaving of independent steps."

[Hofmann-Seal-Tholen 2014] Hofmann, D., Seal, G.J., Tholen, W. (eds). *Monoidal Topology: A Categorical Approach to Order, Metric, and Topology.* Encyclopedia of Mathematics and Its Applications 153, Cambridge University Press, 2014. <https://www.cambridge.org/core/books/monoidal-topology/8E35730B51B8292860787A842965090B>
> Most thorough exposition of $(T, V)$-algebras over quantales, where $V$ is a quantale and $T$ a (laxly extended) monad. Fuses Barr's relational topology with Lawvere's metric-as-enrichment view. The book is the modern standard for quantale-enriched topology and approach theory.

[Itenberg-Mikhalkin-Shustin 2007] Itenberg, I., Mikhalkin, G., Shustin, E. *Tropical Algebraic Geometry.* Oberwolfach Seminars 35, Birkhäuser, 2007 (2nd ed. 2009). <https://link.springer.com/book/10.1007/978-3-0346-0048-4>
> Lecture-note treatment of tropical algebraic geometry. Includes Viro patchworking and Mikhalkin's correspondence theorem. Geometric counterpart to Maclagan-Sturmfels.

[Joyal-Tierney 1984] Joyal, A., Tierney, M. *An Extension of the Galois Theory of Grothendieck.* Memoirs of the American Mathematical Society 51 (309), 1984. <http://www.ams.org/books/memo/0309/>
> Foundational treatment of $\mathbf{Sup}$, locales, and "rings in $\mathbf{Sup}$" (i.e. quantales). Eight chapters: sup-lattices, rings/modules/descent, locales, spaces, open maps, base change, open morphisms of topoi, descent of topoi. Establishes that every Grothendieck topos is the topos of sheaves on a localic groupoid. The categorical-logical lineage of quantale theory.

[Kelly 1982/2005] Kelly, G.M. *Basic Concepts of Enriched Category Theory.* Cambridge University Press 1982; reprinted as TAC Reprints No. 10, 2005. <http://www.tac.mta.ca/tac/reprints/articles/10/tr10.pdf>
> Standard textbook for $V$-enriched category theory over a closed symmetric monoidal $V$. Develops $V$-categories, weighted (co)limits, Yoneda, Kan extensions. Freely available as TAC reprint.

[Lamport 1978] Lamport, L. "Time, Clocks, and the Ordering of Events in a Distributed System." *Communications of the ACM*, 21(7): 558–565, 1978. <https://amturing.acm.org/p558-lamport.pdf>
> The original happens-before relation. Causal partial order on events, scalar logical clocks. The conceptual ancestor of vector clocks and the quantale-causal clocks of §7.5.3.

[Laurence-Struth 2018] Laurence, M., Struth, G. "Concurrent Kleene Algebra: Free Model and Completeness." LNCS 10801, ESOP 2018. <https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-319-89884-1_30>
> Free-model construction and completeness theorem for concurrent Kleene algebra. Establishes the algebraic theory as decidable for the free case.

[Lawvere 1973/2002] Lawvere, F.W. "Metric spaces, generalized logic, and closed categories." *Rendiconti del Seminario Matematico e Fisico di Milano* XLIII: 135–166, 1973. Reprinted in *Reprints in Theory and Applications of Categories* No. 1, 2002. <http://www.tac.mta.ca/tac/reprints/articles/1/tr1.pdf>
> THE foundational paper. Generalised metric spaces are categories enriched in $([0, \infty], \ge, +, 0)$. Triangle inequality is composition; reflexivity is identity; Cauchy completion is absolute-colimit completion. The germ from which Q-enrichment, approach spaces, and quantale-valued logics all grow.

[Le Boudec-Thiran 2001] Le Boudec, J.-Y., Thiran, P. *Network Calculus: A Theory of Deterministic Queuing Systems for the Internet.* LNCS 2050, Springer, 2001. <https://leboudec.github.io/netcal/latex/netCalBook.pdf>
> Canonical reference for min-plus algebra applied to communication networks. Min-plus convolution as service curve combination, end-to-end delay bounds via min-plus deconvolution. Direct application of the Lawvere/tropical quantale to deterministic queueing.

[Lowen 1997] Lowen, R. *Approach Spaces: The Missing Link in the Topology-Uniformity-Metric Triad.* Oxford Mathematical Monographs, Oxford University Press, 1997. <https://global.oup.com/academic/product/approach-spaces-9780198500308>
> Approach spaces unify topological spaces, metric spaces, and uniform spaces in a single category. The categorical realisation (developed in *Monoidal Topology*) is that approach spaces are $(T, \mathcal{P}_{+})$-algebras for the ultrafilter monad and the Lawvere quantale.

[Maclagan-Sturmfels 2015] Maclagan, D., Sturmfels, B. *Introduction to Tropical Geometry.* Graduate Studies in Mathematics 161, AMS, 2015. <https://bookstore.ams.org/gsm-161>
> Modern textbook for tropical geometry. Develops the tropical semiring, tropical varieties, the Newton-polytope correspondence, Berkovich-space connections. The standard reference for tropical mathematics in the post-2010 phase.

[Mattern 1988] Mattern, F. "Virtual time and global states of distributed systems." *Parallel and Distributed Algorithms*, 1988.
> Together with Fidge 1988, the original vector-clock paper. The vector-of-component-counts representation of the Lamport partial order.

[Mislove 2000] Mislove, M.W. "Nondeterminism and Probabilistic Choice: Obeying the Laws." LNCS 1877, CONCUR 2000. <https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/3-540-44618-4_26>
> Mixed powerdomain semantics for the combination of nondeterminism and probability. Background for the probabilistic-quantale material in §7.3.

[Mulvey 1986] Mulvey, C.J. "&." *Supplemento ai Rendiconti del Circolo Matematico di Palermo*, Series II, 12: 99–104, 1986.
> Origin of the term "quantale" (portmanteau of *quantum* + *locale*). Four pages introducing the noncommutative complete-lattice algebra arising from C*-algebra ideal theory. The motivating example was the lattice of closed two-sided ideals with multiplication.

[Mulvey-Pelletier 1992] Mulvey, C.J., Pelletier, J.W. "A quantisation of the calculus of relations." *Canadian Mathematical Society Conference Proceedings* 13: 345–360, 1992.
> Developments of the relations-quantale and its quantisation. Relational quantale as paradigmatic noncommutative quantale.

[Mulvey-Pelletier 2001] Mulvey, C.J., Pelletier, J.W. "On the quantisation of points." *Journal of Pure and Applied Algebra* 159(2-3): 231–295, 2001. <https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0022404900000591>
> Definition of the *point* of a Gelfand quantale via algebraically-irreducible representations on atomic orthocomplemented sup-lattices. Establishes the C*-algebra spectrum as a quantale-theoretic invariant.

[On quantale of quantic nuclei 2019] "On the quantale of quantic nuclei." *Algebra universalis*, 2019. <https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s00012-019-0627-z>
> The set of nuclei on a quantale forms itself a quantale under pointwise order. Iterated nucleus structure for quantale congruences.

[Olarte-Pimentel-Rueda 2014] Olarte, C., Pimentel, E., Rueda, C. "A Proof Theoretic Study of Soft Concurrent Constraint Programming." arXiv:1405.2329, 2014. <https://arxiv.org/abs/1405.2329>
> Proof-theoretic interpretation of soft CCP via subexponential linear logic with c-semiring-ordered subexponentials encoding preferences. Cross-reference §3.3 (Agent 3) for the CCP application; this paper is the proof-theoretic side.

[Pavlovic-Mislove-Worrell 2011] Pavlovic, D., Mislove, M., Worrell, J. "Testing semantics: Connecting processes and process logics." *Theoretical Computer Science*, 2011.
> Coalgebraic-quantale work connecting testing equivalences with logical-equivalence semantics for processes. Background for the quantale-valued coalgebraic logic line.

[Rosenthal 1990] Rosenthal, K.I. *Quantales and Their Applications.* Pitman Research Notes in Mathematics 234, Longman Scientific & Technical, 1990. <https://openlibrary.org/books/OL1870649M/Quantales_and_their_applications>
> First book-length treatment. Sup-lattices and frames; basic theory of quantales and morphisms; ring-ideal-theoretic examples; idempotent right-sided quantales (C*-algebra closed-ideal lattices); quantales in Girard linear logic. ISBN 0582064236.

[Russo 2010] Russo, C. "Quantale Modules and their Operators, with Applications." arXiv:1002.0968, 2010. <https://arxiv.org/abs/1002.0968>
> Categorical theory of $Q$-modules over unital quantales. $Q$-module transforms as homomorphisms between free objects. Applications to fuzzy image compression and mathematical morphology.

[Saraswat-Rinard-Panangaden 1991] Saraswat, V.A., Rinard, M., Panangaden, P. "Semantic Foundations of Concurrent Constraint Programming." *POPL 1991*. <https://people.csail.mit.edu/rinard/paper/popl91.pdf>
> Closure-operator semantics for CCP: agents are bounded closure operators on a constraint store, parallel composition is meet of closures. The conceptual root of the CCP-quantale connection — see Agent 3's §3.3 slice.

[Stewart 2023] Stewart, N. "From Q to R: a leisurely review of Cauchy completion." Manuscript, 2023. <https://nataliesstewart.github.io/files/cauchy.pdf>
> Pedagogical exposition of Cauchy completion in $V$-enriched categories, with the metric-Cauchy and absolute-colimit notions identified per Lawvere.

[Stubbe 2005a] Stubbe, I. "Categorical structures enriched in a quantaloid: categories, distributors and functors." *Theory and Applications of Categories* 14(1): 1–45, 2005. <http://www.tac.mta.ca/tac/volumes/14/1/14-01abs.html>
> Foundational paper of the quantaloid-enriched categories program. $Q$-categories, $Q$-distributors, $Q$-functors for $Q$ a base quantaloid, the many-typed generalisation of a quantale.

[Stubbe 2005b] Stubbe, I. "Categorical structures enriched in a quantaloid: regular presheaves, regular semicategories." *Cahiers de Topologie et Géométrie Différentielle Catégoriques* 46(2): 99–121, 2005. <https://www.numdam.org/item/CTGDC_2005__46_2_99_0/>
> Regular presheaves and semicategories. Background for the Cauchy-completion theory of $Q$-enriched categories.

[Stubbe 2006] Stubbe, I. "Categorical structures enriched in a quantaloid: tensored and cotensored categories." *Theory and Applications of Categories* 16(14), 2006. <http://www.tac.mta.ca/tac/volumes/16/14/16-14abs.html>
> Characterisation of cocomplete $Q$-categories. The doctrine of weighted (co)limits in the quantaloid-enriched setting.

[Stubbe 2013] Stubbe, I. "An introduction to quantaloid-enriched categories." Manuscript / survey, 2013. <https://www-lmpa.univ-littoral.fr/~stubbe/PDF/SurveyQCats.pdf>
> Survey article covering the full quantaloid-enriched program: $Q$-categories, distributors, functors, adjunctions, weighted (co)limits, presheaves and free cocompletion, Cauchy completion, Morita equivalence. The standard introduction to the body of work.

[vector clock] "Vector clock." Wikipedia. <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vector_clock>
> Reference for the vector-clock construction; partially-ordered $\mathbb{N}^P$-valued timestamps capturing the Lamport happens-before relation.

[Wild-Schröder 2024] Wild, P., Schröder, L. "Expressive Quantale-Valued Logics for Coalgebras: An Adjunction-Based Approach." LIPIcs *STACS 2024* 10. <https://drops.dagstuhl.de/entities/document/10.4230/LIPIcs.STACS.2024.10>
> Recent (2024) work deriving fixpoint equations from quantale-valued modal logics characterising behavioural equivalences and metrics. Hennessy-Milner theorems via Galois-connection fixpoint preservation. Bridges quantale theory to modern coalgebraic semantics.

[Yetter 1990] Yetter, D.N. "Quantales and (noncommutative) linear logic." *Journal of Symbolic Logic* 55(1): 41–64, 1990. <https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/journal-of-symbolic-logic/article/abs/quantales-and-noncommutative-linear-logic/1808CA04372612BCB38E8D3A8B4CCCDB>
> Establishes the connection between Mulvey's quantales and Girard's linear logic. Commutative case: MILL/MLL. Noncommutative case: cyclic linear logic. Quantales with a dualising element are *-autonomous and so model classical linear logic.

[nLab Day convolution] "Day convolution." nLab. <https://ncatlab.org/nlab/show/Day+convolution>
> nLab article. The semantics of linear logic obtained from Girard's phase spaces is essentially Day convolution for posets.

[nLab phase semantics] "Phase semantics." nLab. <https://ncatlab.org/nlab/show/phase+semantics>
> Girard's phase-space semantics for linear logic. Star-autonomous posets with exponential modalities; the (0,1)-categorical instance of $*$-autonomy.

[nLab quantale] "Quantale." nLab. <https://ncatlab.org/nlab/show/quantale>
> Comprehensive nLab article. Definition, examples (relations quantale, Lawvere quantale, ideal quantale, divisibility, Day convolution, free quantale on a set), $*$-quantales, $Q$-Cat, congruences and nuclei, connection to *-autonomous categories and linear logic.

---

## §4 Era III: The Industrial Pragmatism (1995–2015)

The third era of asynchronous programming is not a clean intellectual succession. It is the trajectory of an industry trying to deliver concurrency under the operational pressures of telecommunication uptime, web scale, mobile UI responsiveness, and cloud distribution. Where Era II (Hewitt's actors, Hoare's CSP, Saraswat's CCP) was characterised by formal semantic invention, Era III is characterised by *codification under load*: each surviving primitive (Erlang's process-with-mailbox, the JavaScript Promise, the C# Task, the Go channel, the Naiad timestamp) was hardened by years of production rather than chosen on theoretical grounds. The intellectual cost of this pragmatic posture is that no single primitive subsumes the others. The era closes (Nystrom 2015) with an explicit naming of that fact: that asynchronous programming, as practised, has fractured the surface language into colours that do not compose.

This section traces five related lineages — the actor model from Erlang to Akka to Orleans (§4.1), the futures/promises/async-await arc (§4.2), the function-coloring diagnostic that names what those primitives forced upon callers (§4.3), the FRP-to-reactive-extensions spread (§4.4), and the timely/differential dataflow line that reconnects practical streaming systems back to the lattice substrate the project's hypothesis presumes (§4.6). The capability-based futures lineage (E and CapTP) is covered in §4.5 (Agent 6); references in §4.2 below to promise pipelining defer to that section for attribution.

### §4.1 The Erlang/Akka/Orleans actor lineage

Erlang's origin is industrial in the strongest sense: in 1986, the Ericsson Computer Science Laboratory in Stockholm began searching for a better way to program telephony switches whose uptime targets demanded "availability of nine nines" — failure tolerated at a granularity finer than a phone call (Armstrong 2007). The early prototype was a Prolog dialect; what emerged from the constraint of writing software that "runs forever" was a language whose unit of concurrency was not a thread but a sequential, share-nothing process with a single mailbox, addressed by an opaque process identifier.

Joe Armstrong's 2003 PhD thesis at the Royal Institute of Technology in Stockholm (Armstrong 2003) is the canonical statement of the methodology that grew up around the language. Three positions are load-bearing:

(1) *Concurrency-Oriented Programming* (COP). The world is modelled as a population of independent processes that fail independently. Concurrency is not a performance technique; it is the *modelling primitive*. Sequential processes with isolated heaps and asynchronous message-passing are the substrate, and parallelism is incidental.

(2) *Let it crash*. Defensive programming is rejected. A process that encounters a state it cannot handle terminates immediately and raises an exit signal to its supervisor. The supervisor — itself a process — restarts the failed process from a known-good state. The thesis argues, against decades of imperative-language convention, that the alternative (catching every exception locally and trying to restore consistency) is unsound: a process that has lost knowledge of its own state cannot be trusted to repair it (Armstrong 2003, ch. 4–6).

(3) *Supervision trees*. The runtime structure of an Erlang/OTP application is a tree of supervisors, each watching a fixed set of children and following one of a small set of restart strategies (`one_for_one`, `one_for_all`, `rest_for_one`). The tree itself is the unit of recovery; "fault tolerance" is reduced to a set of standard behaviours (`gen_server`, `gen_event`, `gen_fsm`, later `gen_statem`) parameterised over user code.

What is *not* in Erlang is also load-bearing for the present synthesis. Erlang mailboxes are *unbounded buffered queues*, not channels in the CSP sense. The producer never blocks; backpressure is not a property of the channel but a *supervisory* concern, addressed at runtime by selective receive, queue-length monitoring, and explicit flow control protocols. The model is therefore *not* CCP-shaped — there is no shared lattice cell, no monotone merge, no Galois connection between mailboxes. Each actor's state is a private imperative cell mutated by message handlers; the global state is a tuple of local states with no algebraic structure relating them. This is the shape Hewitt's 1973 actor model proposed, and it is the shape the industry institutionalised.

**Akka** (Bonér 2009; first public release Akka 0.5, January 2010) carried the Erlang model to the JVM via Scala. Bonér's announcement (Bonér 2010) names the explicit goal of porting "an availability of six nines (99.9999%) and even more" from Erlang to a strongly-typed mainstream platform. The early Akka API was untyped — actors received `Any` and dispatched on runtime structure — and the supervision strategies were modelled directly on OTP. Over the 2010s, Akka added clustering (Akka Cluster), persistence (Akka Persistence with event-sourcing), and an HTTP toolkit, becoming the de facto JVM substrate for actor-style services at companies including LinkedIn, Walmart, and PayPal.

Akka's principal architectural revision was *Akka Typed* (introduced 2017, stabilised 2018; Bonér et al. 2017). The motivation was the long-criticised loss of type safety: an actor's protocol was implicit in the dynamic shape of its message-handling, undocumented and unenforceable. Akka Typed re-cast each actor as a `Behavior[T]` — a function `T => Behavior[T]` that consumes one message and returns the next behaviour, possibly with a different message type. Type safety came with a structural shift: the supervision wrapper became itself a `Behavior` (`Actor.supervise(...).onFailure[E](...)`), not an out-of-band runtime registration, which made restart-with-backoff and other compositional supervisors expressible in the same calculus. This is closer to the Pi-calculus session-typed actors of Honda et al. than to Erlang's untyped mailbox; one might read Akka Typed as the moment at which the actor model attempted to absorb the session-typing tradition.

**Orleans** (Bernstein–Bykov–Geller–Kliot–Thelin 2014, MSR-TR-2014-41; an earlier 2011 paper introduced the cloud-services framework at SoCC '11) is the most architecturally distinctive actor system of the era. Microsoft Research's Orleans introduced the *virtual actor* (or "grain") abstraction: actors are not allocated by user code at all. Each actor has a perpetual logical identity (a typed reference); the runtime instantiates it on demand on some node, persists its state, and can deactivate it under memory pressure. From the caller's perspective, "the actor exists." This dissolves three classes of distributed-systems problem that Erlang and Akka left to the user: actor placement, failure-driven re-creation, and lifecycle management.

The Orleans paper is explicit that this addresses the *programmability* gap — that the actor model in its classical form forces application authors to write distribution code (placement, failure handling, persistence) that has nothing to do with their domain. The virtual-actor model offloads this to the runtime at the cost of some control over locality. Production usage at scale (Halo 4 telemetry, Skype back-end, Azure services) demonstrated the model in commercial settings.

For the project's hypothesis, the actor lineage represents a *degenerate* form of concurrent constraint programming (cross-ref §3.3, Agent 3): communication occurs, but the cells (mailboxes) are not lattice elements. There is no monotone merge. No Galois connection between actor states. Backpressure is not a structural consequence of the lattice ordering; it is a runtime behaviour negotiated by acknowledgement protocols. The actor model is concurrent *and* asynchronous, but it is not concurrent constraint — and that absence is precisely what the synthesis will frame as a missed opportunity.

### §4.2 Promises, futures, async/await evolution

The mainline industrial primitive of Era III is the future/promise. The conceptual core (a placeholder for a value that may or may not exist yet) traces to Baker–Hewitt 1977 and Liskov–Shrira's 1988 promise pipelining (covered in Era II). The capability-based promise lineage — Mark Miller's E, CapTP, and the *promise pipelining* contribution that descends from it — is treated separately in §4.5 (Agent 6); the lineage covered here is the value-oriented continuation/coroutine arc that produced async/await.

**F# asynchronous workflows** (Syme 2007 informal release; Petricek–Syme 2010; Syme et al. 2011 PADL) were the first integration of an async monadic style into a mainstream production language with a syntactic affordance — the `async { ... }` builder block. F# asynchronous workflows are an instance of the more general F# *computation expression* mechanism (Petricek–Syme 2014), which generalises Haskell-style do-notation to a wider class of effectful builders. The `let!` form binds the result of an asynchronous computation; `do!` sequences one for effect; `return` lifts a value. The semantics is given in continuation-passing style — each computation is a value of type `Async<'T>` that carries a continuation — and Petricek–Syme's exposition (especially "Syntax Matters", Petricek–Syme 2012) emphasises that writing the computations *looks* like ordinary direct-style code while the rewrite into CPS is mechanical. F# async workflows preceded C# `async`/`await` by roughly five years and were a direct conceptual influence on the later proposal.

**C# `async`/`await`** (Hejlsberg–Torgersen et al., shipped in C# 5.0 / .NET 4.5 in August 2012; formalisation in Bierman–Russo–Mainland–Meijer–Torgersen 2012, "Pause 'n' Play: Formalising Asynchronous C#") was the inflection point at which async-await entered the mainstream. The design rationale (Torgersen 2011, 2012) was explicit: existing asynchronous code in C# (the APM `Begin*`/`End*` pattern, the EAP `*Async`/`*Completed` pattern, and explicit `Task` continuations via `ContinueWith`) was producing code that was hard to read, hard to maintain, and incompatible with structured exception handling. The `async`/`await` mechanism rewrites a marked method into a state machine where each `await` becomes a yield to a scheduler; the semantics preserve direct-style control flow (try/catch, using-blocks, loops) over asynchronous suspensions. The Bierman et al. formalisation gave a small-step semantics for the rewrite, which became the template for similar formalisations in subsequent languages.

**JavaScript Promises** were standardised through a community process. Kris Kowal's *Q* library (2009; Kowal 2009) drew design from Mark Miller's E (cross-ref §4.5) and Tyler Close's Waterken Q. The CommonJS *Promises/A* proposal (Kris Zyp, 2009) gained ad-hoc adoption; the *Promises/A+* specification (2012, anonymous community authorship) fixed the `then` semantics, including the resolution procedure that handled promise-of-promise flattening and the requirement that callbacks fire on a fresh stack. ES6 (June 2015) absorbed the Promises/A+ behaviour into the language standard. ES2017 added `async`/`await` (TC39 Stage 4 December 2016, ES8 ratified June 2017), modelled on C#'s.

**Python `asyncio`** (PEP 3156, Guido van Rossum, 12 December 2012; landed in Python 3.4 March 2014) established the standard event-loop API. PEP 3156 was framed as an interoperability layer rather than a competitor to Twisted or Tornado: the goal was a common abstract event-loop interface (`AbstractEventLoop`) that those frameworks could either implement or adapt, plus a `Future` type compatible with `concurrent.futures.Future` from PEP 3148. PEP 492 (Yury Selivanov, 9 April 2015; accepted 5 May 2015 by Guido van Rossum; landed in Python 3.5 September 2015) introduced *native coroutines*: `async def` and `await` syntax that is parsed as a distinct construct rather than retrofit onto generators (the `yield from` mechanism that asyncio originally used). The split between generator-based coroutines and native coroutines, which initially preserved both for compatibility, was effectively complete by Python 3.7 (June 2018) when `async`/`await` became reserved keywords.

**Rust**'s async story was the most painful — and the most architecturally honest — of the era. The futures library (Aaron Turon and Alex Crichton, 2016; Turon 2016a "Zero-cost futures in Rust"; Turon 2016b "Designing futures for Rust") used a *poll-based* design: a `Future` is a value that can be polled, returning either `NotReady` (which schedules a wakeup via a `Task` handle) or a final value. Crucially, a future does *not* drive itself; an *executor* (Tokio, async-std, etc.) does. This decision was load-bearing for "zero-cost" — futures are nothing but state machines that can be lowered to stack-allocatable types — but it pushed wakeup mechanics into user code, making futures hard to compose by hand. The `async`/`await` syntax (RFC 2394, stabilised in Rust 1.39, 7 November 2019; Matsakis 2019) hid the state-machine generation, but the underlying *split executor / pinned future / waker* model remained, and is the source of much of Rust's reputation for async ergonomics being difficult.

**Swift**'s `async`/`await` (SE-0296; Lattner et al.; first exposed to developers at WWDC 2021, shipped in Swift 5.5) followed the C# precedent nearly verbatim. Swift took the further architectural step of pairing `async`/`await` with *structured concurrency* (SE-0304: tasks, task groups, cancellation propagation) at the same release — implicitly conceding the criticism that bare async/await leaks tasks. (This structured-concurrency response is treated in §5.1 by Agent 7.)

**Kotlin** coroutines (designed by Roman Elizarov and others, 2017; Kotlin 1.3 stable release October 2018; Elizarov 2017a, 2017b) chose a different surface: `suspend` functions, with no `await` keyword; calls to a `suspend` function from within a coroutine context implicitly yield. Internally Kotlin uses *single-shot delimited continuations* — the compiler synthesises a continuation-passing-style transformation of suspend functions. The runtime (`kotlinx.coroutines`) builds *structured concurrency* into the model from the start (`CoroutineScope`, `Job` hierarchies), making it the second mainstream language after Erlang to ship a structured-supervision model in its standard concurrency library.

**Go** — the outlier — refused futures entirely. Goroutines (lightweight stack-segmented threads, Pike–Cox–Griesemer 2009, public release 2012) plus channels (Pike 2012, Go Concurrency Patterns) descend from the Newsqueak/Alef/Limbo line at Bell Labs (Cardelli–Pike Newsqueak 1985; Pike 1989 "The Implementation of Newsqueak") and ultimately from Hoare's CSP. Pike's "Concurrency is not Parallelism" talk (Heroku Waza, January 2012; Pike 2012) is the canonical articulation of Go's design rationale: concurrency is *structure*, parallelism is *execution*; goroutines provide the structuring primitive, and channels provide the synchronisation. Go has no async-marker; every function is callable from every other function; blocking is the default, and the runtime multiplexes blocked goroutines onto OS threads. This is the precise architectural choice that the function-coloring diagnostic (§4.3) advocates.

A summary diagram of the lineage:

```
Baker-Hewitt 1977 (futures) ──┐
Liskov-Shrira 1988 (promise   │
  pipelining) ────────────────┤
Mark Miller's E (CapTP, §4.5) ┤
                              ▼
                     Q (Kowal 2009) ──► Promises/A+ (2012) ──► ES6 Promises (2015) ──► ES8 async/await (2017)
                              │
F# computation expressions ──►│ F# async workflows (2007)
                              ▼
                     C# 5.0 async/await (2012)
                              │
                              ├──► Python PEP 3156 asyncio (2012, 2014)
                              │     PEP 492 async/await (2015)
                              │
                              ├──► Rust Future trait (Turon 2016) ──► async/await stable (2019)
                              │
                              ├──► Swift SE-0296 async/await (2021) + SE-0304 structured concurrency
                              │
                              └──► Kotlin coroutines (2017–2018)
                                    + structured concurrency

Newsqueak (Cardelli-Pike 1985) ──► Alef ──► Limbo ──► Go (2009) goroutines + channels
                                                       (rejects function coloring)
```

### §4.3 The function-coloring diagnostic

Bob Nystrom's blog post "What Color is Your Function?" (Nystrom 2015) is the most cited single text on the structural failing of async/await as institutionalised in Era III. The argument is parable-form but rigorous. Nystrom posits a programming language with two function colours, *red* and *blue*, and five axioms describing how they interact:

1. Every function has a colour.
2. The way to call a function depends on its colour.
3. You can only call a red function from inside another red function.
4. Red functions are more painful to call than blue functions.
5. Some core library functions are red.

Nystrom's claim, made transparent by the framing, is that *async-marked* and *non-async-marked* functions are precisely such colours: every async function infects its callers with `await` and `async` markers; every non-async function is restricted from calling async functions without yielding control of the async context (typically by running an ad-hoc event loop, blocking, or using a callback-shaped escape). The fifth rule — that "some core library functions are red" — captures the asymmetry that is operationally most painful: an ecosystem with async I/O has all its non-trivial library functions in the red colour, which forces application code into red, which forces the application's own utility functions into red, recursively, until the entire program is red.

Nystrom's diagnosis of async/await as "merely making red functions not much worse to call than blue ones" is sharp. The key passage:

> "But you still have divided the world in two. Those async functions are easier to write, but they're still poisonous to call. They've taken the property of being asynchronous and made it not just a property of the function, but a property of every function that calls it. Recursively."

The post identifies — though it does not use the term — what we might call the *promiscuity of side-effect typing without monadic discipline*. Async/await re-creates Wadler–Moggi's monadic effect tracking (`async fn` returns a value-in-a-monad), but without the structural compositional advantages monads buy: there is no `mapM`, no monad transformer, no general "for any monad M, here is the pure function lifted into M." Each async ecosystem instead maintains a parallel, ad-hoc dialect of utility functions in the async-monadic style, duplicating work the synchronous standard library has already done. Combined with rule 3 — that red functions can only be called from red contexts — this means that the cost of a function being async is paid by every transitive caller in source code, every transitive caller's *test harness*, every transitive caller's *documentation*, and, in some languages, every transitive caller's *ABI*.

Glyph Lefkowitz's "Unyielding" (Lefkowitz 2014), written from inside the Twisted project — that is, from the position of someone who built and shipped a callback-based asynchronous framework for a decade — frames the same fact through the opposite axis. Lefkowitz argues *for* explicit asynchrony — coroutines, callbacks, futures with explicit composition operators — and *against* implicit asynchrony (thread-based concurrency where context switches occur at any time). His argument is cognitive: a human can hold roughly seven things in working memory; reasoning about correctness requires being able to consider a routine's behaviour with bounded mental state; thread-based interleaving violates that bound by exposing every preempt-able instruction to interference; explicit suspension points (yields, awaits, callback boundaries) restrict the interference set to a small, visible, named set of locations.

The Lefkowitz–Nystrom debate is therefore not a contradiction but a *triangulation*. They agree on the diagnosis: that the function colour boundary in async/await *exists*. They disagree on the prescription. Nystrom would erase the boundary by making concurrency uniform (Go goroutines, Lua coroutines, Smalltalk-style green threads). Lefkowitz would preserve the boundary as a feature, on the cognitive grounds that visible suspension points are necessary for local reasoning under concurrency.

For the project's synthesis, the function-coloring diagnostic is the diagnostic name for an *operational* failure, but the failure is *architectural*: it follows from picking a continuation-passing primitive (the future) without an algebraic structure (a lattice, a monad, a quantale) that would let synchronous and asynchronous computation share a uniform compositional discipline. A quantale-enriched substrate has *no syntactic colouring*. Every function carries an extensional quantale weight; the weight is uniform over sync (zero) and async (positive) computation; there is no `await` in source because there is no operational distinction at the type level. Era III's industrial async story can be read as a long sequence of attempts to reach for that uniformity from the wrong starting point — value-passing continuation primitives without compositional structure — and the function-coloring diagnostic is the report from the road.

The structured-concurrency response (Era IV; Trio 2018, Swift SE-0304 2021, Java Loom JEP 444 2023) is treated by Agent 7 in §5.1; the algebraic-effects response (Eff 2010, Multicore OCaml 2022) is treated by Agent 7 in §5.2. Both can be read as Era IV corrections to the Era III function-coloring problem — though as the synthesis (§8.2) will argue, neither is a full architectural answer; both retain a value-passing operational kernel rather than a constraint-passing one.

### §4.4 FRP and reactive programming

The *Functional Reactive Programming* (FRP) tradition originates in computer animation. Conal Elliott and Paul Hudak's "Functional Reactive Animation" (Elliott–Hudak 1997), the ICFP '97 paper that introduced *Fran* (Functional Reactive ANimation), defined two primitive abstractions:

- *Behaviours*: time-varying values, semantically `Time -> a`, intended to model continuous signals (positions, velocities, sounds).
- *Events*: discrete, time-stamped occurrences, semantically `[(Time, a)]` (a stream of timestamp-value pairs), intended to model user input, collisions, and discrete state transitions.

The Fran semantics is *denotational*: a `Behaviour Double` is, mathematically, a function from real-valued time to a `Double`. A graphics program is a single `Behaviour Picture`; the runtime samples the behaviour at frame rate. Composition is by lifting (`liftA2 (+) speed accel = velocity`) and by event-driven switching (`b1 `until` (e ==> b2)`).

Fran's denotational clarity was its theoretical achievement and its operational liability. The straightforward implementation has a quadratic re-evaluation pattern (every behaviour re-derived at every frame); space leaks were endemic, particularly when behaviours depended on past events; and the original Hugs implementation, although usable, did not scale.

Two programmes of work tried to recover. *Yampa* (Courtney–Nilsson–Peterson 2003 "The Yampa Arcade"; Hudak–Courtney–Nilsson–Peterson 2002 "Arrows, Robots, and Functional Reactive Programming") substituted *signal functions* for behaviours: a `SF a b` is a function from a signal-of-`a` to a signal-of-`b`. Composition is *arrow*-style, not monadic, which restricts the expressible patterns enough to make leak-free implementation feasible. Yampa was used for game simulation (Frag, the Yampa arcade) and robotics. *Reactive Banana*, *Sodium*, and *Reactive* (the latter Elliott's own subsequent work, Elliott 2009 "Push-Pull Functional Reactive Programming") explored push-pull hybrids, where event-driven updates push and continuous computations pull.

Elliott's "Push-Pull FRP" (Haskell '09) deserves separate citation. The paper observes that Fran-style FRP unifies pull-based sampling (continuous behaviours, demand-driven re-evaluation) with push-based notification (events, supply-driven activation), and proposes an implementation that combines both: behaviours that depend on events change *only* when those events fire (push); behaviours that depend on time alone are sampled on demand (pull). The semantics is preserved; the operational efficiency is recovered.

The *commercial* reactive-programming wave that bears the FRP name does *not* share Fran's denotational semantics. Erik Meijer's *Reactive Extensions* (Rx) for .NET (Meijer 2009, "Inside the .NET Reactive Framework"; Microsoft Open Technologies 2010 open-sourcing) introduced the dual `IObservable<T>` / `IObserver<T>` interface pair and a LINQ-shaped operator algebra (`.Select`, `.Where`, `.Merge`, `.Throttle`). Meijer's mathematical framing was elegant: `IObservable` is the categorical *dual* of `IEnumerable`; pull-based iteration and push-based observation are dual in the sense of having dual operational signatures (a consumer pulls values from an enumerable; an observable pushes values to a consumer). The duality argument, and the LINQ-style query operators it supports, gave Rx a compositional discipline that the callback-spaghetti of GUI event handlers had previously lacked.

Rx then proliferated. Netflix's *RxJava* (Christensen 2013) ported the design to the JVM and used it as the structuring primitive for the Netflix API service layer; Microsoft and Netflix collaborators ported to JavaScript (RxJS), Scala (RxScala), Swift (RxSwift), and others under the *ReactiveX* umbrella. Apple's *Combine* framework (WWDC 2019) is structurally Rx by another name (publishers, subscribers, operators); it is integrated with SwiftUI's `@Published` property wrapper and `@ObservedObject` reactive view binding, making Combine the substrate of Apple's declarative UI runtime.

It is important to distinguish *FRP-as-Elliott-defined* from *reactive-programming-as-commercially-deployed*. Rx, RxJava, RxJS, Combine: none of these has continuous-time behaviours; none has a denotational semantics in the Fran sense; their `IObservable` is operationally a callback-multicasting interface with backpressure protocols, error propagation, and termination signals layered on top. They are, in effect, *event streams* — closer to Hoare's CSP channels than to Elliott's behaviours. The reactive-programming community generally accepts this terminological slippage as a matter of historical accident; there have been periodic attempts (notably Heinrich Apfelmus's "Reactive Banana" and the *RxMarbles* visual-semantics community) to recover denotational rigour. For the project's synthesis, the relevant fact is that Era III's reactive-programming layer institutionalised an *operational push-stream* abstraction that is closer to the propagator network's notion of "a cell that updates" than the Fran denotational model is — but without the lattice merge that would make reactive composition CALM-monotone. A `Subject` in Rx merges by *most-recent-write* semantics, which is not monotone in any non-trivial sense; backpressure is handled by buffering, dropping, or sampling, none of which recovers algebraic structure.

### §4.6 Differential and timely dataflow

The closest practical lineage to the project's substrate hypothesis is Microsoft Research's *Naiad* and the *timely dataflow / differential dataflow* line that descends from it.

**Naiad** (Murray–McSherry–Isaacs–Isard–Barham–Abadi 2013, SOSP '13, best-paper award) introduced *timely dataflow*: a distributed-execution model for cyclic dataflow computations annotated with *partial-order timestamps*. A timely-dataflow computation is a directed graph of operators connected by message-passing edges. Each message carries a timestamp from a partially ordered set; operators emit, consume, and forward messages; the runtime computes *frontiers* — for each operator, the set of timestamps that no future incoming message could fall below — and uses frontier completion as the trigger for downstream computation.

The timestamp model is the key technical contribution. A flat (totally ordered) timestamp is the trivial case (streaming with simple windowing). A pair `(epoch, iteration-count)` is the case for iterative computations: outer epochs flow forward as new input arrives; inner iteration-counts increment within a feedback loop until convergence. Higher-dimensional timestamps support nested iteration. Timestamp comparison is the partial order on these tuples; the *frontier* of an operator is a set of incomparable timestamps such that all already-issued messages are below the frontier.

Two operational properties make timely dataflow load-bearing for the present synthesis:

(1) *Monotone semantics within a timestamp*. Within a single timestamp, the computation produces a monotonically growing answer set. This is the streaming analogue of CALM: progress within a timestamp requires no coordination beyond the local operator's state.

(2) *Coordination only at frontier advancement*. Frontiers advance when all upstream operators have certified that no further messages will arrive at timestamps below the frontier. This is a coordination event — an all-reduce — but it occurs at *epoch boundaries*, not at every message. The cost of coordination is amortised across a batch.

**Differential dataflow** (McSherry–Murray–Isaacs–Isard 2013, CIDR '13) builds on top of timely dataflow. The contribution is a method for incremental maintenance of complex queries (joins, group-bys, fixpoint iterations) under streaming input changes. A differential computation tracks not absolute values but *changes* — keyed by (key, time) — so that derived results can be recomputed by composing changes rather than re-running the full query. The 2013 paper demonstrates real-time strongly-connected-component maintenance on the Twitter graph at sub-second latency.

The line continues. *Materialize* (founded 2019; v0.1 beta February 2020; McSherry, Murray, et al.) productionised differential dataflow as a streaming SQL database. A `CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW` statement is compiled to a differential dataflow plan; the view is incrementally maintained as the upstream inputs change; queries against the view return the current materialised state at sub-millisecond latency. Materialize represents the most architecturally ambitious commercial deployment of the timely/differential line.

For the project's synthesis, timely dataflow is *the closest existing practical system to a propagator network*. The mapping is direct:

| Propagator network concept | Timely dataflow concept |
|---|---|
| Cell | Per-operator state (key-value store) |
| Propagator | Operator |
| Lattice merge | Differential collection update |
| BSP round | Frontier advancement |
| Stratification | Iteration depth (nested timestamp) |
| CALM monotonicity | Monotone-within-timestamp progress |

The differences are also load-bearing. Timely dataflow's *monotonicity* is structural (the message timestamps, not the values, define progress); a propagator network's monotonicity is *over the value lattice*. Timely dataflow handles *non-monotone* operators (like aggregation) by introducing additional timestamp dimensions; a propagator network handles them by *stratification*. The two systems have similar operational signatures but different algebraic disciplines.

In the multi-network runtime of the project (the L2 layer, in the framing of the synthesis), timely dataflow's frontier-advancement protocol is a candidate substrate for cross-network coordination — particularly the CALM-monotone fragment. The connection is not novel: Hellerstein et al.'s *Bloom* language (Berkeley) and the broader CALM theorem corpus already articulate this connection; what timely dataflow adds is a *production* implementation with order-of-magnitude latency competitive with custom streaming systems.

The notion that the timely-dataflow timestamp lattice *is itself* a distributed coordination primitive is, when stated precisely, a non-trivial connection to the CCP-quantale hypothesis covered in §3.3. A partially ordered set with a join-semilattice structure (timestamps under componentwise comparison) on which each operator's progress is monotone is precisely a *distributed lattice computation*; the operator's local state is a CCP cell; the messages are *tells*; the frontier is a coordination join. What timely dataflow does *not* yet have is a quantale-enriched cost model: timestamps order progress, but they do not carry quantitative weight (latency, resource consumption, priority). A quantale-enriched timely dataflow is a research direction the project's synthesis (§8.4) is well-placed to articulate.
