## References

### §4.1 The Erlang/Akka/Orleans actor lineage

**Armstrong, Joe.** "Making reliable distributed systems in the presence of software errors." PhD thesis, Royal Institute of Technology, Stockholm, December 2003. *<https://erlang.org/download/armstrong_thesis_2003.pdf>*
*Annotation:* The canonical statement of Concurrency-Oriented Programming, "let it crash", and supervision-tree-as-recovery-unit. 295 pages, six chapters; the methodology chapters (4–6) are the load-bearing portion for present purposes. Establishes that defensive programming is unsound: a process that has lost knowledge of its own state cannot repair it, so the only safe action is termination plus supervised restart. Introduces the OTP behaviour patterns (`gen_server`, etc.) as parameterised templates over user code.

**Armstrong, Joe.** "A History of Erlang." *Proceedings of the third ACM SIGPLAN conference on History of Programming Languages (HOPL III)*, San Diego, CA, 9–10 June 2007, pp. 6-1 – 6-26. DOI: 10.1145/1238844.1238850. *<https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/1238844.1238850>*
*Annotation:* The HOPL III retrospective. Describes the 1986–1989 period at Ericsson Computer Science Laboratory; the early Prolog-dialect prototype; the design choices (no shared state, no locks, mailboxes are unbounded queues) that distinguished Erlang from contemporary concurrent languages. Particularly clear on the rejection of CSP-style synchronous channels: "We considered [synchronous send] but rejected it on grounds that telephone exchanges must keep running even when the recipient is broken."

**Bonér, Jonas.** "Introducing Akka — Simpler Scalability, Fault-Tolerance, Concurrency & Remoting through Actors." Personal blog, 2010. *<https://jonasboner.com/introducing-akka/>*
*Annotation:* The launch announcement for Akka. Explicitly names porting Erlang/OTP's "availability of six nines" to the JVM as the motivation; identifies the actor model, software transactional memory, and remoting as the three pillars. Akka 0.5 was released in January 2010.

**Bonér, Jonas, et al.** Akka Typed series: "Akka Typed: Hello World in the new API" (May 2017); "Akka Typed: Protocols" (May 2017); "Akka Typed: Supervision" (May 2017); "Typed Supervision: why the changes?" (February 2019). *<https://akka.io/blog/>*
*Annotation:* The blog series introducing the Akka Typed re-architecture. The supervision post is structurally significant: it shows that supervision-as-behaviour-wrapper makes restart-with-backoff a compositional feature rather than a runtime registration. Closer to Pi-calculus session-typed actors than to original Erlang.

**Bernstein, Phil, Sergey Bykov, Alan Geller, Gabriel Kliot, and Jorgen Thelin.** "Orleans: Distributed Virtual Actors for Programmability and Scalability." Microsoft Research Technical Report MSR-TR-2014-41, March 2014. *<https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/publication/orleans-distributed-virtual-actors-for-programmability-and-scalability/>*
*Annotation:* The virtual-actor (or "grain") abstraction. Actors have perpetual logical identities; the runtime instantiates and persists them on demand; placement, failure-driven re-creation, and lifecycle management are runtime concerns rather than user concerns. Production usage at Halo 4 telemetry, Skype back-end, Azure services. Note: this is a technical report; the 2011 SoCC paper by the same group introduced the broader Orleans framework but predates the virtual-actor formulation.

### §4.2 Promises, futures, async/await evolution

**Petricek, Tomas, and Don Syme.** "F# Asynchronous Workflows." *Proceedings of the 13th International Symposium on Practical Aspects of Declarative Languages (PADL 2011)*, Lecture Notes in Computer Science 6539, Springer, 2011. *<https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-642-18378-2_15>*
*Annotation:* Confirms F# async workflows as a computation-expression instance with continuation-passing semantics. Often cited as PADL 2010 (the year of the conference; proceedings are formally 2011 LNCS). Predated C# `async`/`await` by approximately five years and was a direct conceptual influence.

**Petricek, Tomas, and Don Syme.** "The F# Computation Expression Zoo." *Proceedings of the 16th International Symposium on Practical Aspects of Declarative Languages (PADL 2014)*. *<https://tomasp.net/academic/papers/computation-zoo/>*
*Annotation:* The general account of F# computation expressions — async workflows are one instance among queries, sequences, and joinads. Provides the unified explanation of how `let!`, `do!`, and `return` are desugared into builder method calls. Useful for understanding why F# async preceded mainstream async/await.

**Petricek, Tomas, and Don Syme.** "Syntax Matters: Writing abstract computations in F#." Pre-proceedings of TFP 2012. *<https://tomasp.net/academic/papers/computation-zoo/syntax-matters.pdf>*
*Annotation:* Argues that the choice of surface syntax for computational abstractions is itself architectural, not cosmetic. Demonstrates the same computation expressed in monadic do-notation, applicative style, and arrowised style, showing how the choice changes which compositional patterns become natural. Useful background for the function-coloring diagnostic (§4.3): the choice to mark async functions syntactically is a syntax-matters choice with structural consequences.

**Bierman, Gavin M., Claudio V. Russo, Geoffrey Mainland, Erik Meijer, and Mads Torgersen.** "Pause 'n' Play: Formalizing Asynchronous C#." *European Conference on Object-Oriented Programming (ECOOP 2012)*. *<https://gavinbierman.github.io/assets/pdf/ecoop2012.pdf>*
*Annotation:* The formalisation of the C# async/await rewrite. Provides a small-step semantics for the source-to-state-machine transformation; proves correctness of the rewrite. Template for subsequent formalisations of similar language features.

**Torgersen, Mads.** "Asynchronous Programming — Pause and Play with Await." *MSDN Magazine*, October 2011. (See also Torgersen's MSDN authored archive: *<https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/archive/msdn-magazine/authors/mads_torgersen>*)
*Annotation:* The C# language designer's plain-language exposition for the developer audience. Frames the design around the existing pain points: APM (`Begin`/`End`), EAP (`*Async`/`*Completed`), and explicit `Task.ContinueWith` continuations all produced asynchronous code that did not compose with structured exception handling.

**Promises/A+ Specification.** Community standard, primary editing 2012–2013, ongoing community maintenance. *<https://promisesaplus.com/>*
*Annotation:* The specification that fixed `then`-method semantics for JavaScript promises, including the resolution procedure handling promise-of-promise flattening and the requirement that `then` callbacks fire on a fresh stack. Authored "by implementers, for implementers"; CC0 public domain. Absorbed into ES6 (June 2015).

**Kowal, Kris.** "q: A promise library for JavaScript." GitHub repository, 2009–. *<https://github.com/kriskowal/q>*
*Annotation:* The Q library, which Kowal cites as drawing design from Mark Miller's E (cross-ref §4.5) and Tyler Close's Waterken Q. Material progenitor of the Promises/A+ specification's `then`-method semantics.

**Saccone, Sam.** "The history of Promises." Personal blog. *<https://samsaccone.com/posts/history-of-promises.html>*
*Annotation:* A useful capsule history of the JavaScript promise tradition, including dojo.Deferred (2009 influence on Kris Zyp's CommonJS Promises/A spec), through Q, through Promises/A+, through ES6.

**van Rossum, Guido.** "PEP 3156 — Asynchronous IO Support Rebooted: the 'asyncio' Module." Python Enhancement Proposal, 12 December 2012. *<https://peps.python.org/pep-3156/>*
*Annotation:* Established the standard event-loop API in Python, framed as an interoperability layer rather than a competitor to Twisted/Tornado. The `Future` type is intentionally compatible with `concurrent.futures.Future` from PEP 3148. Reference implementation under code name *Tulip*; landed in Python 3.4 in March 2014.

**Selivanov, Yury.** "PEP 492 — Coroutines with async and await syntax." Python Enhancement Proposal, 9 April 2015. Accepted 5 May 2015 by Guido van Rossum. *<https://peps.python.org/pep-0492/>*
*Annotation:* Introduced native coroutines as a distinct type, parsed via `async def` and `await` keywords, rather than retrofit onto generators (the `yield from` mechanism that asyncio originally used). Native coroutines became their own type. By Python 3.7 (June 2018) `async`/`await` became reserved keywords.

**Turon, Aaron.** "Zero-cost futures in Rust." Blog post, 11 August 2016. *<http://aturon.github.io/blog/2016/08/11/futures/>*
*Annotation:* The introduction of the poll-based Rust futures design. Argues that a `Future` is simply a state machine — a value that can be polled, returning either `NotReady` (with a wakeup mechanism) or a final value — and that this enables stack-allocatable, zero-cost asynchronous abstractions. The split between *future* and *executor* is established here: futures do not drive themselves; an executor (Tokio etc.) drives them.

**Turon, Aaron.** "Designing futures for Rust." Blog post, 7 September 2016. *<http://aturon.github.io/blog/2016/09/07/futures-design/>*
*Annotation:* The follow-up that explains the design rationale: why poll-based and not callback-based; why the wakeup mechanism uses a `Task` handle. Useful for understanding the architecture that Rust's `async`/`await` syntax (2019) hides behind direct-style code.

**Matsakis, Niko (with the Rust Async Working Group).** "Async-await on stable Rust!" Rust Blog, 7 November 2019. *<https://blog.rust-lang.org/2019/11/07/Async-await-stable/>*
*Annotation:* The stabilisation announcement for Rust 1.39's `async`/`await`. Notes that this is an MVP — many ergonomic and compositional features (notably `async fn` in traits) remained unstable and were the subject of subsequent work over 2020–2024.

**Lattner, Chris, et al. (Apple).** "SE-0296: async/await." Swift Evolution proposal. *<https://github.com/swiftlang/swift-evolution/blob/main/proposals/0296-async-await.md>*
*Annotation:* Adopts the C# precedent. Pairs with SE-0304 (structured concurrency, treated in §5.1 by Agent 7) at the same release. First exposed at WWDC 2021; shipped in Swift 5.5.

**Elizarov, Roman.** "Introduction to Coroutines." KotlinConf 2017 keynote. Slide deck: *<https://resources.jetbrains.com/storage/products/kotlinconf2017/slides/2017+KotlinConf+-+Introduction+to+Coroutines.pdf>*
*Annotation:* The canonical introduction to Kotlin coroutines. Explains the `suspend` modifier, the lack of an `await` keyword, and the underlying single-shot delimited continuations. Pairs with Elizarov's "Deep dive into Coroutines on JVM" (same KotlinConf) for the implementation-level treatment.

**Pike, Rob.** "Concurrency is not Parallelism." Heroku Waza Conference talk, 11 January 2012. *<https://go.dev/blog/waza-talk>* and slide deck *<https://go.dev/talks/2012/waza.slide>*.
*Annotation:* The canonical articulation of Go's design philosophy. "Concurrency is the composition of independently executing processes; parallelism is the simultaneous execution of (possibly related) computations." Foundational for understanding why Go rejects the function-coloring distinction and treats every function as callable from every other function with implicit goroutine multiplexing.

**Pike, Rob.** "The Implementation of Newsqueak." AT&T Bell Laboratories Computing Science Technical Report No. 143, 1989. *<https://swtch.com/~rsc/thread/newsquimpl.pdf>*
*Annotation:* The implementation paper for Newsqueak, the Cardelli–Pike (1985) language whose channels-and-goroutines descended through Alef and Limbo to Go. Newsqueak's channels are first-class objects with dynamic creation, drawing operationally from Hoare's CSP.

### §4.3 The function-coloring diagnostic

**Nystrom, Bob.** "What Color is Your Function?" Personal blog, 1 February 2015. *<http://journal.stuffwithstuff.com/2015/02/01/what-color-is-your-function/>*
*Annotation:* The canonical statement of the function-coloring problem. Five axioms describe how async-marked and sync-marked functions interact (every function has a colour; calling syntax depends on colour; red-only-from-red; red is more painful; core libraries are red). Diagnoses async/await as "merely making red functions not much worse to call than blue ones" — a syntactic concession to direct-style code, not a structural solution to the colour boundary. Advocates languages with uniform concurrency (Go, Lua, Smalltalk-like green threads) as the architectural alternative.

**Lefkowitz, Glyph.** "Unyielding." Personal blog (Twisted/Deciphering Glyph), 17 February 2014. *<https://blog.glyph.im/2014/02/unyielding.html>*
*Annotation:* Written from inside the Twisted project. Argues for *explicit* asynchrony (callbacks, futures, coroutines) and against *implicit* asynchrony (thread-based concurrency) on cognitive-load grounds. Cites Miller's "seven plus or minus two" working-memory bound: thread interleaving exposes every preempt-able instruction to interference; explicit suspension points restrict the interference set. The Lefkowitz–Nystrom debate triangulates the same diagnosis from opposite prescriptive positions.

### §4.4 FRP and reactive programming

**Elliott, Conal, and Paul Hudak.** "Functional Reactive Animation." *Proceedings of the 1997 ACM SIGPLAN International Conference on Functional Programming (ICFP '97)*, pp. 263–273. DOI: 10.1145/258948.258973. *<http://conal.net/papers/icfp97/>*
*Annotation:* The origin paper of FRP. Introduces *behaviours* (semantically `Time -> a`, time-varying continuous values) and *events* (timestamp-value streams). Provides a denotational semantics with proper treatment of real time. Awarded the most influential paper of ICFP '97 (in 2007). The denotational clarity comes at operational cost (space leaks, quadratic re-evaluation), motivating the Yampa and Reactive successor lines.

**Elliott, Conal.** "Push-Pull Functional Reactive Programming." *Proceedings of the 2nd ACM SIGPLAN Symposium on Haskell (Haskell '09)*, Edinburgh, Scotland, 3 September 2009, pp. 25–36. DOI: 10.1145/1596638.1596643. *<http://conal.net/papers/push-pull-frp/push-pull-frp.pdf>*
*Annotation:* Hybrid push-pull implementation. Behaviours that depend on events change only when those events fire (push); behaviours that depend on time alone are sampled on demand (pull). Preserves Fran-style semantics while recovering operational efficiency.

**Courtney, Antony, Henrik Nilsson, and John Peterson.** "The Yampa Arcade." *Proceedings of the 2003 ACM SIGPLAN Workshop on Haskell*, pp. 7–18. DOI: 10.1145/871895.871897. *<https://dl.acm.org/doi/abs/10.1145/871895.871897>*
*Annotation:* The Yampa arrowised-FRP system applied to game programming. Introduces signal functions (`SF a b`) as the primitive abstraction; arrow-style composition restricts expressible patterns enough to make leak-free implementation feasible.

**Hudak, Paul, Antony Courtney, Henrik Nilsson, and John Peterson.** "Arrows, Robots, and Functional Reactive Programming." *Lecture Notes from the 4th Summer School on Advanced Functional Programming*, Oxford 2002. *<https://www.cs.yale.edu/homes/hudak/CS429F04/AFPLectureNotes.pdf>*
*Annotation:* Tutorial-style introduction to arrowised FRP using robotics applications. Useful pedagogical companion to the Yampa Arcade paper.

**Meijer, Erik.** Reactive Extensions (Rx) for .NET, Microsoft 2009 onwards. Open-sourcing announcement: *<https://www.hanselman.com/blog/reactive-extensions-rx-is-now-open-source>*. InfoQ overview (2009): *<https://www.infoq.com/news/2009/07/Reactive-Framework-LINQ-Events/>*
*Annotation:* The dual `IObservable<T>` / `IObserver<T>` interface pair. `IObservable` is the categorical dual of `IEnumerable`; pull-based iteration and push-based observation are dual. LINQ-style query operators (`.Select`, `.Where`, `.Merge`, `.Throttle`) provide compositional discipline to event handling. Note: this is *not* FRP-as-Elliott-defined — `IObservable` is operationally a callback-multicasting interface, not a continuous-time behaviour.

**Christensen, Ben.** "Reactive Programming in the Netflix API with RxJava." Netflix Technology Blog, 4 February 2013. *<https://netflixtechblog.com/reactive-programming-in-the-netflix-api-with-rxjava-7811c3a1496a>*
*Annotation:* Netflix's adoption of RxJava as the structuring primitive for the entire API service layer. Every service method returns `Observable<T>`. Initial language support for Groovy, Clojure, Scala, JRuby beyond Java itself. Marks the moment Rx ceased being a Microsoft .NET framework and became a cross-platform standard (the broader ReactiveX umbrella).

**Apple.** *Combine Framework*. Introduced WWDC 2019. Documentation: *<https://developer.apple.com/documentation/combine>*. Overview and examples: *<https://www.avanderlee.com/swift/combine/>*.
*Annotation:* Apple's first-party reactive programming framework. Publishers, subscribers, operators — structurally Rx by another name. Integrated with SwiftUI's `@Published` and `@ObservedObject` property wrappers; effectively the substrate of Apple's declarative UI runtime. Memory management via `AnyCancellable` (analogous to RxSwift's `DisposeBag`).

### §4.6 Differential and timely dataflow

**Murray, Derek G., Frank McSherry, Rebecca Isaacs, Michael Isard, Paul Barham, and Martín Abadi.** "Naiad: A Timely Dataflow System." *Proceedings of the 24th ACM Symposium on Operating Systems Principles (SOSP '13)*, Farmington, PA, November 2013, pp. 439–455. DOI: 10.1145/2517349.2522738. Best-paper award. *<https://sigops.org/s/conferences/sosp/2013/papers/p439-murray.pdf>*
*Annotation:* Introduces timely dataflow: distributed cyclic dataflow with partial-order timestamps. Frontiers (sets of incomparable timestamps below which all messages have been issued) provide lightweight coordination. Demonstrates batch throughput, stream latency, and iterative computation in a unified model. The closest practical predecessor to a propagator network.

**McSherry, Frank, Derek G. Murray, Rebecca Isaacs, and Michael Isard.** "Differential Dataflow." *Proceedings of CIDR 2013*, January 2013. *<https://www.cidrdb.org/cidr2013/Papers/CIDR13_Paper111.pdf>*
*Annotation:* Incremental computation atop timely dataflow. Tracks changes keyed by (key, time); arbitrarily nested iterations are supported. Real-time strongly-connected-component maintenance on the Twitter graph at sub-second latency.

**Abadi, Martín, Frank McSherry, Derek G. Murray, and Rebecca Isaacs.** "Foundations of Differential Dataflow." *Lecture Notes in Computer Science 9034*, Springer 2015. *<https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-662-46678-0_5>*
*Annotation:* The mathematical formalisation. Differential dataflow's update calculus is shown to compose; correctness of incremental view maintenance is proved relative to the underlying total-state computation.

**McSherry, Frank, et al. / Materialize Inc.** *Materialize Streaming Database*. Founded 2019; v0.1 beta February 2020. Carnegie Mellon DB Group seminar (Spring 2020): *<https://db.cs.cmu.edu/events/db-seminar-spring-2020-db-group-building-materialize-a-streaming-sql-database-powered-by-timely-dataflow/>*. Roadmap blog: *<https://materialize.com/blog-roadmap>*
*Annotation:* The commercial productionisation of timely + differential dataflow. SQL `CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW` compiles to a differential dataflow plan; views are incrementally maintained as inputs change; queries return current materialised state at sub-millisecond latency. The most architecturally ambitious commercial deployment of the timely/differential line.

### Cross-references

- **§3.3 (Agent 3)**: CCP and the quantale connection. The actor model (§4.1) is best understood as a degenerate form of CCP without lattice merge — actors communicate, but mailboxes are unbounded queues, not constraint stores. Worth flagging in the synthesis.
- **§4.5 (Agent 6)**: E and CapTP. The promise-pipelining contribution that descends from Mark Miller's group is attributed to §4.5; references in §4.2 above to "Q drew design from Mark Miller's E" defer to that section for full attribution.
- **§5.1 (Agent 7)**: Structured concurrency. Trio (2018), Kotlin coroutines' `CoroutineScope` (2017–2018), Swift SE-0304 (2021), Java JEP 444 / Loom (2023) form an Era IV correction to the function-coloring problem named in §4.3.
- **§5.2 (Agent 7)**: Algebraic effects. Eff, Multicore OCaml — another Era IV response to function colouring, pursuing the architectural rather than ergonomic correction.
- **§8.2 (Synthesis)**: The function-coloring diagnostic is load-bearing for the synthesis's claim that quantale-enriched cells dissolve the colour boundary structurally rather than syntactically.
- **§8.4 (Synthesis)**: Timely dataflow is the closest existing system to a propagator network. The mapping (cell ≈ operator state, propagator ≈ operator, lattice merge ≈ differential update, BSP round ≈ frontier advancement) is direct; the missing piece is quantale-enriched cost. This is a research direction worth foregrounding.

---

## §4.5 (Era III) and §6.2 (Era V) — Mark Miller, E, CapTP, and the Capability-Async Lineage

This slice traces a single intellectual line that begins in capability-based operating systems of the early 1980s, passes through Project Xanadu and a sequence of small experimental languages, crystallises in the E programming language and the CapTP wire protocol around the turn of the millennium, and is currently being revived — in materially the same architectural form — by the Spritely Institute under the OCapN umbrella. The line is unusual among Era III asynchrony research in that it is simultaneously (a) a *programming-language* tradition, (b) a *security* tradition, and (c) a *distributed-systems* tradition, and treats those three concerns as a single problem with a single answer. The answer — *vat-structured single-threaded event loops, asynchronous eventual sends returning promises, promise pipelining for round-trip elimination, and capability discipline as the unifying account of both authority and synchronisation* — is the most thoroughly engineered practical antecedent extant for a multi-network distributed propagator runtime. For the synthesis chapter's L2 architecture sketch, this slice is the design-precedent ballast: every load-bearing structural decision the synthesis will reach for has been deployed, debugged, productionised, and re-deployed in this lineage already, and so the rest of the architectural argument can stand on what is essentially settled engineering.

### §4.5 — Origins and Foundations (1985–2015)

#### §4.5.1 — Pre-history: KeyKOS, Xanadu, Joule, and the Lineage to E

The capability-async lineage does not begin with a programming language; it begins with an operating system. In October 1985 Norman Hardy, working at Tymshare (later Key Logic), published "The KeyKOS Architecture" in *ACM SIGOPS Operating Systems Review* [Hardy 1985]. The paper documented a system that had been in production on IBM 4341 mainframes since January 1983, supporting commercial timesharing customers under the requirements of *security, data sharing, pricing, reliability, and extensibility* — almost precisely the requirements that would, fifteen years later, be ascribed to "the Internet." KeyKOS organised computation around two primitives: *processes* with private memory, communicating exclusively by sending messages along *keys* — unforgeable, transferable references that named the recipient *and* encoded the right to send to it. A process holding no key to a resource had no path to that resource; a process could only delegate authority it had itself been granted; and the entire system state — all running processes, all memory, all keys — was checkpointed periodically, so that a crash and a power loss were operationally equivalent and recovery was simply a matter of resuming from the last checkpoint. KeyKOS thus made *capabilities* (in the Dennis–Van Horn sense) and *transparent persistence* into a single composite primitive: a key was *both* the right to invoke and the durable name. Bomberger, Frantz, Hardy, Hardy, Landau and Shapiro [1992] later refined the architecture as a nanokernel design, and the system's intellectual successor — Shapiro's EROS [1999] and CapROS — kept the capability primitive while modernising the implementation. KeyKOS established the load-bearing engineering claim of the lineage: that *capability discipline scales to a complete, persistent, multi-tenant, commercial system without compromise on either security or performance*. Every subsequent layer in this lineage assumes that fact and builds linguistic abstractions on top of it.

In parallel, an independent thread at Project Xanadu — the hypertext system founded by Ted Nelson and developed during the late 1970s and 1980s — was producing the asynchronous-message-passing primitives that would later cohere with the capability primitive. Working on Xanadu's transclusion-and-versioning back-end, Mark S. Miller, Dean Tribble, Eric Dean Tribble, and Rob Jellinghaus invented around 1988–1989 the *promise pipelining* technique now embedded in every CapTP-compatible system [E (programming language) 2024; Wikipedia "Promise pipelining" 2024; Lemmer-Webber, Farmer, and Sims 2025, §3.2.6, footnote 15]. The motivating problem was Internet latency: a Xanadu document might transclude content from many distinct servers; the cost of resolving a transclusion graph is N round-trips if each must complete before the next begins, and this cost grew unacceptable as Xanadu's vision moved from one machine to a global network. Pipelining allowed the client to send the next message *to the result of the previous message*, even though the previous message had not yet returned, so long as both messages would ultimately be processed by the same server: the server, on receiving the first message, retains the result and uses it as the receiver of the second. Independently, Barbara Liskov and Liuba Shrira, working in the Argus distributed-programming project at MIT, formalised the same insight under the name *call-streams* in their 1988 PLDI paper "Promises: Linguistic Support for Efficient Asynchronous Procedure Calls in Distributed Systems" [Liskov and Shrira 1988]; Liskov coined the modern term *promise* in that paper, though her promises did not initially permit further pipelined operations on an unresolved promise itself. The Miller-Tribble-Jellinghaus formulation, by contrast, treated a promise as a *first-class object that could itself receive messages*, and message forwarding by the resolver was the central mechanism. This stronger form is what survived into E, into CapTP, and now into OCapN.

Mark Miller, Dean Tribble, Norm Hardy, Chip Morningstar, and others co-founded Electric Communities in 1994. Within Electric Communities, two languages emerged: *Joule*, an experimental concurrent-constraint-flavoured language whose final report — "Joule: Distributed Application Foundations" by E. Dean Tribble, Mark S. Miller, Norm Hardy, and David Krieger (Agorics technical report, 1995) — laid down the vat-structured event-loop concurrency model and the eventual-send-with-promise primitive that would survive into E [Tribble, Miller, Hardy, and Krieger 1995]; and *Original-E*, an extension of Java with capability discipline and eventual sends. Joule was never commercially deployed but its semantic apparatus, in particular its account of *channels* and *promises* under a closure-of-references discipline, is the immediate ancestor of E's vat. Lemmer-Webber, Farmer and Sims [2025, footnote 15] note explicitly that "the designs of promises and promise pipelining are derived from those of Joule, and have been influential on the appearance of promises in languages such as JavaScript." Miller's own thesis [Miller 2006, §16.2] traces the line directly from Joule's promises through E's vows to the JavaScript Promise, and laments — accurately — that the JavaScript Promise lost the pipelining property in transit.

Meanwhile Miller and K. Eric Drexler had, in 1988, published "Markets and Computation: Agoric Open Systems" in *The Ecology of Computation* (Huberman, ed., North-Holland) [Miller and Drexler 1988]. The paper argued that resource allocation in a sufficiently large, sufficiently heterogeneous computational system would have to be priced — that is, that the same market mechanisms that integrate dispersed local knowledge in human economies also offered a tractable account of distributed computation. The agoric line is sometimes treated as a separate intellectual strand from the capability line, but they are the same strand: a market requires *property*, property requires *unforgeable title*, and unforgeable title is, technologically, a capability. Miller has never separated the two; the founding of Agoric Inc. in 2018 (and the Endo / Hardened-JavaScript / Jessie effort that followed) treats capability discipline as a precondition for safe smart-contracts-as-software-objects, with E's vat-and-eventual-send semantics simulated on top of Hardened JavaScript and orchestrated under SwingSet [Miller 2006, §16; Tribble forthcoming via Agoric publications]. The agoric framing is most relevant to L2 design as a reminder that *cooperation under mutual suspicion* — strangers on a network whose code one did not write and may not trust — is the load-bearing design constraint of the lineage; it is not a security afterthought layered over a permissive runtime, but the substrate.

#### §4.5.2 — The E Programming Language (1997–2005)

E was developed at Electric Communities during 1997 by Mark S. Miller, Dan Bornstein, Douglas Crockford, Chip Morningstar, and others, and at Combex thereafter [E (programming language) 2024]. Its surface syntax is Java-like but its semantics are unusual: the language consists entirely of expressions; assignment is `:=` and equality is `==`, with the bare `=` deliberately absent so that a misread token cannot silently turn a comparison into a destructive assignment; types are dynamic but optional *guards* (e.g. `:int`, `:(int >= 0)`) attach behavioural constraints; and lexical scoping is the *only* mechanism for capability transfer — there is no global mutable namespace. The W7 secure-Scheme dialect [Rees 1996] codifies the same minimum-language requirements: no ambient authority, no global mutable state, lexical scoping with reference-passing, and library imports that grant no authority by default. Spritely's introductory paper [Lemmer-Webber, Farmer and Sims 2025, §2 footnote 2] cites W7 explicitly as the precondition for object-capability safety in any host language.

The architectural unit of E is the *vat*. A vat is a single-threaded event loop owning a private object heap and a message queue; objects whose references reside in the same vat are termed *near* and may invoke each other synchronously via the immediate-call operator (in E, `.`; in Goblins, `$`); objects whose references span vats are termed *far* and may be invoked only via the *eventual-send* operator `<-`, which delivers a message asynchronously and immediately returns a *vow* (E's term; *promise* is the generic name) — a placeholder that will be resolved (or broken) when the message has been processed [Lemmer-Webber, Farmer and Sims 2025, §3.5; ERights "The Vat" via E (programming language) 2024]. Within a vat, the *current turn* — the processing of one message from the queue — runs to completion without interruption; only after the turn completes does the vat dequeue the next message. Within a turn there are no data races because there is no concurrency; across turns there is no shared mutable state because vats do not share heaps. The vat thus partitions the global concurrency space into a coarse grain (between vats, fully asynchronous, fully race-bearing in the sense that messages may interleave) and a fine grain (within a vat, sequential, race-free by construction). Miller, Tribble and Shapiro [2005] argue in "Concurrency Among Strangers: Programming in E as Plan Coordination" that this two-level structure is essentially the *only* way to scale a programming model both inward (into the small intra-vat structure where conventional sequential reasoning works) and outward (into the inter-vat structure where partial failure, latency, and mutual suspicion dominate); intermediate models — pre-emptive threads with shared memory, locks, monitors — fail in both directions simultaneously, racing with allies and deadlocking with strangers.

E's eventual send returns a vow immediately. The caller proceeds; when the result becomes available, the vow *resolves*. Reactive code on the resolution is written in E with the `when` form: `when (vow) -> { body }` schedules `body` for execution, in the caller's vat, at some future turn after `vow` resolves. The `when` form takes a `catch` clause for broken vows (the asynchronous analogue of an exception handler) and a `finally` clause for unconditional cleanup. Goblins inherits all three [Lemmer-Webber, Farmer and Sims 2025, §3.2.4, §3.2.7].

Two further capability primitives in E deserve note. *Sealer/unsealer pairs* are an asymmetric-rights primitive — given a freshly-minted pair `(sealer, unsealer)`, anyone holding `sealer` may seal a value into an opaque envelope, and only a holder of the matching `unsealer` may extract it. This is the language-level analogue of public-key encryption, but built from references rather than mathematics, and it underpins E's pattern for secret-keeping under mutual suspicion. *Membranes*, due to Miller and Van Cutsem, wrap a sub-graph of objects and rewrite all references that cross the wrapper — they enable revocable delegation, transparent forwarding, and bidirectional access control with a single small abstraction [Miller 2006, ch. 9 and 11]. Both primitives became part of the JavaScript object-capability vocabulary via the SES (Secure ECMAScript) and Hardened-JavaScript work that Miller drove on TC39 between 2007 and the present.

#### §4.5.3 — CapTP: The Capability Transport Protocol

CapTP is the wire protocol that lets one vat's eventual send to a far reference reach across a TCP connection, a Tor circuit, an I2P tunnel, or any other ordered reliable channel into a vat in a different OS process or on a different machine. The original CapTP, designed by Miller, Tribble, and Hardy at Electric Communities and refined under Combex and Agoric, organises each pairwise inter-vat session around four tables: an *exports table* listing local objects this vat has revealed to the remote vat (with reference counts that increment on send and decrement on the remote's `op:gc-exports` message, enabling cross-vat distributed garbage collection); an *imports table* listing remote objects this vat has been told about; a *questions table* listing eventual sends this vat has issued whose vows have not yet resolved; and an *answers table* listing eventual sends this vat has received whose results have not yet been returned. A reference passed in a message is encoded as a position in one of these tables, with `desc:import-object` for a fresh export, `desc:export` for an already-known import, and `desc:answer` for a *promise reference* — a placeholder for a not-yet-resolved result that the recipient may pipeline further messages to. The OCapN draft specification [OCapN 2024–2026] preserves this four-table structure under modernised names (`exports`, `imports`, with promise tables folded in via `answer-pos`).

Promise pipelining over CapTP is the key efficiency claim. Lemmer-Webber, Farmer, and Sims [2025, §3.2.6] worked example for fork-motors makes the claim concrete: without pipelining, instructing a remote car factory to build a car and then drive it requires the message flow `B → A → B → A → B` (call factory, return car-vow, call drive on car, return drive-result, finally observe), three round-trips. With pipelining, `B` issues both calls back-to-back without waiting; `A` resolves the first locally, forwards the second to the local result, and replies with the final value: `B → A → B`, one round-trip. Miller's epigraph in §3.2.6 of that paper distils the urgency: "Machines grow faster and memories grow larger. But the speed of light is constant and New York is not getting any closer to Tokyo." Promise pipelining is, operationally, a way of letting the *plan* — the dataflow shape the programmer expressed locally — be evaluated *non-locally*: the wire becomes a queue not of values but of *operations on values*, and the receiver acts as a small dataflow scheduler that resolves the local subgraph and only the irreducibly cross-vat residual returns over the wire. This pattern is conceptually adjacent to *propagator-network closure-of-references* in the Saraswat sense — a remote vat receiving a stream of pipelined messages is effectively running an embedded constraint-store fragment whose updates are forwarded by the wire — but the connection is informal; CapTP is not formally cc(D), and the synthesis chapter, not this slice, is the place to argue the formal correspondence.

CapTP also handles three-vat introductions. Suppose vat A holds a reference to an object in vat B and wishes to give that reference to vat C. A naive design would have A relay messages between B and C indefinitely, but this triples latency and forces A to remain available. CapTP's *handoff* protocol [OCapN 2024–2026, §desc:handoff-give and §desc:handoff-receive] arranges that A *deposits* the reference at B's bootstrap object as a "gift" tagged with a random gift-ID, then sends C a signed `desc:handoff-give` certificate naming B's locator, the session ID, and the gift-ID; C then connects to B (if not already connected), presents a signed `desc:handoff-receive`, and B verifies both signatures and yields the deposited gift. After the handoff, C and B communicate directly; A is no longer in the path. The cryptographic structure ensures that even if the certificate leaks in transit, only C's keypair can complete the withdrawal. *Sturdy refs* and *live refs* are a parallel distinction: a sturdy ref is a serialisable, persistent, location-aware capability — typically encoded as a URI plus a swiss number — that survives vat restarts and can be stored in a database; a live ref is the in-session pointer that exists only for the lifetime of the CapTP session [E (programming language) 2024]. Sturdy refs are the bootstrap mechanism: a system stands up by reading sturdy refs from configuration, dialling the named vats, and converting the sturdy refs into live refs for the duration of the session.

The comparison with conventional remote-invocation protocols — Java RMI, CORBA, gRPC, and the older XML-RPC and SOAP families — is structural, not merely stylistic. RMI/CORBA assume an *authority* model in which the client either has the right to invoke a method or does not, with the right encoded in a transport-level identity (TLS client certificate, OAuth bearer token, Kerberos ticket); call-graphs are open by default and the network defends them. CapTP assumes a *capability* model in which the right to invoke *is* the reference — possession of the reference is permission, ambient authority does not exist, and the network defends nothing because there is nothing on the network the recipient is not entitled to invoke (capabilities being unforgeable means that to receive a capability is to receive nothing more than the right to invoke it). The closest mainstream analogue is Cap'n Proto [Varda 2013–2024], itself influenced by E and authored by an alumnus of Sandstorm.io (which deployed object-capability discipline for end-user web applications). Cap'n Proto's RPC layer is essentially CapTP with a different wire format and an emphasis on schema evolution; its tables and pipelining behaviour are nearly isomorphic. gRPC, by contrast, has no first-class promise-pipelining primitive, no first-class three-party introduction, and no capability discipline at all; its bidirectional streaming and deadlines compose, with effort, into an approximation of pipelining for the simplest case, but the architecture is incidentally async, not constitutively so.

#### §4.5.4 — *Robust Composition*: The Unification Claim

In May 2006, Mark S. Miller submitted his doctoral dissertation, "Robust Composition: Towards a Unified Approach to Access Control and Concurrency Control," to the Whiting School of Engineering at Johns Hopkins University, under the supervision of Jonathan S. Shapiro [Miller 2006]. The thesis is the canonical statement of the lineage's design claim. The starting observation is that *separately written programs that are composed to cooperate may instead destructively interfere*, and that this hazard appears in two ostensibly distinct guises — security failures (one program subverts another's invariants from outside its specification) and concurrency failures (one program subverts another's invariants from within an interleaving the author did not anticipate). Both phenomena are, on Miller's reading, *interference between plans*: the plan an author wrote contemplated the program executing certain references and certain causal sequences, and the failure mode in either case is that some other plan, accessing references or interleavings the author had no model of, breaks the assumptions on which the original plan rested.

The unifying claim of the thesis is that *capability discipline*, properly construed, is the answer to *both* problems simultaneously. A capability is a reference, and a reference encodes both *authority* (one can only invoke what one has a reference to) and *causality* (intra-vat references admit synchronous invocation in a single turn; inter-vat references admit only asynchronous invocation that yields the turn). The principle of least authority ("if you don't have it, you can't use it" — the slogan Spritely retains as its core marketing claim) becomes simultaneously a security principle (no ambient authority means malicious code cannot steal what it was not handed) and a concurrency principle (no shared mutable state means ally code cannot race with what it was not handed). The vat model is the operational realisation of this unification: a single-threaded turn within a vat *cannot* race with itself because there is no concurrency; cross-vat eventual sends *cannot* leak authority because the vat boundary is the capability boundary, and the only thing that crosses is the message and whatever capabilities its arguments carry. *Defensive consistency* — the property that an object preserves its invariants regardless of how its callers misbehave — is presented in the thesis as the constructive criterion: an object is defensively consistent if, against any plan composed against it, it preserves its invariants. Miller's argument is that defensive consistency is achievable in the vat model by ordinary careful programming, whereas in the threads-and-locks model it requires either the impossible (proving the absence of races against unknown adversarial code) or the unacceptable (locking everything globally and accepting deadlock).

The thesis is in three parts. Part I ("Programming Practice") catalogues the patterns: facets, caretakers, sealers, membranes, the auditor pattern; these are practical mechanisms by which capability programmers build robust composite systems. Part II ("From Objects to Capabilities") argues that conventional object-oriented languages are *almost* capability-safe, and that a small set of restrictions (no ambient authority, no global mutable state, no synchronous access to far objects) suffice to make them so. Part III ("Concurrency Control as Access Control") is the load-bearing piece for the present slice: it argues, across chapters 16–18, that promise pipelining, the vat model, and the `when`-form between them constitute a complete account of asynchronous programming under mutual suspicion. The thesis explicitly retires the `await`/coroutine pattern in favour of the `when`-form on the grounds that coroutines re-introduce the *re-entrancy* hazard — between the suspension of an `await` and its resumption, the world can change, and code that "looked sequential" silently held a stale view of state. The Spritely paper [Lemmer-Webber, Farmer, and Sims 2025, footnote 16] notes that this same re-entrancy hazard "became the number one way money has been stolen in Ethereum" via smart-contract reentrancy attacks; Goblins, like E, omits coroutines entirely.

#### §4.5.5 — Why This Lineage Matters for Propagator-Network Distributed Runtimes

The capability-async lineage matters for L2 design because it is the most thoroughly engineered practical antecedent for what L2 must be. The structural correspondences are direct enough to enumerate without forcing them; that the synthesis chapter will argue the case for the connection more strongly is signalled here, but not pre-empted.

A *vat* is a single-threaded event loop owning a private heap and a message queue; turns within it run to completion under quiescence-style semantics. A *single-stratum BSP propagator network* is a single-threaded scheduler firing propagators in rounds against a shared cell graph; rounds within it run to quiescence under monotone-merge semantics. The structural shape is the same: a bounded, internally-sequential unit of computation that yields between turns/rounds, with its boundary marking the limit of synchronous reasoning and the beginning of asynchronous information flow. An *inter-vat eventual send* injects a message into the recipient vat's queue; a *cross-network topology request* injects a structural-change request into the recipient network's stratum-handler queue. Both are CALM-safe under their respective monotone disciplines (vats by single-threading, propagator networks by lattice merge) and both exit that safety only at boundaries they explicitly cross. A *promise resolving from unresolved to resolved* is a cell becoming concrete from `bot`; both are monotone refinements of an initially-uninformative placeholder. A *capability* — an unforgeable reference whose possession is permission — is the bearer-instrument analogue of the project's `capabilities.prologos` hierarchy (`ReadCap <: FsCap <: IOCap <: SysCap`), and the discipline ("if you don't have it, you can't use it") is identical.

What the lineage offers, beyond structural alignment, is *engineering closure* on questions L2 design will otherwise have to re-discover. Distributed garbage collection of cross-network references is solved (CapTP's per-table reference counting plus `op:gc-exports`). Three-network introduction without permanent relaying is solved (CapTP's signed handoff). Persistent durable references are solved (sturdy refs / swiss numbers). Reference-tracking of revocable delegations is solved (membranes plus caretaker pattern). Re-entrancy hazards are diagnosed and ruled out (no coroutines, only `when`-blocks). Time-travel debugging across vat boundaries is solved (Goblins' transactional-heap mechanism, inherited from E's Causeway debugger; see §6.2.2). The synthesis chapter's distributed-propagator-with-quantale-causal-clocks (§8.4) leans on E's vat model as the design precedent; the present slice supplies the design-precedent evidence on which that lean rests. Where the synthesis must extend the lineage — for example, by replacing CapTP's pairwise per-message wire with a quantale-enriched causal-clock substrate that more faithfully captures the joint information lattice across multiple networks — it does so by *generalising* a structure the lineage has already validated, not by inventing a new one from scratch.

### §6.2 — Spritely Revival (2023–2026)

#### §6.2.1 — Spritely Institute and OCapN

The capability-async lineage went largely quiet in mainstream programming-language venues between roughly 2010 and 2020. E development slowed; the Combex-funded effort wound down; Miller moved to Google (2007–2017) where his energies went into TC39 standardisation, the Caja/SES/Hardened-JavaScript line, and the eventual founding of Agoric. CapTP was kept alive within Agoric's SwingSet as a private artefact; the protocol's external profile remained low. The revival began with the founding of Spritely in 2020 by Christine Lemmer-Webber, who had previously co-authored the W3C ActivityPub specification and observed that ActivityPub's federation model — server-centric, rooted in HTTP request-response, lacking capability discipline — had reached the limits of what a social network architected on REST primitives could safely do. Spritely Institute, the 501(c)(3) research nonprofit incorporated to host the work, was constituted around 2023 [Spritely Institute 2023–2026]. Lemmer-Webber, working initially with Jessica Tallon, David Thompson, Randy Farmer, and Juliana Sims (Spritely's "Heart of Spritely" paper authors as of May 2025), explicitly framed Spritely as a continuation and modernisation of the E-Joule-CapTP line rather than a fresh-start design.

OCapN — the Object Capability Network — was announced on 16 November 2023 by Jessica Tallon [Spritely Institute "Introducing OCapN" 2023]. OCapN is a suite of three layered specifications: *CapTP*, the modernised Capability Transport Protocol, covering message passing, promises, promise pipelining, and third-party handoffs; *Netlayers*, an abstraction over transport substrates that lets CapTP run identically over TCP+TLS, Tor onion services, I2P, libp2p, or any other ordered-reliable channel including encrypted "sneakernets" of physical media; and *Locators*, the URI scheme and certificate discipline by which an OCapN node bootstraps its initial connections [Lemmer-Webber, Farmer, and Sims 2025, §4]. The standardisation effort is multi-vendor: Spritely leads it, but Agoric, the Cap'n Proto community, Alexander Bondarenko's Haskell implementation, and others all participate; the explicit goal is interoperable capability-secure networking across multiple programming-language implementations, with eventual submission to a standards body (IETF or W3C) following community consensus. Both Goblins implementations — Racket and Guile — pass the OCapN interoperability test suite as of the announcement. The presentation paper notes [§4] that OCapN is "fully capable of functioning with no central authorities, even on peer-to-peer networks with the default assumption of hostile participants" — a near-direct restatement of Miller's "concurrency among strangers" framing.

#### §6.2.2 — Goblins and Contemporary Capability-Async

Goblins is the reference implementation. Originally written in Racket (initial public release c. 2020–2021; v0.7 in 2021), then ported to Guile Scheme as the primary line of development from 2022 onward, Goblins reaches v0.18 by 2025 with v0.11.0 (released 8 May 2023) being the milestone that introduced both time-travelling distributed debugging and OCapN-conformant CapTP semantics including acyclic distributed garbage collection [Spritely Institute "Goblins v0.11.0" 2023]. The Racket implementation is now in maintenance mode (bug-fixes and OCapN compatibility only); Guile Goblins is the active line and the substrate on which the rest of Spritely is being built [Lemmer-Webber, Farmer, and Sims 2025, footnote 8].

Goblins programmes look, syntactically, like ordinary Scheme — expressions, lexical scoping, first-class procedures — but the layered semantics encode the capability-async lineage faithfully [Lemmer-Webber, Farmer, and Sims 2025, §3.2]. An *actor* (Goblins' term, used interchangeably with *object*) is constructed by `spawn`, which takes a *constructor* procedure (conventionally named with a `^` prefix — a "hard hat"), allocates a fresh actor in the current vat's *actormap* (the per-vat transactional heap), and returns a reference. The constructor returns a *behaviour* — the procedure invoked on each subsequent message — and may close over a *bcom* capability that lets the actor specify its *next* behaviour for the *next* turn (Goblins is "quasi-functional": within a turn an actor's behaviour appears immutable, and `bcom` schedules a behaviour change for the subsequent turn rather than mutating in place; this is what enables the transactional roll-back property documented in §3.2.5). Synchronous near-invocation is `($ actor-ref message args ...)`; eventual far-invocation is `(<- actor-ref message args ...)` and returns a vow. The `on` form is Goblins' `when`: `(on vow (lambda (val) ...) #:catch (lambda (err) ...) #:finally (lambda () ...))`. Vats are entered via the REPL meta-command `,enter-vat` or constructed statically with the `with-vat` form. Promise pipelining is automatic: `(<- (<- factory 'make-car "Explorist" "blue") 'drive)` issues the pipelined message before the factory has resolved the car, and the network dispatch is `B → A → B` rather than `B → A → B → A → B` [§3.2.6, figures 22–25].

The two innovations Goblins brings beyond E are *transactional turns* (§3.2.5, §3.6) and *time-travel distributed debugging* (§3.7). A vat's turn is run as a transaction against its actormap: synchronous calls within the turn allocate, mutate, and `bcom` against a working copy; if the turn raises an unhandled exception, the working copy is discarded and the actormap reverts to its pre-turn state. Errors in a Goblins turn thus cause *no observable damage* — the system rolls back as though the message had never been processed — and asynchronous messages enqueued during a now-doomed turn are *not* sent. This makes error handling far simpler than under either thread-based concurrency or thread-pooled event loops, where partial state changes from a failed handler typically remain. Time-travel debugging exploits the same mechanism: the per-turn snapshots that enable rollback are also persistable, and the distributed debugger announced on 12 April 2023 by David Thompson [Spritely Institute "Distributed debugger" 2023] uses them to let a developer inspect the state of the actormap *as it was* at the moment of any past message delivery, with `,vat-tree` and `,vat-graph` REPL meta-commands rendering Lamport-style causality diagrams across multiple in-process vats. The debugger credits E's Causeway as direct prior art and notes that cross-process / cross-machine debugging via CapTP is a planned future capability. Hoot — a Scheme-to-WebAssembly compiler, also from Spritely — is the deployment vector by which Goblins reaches the browser; v0.8 of Hoot is current as of mid-2025 [Spritely Institute 2023–2026, news listing].

The contemporary Goblins library covers, beyond the core: a *standard library of common actor types* (cells, mutable variables, record-cells, mints / banking patterns, opportunistic proxying, pushdown automata for protocol implementation, simple-mint, let-on for promise sequencing); a *safe serialisation/upgrade* mechanism descended from E's "Safe Serialization Under Mutual Suspicion" (Rees 2007) which serialises only what an object self-describes via a sealed serialiser-method, preventing capability theft via deserialisation; an *unum pattern* for distributed objects that have multiple presences across machines (a teacup may exist as a server-side canonical object, a client-side local representation, and an optional second-client-side representation, all coherently linked); and *CRDT support* as the convergent-replicated-data-type analogue of the unum pattern, providing eventual-consistency primitives that compose with capability discipline [Lemmer-Webber, Farmer, and Sims 2025, §3.8, §3.9].

#### §6.2.3 — Connections to the Broader Frontier

The Spritely revival reconnects the capability-async lineage to several adjacent threads of contemporary research; the present slice flags the connections without arguing them, leaving the synthesis chapter to weight the cross-cutting evidence.

Capability-passing as choreography. Choreographic programming, as covered by Agent 8's §6.1 slice, treats a distributed protocol as a single program from which per-party endpoint code is projected; the programmer reasons about the global ballet rather than each dancer's local view. OCapN's three-vat handoff is, structurally, a small choreography: the gifter, the exporter, and the receiver each play a scripted role, and the protocol specification reads as a global narrative of who sends what to whom. The connection invites investigation of whether OCapN-style protocols admit a choreographic projection — one can imagine writing the handoff as a single choreographic statement that compiles to the gifter, exporter, and receiver code. This is unstudied; the slice flags it for the synthesis chapter.

Capability-based effect systems. Agent 7's §5.4 slice covers Brachthäuser–Schuster effekt and the broader algebraic-effects-as-capabilities programme, which descends transparently from Miller's tradition: effekt's *capability passing as effect handling* is a lambda-calculus–level realisation of what Miller argued at the language-design level, namely that ambient authority (an effect handler installed in a dynamic scope) is the wrong default and that explicit reference-passing is the right one. Cross-fertilisation between effekt-style systems and OCapN-style runtimes is an open frontier; the slice flags it.

Composition with structured concurrency. Structured concurrency (Agent 7's §5.1) imposes a syntactic discipline on task lifetimes — every spawned task is bounded by a lexical scope, no task outlives its parent's scope, errors propagate up the scope tree. The vat model offers a coarser discipline: every actor lives within some vat, and the vat's lifetime bounds all its actors. Whether OCapN composes with structured concurrency in the small (within a vat) is open; an `on`-block that spawns a vow is *not* structurally bounded in the Trio/Project Loom sense, since the vow's resolution is asynchronous and may outlive the textual scope. A reasonable reading is that the vat itself is the structured-concurrency unit, and intra-vat code uses ordinary lexical scoping; that reading aligns with the lineage's claim that the vat is the *only* asynchronous boundary that matters. The synthesis chapter is the right place to evaluate this claim against the structured-concurrency literature.

Persistent capabilities and decentralised identity. Sturdy refs are durable bearer instruments; they sit naturally adjacent to the work of the W3C Decentralized Identifiers (DIDs) working group and the broader self-sovereign-identity ecosystem (Agoric's interest in this connection is explicit, and the unum pattern is essentially a generalisation of "the same logical agent has multiple concrete presences"). Connections to capability-based blockchain primitives — Agoric's smart-contract platform built on Hardened JavaScript and SwingSet — are the most thoroughly engineered current deployment and warrant a synthesis-level discussion in their own right.

Bomberger, Alan C., A. Peri Frantz, William S. Frantz, Ann C. Hardy, Norman Hardy, Charles R. Landau, and Jonathan S. Shapiro. 1992. "The KeyKOS Nanokernel Architecture." *Proceedings of the USENIX Workshop on Micro-Kernels and Other Kernel Architectures*. Refines the 1985 architecture as a nanokernel for object-capability systems; the most technically detailed surviving description of KeyKOS internals.

E (programming language). 2024. *Wikipedia*. https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E_(programming_language) . Consulted as a fact-checking aid; not authoritative on details, but useful for cross-referencing creators (Miller, Bornstein, Crockford, Morningstar, et al., 1997 at Electric Communities), syntactic decisions (no `=` operator, optional guard syntax), and the vat-eventual-send-promise architectural triad.

Hardy, Norman. 1985. "The KeyKOS Architecture." *ACM SIGOPS Operating Systems Review* 19 (4): 8–25. https://doi.org/10.1145/858336.858337 . Founding paper of the lineage on the operating-systems side. Documents a system that had been in production since 1983 on IBM 4341 hardware, supporting capability-based processes with transparent persistence via system-wide checkpointing, with security, pricing, reliability, and extensibility as design requirements. The 1985 paper is the most-cited; the underlying engineering is the unstated load-bearing claim of the entire lineage — that capability discipline scales to a real, commercial, multi-tenant system.

Lemmer-Webber, Christine, Randy Farmer, and Juliana Sims. 2025. "The Heart of Spritely: Distributed Objects and Capability Security." Spritely Institute, 21 May 2025. https://files.spritely.institute/papers/spritely-core.pdf . Currently the most authoritative single document on the contemporary state of the capability-async lineage. 73 pages of dense technical content; the second of a three-paper series. The introduction (§1–2) argues capability discipline as ordinary programming via the principle of least authority; §3 walks through Goblins by example, including the vat model (§3.4–3.5), turns as transactions (§3.6), time-travel debugging (§3.7), safe serialisation (§3.8), and the unum pattern for distributed identity (§3.9); §4 documents OCapN's layered structure (CapTP + Netlayers + Locators); §5 covers application and library safety. The paper explicitly situates Goblins as descended from W7 [Rees 1996] for its host-language minimums and from E [Miller 2006] for its distributed-object architecture, with transactional turns as Spritely's primary innovation beyond E. Footnote 15 is the key historical attribution: promise pipelining derives from Joule, JavaScript Promises descend from E and Joule, and the full pipelining property (which JavaScript lost) is preserved in Goblins.

Liskov, Barbara, and Liuba Shrira. 1988. "Promises: Linguistic Support for Efficient Asynchronous Procedure Calls in Distributed Systems." *Proceedings of the ACM SIGPLAN 1988 Conference on Programming Language Design and Implementation* (PLDI '88), Atlanta, GA, 22–24 June 1988, 260–267. https://doi.org/10.1145/53990.54016 . The independent invention of promises as a linguistic primitive in the Argus distributed-programming project at MIT. Liskov and Shrira coined the modern term *promise*; their *call-streams* were the precursor to promise pipelining, though the version they implemented in Argus did not yet treat unresolved promises as fully first-class receivers of further pipelined operations. The Miller-Tribble-Jellinghaus line that fed E's vows generalised this property into the form CapTP uses today.

Miller, Mark S. 2006. "Robust Composition: Towards a Unified Approach to Access Control and Concurrency Control." Ph.D. dissertation, Johns Hopkins University, May 2006. Advisor: Jonathan S. Shapiro. http://www.erights.org/talks/thesis/markm-thesis.pdf . The canonical statement of the lineage's central design claim: that capability discipline unifies access control and concurrency control. Three parts (Programming Practice / From Objects to Capabilities / Concurrency Control as Access Control), with the third part — chapters 16–18 — establishing the vat model, eventual sends, the `when`-form, promise pipelining, and *defensive consistency* as the design criterion. §16.2 traces the genealogy through Joule and into JavaScript Promises and laments the loss of pipelining. §16 also retires `await`/coroutines in favour of `when`-blocks on re-entrancy grounds — a position Spritely and Agoric both maintain.

Miller, Mark S., and K. Eric Drexler. 1988. "Markets and Computation: Agoric Open Systems." In *The Ecology of Computation*, edited by Bernardo A. Huberman, 133–176. Amsterdam: Elsevier Science Publishers / North-Holland. Reprinted at https://papers.agoric.com/papers/markets-and-computation-agoric-open-systems/ . The agoric-systems framing argues that computation in heterogeneous, large-scale, mutually-suspicious settings requires market-mechanism resource allocation, and that market mechanisms presuppose property, which presupposes unforgeable title — i.e., capabilities. The connection between the agoric line and the capability line in Miller's work is not after-the-fact synthesis but original co-design.

Miller, Mark S., E. Dean Tribble, and Jonathan Shapiro. 2005. "Concurrency Among Strangers: Programming in E as Plan Coordination." In *Trustworthy Global Computing: International Symposium, TGC 2005, Edinburgh, UK, April 7–9, 2005, Revised Selected Papers*, edited by Rocco De Nicola and Davide Sangiorgi, 195–229. Lecture Notes in Computer Science 3705. Berlin: Springer. https://doi.org/10.1007/11580850_12 . Reprint at https://papers.agoric.com/papers/concurrency-among-strangers/ . Position-paper-style argument that the vat model is the only scalable account of cooperation between parties who do not trust one another and who must communicate across latency and partial failure. The paper introduces "plan coordination" as the framing: composition of plans is the goal, and locks-and-threads break composition by either deadlocking allies or surrendering to adversaries. Cited consistently in Goblins documentation as the conceptual backbone.

OCapN (Object Capability Network). 2024–2026. *Draft Specifications*. GitHub: https://github.com/ocapn/ocapn . Three layered specifications: CapTP (covers messages, promises, promise pipelining, third-party handoffs), Netlayers (transport-protocol abstraction supporting TLS, Tor onion services, I2P, libp2p, and offline store-and-forward), and Locators (URI scheme and certificate discipline for bootstrap). Multi-vendor effort: Spritely lead, with Agoric, Cap'n Proto community, Bondarenko's Haskell implementation, and others. Both Racket and Guile Goblins pass the interoperability test suite. Public website at https://ocapn.org/ .

Rees, Jonathan A. 1996. "A Security Kernel Based on the Lambda-Calculus." MIT AI Lab Technical Report AITR-1564. https://dspace.mit.edu/handle/1721.1/5944 . The W7 dialect of Scheme, demonstrating that a small set of constraints on a Lisp-family language (no ambient authority, no global mutable state, lexical scoping with reference-passing as the only capability-transfer mechanism) suffices for object-capability safety. Cited by Spritely as the foundation of Goblins' host-language requirements.

Maintains Goblins (Racket and Guile implementations), Hoot (Scheme-to-WebAssembly compiler), the OCapN protocol effort (with Agoric and others), and the Spritely Goblins documentation manual. Founded around 2023 with Christine Lemmer-Webber as founder; current key contributors include Lemmer-Webber, Randy Farmer, Juliana Sims, Jessica Tallon, David Thompson, Vivianne Langdon, Geoffrey J. Teale, and others. (8 May 2023), "Introducing: A distributed debugger for Goblins with time travel!" (Thompson, 12 April 2023), and the Goblins documentation at https://docs.racket-lang.org/goblins/ (Racket) and https://files.spritely.institute/docs/guile-goblins/ (Guile).

Cited as such in Lemmer-Webber, Farmer, and Sims [2025, footnote 6 and Lemmer-Webber, Farmer and Sims 2025 §3.2.6 footnote 15] and in Miller [2006, §16]. Joule was an experimental concurrent-constraint-flavoured language whose vat model and promise primitives are the immediate ancestors of E's. The 1995 report is the canonical citation though copies are scarce; the design is partly preserved by the running text of Miller's thesis chapters 16–17.

### Key Findings

1. *The capability-async lineage is the most thoroughly engineered antecedent for L2.* From KeyKOS (production 1983, paper 1985) through Joule (1995) and E (1997–2006) to OCapN/Goblins (2023–2026), the same architectural primitives — vats, eventual sends returning promises, promise pipelining for round-trip elimination, capability discipline as the unification of authority and synchronisation — have been deployed, productionised, retired, and re-deployed in continuous evolution for forty-plus years. L2 design need not invent these primitives; it inherits them.

2. *Promise pipelining has weak-form (Liskov-Shrira 1988 / Argus call-streams) and strong-form (Miller-Tribble-Jellinghaus c. 1989 / Joule / E) variants, and the strong form is materially different.* In the strong form, an unresolved promise is itself a first-class addressable receiver of further pipelined messages; this is what permits the network round-trip count to drop from O(N) to O(1) in deeply chained dataflow. JavaScript Promises descend from E's vows but lost this property; a propagator-network distributed runtime must recover the strong form to capture the latency win.

3. *Miller's "Robust Composition" thesis claims and demonstrates that access control and concurrency control are the same problem under capability discipline.* In the vat model, intra-vat code is sequential by construction (no concurrency hazards) and inter-vat communication is asynchronous by construction (no shared mutable state hazards); both are special cases of the capability-discipline rule that one can only invoke what one has a reference to. This unification is structurally identical to the Prologos design choice to make the propagator-network monotone-merge discipline (CALM-safe within strata) and the cross-stratum topology requests (the only place non-monotone change is permitted) the joint account of safety and concurrency.

4. *Spritely's transactional-turn extension to E is novel and load-bearing.* Goblins runs each vat turn as a transaction against the actormap: an unhandled exception causes the turn to roll back atomically, with no observable side-effects either to local state or to outbound asynchronous messages. This makes failure-handling cheaper than in either E or any thread-based system, and it is the mechanism that enables time-travel debugging across vat boundaries. A propagator-network analogue (a BSP round as a transaction whose merges roll back atomically on failure) is implementable on the same lattice infrastructure already in place.

5. *OCapN's three-vat handoff is a small choreography and admits a structural identification with cross-network topology requests in a multi-network propagator runtime.* The protocol — gifter deposits a gift at the exporter, sends a signed certificate to the receiver, receiver presents the certificate at the exporter and withdraws — is, structurally, a four-message exchange whose cryptographic discipline ensures that interception cannot complete the withdrawal. The same structural shape applies to introducing one propagator network's cell to another network's propagator: a signed handoff via a third party is the natural realisation when networks have no prior contact and one network must broker.

### Open Questions

1. *Is CapTP formally cc(D)?* The conjecture that CapTP's wire semantics — pipelined messages as forwarded operations on not-yet-resolved promises — formally instantiates Saraswat's concurrent-constraint-programming closure-of-references is intuitive but unstudied. The synthesis chapter is the right place to argue it; the present slice flags the connection without committing.

2. *Does OCapN compose with structured concurrency in the small (within a vat)?* The vat itself is plausibly the structured-concurrency unit, but `on`-blocks that spawn vows are not structurally lifetime-bounded in the Trio/Loom sense. The right reading may be that the vat's lifetime *is* the bound, and intra-vat code is structurally concurrent in the conventional Scheme sense; this needs to be made precise.

3. *Can the vat-model two-level concurrency split (sequential within / asynchronous between) extend to N>2 levels?* The propagator-network strata pattern is a generalisation: S0 monotone, S1 NAF, etc. A capability-async lineage analogue might have intra-vat synchronous, intra-cluster asynchronous-but-trusted, inter-cluster asynchronous-and-mutually-suspicious. Whether OCapN's flat model collapses this artificially, or whether it correctly captures all that needs to be captured, is open.

4. *Is sturdy-ref persistence semantically a quantale-enriched bearer instrument?* Sturdy refs are durable, transferable, time-indexed authorisation tokens; the natural ambient algebra is closer to a (bounded, monotone, tropical) quantale than to a Boolean lattice. The synthesis chapter's interest in quantale-enriched categories aligns; the alignment needs argument.

5. *Are E-style sealer/unsealer pairs and Goblins serialisers a special case of polymorphic capabilities under linear typing?* The asymmetric-rights primitive (sealer can write, unsealer can read, neither can do the other's job) is a degenerate two-party split of authority that QTT linear typing arguably subsumes: a sealed envelope is a value of type `Sealed[A]` whose elimination requires consumption of a linear unsealer-token. Whether this is a re-derivation or a generalisation depends on details the synthesis can pin down.

## §5 Era IV: The Structured-Concurrency Turn (2015–2024)

The fourth era is the period in which mainstream programming-language design openly admits that the function-coloring discipline of the third era — `async`/`await`, futures-as-functions, callback-style continuations layered onto a non-restructured call stack — was a tactical mistake. The admission is not framed as a retraction. It is framed as a re-encounter with structured programming: the recognition that the same arguments Edsger Dijkstra advanced against `goto` apply, mutatis mutandis, to the unrestricted `go` (or `spawn`, or `Task.detached`, or `launch` without a scope) of post-2005 concurrency. Era IV runs in parallel along five tracks: (5.1) the structured-concurrency reformation in mainstream languages; (5.2) algebraic effects becoming an industrial primitive; (5.3) the maturation, and decidability fragmentation, of asynchronous session types; (5.4) the resurgence of capability-based effect systems; and (5.5) the lattice-based monotonicity-as-coordination-freedom programme of CALM, Bloom, and BloomL. The five threads are independent in surface form but converge on a shared diagnostic: asynchrony is not a feature to bolt onto a sequential calculus; it is the operational form of a more disciplined account of computation in which scope, cancellation, ordering, and resource use are first-class type-level concerns.

The §5.5 BloomL track, in particular, is direct evidence for the Prologos working hypothesis: monotone joins on a lattice substrate are exactly the algebraic surface CALM identifies with coordination-free distributed evaluation. Read alongside §5.2's algebraic-effect handlers, the picture that Era IV draws — though no single paper draws it explicitly — is that of a quantale-enriched propagator network on which scope, ordering, cost, and effect are projection-views of one underlying structure. This synthesis is reserved for §8 of the Phase 1 deliverable; this slice supplies the evidence.

### §5.1 Structured concurrency: reclaiming the call stack

The structured-concurrency turn has a single load-bearing diagnostic document: Nathaniel J. Smith's 2018 essay "Notes on structured concurrency, or: Go statement considered harmful," which inaugurated the Trio project's design and propagated, by direct citation and by independent rediscovery, into Kotlin, Swift, and Java. Smith's essay is not a survey: it is a polemic in the direct lineage of Dijkstra's "Go To Statement Considered Harmful" (1968), and it argues that the unrestricted `go`/`spawn`/`launch` operator in modern languages exhibits the same pathologies as `goto` — not by analogy but structurally [Smith 2018].

Smith's central observation is what he calls the violation of the **black box rule**. When a programmer calls a function in a language that admits unrestricted task spawning, "whenever you call a function, it might or might not spawn some background task. The function seemed to return, but is it still running in the background? There's no way to know without reading all its source code, transitively" [Smith 2018]. The function's apparent control-flow signature — "I take inputs, I return an output" — is a lie: the function's true signature includes an implicit, untypable side-effect of installing background work whose lifetime is unbounded. This is a direct echo of Dijkstra's argument that `goto` makes local reasoning impossible because the call graph admits non-local exit paths invisible at the call site.

Smith's prescriptive contribution is the **nursery**: a lexically-scoped concurrency primitive that enforces a parent-child task hierarchy. The Trio formulation in Python is

```python
async with trio.open_nursery() as nursery:
    nursery.start_soon(myfunc)
    nursery.start_soon(anotherfunc)
```

with the invariant that "the nursery block doesn't exit until all the tasks inside it have exited — if the parent task reaches the end of the block before all the children are finished, then it pauses there and waits for them" [Smith 2018]. This restores the black-box rule: a function that contains a nursery still has a single entry and a single exit, even though it spawns N concurrent children, because the children's lifetimes are statically bounded by the nursery's lexical scope. Errors are propagated up the task tree in the same way exceptions propagate up the call stack: "If a background task terminates with an exception, we can rethrow it in the parent task. The intuition here is that a nursery is something like a 'concurrent call' primitive...exceptions propagate up this call tree towards the root, just like they propagate up a regular call stack" [Smith 2018].

Smith further insists on **level-triggered** rather than edge-triggered cancellation. In a level-triggered system, a cancellation request is a *standing condition*: any subsequent operation inside the cancelled scope re-encounters and re-respects the cancellation. In an edge-triggered system, the cancellation fires once and is consumed; subsequent operations inside the cancelled scope can proceed unaware. Smith argues that edge-triggered cancellation is unimplementable in the presence of cleanup handlers, because any cleanup that performs its own awaits will silently dodge the cancellation it was meant to honour. Trio therefore implements `CancelScope` as a level-triggered, stateful object: every checkpoint operation re-checks all enclosing scopes, and a cancelled scope continues to raise `Cancelled` from every checkpoint until execution leaves the scope [Trio Reference 2024]. The cancellation discipline is summarised: "We *never* terminate a task without giving it a chance to run cleanup handlers, and we *never* leave a task to run unsupervised outside of the nursery, even if it's in the process of being cancelled" [Smith 2018].

The Trio design propagated by independent rediscovery, not by direct citation. Roman Elizarov, lead architect of Kotlin Coroutines, characterised the introduction of `CoroutineScope` in `kotlinx.coroutines 0.26.0` (October 2018) as "more than just a feature — it marks an ideology shift," with the diagnostic that "a simple `launch { … }` is easy to write, but it is not what you should be writing" and the prescription that "coroutines are always related to some local *scope*…with a limited life-time" [Elizarov 2018]. Children launched in a `CoroutineScope` inherit cancellation: "if the scope fails with an exception or is cancelled, all the children are cancelled, too" [Elizarov 2018]. Elizarov's blog post does not cite Smith, despite landing four months after Smith's essay, suggesting that the design was developed in parallel (at minimum) and that the structured-concurrency thesis had already passed into the air. Subsequent retrospectives [Elizarov 2019] acknowledge convergent evolution.

Swift's structured-concurrency track is the most self-conscious of the three.
