SE-0304, "Structured Concurrency," went through four review rounds between March and July 2021 before acceptance [Swift Evolution SE-0304]. The motivating section explicitly distinguishes structured from unstructured forms by appeal to information flow: "without structure, systems struggle with timeout propagation, cancellation management, priority escalation, and contextual information flow across abstraction layers" [SE-0304]. The proposal acknowledges that the prior async/await proposal, SE-0296, "does not introduce concurrency per se" — async/await is a sequencing construct over a single task; concurrency requires a child-task-spawning construct, and the only sound such construct is one that bounds its children's lifetimes. Swift's primitive is `TaskGroup` (the proposal records "nursery" as an early candidate name, replaced by "task group" for Anglophone clarity) with the invariant that "a function that creates a child task must wait for it to end before returning" and "if the scope exits via a thrown error, the child task will be implicitly cancelled" [SE-0304]. The companion proposal SE-0317 introduces `async let` as a sugar over single-child task groups [SE-0317]. SE-0306 adds Actors, "concurrency domains" with serial executors, with the justification that concurrent shared mutable state must be either eliminated (by ownership tracking, in the spirit of Rust) or serialised (by actor isolation, in the spirit of Erlang and Hewitt). Swift opts for actors-as-default, with reentrancy by default to avoid Akka-style deadlock cascades, and references Orleans's call-chain reentrancy as a candidate future direction [SE-0306]. SE-0316 then introduces global actors (the canonical example being `@MainActor`), which are singleton-lifetime actors threaded through the type system as effect-like annotations [SE-0316].

The Java track is by far the most restrained: virtual threads, the M:N user-mode-thread implementation that makes "thread per request" cheap again, and `StructuredTaskScope`, the structured-concurrency primitive layered on virtual threads, are introduced over a sequence of JEPs spanning JDK 19 through the present. JEP 425 introduced virtual threads as a preview in JDK 19; JEP 436 continued preview in JDK 20; JEP 444 finalised them in JDK 21 [JEP 444]. The motivation is the resource exhaustion of platform threads: a million platform threads consumes order 10 GB of memory (one OS thread per request), while a million virtual threads consumes order 200 MB [Pressler & Goetz cited in InfoQ 2023]. Structured concurrency was introduced as JEP 437 (incubator, JDK 19), JEP 453 (preview, JDK 21), JEP 462 (second preview, JDK 22), and continues through JEPs 505, 525, 533 with no current finalisation date [JEP 462]. The principal class is `StructuredTaskScope`, which "confines the lifetimes of the subtasks to a clear lexical scope in which all of a task's interactions with its subtasks — forking, joining, handling errors, and composing results — takes place" [JEP 462]. Two subclasses, `ShutdownOnFailure` and `ShutdownOnSuccess`, encode the racing patterns common in structured concurrency literature. The Java design is notably late and cautious — the seven preview iterations are themselves evidence that retrofitting structured concurrency onto a platform with a 30-year history of unstructured `Thread` objects is hard.

The CSP heritage is the deep history of this story. Newsqueak (Cardelli & Pike, Bell Labs, mid-1980s) introduced first-class channels and dynamic process creation as a refinement of Hoare's CSP [Pike 1989]. Limbo (Dorward, Winterbottom, Pike, late 1990s) carried CSP into Plan 9's Inferno OS. Go (Pike & Thompson, Google, 2009) carried it into mainstream. Go's `go` statement is exactly the unrestricted task-spawn that Smith critiques [Smith 2018]; the Go team has acknowledged the structured-concurrency point in the form of `errgroup` and the broader `context` discipline, but has not introduced a structural primitive. The CSP heritage is therefore a precondition of the structured-concurrency turn — Era IV reacts to CSP-style concurrency as much as it reacts to async/await — but it is not itself part of it.

The structured-concurrency turn admits a clean lattice-theoretic restatement that the original literature does not make: the parent-child task hierarchy is a tree, the cancellation propagation is downward closure on that tree, the error propagation is upward closure on that tree, and the lifetime constraint is the requirement that the join semilattice of cleanup obligations be quiesced before the parent scope exits. This is exactly the structure that a CALM-monotone evaluation would induce automatically (§5.5); the structured-concurrency primitive is, in effect, a hand-implemented monotone scheduler over a tree-shaped lattice of task lifetimes. The opportunity for a quantale-enriched propagator substrate is to deliver this scheduler structurally, not by convention.

### §5.2 Algebraic effects: from FoSSaCS to OCaml 5

The algebraic-effect tradition, as a programming-language phenomenon, runs from Plotkin and Power's [2002] "Notions of computation determine monads" through Plotkin and Pretnar's [2009] "Handlers of algebraic effects" to Bauer and Pretnar's [2013] Eff language, Lindley, McBride, and McLaughlin's [2017] Frank, Leijen's [2014–2021] Koka series, and Sivaramakrishnan et al.'s [2021] retrofit of effect handlers onto OCaml. Each step in the sequence is a step toward making algebraic effects an industrial primitive, with async/await as one effect among many — fork, yield, await, ask, get, raise — rather than as a privileged language-level construct.

Plotkin and Power's "Notions of computation determine monads" inaugurates the modern algebraic account [Plotkin & Power 2002]. The paper's contribution is the observation that "a number of commonly-used monads are in fact free for an appropriate algebraic theory," which inverts Moggi's [1991] account of effects: rather than choosing a monad and then asking what operations it supports, one chooses an *algebraic theory* — operations and equations — and the monad is then determined as the free algebra over that theory. State, exceptions, nondeterminism, interactive I/O, and concurrency all admit algebraic theories of this kind; continuations notably do not, and are a recurrent edge case. The categorical machinery is enriched-category-theoretic: a Lawvere theory presents an algebraic theory as a small category with finite products, and a free model is a left adjoint to the forgetful functor. The connection to §3.2 game semantics and to §3.3 CCP is direct: closure operators on a quantale are themselves a Lawvere theory, and CCP's tell/ask discipline is a particular such theory whose carrier is the constraint lattice.

Plotkin and Pretnar's "Handlers of algebraic effects" [Plotkin & Pretnar 2009] adds the operational primitive that turns algebraic effects from a denotational curiosity into a programming construct. A handler is a homomorphism from the free algebra induced by the theory's operations to a target algebra: in programmer terms, a handler is an interpreter that catches each effect operation as it is invoked and supplies a continuation-aware reply. The paper presents an algebraic treatment of exception handlers and shows the same machinery handles "nondeterminism, interactive input/output, concurrency, state, time, and their combinations" [Plotkin & Pretnar 2009]. The operational semantics is given by a small-step relation in which an effect operation `op(v; k)` on a free term is captured by an enclosing handler, which dispatches on `op` and supplies its own definition for what `k` should receive. The journal version [Plotkin & Pretnar 2013] generalises to local algebraic effect theories.

Bauer and Pretnar's Eff [Bauer & Pretnar 2013] is the first programming language to expose handlers as a first-class language construct. The paper demonstrates that "standard effects are treated in Eff" and that the language "supports programming techniques using various forms of delimited continuations, such as backtracking, breadth-first search, selection functionals, [and] cooperative multi-threading" [Bauer & Pretnar 2013]. The cooperative multi-threading example is the load-bearing one for Era IV: it shows that a scheduler is just a handler, that fork/yield/await are just operations, and that the entirety of the async/await discipline collapses into a four-line library on top of a generic effect-handler primitive. This is the moment at which the Era III function-coloring discipline becomes visible as a contingent design choice rather than a fundamental commitment.

Frank [Lindley, McBride & McLaughlin 2017] generalises the effect-handler abstraction by collapsing the distinction between functions and handlers. A Frank function is "the special case of a Frank operator that interprets no commands"; the general case is a multihandler that "simultaneously interpret[s] commands from several sources at once, without disturbing the direct style of functional programming with values" [Lindley et al. 2017]. The bidirectional type system propagates an "ambient ability" inwards rather than accumulating "unions of potential effects outwards," which sidesteps the effect-variable proliferation that disfigures effect-row systems in the Koka style. Frank is significant for Prologos's account because its bidirectional discipline matches the bidirectional elaboration discipline that PPN imposes on the propagator-native typing pipeline; the operational claim "a function is a degenerate handler" is exactly the claim that propagator-driven elaboration generalises function-call elaboration as a special case.

Daan Leijen's Koka programme is the most sustained industrial-language exploration of algebraic effects. The four load-bearing papers are: "Koka: Programming with row-polymorphic effect types" [Leijen 2014], which introduces row-polymorphic effects with duplicate labels, allowing flexible composition without the encapsulation pathologies of effect-set systems; "Type Directed Compilation of Row-Typed Algebraic Effects" [Leijen 2017], which shows how to compile effect handlers efficiently to common runtime platforms (JavaScript, JVM, .NET) via a type-directed selective CPS translation; "Effect Handlers in Haskell, Evidently" [Xie & Leijen 2020], which presents the evidence-passing implementation strategy as a Haskell library; and "Generalized Evidence Passing for Effect Handlers" [Xie & Leijen 2021], which presents a sequence of refinements — multi-prompt delimited control, generalized evidence passing, yield bubbling, and a final monadic translation into plain lambda calculus — culminating in a C backend for Koka with performance competitive with Multicore OCaml's native runtime [Xie & Leijen 2021]. The Koka series is technically the most advanced of the algebraic-effect compilation efforts; its evidence-passing translation is reusable across host languages and is, in particular, the basis for the OCaml 5 effect-handler implementation.

Sivaramakrishnan, Dolan, White, Kelly, Jaffer, and Madhavapeddy's "Retrofitting Effect Handlers onto OCaml" [Sivaramakrishnan et al. 2021] is the Era IV closing act: an industrial language adds effect handlers as a primitive, with the explicit motivation that "non-local control flow mechanisms such as generators, async/await, lightweight threads and coroutines [should] be composably expressed" [Sivaramakrishnan et al. 2021]. The implementation imposes "a mean 1% overhead on a comprehensive macro benchmark suite that does not use effect handlers," remains compatible with stack-inspecting program analysis tools, and is efficient for new code. OCaml 5's effect handlers ship with Multicore OCaml in 2022 [OCaml 5.0 release]. The async-as-effect framing is now operative in production code: schedulers are libraries, "user-level thread schedulers themselves expressed as OCaml libraries, allowing the scheduling policy to be changed by swapping out the scheduler library for a different one with the same interface" [Multicore OCaml documentation]. The unification claim — that fork, yield, await, generators, coroutines, and lightweight threads all reduce to one primitive (effect handlers) — is no longer speculative.

Ahman and Pretnar's "Asynchronous Effects" [Ahman & Pretnar 2021], appearing at POPL 2021, makes the connection between algebraic effects and asynchrony fully explicit. The paper "decouples operation execution into two phases: signalling that an operation's implementation needs to be executed, and interrupting a running computation with the operation's result" [Ahman & Pretnar 2021]. Computations "react by installing interrupt handlers" to process results returned from signalled operations. The formalism is the calculus λ_ae, with applications "across web applications, multi-threading, remote calls, and parallel effect runners." This is the canonical algebraic account of async/await: a `signal` operation is a fork-point, an `interrupt` is a callback-arrival, and a handler is a scheduler. The paper does not pursue quantale-enrichment explicitly — the categorical formalism remains classical algebraic — but the structure is exactly the one a quantale-enriched effect calculus would inherit.

The §5.2 thread sets the stage for §6.3 (Era V frontier work): the open question is whether a quantale-enriched account of algebraic effects exists, in which the effect theory's free algebra is taken in a quantale-enriched-categorical setting and the monoidal structure on the quantale gives a structural account of cost, ordering, and resource as projections of one underlying multiplication. This is precisely the construction Prologos's ⟨tropical-min, +⟩ cost semantics on its propagator substrate is reaching for, and it is the load-bearing claim of the Phase 1 synthesis §8.6.

### §5.3 Asynchronous session types and the decidability frontier

The asynchronous-session-types track is the most technically mature of Era IV's threads and the most relevant to Prologos's existing session-type infrastructure. The track begins with Honda, Yoshida, and Carbone's "Multiparty Asynchronous Session Types" [Honda, Yoshida & Carbone 2008], which extends the binary-session-type theory of Honda [1993] and Honda, Vasconcelos, and Kubo [1998] to *multiparty* protocols expressed as global types projected onto local (endpoint) types. The asynchronous semantics is the standard π-calculus-with-buffers semantics: send and receive are decoupled, a send buffers a message, and a receive consumes from the buffer. The type system guarantees communication safety, session fidelity, and freedom from deadlock for well-typed processes following a coherent global type [Honda et al. 2008]. The journal version [Honda, Yoshida & Carbone 2016, JACM] consolidates the theory.

Mostrous, Yoshida, and Honda's "Global Principal Typing in Partially Commutative Asynchronous Sessions" [Mostrous, Yoshida & Honda 2009] introduces *asynchronous communication subtyping*, a refinement of the multiparty theory in which actions are allowed to commute when their dependencies permit, enabling "partial commutativity of actions with maximal flexibility and safe optimisation in message choreography" [Mostrous et al. 2009]. The paper presents a sound and complete algorithm for the subtyping relation. This is the entry point to a decade of work on the *decidability* of asynchronous subtyping, which establishes one of Era IV's most surprising negative results.

The decidability story unfolds as follows. Bravetti, Carbone, Lange, Yoshida, and Zavattaro's "Undecidability of Asynchronous Session Subtyping" [Bravetti et al. 2017, arXiv 1611.05026] establishes that "the three notions of asynchronous subtyping defined so far for session types are all undecidable." This is a strong negative result: any sound and complete decision procedure for asynchronous session subtyping is impossible in the general case. The undecidability proof reduces a queue-machine halting problem to the subtyping question; the bounded-buffer asynchrony required for queue-machine simulation is precisely what the subtyping rules permit. Subsequent work [Bravetti et al. 2019, "On the Boundary between Decidability and Undecidability"] characterises decidable fragments by restricting the form of communication patterns that can be commutated.

Lange and Yoshida's "Verifying Asynchronous Interactions via Communicating Session Automata" [Lange & Yoshida 2019, CAV] introduces *k-multiparty compatibility* (k-MC), a strict superset of the synchronous multiparty compatibility of Deniélou and Yoshida [2013]. k-MC is decomposed into k-safety (within the bound k, all sent messages can be received and each automaton can move) and k-exhaustivity (all k-reachable send actions can fire within the bound). k-MC checking is PSPACE-complete. The k parameter is the buffer bound: at small k the property is more tractable; as k grows, the system approaches the undecidable general case but in practice k=1 or k=2 suffices for most session protocols [Lange & Yoshida 2019].

The decidability frontier is, for Prologos, mostly informational: the project's session-type infrastructure is designed for synchronous typing with deferred runtime, and the asynchronous fragment is on the deferred path. The relevant extracted lesson is structural rather than algorithmic: asynchronous session subtyping is undecidable in general, and the decidable fragments are characterised by *structural* restrictions (bounded buffers, partial commutativity restrictions) that have direct lattice-theoretic interpretation. The buffer-bound k is, in lattice terms, a height bound on the protocol-state Hasse diagram; k-safety and k-exhaustivity are, respectively, downward and upward closure conditions on that bounded lattice. The Prologos session-type stratum will inherit this structure when async runtime arrives.

The post-2019 literature on fair session subtyping [Bravetti et al. 2025], deconfined global types [Castagna et al. 2021], and the decidable fragments for asynchronous multiparty implementability [Bezem et al. 2023] continues the programme. None of it changes the basic Era IV picture: asynchronous session types are well-understood at the synchronous-multiparty level and well-mapped at the bounded-asynchronous level, with a sharp undecidability boundary in between.

### §5.4 Capability-based effect systems

The capability-based-effects track is the smallest of Era IV's threads but the most direct descendant of §4.5's Mark Miller / E-language / object-capability tradition. Brachthäuser and Schuster's Effekt language [Brachthäuser & Schuster 2017, "Effekt: Extensible Algebraic Effects in Scala," Scala Symposium] introduces effect handlers in Scala via a library implementation built on multi-prompt delimited continuations. The technical novelty is the choice to encode effects as *capabilities* — second-class function-like values that confer the right to invoke a particular effect operation — rather than as type-level effect rows in the Koka style.

The mature theory appears in two papers: "Effects as Capabilities: Effect Handlers and Lightweight Effect Polymorphism" [Brachthäuser, Schuster & Ostermann 2020, OOPSLA] and "Effekt: Capability-passing style for type- and effect-safe, extensible effect handlers in Scala" [Brachthäuser et al. 2020, JFP]. The OOPSLA paper makes the design claim explicit: "in Effekt, effect types express which capabilities a computation requires from its context. ... This new point in the design space of effect systems significantly simplifies the treatment of effect polymorphism and the related issues of effect parametricity and effect encapsulation" [Brachthäuser et al. 2020]. Crucially, "Effekt does not have language constructs for type-level effect variables or quantification over effects. Still, it supports effect polymorphic reuse of functions and guarantees effect safety" [Brachthäuser et al. 2020]. The mechanism is that "to guarantee effect safety, Effekt separates functions from values and treats all functions as second-class. The semantics of Effekt is defined as a translation to System Xi, a calculus in explicit capability-passing style" [Brachthäuser et al. 2020]. The translation makes capability-passing the operational basis: every effect operation is a method call on an explicit capability value; capability values cannot be stored in first-class data structures (the second-class restriction); and effect polymorphism becomes capability-set polymorphism, expressible without explicit effect variables.

The connection to Mark Miller's E-language tradition (§4.5) is structural and explicit. E's promise pipelining [Miller, Tribble & Shapiro 2005] is a particular instance of Effekt's capability-passing style: an unresolved promise is a capability to a future value, and pipelining is the same operation as capability composition. The deep claim is that effect handlers, capabilities, and promises are the same primitive viewed at different granularity. Effekt makes this the operational basis. The Prologos capability-hierarchy infrastructure (`capabilities.prologos`) is in this lineage; the architectural opportunity is to recognise effect handlers as capability-passing style under another name and to unify the two systems.

Compiling effect handlers in capability-passing style [Schuster, Brachthäuser & Ostermann 2020, ICFP] presents the reduction strategy: the type-and-effect system is translated into an explicit-capability λ-calculus, and the residual capability-passing program is compiled to a target without delimited continuations. This is the technical machinery that makes Effekt the most efficient effect-handler implementation that does not require runtime stack-segmentation support. For Prologos, the load-bearing observation is that capability-passing is *not* a performance hack: it is the natural operational semantics of effect handlers, in the same sense that propagator firing is the natural operational semantics of CCP's tell/ask discipline.

### §5.5 CALM, Bloom, BloomL: monotonicity-as-coordination-freedom

The §5.5 thread is, for Prologos's working hypothesis, the load-bearing one. It begins with Hellerstein's "The Declarative Imperative: Experiences and Conjectures in Distributed Logic" [Hellerstein 2010, SIGMOD Record], an extended version of his ACM PODS 2010 invited lecture, which introduces the **CALM conjecture** as Conjecture 1 of the paper:

> **CALM (Consistency And Logical Monotonicity).** A program has an eventually consistent, coordination-free execution strategy if and only if it is expressible in (monotonic Datalog).

The conjecture is the formal articulation of an experiential claim Hellerstein and his group had developed over seven years of building distributed systems in Datalog extensions: the parts of a system that compute *monotonically* (only ever growing, never shrinking) are coordination-free; the parts that compute *non-monotonically* (retraction, aggregation-with-defaults, negation-as-failure) require coordination. The conjecture is therefore a structural statement about the boundary between coordination-free and coordination-required computation, and the boundary is exactly the boundary between monotone and non-monotone fragments of Datalog [Hellerstein 2010]. The diagnostic is: imperative parallel programming fails because it fights monotonicity; declarative parallel programming succeeds because it embraces it.

Ameloot, Neven, and Van den Bussche's "Relational Transducers for Declarative Networking" [Ameloot et al. 2011, PODS] proves the conjecture in its first formal form. The paper "proposes a formal computation model for 'eventually consistent' distributed querying, based on relational transducers, motivated by a recent conjecture concerning the expressiveness of declarative networking" [Ameloot et al. 2011]. The proof formalises *coordination-freeness* in the relational-transducer model and shows that the class of *monotone queries* is exactly the class of coordination-free transducer networks. The journal version [Ameloot, Neven & Van den Bussche 2013, JACM] consolidates the result. Subsequent work [Ameloot et al. 2015, "Weaker Forms of Monotonicity for Declarative Networking," ACM TODS] refines the conjecture to characterise more subtle monotonicity classes.

The Bloom programming language [Alvaro et al. 2010, "Consistency Analysis in Bloom: a CALM and Collected Approach"] is the operational realisation of CALM: a Datalog-derived declarative-networking language in which the CALM analysis is built into the type system. Programs in Bloom that pass the monotonicity check are statically guaranteed to be eventually consistent without coordination. The Berkeley group's subsequent BloomL paper [Conway, Marczak, Alvaro, Hellerstein & Maier 2012, SOCC] is the load-bearing one for Prologos: it generalises Bloom to support arbitrary lattices, not just sets-under-union.

The BloomL contribution is summarised in the paper's abstract: "BloomL extends Bloom to support efficient evaluation of lattice-based code" and "encourages the safe composition of small, easy-to-analyse lattices into larger programs" [Conway et al. 2012]. The technical machinery is direct: a BloomL program declares its state as a collection of lattice-valued cells (where a "lattice" is required to be a join-semilattice with a specified bot, a specified join, and a specified set of monotone functions), and computation is constrained to monotone functions over those lattices. The CALM analysis lifts to the lattice setting: a BloomL program that uses only monotone functions is statically guaranteed to be eventually consistent and coordination-free [Conway et al. 2012]. Cross-lattice morphisms compose: a monotone function from lattice A to lattice B, composed with a monotone function from B to C, is a monotone function from A to C, and the composite computation remains in the CALM-monotone fragment.

The paper's case study is "a distributed key-value store similar to Amazon Dynamo," which "supports eventual consistency, object versioning using vector clocks, and quorum replication" [Conway et al. 2012]. The Dynamo example is structurally important because vector clocks are themselves a lattice (the join-semilattice of partial orders on event identifiers), and quorum replication is a monotone aggregation (the count of acknowledgements only grows). BloomL is therefore not a proof-of-concept of CALM in the lattice setting; it is a working operationalisation in a non-trivial industrial-quality target.

The connection to Prologos's propagator substrate is not merely analogical: it is structural. A propagator network with monotone merge functions on its cell values is exactly a BloomL program viewed through a different surface syntax. The CALM theorem, applied to such a network, says: the network's S0 stratum is coordination-free in the precise CALM sense, because S0 is by construction monotone-only. The Prologos stratification design (§S0 monotone, S(-1) retraction, S1 NAF, topology) is a refinement of the CALM picture: each stratum is internally CALM-monotone, the stratum boundaries are the points where coordination is needed, and the stratification minimises the coordination footprint by isolating non-monotone work to identifiable strata. This is exactly the structure Hellerstein conjectured and Ameloot et al. proved.

The deeper claim — that the Prologos working hypothesis ("asynchrony is the operational semantics of computation in a quantale-enriched category over a propagator substrate") is a quantale-enriched generalisation of CALM/BloomL — relies on the observation that join-semilattices are commutative idempotent monoids, that quantales are sup-preserving lattices with an additional monoidal structure (the quantale's "and"), and that the additional structure is exactly what is needed to internalise *cost*, *order*, and *resource* as algebraic operations on the same lattice. BloomL's join-only setting is the ⊥-quantale (boolean) special case; the tropical (min, +) quantale on which Prologos's cost system runs (PPN 4C addendum) is a different special case; the general case is an open frontier (§6.3, §8). The §5.5 evidence is direct support for this construction's existence: a non-trivial lattice-based distributed-programming model already exists, with an industrial-quality implementation, and the CALM theorem connects it to coordination-freedom — and therefore to asynchrony — at the structural level.

The connection back to §3.3 CCP is also direct. Saraswat's [1993] cc(D) is a closure-operator semantics on a constraint lattice; tell adds information, ask blocks until information is sufficient, and the network of agents is a fixpoint computation on the lattice. BloomL is, in essence, cc(D) with explicit lattice values rather than constraint-system values, and with the CALM theorem replacing Saraswat's logical-correctness arguments. Reading the two together, the picture is: Saraswat formalised the lattice-based concurrent model; Hellerstein and Conway operationalised it; the quantale-enriched generalisation (§6.3) is the next step.

### §5.6 The diagnostic admission

Era IV does not contain a single load-bearing paper that frames itself as a retraction of Era III's async/await discipline. The retraction is implicit in the structure of the contributions: SE-0304's appeal to a structured discipline is a tacit admission that SE-0296's async/await is insufficient; Sivaramakrishnan et al.'s effect-handler retrofit is a tacit admission that OCaml's prior monadic-async libraries were the wrong primitive; Conway et al.'s BloomL is a tacit admission that the imperative-parallel-programming tradition was on the wrong side of the CALM boundary. The diagnostic is consistent across the five threads:

1. Async/await without structured concurrency is unsafe (§5.1).
2. Async/await as a primitive is the wrong abstraction; effect handlers are a more general primitive of which async/await is one instance (§5.2).
3. Asynchronous session types are tractable in bounded fragments and undecidable in general; the decidable boundary is structural (§5.3).
4. Effect handlers as capabilities recover the object-capability tradition; capability-passing is the natural operational semantics of effects (§5.4).
5. Coordination-free distributed evaluation is exactly the monotone-Datalog fragment, which lifts to the lattice setting via BloomL (§5.5).

The five claims do not contradict; they reinforce. The Era IV reading is that the field has converged, by independent contributions across multiple research traditions, on a small set of structural commitments: scope is mandatory, ordering should be derived from dataflow rather than imposed by syntax, effects should be a unified algebraic primitive rather than a sequence of bolt-on language features, distributed coordination should be statically analysable from the lattice structure of state, and capabilities should be the operational semantics of access. Each of these commitments has direct correspondents in the Prologos design as inherited; Era IV is therefore not a critique of the project's direction but a body of evidence that the direction is structurally right and that the field is converging on it.

What Era IV does *not* yet accomplish is the unification: no Era IV paper makes the case that scope, ordering, effects, coordination, and capability are projections of a single quantale-enriched-categorical structure. The unification is the frontier work of §6.3 and the synthesis claim of §8. The §5 evidence is the precondition for that claim, not the claim itself.

---

## References

### Structured concurrency (§5.1)

**Smith, N. J. (2018).** "Notes on structured concurrency, or: Go statement considered harmful." Personal blog post, 25 April 2018. Available at https://vorpus.org/blog/notes-on-structured-concurrency-or-go-statement-considered-harmful/. The load-bearing diagnostic document of the structured-concurrency turn. Argues by direct analogy to Dijkstra (1968) that unrestricted task-spawn primitives violate the black-box rule of structured programming. Introduces the nursery as the structural primitive that restores it, with level-triggered cancellation as a non-negotiable correctness condition. Companion: Smith, "Timeouts and cancellation for humans," https://vorpus.org/blog/timeouts-and-cancellation-for-humans/.

**Trio Project (Smith et al., 2017+).** Trio Python library, structured-concurrency-first async I/O. Documentation at https://trio.readthedocs.io/. Reference-core documentation describes the `Nursery` and `CancelScope` primitives and the level-triggered cancellation discipline. The first language environment to implement Smith's prescription end-to-end.

**Elizarov, R. (2018).** "Structured concurrency." Medium post, October 2018. https://elizarov.medium.com/structured-concurrency-722d765aa952. Announces `kotlinx.coroutines 0.26.0` and the introduction of `CoroutineScope` as the structural primitive in Kotlin. Frames the change as "an ideology shift" and prescribes that "a simple `launch { … }` is easy to write, but it is not what you should be writing."

**Elizarov, R. (2019).** "Structured concurrency anniversary." Medium post, October 2019. https://elizarov.medium.com/structured-concurrency-anniversary-f2cc748b2401. Retrospective on the first year of structured-concurrency-by-default Kotlin coroutines. Acknowledges convergent evolution with Trio.

**Swift Evolution Proposal SE-0296 (2020).** "Async/await." https://github.com/swiftlang/swift-evolution/blob/main/proposals/0296-async-await.md. The async/await primitive without structured concurrency. SE-0304 is the structural correction.

**Swift Evolution Proposal SE-0304 (2021).** "Structured Concurrency." Authors: Doug Gregor, John McCall, Joe Groff, Konrad Malawski, Philippe Hausler. Four review rounds, March–July 2021. Accepted with modifications. https://github.com/swiftlang/swift-evolution/blob/main/proposals/0304-structured-concurrency.md. Introduces `Task`, `TaskGroup`, child-task lifetime bounding, and the requirement that "a function that creates a child task must wait for it to end before returning."

**Swift Evolution Proposal SE-0306 (2021).** "Actors." https://github.com/swiftlang/swift-evolution/blob/main/proposals/0306-actors.md. Introduces actor isolation as the data-race-safe concurrent-state primitive. References Erlang, Akka, and Orleans; does not cite Hewitt directly. Reentrant by default to avoid Akka-style deadlock cascades.

**Swift Evolution Proposal SE-0316 (2021).** "Global actors." https://github.com/swiftlang/swift-evolution/blob/main/proposals/0316-global-actors.md. Introduces `@MainActor` and the global-actor type-annotation discipline.

**Swift Evolution Proposal SE-0317 (2021).** "async let." https://github.com/swiftlang/swift-evolution/blob/main/proposals/0317-async-let.md. Sugar over single-child task groups.

**JEP 425 (2022).** "Virtual Threads (Preview)." JDK 19. https://openjdk.org/jeps/425. Initial preview of M:N user-mode threads for the JVM.

**JEP 444 (2023).** "Virtual Threads." JDK 21 final. https://openjdk.org/jeps/444. Finalises virtual threads. Authors: Ron Pressler, Alan Bateman, et al. Motivation: the platform-thread cost prevents thread-per-request architectures at scale; virtual threads cost order-of-magnitude less memory.

**JEP 437 (2022), JEP 453 (2023), JEP 462 (2024).** "Structured Concurrency" (incubator, first preview, second preview). https://openjdk.org/jeps/462. Introduces `StructuredTaskScope`, `ShutdownOnFailure`, `ShutdownOnSuccess`. Continues through JEPs 505, 525, 533. The seven-iteration preview cycle is itself evidence that retrofitting structured concurrency is hard.

**Pike, R. (1989).** "The Implementation of Newsqueak." AT&T Bell Laboratories Computing Science Technical Report 143. https://swtch.com/~rsc/thread/newsquimpl.pdf. The CSP-with-first-class-channels heritage that becomes Limbo and then Go.

**Cox, R. (2008+).** "Bell Labs and CSP Threads." https://swtch.com/~rsc/thread/. The Bell-Labs lineage of concurrent-programming languages: Newsqueak → Alef → Limbo → Go.

### Algebraic effects (§5.2)

**Plotkin, G. & Power, J. (2002).** "Notions of computation determine monads." FoSSaCS 2002, LNCS 2303, pp. 342–356. https://homepages.inf.ed.ac.uk/gdp/publications/Comp_Eff_Monads.pdf. The foundational paper of the modern algebraic-effects programme. Inverts Moggi's account: an algebraic theory determines its monad as the free algebra over the theory.

**Plotkin, G. & Pretnar, M. (2009).** "Handlers of Algebraic Effects." ESOP 2009, LNCS 5502, pp. 80–94. https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-642-00590-9_7. Introduces handlers as homomorphisms from the free algebra to a target algebra. Treats nondeterminism, I/O, concurrency, state, time, and combinations.

**Plotkin, G. & Pretnar, M. (2013).** "Handling Algebraic Effects." Logical Methods in Computer Science. The journal version of the ESOP 2009 paper, with local algebraic effect theories.

**Bauer, A. & Pretnar, M. (2013).** "Programming with Algebraic Effects and Handlers." Journal of Logical and Algebraic Methods in Programming 84(1):108–123. arXiv:1203.1539. https://arxiv.org/abs/1203.1539. The Eff language. Demonstrates that cooperative multi-threading is just a handler, with fork/yield/await as effect operations.

**Lindley, S., McBride, C. & McLaughlin, C. (2017).** "Do be do be do." POPL 2017. https://arxiv.org/abs/1611.09259. The Frank language: bidirectional effect typing, multihandlers, and the "function is a degenerate handler" claim. Effect polymorphism without explicit effect variables.

**Leijen, D. (2014).** "Koka: Programming with Row Polymorphic Effect Types." MSFP 2014, EPTCS 153, pp. 100–126. arXiv:1406.2061. https://arxiv.org/abs/1406.2061. Introduces row-polymorphic effects with duplicate labels.

**Leijen, D. (2017).** "Type Directed Compilation of Row-Typed Algebraic Effects." POPL 2017, pp. 486–499. https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/wp-content/uploads/2016/12/algeff.pdf. The selective-CPS compilation strategy that makes Koka efficient on JavaScript, JVM, .NET.

**Xie, N. & Leijen, D. (2020).** "Effect Handlers in Haskell, Evidently." Haskell Symposium 2020. https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/wp-content/uploads/2020/07/effev.pdf. Evidence-passing implementation as a Haskell library.

**Xie, N. & Leijen, D. (2021).** "Generalized Evidence Passing for Effect Handlers." ICFP 2021, Proc. ACM Program. Lang. 5(ICFP):71. https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3473576. Multi-prompt delimited control, generalised evidence passing, yield bubbling, and a final monadic translation. C backend competitive with Multicore OCaml.

**Sivaramakrishnan, K. C., Dolan, S., White, L., Kelly, T., Jaffer, S. & Madhavapeddy, A. (2021).** "Retrofitting Effect Handlers onto OCaml." PLDI 2021. https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3453483.3454039. The industrial closing act of Era IV's algebraic-effects track. Effect handlers in OCaml 5 (released 2022); 1% mean overhead on the macro suite. fork/yield/await/generators/coroutines all reduce to one primitive.

**Ahman, D. & Pretnar, M. (2021).** "Asynchronous Effects." POPL 2021. arXiv:2003.02110. https://arxiv.org/abs/2003.02110. The canonical algebraic account of async/await: signal/interrupt as the two-phase decoupling, handlers as schedulers. The calculus λ_ae.

**Schuster, P., Brachthäuser, J. & Ostermann, K. (2020).** "Compiling effect handlers in capability-passing style." ICFP 2020, Proc. ACM Program. Lang. 4(ICFP):93. https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3408975. The capability-passing compilation strategy.

**OCaml 5.0 release (2022).** Multicore OCaml with effect handlers. https://ocaml.org/manual/5.0/effects.html. "User-level thread schedulers themselves expressed as OCaml libraries."

### Asynchronous session types (§5.3)

**Honda, K., Yoshida, N. & Carbone, M. (2008).** "Multiparty Asynchronous Session Types." POPL 2008, pp. 273–284. https://doi.org/10.1145/1328438.1328472. Multiparty session types with global types and endpoint projections.

**Honda, K., Yoshida, N. & Carbone, M. (2016).** "Multiparty Asynchronous Session Types." JACM 63(1). https://mrg.cs.ox.ac.uk/publications/multiparty-asynchronous-session-types-jacm/jacm.pdf. Journal version.

**Mostrous, D., Yoshida, N. & Honda, K. (2009).** "Global Principal Typing in Partially Commutative Asynchronous Sessions." ESOP 2009. https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-642-00590-9_23. Asynchronous communication subtyping with partial action commutativity.

**Bravetti, M., Carbone, M., Lange, J., Yoshida, N. & Zavattaro, G. (2017).** "Undecidability of Asynchronous Session Subtyping." arXiv:1611.05026. https://arxiv.org/abs/1611.05026. Establishes undecidability of the three principal asynchronous-subtyping definitions.

**Bravetti, M., Carbone, M. & Zavattaro, G. (2017).** "On the Boundary between Decidability and Undecidability of Asynchronous Session Subtyping." arXiv:1703.00659. https://arxiv.org/abs/1703.00659. Decidable fragment characterisation.

**Lange, J. & Yoshida, N. (2019).** "Verifying Asynchronous Interactions via Communicating Session Automata." CAV 2019. https://arxiv.org/abs/1901.09606. k-multiparty compatibility, k-safety, k-exhaustivity. PSPACE-complete checking.

**Castagna, G., Padovani, L. & Coppo, M. (various).** Foundational work on session types for π-calculus and beyond. Cited collectively for the synchronous-multiparty heritage.

### Capability-based effects (§5.4)

**Brachthäuser, J. I. & Schuster, P. (2017).** "Effekt: Extensible Algebraic Effects in Scala." Scala Symposium 2017. The original Effekt library design.

**Brachthäuser, J. I., Schuster, P. & Ostermann, K. (2020).** "Effects as Capabilities: Effect Handlers and Lightweight Effect Polymorphism." OOPSLA 2020, Proc. ACM Program. Lang. 4(OOPSLA):126. https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3428194. The mature theory: effects as capabilities, second-class function discipline, capability-set polymorphism without effect variables.

**Brachthäuser, J. I., Schuster, P. & Ostermann, K. (2020).** "Effekt: Capability-passing style for type- and effect-safe, extensible effect handlers in Scala." Journal of Functional Programming 30:e8. https://doi.org/10.1017/S0956796820000027. The journal version, with the System Xi capability-passing translation.

**Schuster, P., Brachthäuser, J. I. & Ostermann, K. (2022).** "What You See Is What You Get: Practical Effect Handlers in Capability-Passing Style." POPL 2022 / OOPSLA. The practical-language follow-up.

### CALM, Bloom, BloomL (§5.5)

**Hellerstein, J. M. (2010).** "The Declarative Imperative: Experiences and Conjectures in Distributed Logic." SIGMOD Record 39(1):5–19. https://dsf.berkeley.edu/papers/sigrec10-declimperative.pdf. The CALM conjecture statement. Extended version of the PODS 2010 invited lecture.

**Ameloot, T. J., Neven, F. & Van den Bussche, J. (2011).** "Relational Transducers for Declarative Networking." PODS 2011, pp. 283–292. arXiv:1012.2858. https://arxiv.org/abs/1012.2858. The first formal proof of CALM in the relational-transducer model.

**Ameloot, T. J., Neven, F. & Van den Bussche, J. (2013).** "Relational transducers for declarative networking." JACM 60(2). https://doi.org/10.1145/2450142.2450151. Journal version.

**Ameloot, T. J. et al. (2015).** "Weaker Forms of Monotonicity for Declarative Networking: A More Fine-Grained Answer to the CALM-Conjecture." ACM TODS 40(4). https://doi.org/10.1145/2809784. Refinement of the conjecture to characterise more subtle monotonicity classes.

**Alvaro, P., Conway, N., Hellerstein, J. M. & Marczak, W. R. (2010).** "Consistency Analysis in Bloom: a CALM and Collected Approach." CIDR 2011 / SOCC 2011 work. The Bloom language operational realisation of CALM.

**Conway, N., Marczak, W. R., Alvaro, P., Hellerstein, J. M. & Maier, D. (2012).** "Logic and Lattices for Distributed Programming." SOCC 2012, Article 1. https://www.neilconway.org/docs/socc2012_bloom_lattices.pdf. **The load-bearing paper for Prologos.** BloomL: lattice-extended Bloom. Cross-lattice morphisms compose. CALM analysis lifts to the lattice setting. Dynamo-style key-value store case study with vector clocks (lattice of partial orders) and quorum replication (monotone aggregation).

**Hellerstein, J. M. & Alvaro, P. (2019).** "Keeping CALM: When Distributed Consistency is Easy." Communications of the ACM 63(9):72–81. arXiv:1901.01930. https://arxiv.org/abs/1901.01930. The retrospective on a decade of CALM, with the formal theorem proven.

### Cross-references to neighbouring slices

- **§3.2 (game semantics)** — Plotkin & Power [2002] uses enriched-categorical structure that connects with the strategy-based semantics of §3.2. The Lawvere-theory framing in §5.2 is dual to the linear-logic framing of game semantics.
- **§3.3 (CCP)** — BloomL is essentially Saraswat's cc(D) with explicit lattices instead of constraint systems. The CALM theorem lifts cc(D)'s closure-operator semantics to the distributed setting.
- **§4.3 (function coloring)** — the structured-concurrency turn of §5.1 is the response to function coloring; the algebraic-effects turn of §5.2 is the response to async/await as a privileged primitive.
- **§4.5 (Mark Miller, capabilities)** — Effekt (§5.4) is the direct technical descendant. Capability-passing style is the operational form of the object-capability tradition.
- **§6.3 (frontier quantale-effect-handler work)** — Ahman & Pretnar's Asynchronous Effects (§5.2) provides the algebraic basis; the quantale-enriched generalisation is the §6.3 frontier.
- **Synthesis §8.4 (CALM and the propagator substrate)** — leans on §5.5 BloomL.
- **Synthesis §8.6 (cost/order/fairness/resource as projections)** — leans on §5.2 algebraic effects.

---

## §6 Era V — The Frontier (2020-2026): Choreography, Quantale-Enriched Concurrency, Effect-Handler Categorification, Tropical Reactivity

This section surveys frontier research bearing on the working hypothesis that asynchrony is the operational semantics of computation in a quantale-enriched category over a propagator substrate. It covers (i) the choreographic-programming wave that unifies a centralized program with provable endpoint projection (§6.1); (ii) recent (post-2020) quantale-based concurrent semantics, including the still-incipient program of quantale-valued logics for coalgebraic concurrency (§6.3); (iii) the categorification of algebraic effect handlers along fibrational, parameterized-algebraic, and parallel lines (§6.4); (iv) tropical / min-plus structures in scheduling, networking, and adjacent quantitative-program-semantics work (§6.5); and (v) explicit cataloguing of what the frontier has not yet produced (§6.6) — gaps that bear on the substrate hypothesis without arguing it. Era-V coverage is restricted to peer-reviewed-or-accepted papers and credible preprints from 2020 onward, except where surveying continuous research programs (notably Melliès' tensorial-logic / dialogue-category program and the Cohen-Gaubert-Quadrat tropical lineage) demands a longer arc. Section §6.2 (Spritely / OCapN revival) is covered separately and is referenced where relevant.

### §6.1 Choreographic Programming as Frontier Asynchrony

#### §6.1.1 Core Choreographies and the Montesi School

The choreographic-programming research program, anchored at the University of Southern Denmark under Fabrizio Montesi, took definitive shape with Cruz-Filipe and Montesi's "A Core Model for Choreographic Programming" (TCS 2020; conference version CompoSER 2017) [CFM20]. The work introduces *Core Choreographies* (CC), a minimal calculus capturing the essential primitives of choreographic programming: a choreography is a single global program that describes a system of communicating processes from a holistic viewpoint, and *endpoint projection* (EPP) automatically synthesises a process implementation for each role, with deadlock-freedom as a structural-by-construction guarantee. Cruz-Filipe and Montesi prove that every computable function can be implemented as a CC choreography, from which a process implementation can be synthesised that runs independent computations in parallel — a Turing-completeness result for the core paradigm, and a positioning of CC as the canonical model for choreographic programming.

The Montesi school subsequently developed a more recent and comprehensive treatment in "A Formal Theory of Choreographic Programming" (Journal of Automated Reasoning 2023) [CFM23], establishing endpoint-projection soundness and completeness in a Coq-mechanised setting. The associated textbook "Introduction to Choreographies" (Cambridge University Press 2023) [Mon23] consolidates the field's pedagogical foundation. Choral, the object-oriented choreographic language compiling to Java libraries, was first published in 2020 and updated in TOPLAS 2024 as "Choral: Object-oriented Choreographic Programming" [GMP24]. Choral is to date the only choreographic language with full higher-order parameters and decentralised data structures suitable for production-scale Java integration.

#### §6.1.2 Pirouette and the Higher-Order Functional Wave

Hirsch and Garg's "Pirouette: Higher-Order Typed Functional Choreographies" (POPL 2022) [HG22] is the inflection point that pulls choreographic programming into the typed-functional-language community. Pirouette is the first language for *typed higher-order* functional choreographic programming, parametric in a *local* language of messages and lifting message-type-soundness guarantees to the choreography level. Critically, message type soundness implies deadlock-freedom — the type system's correctness is the protocol's correctness. The development is mechanised in Coq (the entire metatheory).

Pirouette opened the door to a flurry of functional-choreographic work. Cruz-Filipe, Graversen, Lugovic, Montesi, and Peressotti's "Functional Choreographic Programming" (ICTAC 2022) [CGL+22] independently introduced *Chorλ*, a λ-calculus-based choreographic language tracking which roles are involved in each term (a *located-types* discipline). HasChor (Shen, Kashiwa, Kuper; ICFP 2023) [SKK23] presents choreographic programming as a Haskell monad — choreographies are computations in a monad supporting higher-order and location-polymorphic forms; the library reduces endpoint projection to its short essential core via Haskell's algebraic-type machinery. The 2023-2025 wave continued with ChoRus (Shen et al., CP 2024) — a Rust library-level choreographic programming framework with first-class location sets [Shen24] — and the Plyukhin-Peressotti-Montesi "Ozone: Fully Out-of-Order Choreographies" (ECOOP 2024) [PPM24], introducing futures-based non-blocking communication while preserving communication-integrity-violation freedom.

The most recent significant paper, Samuelson-Hirsch-Cecchetti's "Choreographic Quick Changes: First-Class Location (Set) Polymorphism" (OOPSLA 2025; arXiv 2506.10913) [SHC25], introduces λ_QC — the first typed choreographic language with first-class process names and polymorphism over both types and *sets of locations*. It supports algebraic and recursive data types as well as multiply-located values, and is mechanised in Rocq with deadlock-freedom guarantees. Lam, Hirsch, and Cecchetti's "We Know I Know You Know" (arXiv 2403.05417, 2024) [LHC24] develops choreographic programming with multicast and multiply-located values, addressing common-knowledge structure between roles.

A modal-type-theoretic interpretation appears in Bak and Urschumzew's "Choreographic Programming in Modal Type Theory" (CP 2024 talk) [BU24], which represents location annotations as modalities — a translation of Chorλ programs into terms of MTT requiring two novel concepts (common knowledge between roles, and locally-referenced choreographies). The conjecture is that all of Chorλ can be faithfully represented in MTT, making choreography a fragment of multimodal dependent type theory.

#### §6.1.3 Empirical Validation: Real-World Protocols

Lugović and Montesi's "Real-World Choreographic Programming: Full-Duplex Asynchrony and Interoperability" (Programming Journal 8(2):8, 2024; arXiv 2303.03983) [LM24] provides the first empirical evidence that choreographic programming can faithfully codify a widespread real-world protocol: the Internet Relay Chat (IRC) client-server protocol, implemented in Choral and tested against publicly-available IRC conformance tests. Two Choral features prove load-bearing: higher-order choreographies (modelling complex IRC interaction patterns under asynchrony) and user-definable communication semantics (achieving interoperability with third-party implementations). This is the first demonstration that the paradigm scales from formal toy examples to production protocols "in the wild."

#### §6.1.4 The Choreography-as-Quantale Connection

The substrate question for choreographic programming is whether endpoint projection is a *quantale-enriched* construction. The intuition runs as follows: a choreography has a *cost-of-projection* — message counts, latency, fairness obligations — that should be assigned to each projected endpoint compositionally. Multiparty session types capture causality of message exchanges (Honda-Yoshida-Carbone style); choreography-as-EPP can be viewed dually as a *projection morphism* from a global protocol type into per-participant local protocol types. The composition of EPP across nested choreographies (higher-order, location-polymorphic) is naturally graded by a cost monoid (length of communication trace, deepest causal depth, or worst-case latency under min-plus). No published frontier paper has explicitly written down "choreographic projection as a quantale-enriched morphism" — Pirouette's metatheory and Chorλ's typing rules treat correctness, not cost, and HasChor / ChoRus / Choral's library implementations are programming-engineered rather than denotationally framed in quantale terms. This is gap (a) in §6.6.

The closest connection through the literature passes through *causality structure*. Castellani, Dezani-Ciancaglini, Padovani, and Pérès' "Global Types and Event Structure Semantics for Asynchronous Multiparty Sessions" (Fundamenta Informaticae 2024) [CDP+24] interprets asynchronous multiparty sessions as Flow Event Structures, with associated asynchronous types reflecting asynchrony "more directly and more precisely than standard global types." Event structures are a coalgebraic concurrency model with monotone consistency relations — the bridge to a quantale-enriched view is through the event-structure-as-cost-presheaf perspective, not yet developed. The PSPACE-completeness result for asynchronous multiparty session type implementability (Stutz et al., ECOOP 2023; "Complete Multiparty Session Type Projection with Automata," CAV 2023) [Stu23, LSS+23] sets the decidability frontier for projection — quantale-graded variants remain open.

### §6.3 Quantale-Based Concurrent Semantics: Recent Academic Work (Post-2020)

#### §6.3.1 The Quantale-Enriched Logic Frontier

The most active recent academic frontier connecting quantales to concurrency runs through *quantale-valued coalgebraic logic*. Beohar, Gurke, König, Messing, Forster, Schröder, and Wild's "Expressive Quantale-Valued Logics for Coalgebras: An Adjunction-Based Approach" (STACS 2024) [BGK+24] and the precursor "Quantitative Hennessy-Milner Theorems via Notions of Density" (CSL 2023) [BGK+23] establish a coalgebraic-generality framework for quantitative behavioural logics, parametric in a quantale that abstracts over the value-type of distances and truth values. The framework subsumes the two-valued case (ordinary equivalence), pseudometric/ultrametric-valued cases, and probabilistic-pseudometric variants under one coalgebraic theorem-schema. Hennessy-Milner-style expressiveness theorems are obtained as corollaries to a fixpoint preservation property along *Galois connections* between suitable quantale-valued lattices, instantiated to the coalgebraic setting.

This work continues the program initiated by Wild and Schröder, by Goncharov, Hofmann, Nora, Schröder, and Wild on "Kantorovich Functors and Characteristic Logics for Behavioural Distances" (FoSSaCS 2023) [GHN+23]. The Kantorovich-functor lifting framework and adjunction-based density approach are the closest existing technology to a "quantale-as-cost-substrate-for-concurrent-process-semantics." However — and this is critical for the substrate hypothesis — these papers operate on coalgebras of behaviour-functors (transition systems, weighted transition systems), not on *propagator networks* qua independent computational substrate. The propagator-network presentation is not addressed in this literature and is gap (b) in §6.6.

#### §6.3.2 Recent CCP-Quantale Connections

The post-2020 literature on concurrent constraint programming (CCP) has been quieter than the 1990s-2010s peak (Saraswat-Rinard-Panangaden, Aristizábal-Bonchi-Pino-Valencia). Ceragioli, Gadducci, Lomurno, and Tedeschi's "Effect Semantics for Quantum Process Calculi" (CONCUR 2024) [CGL+24] provides a 2024 anchor on the broader quantale-concurrency connection — quantum process calculi are interpreted in an effect-monoid framework where the underlying lattice of states forms a quantale-enriched structure. While not a direct CCP paper, the effect-semantics framing follows the Saraswat lineage of *processes as information transducers over a constraint lattice*.

The classical result that determinate CCP forms a hyperdoctrine (Panangaden-Saraswat-Scott-Seely 1993; revisited by López-Acosta and Valencia in "Soft Concurrent Constraint Programming with Local Variables," 2022 [LAV22]) remains the tightest categorical formulation. Hyperdoctrines are categorical first-order logic structures over cylindric algebras, and *quantale modules* generalise this in the direction of resource-graded constraint systems (Gonda's thesis "Resource Theories as Quantale Modules" [Gon21] frames resource theories as quantale-module actions on symmetric monoidal categories). Soft CCP — where constraints carry semiring-valued degrees rather than Boolean entailment — is formally a *quantale-graded* CCP, but published 2022-2024 work treats this primarily as a programming-language-design question (operational semantics, expressiveness) rather than as an investigation of the underlying quantale algebra. The full CCP-as-quantale-enriched-cc(D) formalisation remains open and is gap (c) in §6.6.

#### §6.3.3 Quantitative Process Algebra and Bisimulation

The Fahrenberg-Legay-Thrane lineage on quantitative bisimulation distances [FLT11] continued through the Bauer-Fahrenberg, Fahrenberg-Larsen-Legay-Thrane work on weighted modal transition systems and quantitative branching distances [BFL+12, FLT14]. The 2022 generic-framework paper "A Generic Approach to Quantitative Verification" (arXiv 2204.11302) [FL22] consolidates these into a uniform fixpoint setup for quantitative bisimulation that is parametric in a hemimetric / quantale structure. Castiglioni, Gebler, and Tini's later work on quantitative process equivalences is the closest 2020s extension. None of this work explicitly identifies the propagator-network substrate as the operational realisation of quantale-enriched bisimulation, despite the formal kinship.

#### §6.3.4 Probabilistic Quantale Semantics

The Mislove-Worrell-Pavlovic-Ouaknine line on probabilistic process semantics (originating in "Duality for Labelled Markov Processes" 2004; "Testing Semantics: Connecting Processes and Process Logics" 2006) has been recently complemented by Goncharov-Hofmann-Nora-Schröder-Wild on Kantorovich-functor-based behavioural distances. Forster, Hofmann, Schröder, and Wild's "Isomorphism Theorems between Models of Mixed Choice (Revised)" (arXiv 2411.13500) [FHS+24] connects quantale-enriched and probabilistic models of mixed choice, situating probabilistic-quantale semantics within the recent coalgebraic-quantitative wave.

#### §6.3.5 Bonchi School: Diagrammatic and Coalgebraic Process Semantics

Bonchi and collaborators (Piedeleu, Sobociński, Zanasi) continue developing bialgebraic and string-diagrammatic process semantics, with "Bialgebraic Foundations for the Operational Semantics of String Diagrams" (Information and Computation, 2021) [BPS+21] and CALCO 2025 contributions on tape diagrams for monoidal monads. Bonchi's "Effectful Mealy Machines: Coalgebraic and Causal Traces" (LiCS 2025 invited) [Bon25] gives effectful Mealy machines a bisimilarity-and-trace semantics characterised syntactically via free uniform feedback, with traces in a novel semantic coinductive universe of effectful streams. This is a direct frontier example of *causal-traced concurrency* that could in principle be quantale-graded but is not so framed in the published version.

### §6.4 Effect Handlers as Fibrations / Quantale Projections

#### §6.4.1 Fibrational Categorical Semantics

Plotkin and Pretnar's algebraic effect handlers, combined with Plotkin and Power's algebraic effects, form the foundation; the fibrational lift was articulated by Ahman in "Handling Fibred Algebraic Effects" (POPL 2018) [Ahm18], with continued development in the dependent-type-theoretic setting through "Dependent Types and Fibred Computational Effects" (Ahman-Ghani-Plotkin, FoSSaCS 2016) [AGP16].

The 2023-2024 frontier paper Dagnino, Pasquali, and Streicher's "A Fibrational Tale of Operational Logical Relations: Pure, Effectful and Differential" (LMCS 20(2):1, 2024; arXiv 2303.03271) [DPS24] establishes operational logical relations *uniformly* via fibrations, applied to a λ-calculus with generic effects. The paper introduces *operational structures* (cartesian categories with monadic-evaluation arrows and coherence conditions encoding base evaluation dynamics) and *logical fibrations* over them, with a fundamental lemma subsuming many concrete instances. This is the most categorically-formalised account of effectful program-semantic logical relations to date, and the framework is parametric — different effect monads instantiate different operational structures, with fibrational uniformity preserved.

Wood and Atkey's "A Framework for Higher-Order Effects and Handlers" (Science of Computer Programming 233:103086, 2024) [WA24] consolidates the higher-order effect-handler formalism (continuing Wu-Schrijvers-Hinze's "Effect Handlers in Scope," Haskell 2014) into a unified categorical framework, directly addressing scoped/parameterised effect operations that don't fit purely-algebraic monad structure. Lindley, Matache, Moss, Staton, Wu, and Yang's "Scoped Effects as Parameterized Algebraic Theories" (ESOP 2024) [LMM+24] gives scoped effects a parameterised algebraic theory — algebraic theories where operations carry parameters from a separate parameter category — restoring algebraic-effect machinery to scoped effects. Bach Poulsen and van der Rest's "Hefty Algebras: Modular Elaboration of Higher-Order Algebraic Effects" (POPL 2023) [BPV23] provides modular elaboration of higher-order effects into first-order algebraic effects + handlers.

#### §6.4.2 Parallel and Substructural Effect Handlers

Xie, Johnson, Maclaurin, and Paszke's "Parallel Algebraic Effect Handlers" (ICFP 2024) [XJM+24] introduces λp, the first lambda calculus combining effect handlers with explicit parallel computations (a `for` expression inspired by Dex). The accompanying Fp polymorphic value-dependent type system preserves λp semantics while ensuring soundness, and the Haskell implementation reveals connections to *free applicative functors*. This is the first study of user-defined effect handlers + parallel computations and bears directly on the propagator-substrate hypothesis: parallel handlers are algebraically a *commutative* fragment of the handler monoid, and free-applicative connection suggests a polynomial-functor view (Joyal-Kock-Melliès lineage).

Brachthäuser, Schuster, and Ostermann's "Capabilities for Control" (ICFP 2024) [BSO24] develops a capability-based effect-system foundation where effects are first-class capabilities, generalising prior work (effekt language). On the substructural side, the POPL 2024 paper "Affect: An Affine Type and Effect System" [Aff25] (POPL 2025) provides an affine-resource effect system with effect handlers — handlers consume capabilities affinely, enabling deterministic resource accounting. Sergey-Devriese-Patrignani-Piessens' line on *secure compilation of effect handlers* continues at the periphery of optimisation; the specific 2024 paper appears in preparation but is not yet a peer-reviewed citation as of the current bibliography. Lanckriet, Piessens, and Devriese's "SoK: Robust Properties, Robust Abstractions and Back-Translations" (2026, JCS) [LPD26] addresses fully-abstract compilation broadly and is adjacent to handler-optimisation.

#### §6.4.3 Iris-Based Logics for Effect Handlers

Tian, Madiot, and Pottier's "Formal Semantics and Program Logics for a Fragment of OCaml" (ICFP 2025) [TMP25] gives OCaml-with-effect-handlers a monadic-interpreter dynamic semantics plus a stateless Hoare logic for pure programs and an Iris-based separation logic for arbitrary programs. The Iris framework already supports concurrency reasoning, and combining Iris's invariant-based reasoning with effect-handler specifications is the first substantial concurrent-effect-handler logic at production scale. While not categorically fibrational, this work bridges the practical gap between effect-handler formalism and verified concurrent code.

#### §6.4.4 The Quantale-Effect Connection

The connection between quantale-graded effect systems and effect-handler categorical semantics passes through *graded modal type theory* and *effect quantales*. Gordon's "Polymorphic Iterable Sequential Effect Systems" (TOPLAS 2021) [Gor21] introduces *effect quantales* — partial join-semilattices with partial monoid structure where multiplication distributes over joins — as the algebraic substrate for sequential effect systems, subsuming many instances. Orchard, Liepelt, and Eades' "Quantitative Program Reasoning with Graded Modal Types" (ICFP 2019) [OLE19] and Moon-Eades-Orchard's "Graded Modal Dependent Type Theory" (ESOP 2021) [MEO21] establish graded-modal-dependent-type-theoretic foundations.

The very recent "Resource-Bounded Type Theory: Compositional Cost Analysis via Graded Modalities" (arXiv 2512.06952; 2025-2026) [RBT25] uses an *abstract resource lattice* for synthesising compositional cost bounds — uniform treatment of time, memory, gas, and other resources — closely paralleling the Prologos tropical-quantale-on-cells design. Atkey's "Polynomial Time and Dependent Types" (POPL 2024) [Atk24] extends QTT-style multiplicities into polynomial-time-cost guarantees in a fully dependent setting. None of these works explicitly identifies effect-handler categorical semantics as a *quantale-enriched fibration*, but the materials are all in place: effect quantale + effect handler + dependent fibration. This is gap (d) in §6.6.

### §6.5 Tropical / Min-Plus in Scheduling, Reactivity, and Quantitative Semantics

#### §6.5.1 Network Calculus and Deterministic Networking

The Le Boudec-Thiran line continues to be the foundational reference. "Network Calculus: A Theory of Deterministic Queuing Systems for the Internet" (Springer LNCS 2050, 2001; updated online edition leboudec.github.io/netcal) [LBT01] establishes that network calculus is a *min-plus algebra system theory* — convolutions of arrival and service curves under min-plus semiring give worst-case end-to-end delay and buffer-space bounds. This *is* the tropical-on-time-series story directly. Recent (2022-2024) consolidation includes the Dagstuhl Seminar 24141 ("Network Calculus," April 2024) [DS24] and Bondorf-Schmitt's open-source Nancy library [BS22] for arbitrary piecewise-affine min-plus / max-plus operations including subadditive-closure. The arXiv 2403.18042 paper "Extending Network Calculus to Deal With Partially Negative Service Curves" (2024) [EXT24] extends the foundational framework to negative-service-curve regimes, with novel bounds outperforming prior state-of-the-art.

This literature has not crossed paths with the programming-language community in any sustained way. Network calculus is overwhelmingly published in performance-evaluation venues (IEEE/ACM Networking, RTSS, MASCOTS) rather than POPL/ICFP/CONCUR. The tropical-quantale-cell approach in Prologos (PPN 4C addendum, 2026-04-26) is, to the best of available evidence, the first serious attempt to bring the network-calculus min-plus calculation onto a programming-language type-system substrate. This crossover gap is gap (e) in §6.6.

#### §6.5.2 Discrete-Event Systems and Max-Plus Algebra

The Cohen-Gaubert-Quadrat program on max-plus / tropical algebra for discrete-event-system modelling has continuous output through 2024. Gaubert's recent papers include "Tropical Linear Regression and Mean Payoff Games" (SIAM J. Disc. Math 37(2), 2023) [Gau23a], "Exact Quantization of Multistage Stochastic Linear Problems" (SIAM J. Opt., 2024) [Gau24], and "Semiring Systems Arising from Hyperrings" (J. Pure Appl. Algebra 228(6), 2024) [Gau24b]. The Akian-Bapat-Gaubert program on tropical-spectral methods continues in parallel. Within the broader tropical-DES community, max-plus model-predictive control, max-plus reachability, and discrete-event-system stability are the dominant application domains, with programming-language-semantic connections being marginal.

#### §6.5.3 Tropical Mathematics and the Lambda Calculus

A pivotal recent development is the Barbarossa-Pistone "Tropical Mathematics and the Lambda-Calculus" series:

- Part I: "Metric and Differential Analysis of Effectful Programs" (CSL 2024; arXiv 2311.15704) [BP24a]
- Part II: "Tropical Geometry of Probabilistic Programming Languages" (POPL/PACMPL 2025; arXiv 2501.15637) [BP25]

Part I establishes that the tropical semiring as the weight-base of a relational model unifies (i) Lipschitz-condition-based program metrics and (ii) linear-logic-and-higher-order-differentiation-based resource analysis — two quantitative semantic traditions that had been developed independently. The general foundation is "an abstract correspondence between tropical algebra and Lawvere's theory of generalized metric spaces" [BP24a]. Lawvere's framework is precisely the quantale-enrichment-of-Cat tradition initiated by Lawvere's "Metric Spaces, Generalized Logic, and Closed Categories" (1973), making this paper one of the closest published frontier connections between tropical/quantale semantics and programming-language semantics.

Part II uses tropical geometry to perform *statistical inference over higher-order probabilistic programs*: each probabilistic program is associated with a degree and an n-dimensional polyhedron encoding its most-likely runs, and an intersection type system estimates most-likely runs compositionally and efficiently. This is a frontier example of tropical-semiring-as-program-semantic-substrate but operates at the relational-model / weighted-categorical-semantics level rather than at the propagator-network level.

The Lawvere-metric-space connection is the closest existing technology to a "propagator-network as tropical-quantale-enriched category." But explicit identification of *cells with tropical-quantale-valued state* under monotone-merge as the substrate operational form has not been published — gap (b) in §6.6 sharpened.

#### §6.5.4 Tropical Geometry in Machine Learning (Adjacent)

Zhang, Naitzat, and Lim's "Tropical Geometry of Deep Neural Networks" (ICML 2018) [ZNL18] establishes that feedforward ReLU neural networks are *tropical rational maps*, with linear regions corresponding to vertices of polytopes associated with tropical rational functions. This is adjacent to the substrate hypothesis only insofar as ReLU networks are a parallel-structure realisation of min-plus computation; the connection to *programming-language* semantics is indirect. More relevant is the broader observation that tropical algebra is now established as a multi-domain quantitative-computation substrate (network calculus, scheduling, deep networks, lambda-calculus relational models, real-time systems) — the substrate hypothesis claims this is not coincidence.

#### §6.5.5 Resource-Aware Type Systems and Cost Analysis

Dal Lago, Gaboardi, and recent Drexel-Edinburgh-Cambridge collaborations on resource-bounded computation frame the cost-analysis-via-quantale picture. Bianchini, Dagnino, Giannini, and Zucca's "Resource-Aware Soundness for Big-Step Semantics" (PACMPL 7(OOPSLA2), 2023) [BDG+23] extends a λ-calculus to be parametric in an arbitrary *grade algebra*, with type-system soundness ensuring that well-typed programs have a computation where no needed resource is exhausted. Bono-Castagna et al.'s "Fair Termination for Resource-Aware Active Objects" (FoSSaCS / ETAPS 2024) [BC24] combines graded-semantics techniques with fair-termination type systems — both grade algebra and effect quantales appear as substrate.

### §6.6 Gaps in the Frontier (Setup for Synthesis)

This subsection identifies missing literature without arguing that the working hypothesis is correct or unique. The synthesis chapter argues from these gaps; here they are catalogued.

**Gap (a) — Choreography projection as quantale morphism**. No published frontier paper explicitly formulates endpoint projection (Pirouette, Choral, Chorλ, ChoRus, λ_QC) as a *quantale-enriched* construction graded by communication cost, latency, fairness, or causal depth. The choreographic-programming community treats correctness exclusively (deadlock-freedom, type soundness); the cost-of-projection question is unaddressed. Multiparty-session-type causality-extraction papers (Castellani et al. 2024, Stutz et al. 2023) are the closest but treat causality combinatorially via event-structure semantics rather than via quantale-graded morphisms.

**Gap (b) — Propagator network as quantale-enriched category**. Direct search for "propagator network" + "quantale-enriched category" yielded no hits in 2020-2026 peer-reviewed or preprint literature. The closest existing technology comes via:
- Quantale-valued coalgebraic logic (Beohar-Gurke-König-Messing-Forster-Schröder-Wild STACS 2024)
- Lawvere-metric-space correspondence in tropical lambda-calculus (Barbarossa-Pistone CSL 2024)
- Resource-theory-as-quantale-module formalism (Gonda, 2021 thesis; Gonda-Spekkens, Compositionality 5, 2023 [GS23])
None of these papers identify the propagator-network *operational substrate* (cells with monotone-merge, BSP-scheduled rounds, CALM-monotonicity) as a quantale-enriched category. This is the original substrate-hypothesis territory.

**Gap (c) — CCP-as-quantale-enriched-cc(D)**. Saraswat's cc(D) + Panangaden-Saraswat-Scott-Seely's hyperdoctrinal interpretation is the foundation. Soft CCP (semiring-graded constraint entailment) is in publication 2022-2024. But the explicit equivalence "Saraswat's cc(D) is the operational form of computation in a quantale-enriched category over a propagator substrate" — which the working hypothesis holds — is unstated in published work. This is the central CCP-quantale connection that §3.3 (Agent 3, historical CCP-quantale) and §3.4-3.5 (Agent 4, foundations) provide background for; the frontier reveals it has not been made explicit in 2020-2026 literature.

**Gap (d) — Unified treatment of cost + order + fairness + resource as quantale projections**. The components exist independently in published work:
- Cost: tropical lambda calculus (Barbarossa-Pistone), network calculus (Le Boudec lineage), graded modalities (Orchard-Liepelt-Eades, Moon-Eades-Orchard, Atkey)
- Causal order: event structures for multiparty sessions (Castellani et al.), happens-before in CRDTs / causal logical clocks
- Fairness: Bono-Castagna fair-termination + grade-algebra type systems (FoSSaCS 2024)
- Resource: Atkey-McBride QTT, dependent multiplicity, effect quantales, Idris 2

What no frontier paper does is *unify* them as different *quantale projections* of one substrate. Each tradition treats its own quantale separately. The Hyperlattice-Conjecture-aligned thesis "every async semantic dimension is a quantale projection of the same substrate" is the unification that Era V leaves open.

**Gap (e) — Network-calculus min-plus on programming-language type substrate**. Network calculus is published exclusively in networking/performance venues. Programming-language type systems for cost are published exclusively in POPL/ICFP/PLDI venues. Despite both using min-plus algebra (network calculus directly; PL cost-types via effect quantales / graded modalities), no 2020-2026 paper bridges them with a single algebraic substrate covering both deterministic-networking-bound calculation and program-cost-type checking. The Prologos tropical-quantale-cell design (PPN 4C addendum 2026-04-26) appears, on available frontier evidence, to be the first such crossover.

**Gap (f) — Quantale-causal clocks (vs Lamport / vector clocks)**. Lamport clocks and vector clocks are well-known to form monotone join-semilattices [CLM-Logic-and-Lattices, Berkeley 2014; Weidner CRDT survey 2023]. The natural extension to *quantale-graded clocks* — where the clock value is not a counter or vector but a quantale-valued cost (latency, message bytes, edge-fairness) accumulated along causal paths — is not published. This is the most concrete missed frontier item: distributed-systems metadata is *already* quantale-shaped operationally (CRDT lattice merge is a join-semilattice operation), but the published formalisation stops at lattices and does not progress to quantales except in the network-calculus-on-data-paths reading.

**Gap (g) — CapTP / OCapN in quantale formalism**. The Spritely / OCapN distributed-capability tradition (covered by §6.2 separately) has not been formally connected to quantale or coalgebraic semantics. Capabilities are typically treated as *resources* (in a unrestricted, set-membership sense) rather than as *quantale-graded* resources. No published work formalises CapTP message-flow as a Galois-connection-with-resource-projection structure. The natural cross-pollination is: capability-passing async = quantale-valued message-cost-graded morphism between actor-cell-states. This is gap (g), connecting §6.2 to the quantale frontier.

**Gap (h) — Function coloring at the substrate level**. The function-coloring observation (Nystrom 2015; widely-cited blog discussion; algebraic-effects-as-solution narrative through 2018-2024) recognises that algebraic-effect frameworks dissolve the async/non-async coloring at the *type-system* level. The next-level claim — that function coloring dissolves at the *substrate* level (every "async" call is just a propagator-fire-event under quantale-graded cost and ordering) — is not articulated in the published programming-languages frontier. The algebraic-effect-handler lineage (Forster-Kammar-Lindley-Pretnar JFP 2019 [FKL+19]; Bauer-Pretnar; Plotkin-Pretnar; recent Bach Poulsen, Lindley-Matache-Moss-Staton-Wu-Yang) recognises the type-level dissolution but does not advance the substrate-level claim. The Prologos working hypothesis is that this is one and the same dissolution at two abstraction layers.

### Cross-References to Neighbouring Slices

- §6.2 (Spritely / OCapN): gap (g) above interfaces with §6.2's coverage of distributed capability foundations — neither tradition has been formally connected to quantale semantics.
