- §3.3 (CCP historical, Agent 3): gap (c) flags that the post-2020 literature has not advanced the explicit CCP-quantale formalisation; §3.3's coverage of pre-2020 CCP-quantale connections is the foundation gap (c) is built on.
- §3.4-3.5 (quantale theory foundations, Agent 4): the quantale-theoretic framework gap (b) and gap (d) reference is established at the abstract level by §3.4-3.5; §6.3 surfaces that the bridge to *propagator-network operational substrate* is not made in 2020-2026 work.
- §3.2 (game semantics, Agent 2): Melliès' tensorial-logic / dialogue-category program (tracked through "Convolution Products on Double Categories" with Behr-Zeilberger, FSCD 2023; "Profinite Lambda-Calculus and Parametricity" with van Gool-Moreau MFPS 2023; "Categorical Contours of the Chomsky-Schützenberger Representation" with Zeilberger 2024) [BMZ23, MGM23, MZ24] continues but does not enter the propagator-substrate / quantale-enrichment-of-concurrency territory directly. Cross-coordinate with §3.2 on whether the polynomial-functor lineage (Gambino-Kock; Joyal-Kock-Melliès) provides the categorical bridge from dialogue games / strategies to propagator-network semantics.
- §5.2 (algebraic effects, Agent 7): §6.4 frontier-effect-handler work (Xie-Johnson-Maclaurin-Paszke parallel handlers ICFP 2024; Bach Poulsen-van der Rest hefty algebras POPL 2023; Lindley-Matache-Moss-Staton-Wu-Yang scoped effects ESOP 2024; Tian-Madiot-Pottier OCaml + Iris ICFP 2025) coordinates with §5.2's broader algebraic-effects coverage. The frontier here is *parallel* and *fibrational* and *Iris-formalised* — three substrate-relevant directions.
- §7 (synthesis chapter): the seven gaps (a)-(h) above set up §8.7 ("what's missing from substrate today") — the synthesis chapter argues the unifying claim from these specific frontier absences.

## §8 Synthesis: A Quantale-Enriched cc(D) Substrate

The eight slices that precede this chapter were written without conclusions. Each surveyed its slice with rigour and stayed neutral on the working hypothesis, by design. This chapter is the place where the slices' evidence is read against the hypothesis the entire artifact was structured to test:

> *Asynchrony is the operational semantics of computation in a quantale-enriched category over a propagator substrate. Futures, awaits, fairness, cost, and ordering are projections from a single algebraic object. Propagator networks instantiate Saraswat's cc(D) family for a particular constraint system D; the quantale-enriched extension is a frontier hypothesis with established formal antecedents.*

The claim has two parts. The first — that propagator networks instantiate cc(D) — is, on the evidence in §3.3, settled: the structural correspondence is line-by-line tight, the closure-operator semantics of Saraswat-Rinard-Panangaden 1991 carries through verbatim, and the divergences (dynamic topology, stratification, cell-locality) are extensions of the cc(D) framework rather than refutations of it. The second — that the quantale-enriched extension carries the algebraic content of asynchrony — is the frontier hypothesis. The evidence assembled across §3.4–§3.5 (quantale foundations and Lawvere metric), §5.5 (BloomL and CALM), §6.3 (post-2020 quantale-coalgebraic logics), §6.5 (tropical structures bridging to programming-language semantics), and §6.6 (the explicit gap inventory) supports the hypothesis without yet *demonstrating* it — that demonstration is the work of the design tracks this artifact will inform, not of the artifact itself.

What the synthesis chapter argues here is *that the hypothesis is the right one to test*. Seven subsections develop the argument: §8.1 establishes the genealogical claim; §8.2 dissolves function coloring at the substrate level; §8.3 recasts structured concurrency as a concurrent Kleene quantale operation; §8.4 sketches the distributed-propagator-with-quantale-causal-clocks architecture for the L2 runtime layer (frontier (c)); §8.5 treats decidability and runtime verification lightly per the artifact's framing (frontier (a)); §8.6 articulates cost / order / fairness / resource as projections from one substrate (frontier (b)); §8.7 catalogues what is missing.

### §8.1 The Genealogical Claim

The strongest single piece of evidence in the artifact is the parallel rediscovery of the same algebraic primitive in four research traditions across roughly twenty-five years (§3.3.9). The constraint-propagation lineage runs Sutherland 1963 (Sketchpad / CAD) → Steele 1980 → Borning 1981 (ThingLab / object-oriented graphics) → McAllester 1989 (TMS / dependency networks) → Sussman-Radul 2009 [Sussman-Radul 2009] and produces *propagator networks*. The concurrent-logic-and-constraint lineage runs Maher 1987 → Saraswat 1989 [Saraswat 1989] → Saraswat-Rinard-Panangaden 1991 [SRP91] → Saraswat 1993 → Mendler-Panangaden-Scott-Seely 1995 and produces *concurrent constraint programming* with closure-operator semantics. The deterministic-parallelism lineage runs Tesler-Enea 1968 → Kahn 1974 → Arvind-Nikhil-Pingali 1989 → Concurrent Collections (Budimlić et al. 2010) → Kuper-Newton 2013 [Kuper-Newton 2013] and produces *I-structures* and then *LVars* (note: Reppy 1999's CML belongs to a different lineage of first-class synchronisation events, not write-once cells, and is not a member of this chain). The distributed-database lineage runs Hellerstein 2010 → Alvaro-Conway-Hellerstein-Marczak 2011 → Conway-Marczak-Alvaro-Hellerstein-Maier 2012 [Conway et al. 2012] and produces *Bloom* and *BloomL*. Each of the four arrived at lattice-valued shared state, monotone update, and threshold reads as the right answer to the question of how to design a deterministic monotone shared-memory primitive. None cites the others materially. The Kuper-Newton LVars paper does not engage Saraswat directly (the connection passes through Concurrent Collections at one remove); the BloomL paper does not engage Saraswat; the Sussman-Radul propagator paper does not engage either CCP or BloomL.

The convergence is not coincidence. There is essentially one good answer to the question, and the four communities produced four presentations of that answer in their own languages. The propagator-network presentation is the one Prologos has chosen, and the project has been working out the details of that presentation in the constraint-propagation vocabulary. The synthesis claim of this chapter is that the *concurrent-constraint-programming* vocabulary is the more precise one for what the project is actually building, and that recasting the project's foundations in cc(D) terms unlocks a body of formal results — Saraswat-Rinard-Panangaden's closure-operator semantics, Mendler-Panangaden-Scott-Seely's hyperdoctrinal categorical model, Bistarelli-Montanari-Rossi's quantale-graded soft CCP, Fujii-Iwamasa-Kimura's quantaloidal CSP — that the project does not yet leverage but already implicitly satisfies.

The structural correspondence (§3.3.7) is *tight at the per-cell store level and architecturally divergent at the network level*. At the per-cell-store level: a constraint store is a lattice-valued cell. A `tell` is a monotone cell-write. An `ask` is a threshold-read. A propagator is the operational realisation of a CCP agent. The fire function of a propagator at fixpoint is, mathematically, a closure operator on the cell's lattice carrier — extensive (the cell only grows under monotone merge), idempotent (re-firing on the same input is a no-op once quiescence is reached), monotone (more input information yields at least as much output information). The parallel composition of propagators is the meet of closure operators on the joint lattice — equivalently the intersection of fixed-point sets — exactly the SRP 1991 parallel-as-intersection equation. Network quiescence is the Knaster-Tarski / Kleene least fixed point. Existential quantification (CCP's hiding via cylindric algebra) is the propagator-network's compound-cell decomposition with component-indexed access. The propagator network's mantra — "all-at-once, all in parallel, structurally emergent information flow ON-NETWORK" — is the operational form of CCP's tell-as-only-primitive discipline applied to a per-cell-decomposed store.

The architectural divergences are real and load-bearing. Slice §3.3.8 catalogues three: (D1) classical cc(D) has a fixed network of agents, while propagator networks support cell creation and propagator installation mid-computation, requiring a topology stratum the cc(D) framework does not provide; (D2) classical cc(D) has no stratification beyond the closure-operator Galois connection, while Prologos's stratification (S0, S(-1), S1, topology) is a generalised structural pattern; (D3) classical cc(D) has a single global constraint store, while propagator networks have many local cells with per-cell merge functions and per-cell SRE structural decomposition. The synthesis claim is that these are *extensions* of cc(D) — meaning the cc(D) framework is preserved at the per-cell-store level and the propagator-network architecture adds machinery on top — but they are non-trivial extensions. The published cc-with-mobile-or-higher-order extensions (Saraswat-Lincoln 1992 LCC; Cardelli-Mendler 1996) introduce some of this flexibility but do not provide the full propagator-style discipline. (D1)–(D3) are areas where the project's design is original work, not inheritance from the cc literature.

The era-I evidence in §2 reinforces the claim from the opposite direction. Three independent communities — dataflow (I-structures, M-structures), logic programming (logic variables under unification), and applicative programming (futures, promises) — each arrived at *write-once cells with monotone refinement* as the substrate primitive for concurrency, and each named it differently (§2.4, §2.7). Era II's game-semantic developments (§3.2.2, §3.6.2) produce the same primitive yet again under different names: Melliès' asynchronous-game *positions* with the cube property are propagator-network cells with CALM-monotone parallel firing, and Winskel-style event-structure *configurations* with conflict are cells whose lattice merge produces a contradiction when two propagator firings would write incompatible values.

The genealogical claim is therefore that the propagator-network substrate of Prologos is a CCP — concretely, an instance of cc(D) for D = an arbitrary lattice with monotone merge — that descends from the AI/dataflow rather than the concurrent-logic intellectual line, but instantiates the same underlying structure. The quantale-enriched extension that the working hypothesis posits is the natural next step within the cc family: Bistarelli-Montanari-Rossi's c-semirings (J. ACM 1997) [Bistarelli-Montanari-Rossi 1997], identified explicitly as commutative quantales in subsequent work, are quantale-valued constraint stores; soft CCP (Bistarelli-Montanari-Rossi 2006, ACM TOCL) is closure-operator semantics over quantale-valued lattices; and the project's tropical-quantale ((min, +)) cost layer is an instance of soft cc(Q) for Q the Lawvere quantale (§7.2.5). Where the project's current presentation in the AI/dataflow lineage describes "cells with tropical cost merge" as a recent design innovation, the cc(D) reframing reveals it as an instance of a research programme with thirty years of formal development. The propagator-network operational realisation is genuinely original; the algebraic content is not.

This reframing has consequences beyond intellectual housekeeping. Three are load-bearing for subsequent design tracks. *First*, the SRP 1991 closure-operator semantics applies to every propagator: a propagator that is not extensive, monotone, and idempotent is not a CCP agent and therefore not a closure operator and therefore not part of a CALM-safe network. Mantra-violation audits acquire a precise mathematical statement. *Second*, the Mendler-Panangaden-Scott-Seely 1995 hyperdoctrinal model gives the project a categorical foundation it has been working without: a propagator network with per-cell decomposition is, conjecturally, a *fibred* hyperdoctrine over the cell graph (an open question, §3.3 open question 2). *Third*, the soft-CCP framework provides a turnkey body of theory for cost-aware computation: every result about quantale-valued constraint stores transfers to quantale-valued cells, and the project's tropical-quantale work inherits formal antecedents that need no separate justification.

### §8.2 Function Coloring Dissolved at the Substrate

Bob Nystrom's "What Color is Your Function?" essay (Nystrom 2015, §4.3) named the structural pathology of Era III async/await: every async function infects every transitive caller with the async marker; library functions that perform I/O are therefore async; application code calling those libraries becomes async; tests of the application become async; and the infection propagates until either every function in the program is colored or the developer pays a synchronisation cost at the boundary. Nystrom's diagnosis is precise: async/await is a *syntactic* concession to direct-style code that does not address the underlying architectural problem. The function-coloring boundary is real — async and synchronous functions cannot interoperate without explicit conversion — and the boundary is not architectural in the sense of reflecting an underlying computational distinction; it is *syntactic*, an artefact of treating asynchrony as a property of the function rather than as a property of the call site or the cost lattice.

The structured-concurrency turn of Era IV (Smith 2018, §5.1) responded to a related but distinct pathology: that unrestricted task spawn primitives violate the black-box rule of structured programming because a function may install background work whose lifetime is not bounded by its lexical scope. Trio, Kotlin coroutines, Swift Tasks, Java StructuredTaskScope, and Goblins all introduce a *scope* primitive that bounds task lifetimes; this corrects the black-box rule but does not dissolve the function-coloring problem itself. The deeper Era IV correction comes from algebraic effects (§5.2). OCaml 5 ships fork, yield, await, generators, coroutines, and lightweight threads as instances of a single primitive — effect handlers — at 1% mean overhead on macro benchmarks (Sivaramakrishnan et al. 2021, §5.2). The unification claim is operative in production: schedulers are libraries, not language primitives, and the effect-handler abstraction subsumes async/await as a derived rather than primitive form. Ahman-Pretnar's *Asynchronous Effects* calculus λ_ae (POPL 2021) gives the canonical algebraic account: a `signal` is a fork-point, an `interrupt` is a callback-arrival, and a handler is a scheduler. The Effekt language (Brachthäuser-Schuster-Ostermann 2020, §5.4) provides the operational form: effects are capabilities, capability-passing is the operational semantics of effect handlers, and the connection to Mark Miller's E-language tradition (§4.5) is direct — an unresolved promise is a capability to a future value, and pipelining is capability composition.

The Era IV dissolution of function coloring is therefore at the *type-system* level. An algebraic-effect handler treats fork/yield/await as effect operations whose handler determines their semantics; the handler-as-scheduler can dispatch effect operations from sync and async functions uniformly because the type system records the effect set, not a binary async marker. This is the dissolution Era IV recognises.

The synthesis claim of this chapter — and this is a hypothesis to be tested by design-track work, not a theorem the artifact has demonstrated — is that there is a candidate *second*, *substrate-level relocation* of the function-coloring problem that the algebraic-effects literature does not articulate. In a quantale-enriched propagator network, every function carries an extensional weight in the underlying quantale — its tropical-cost projection, its causal-depth projection, its resource-consumption projection. This weight is a property of the value's position in the cost lattice rather than a property of the syntactic marker on the function name. A function that performs no I/O has the multiplicative identity 1 of the quantale as its weight; a function that performs N units of asynchronous work has weight N in the (min, +) projection; a function that requires resource R has R in the resource-quantale projection. The architectural claim is that there is no *syntactic* distinction between "async" and "sync" functions because both are propagator firings weighted by the substrate's quantale — but the operational distinction is *not* eliminated. It is *relocated*: the boundary moves from the function name to the cost-bound at the call site (which propagator firings can complete within budget B?). Whether this relocation constitutes a "dissolution" depends on whether the cost-projection carries the *locus information* that async/await syntactically encodes — and that is a question the substrate has to answer with concrete machinery, not a result the algebra produces automatically.

The argument the chapter wants to advance, but cannot complete in a Stage-0/1 synthesis, is that the cost weight on a propagator's call edge IS the locus information: the propagator graph itself is the locus, and the quantale-grading on graph edges encodes "where in the graph the asynchronous boundary is." A row-effect type system records the same information at the type level; a propagator-network substrate would record it at the structural level. The relationship is not "two layers of one phenomenon" — that framing was overreach in earlier drafts — but rather "two presentations of related information, with a candidate substrate-level relocation that follow-on design tracks must validate or refute." Where the algebraic-effects programme makes the type-level relocation work, the substrate-level relocation (gap (h) in §6.6) is the open hypothesis.

The polarity connection is suggestive but secondary. Pfenning and Griffith's 2015 *Polarized Substructural Session Types* (§3.1.4) makes explicit that the asymmetry between the sender and receiver of an asynchronous session is the same asymmetry as positive and negative connectives in linear logic, *within a single linear-logic-typed protocol*. The function-coloring problem is about transitive infection of `async` markers across the call graph, which is a different phenomenon. The structural connection is that both involve polarity-asymmetry; the operational connection — that the substrate's *-autonomous structure (§3.4.3) recovers the function-coloring boundary as a polarity projection — is a candidate algebraic move, not an established fact. Treating polarity as *the* explanation for async would over-claim. Treating polarity as *one* algebraic resource the substrate brings to the function-coloring problem is the right framing.

### §8.3 Cancellation and Structured Concurrency as Concurrent-Kleene-Quantale Operations

The structured-concurrency turn (§5.1) admits a clean lattice-theoretic restatement that the original literature does not articulate: the parent-child task hierarchy is a tree, the cancellation propagation is downward closure on that tree, the error propagation is upward closure on that tree, and the lifetime constraint is the requirement that the join semilattice of cleanup obligations be quiesced before the parent scope exits. Smith's level-triggered cancellation discipline (Trio 2018, §5.1) requires that every checkpoint operation re-check all enclosing cancelled scopes; this is structurally a *monotone state-bit propagating downward through scope adjacency*. The discipline is hand-implemented in Trio, Kotlin, Swift, and Java because the host languages do not provide a substrate that produces it automatically.

In a propagator-network substrate, the discipline arises structurally. A scope is a compound cell whose components are the cells of its child tasks. Cancellation is a write to the scope's cancellation component; the monotone merge propagates the cancellation to all child components by structural decomposition. Error propagation is a write to the scope's error component; the monotone merge propagates the error upward by the dual structure. Quiescence of cleanup obligations is reached when no propagator can fire in the scope's subgraph, which is the standard BSP termination condition. The structured-concurrency primitive is *not implemented* in such a substrate; it is *recognised* in the substrate's existing structure.

The algebraic content of this recognition is the *concurrent Kleene algebra* of Hoare-Möller-Struth-Wehrman (CONCUR 2009 [Hoare et al. 2009]) — a structure that, when augmented with the requirement that joins exist for arbitrary subsets, becomes a *concurrent Kleene quantale* (§7.3, §7.5.1). The HMSW 2009 paper writes "Kleene algebra"; the quantale-strength upgrade to unconditional joins is the move slice 04 makes to fit the structure into the broader quantale-theoretic frame. With that completion in hand, the structure is a double quantale on the same underlying lattice: a parallel product ⊗_∥ and a sequential product ⊗_; linked by the *exchange law*

    (a ⊗_∥ b) ⊗_; (c ⊗_∥ d)  ≤  (a ⊗_; c) ⊗_∥ (b ⊗_; d)

Both products distribute over arbitrary joins. The interpretation is direct: ⊗_∥ is the parallel composition of independent firings (the BSP-round simultaneous-firing operation), ⊗_; is the sequential composition along the dataflow direction (the structural ordering that emerges from cell-dependency), and the exchange-law inequality formalises that any sequential interleaving of independent steps refines the genuinely-concurrent execution but not conversely. This is the algebraic shadow of the project's design mantra — "all-at-once, all in parallel, structurally emergent information flow ON-NETWORK" — and the synthesis claim is that the mantra is precisely the assertion that the propagator network is a model of a concurrent Kleene quantale.

Three structured-concurrency disciplines map directly into this algebra. *Sequential composition* of effects within a single task is ⊗_;. *Parallel composition* of sibling tasks under the same nursery is ⊗_∥. *Cancellation* sits in a different algebraic register: where ⊗_; produces a successor state by applying an operation to a precursor, cancellation produces a predecessor state by retracting an operation from a successor. The S(-1) retraction stratum in the Prologos stratification design (rules/stratification.md) is the substrate's operational realisation of this retraction direction. To capture both forward composition and retraction in one algebra would require *additional* structure beyond CKA/CKQ — by analogy with involutive quantales (Mulvey lineage; §3.4.1), one might propose an *involutive* concurrent Kleene quantale with an involution `(·)^*` exchanging retraction and forward composition, satisfying `(a ⊗_; b)^* = b^* ⊗_; a^*`. **This structure is a candidate algebraic frame proposed in this chapter, not an established theory of HMSW 2009 or any successor work.** Whether the involutive variant exists with the right algebraic properties, and whether it admits the operational interpretation the substrate needs, is open work for the project's design tracks. Goblins' transactional turns (§6.2.2) are an operational analogue but not an algebraic instance: Goblins' rollback is atomic-checkpoint discard, not structural involution. The proposal that BSP rounds can be made transactional in the same sense — the merges of a round commit only if the round reaches quiescence, and roll back atomically otherwise — is plausible and would recover Goblins' fault-isolation property; whether the substrate's algebra makes this fall out structurally requires the involutive-CKQ candidate to be worked out concretely.

The synthesis claim, *with the candidate-algebra caveat in place*, is that structured concurrency, level-triggered cancellation, transactional rollback, and parent-child error propagation can plausibly be recognised as algebraic operations in a CKA/CKQ extended with retraction structure, rather than primitives the substrate must provide. The Era IV literature treats them as primitives the language must add. The synthesis hypothesis is that they should fall out structurally from the right algebraic substrate; concretely demonstrating that they do is design-track work.

### §8.4 Distributed Propagation with Quantale-Causal Clocks (Frontier (c))

The L2 layer of the project — multi-network runtime, cross-network message delivery — is currently empty (DEFERRED.md, "Session Types — Concurrent Runtime"). Architecture A+D (§3.3.4 of the [Stage-1 reading map; AD-F3 in the existing project tree]) shipped a `current-effect-executor` parameter with a sequential default and a placeholder concurrent stub. The synthesis claim of this section is that the L2 architecture is *structurally determined* by the quantale-enriched cc(D) substrate: a distributed propagator network with quantale-valued causal clocks, with vat-model boundaries as single-stratum BSP propagator networks and cross-vat communication as cross-network topology requests.

The vat-model design precedent (§4.5.5) supplies the practical antecedent. A *vat* is a single-threaded event loop owning a private object heap and a message queue; turns within it run to completion under quiescence-style semantics; inter-vat communication is asynchronous via eventual sends returning promises. A *single-stratum BSP propagator network* is a (potentially multi-threaded within a round) scheduler firing propagators in rounds against a shared cell graph; rounds within it run to quiescence under monotone-merge semantics; inter-network communication can be asynchronous via cross-network topology requests. The structural correspondence is genuinely close — vat ≈ single-stratum BSP propagator network, inter-vat eventual-send ≈ cross-network topology request, promise ≈ cell becoming concrete from `bot`, capability-as-reference ≈ the project's `capabilities.prologos` hierarchy — but it is *not* a structural identity. Vats are single-threaded with run-to-completion turns; BSP rounds permit parallel propagator firing within a round, and parallelism within a round is in fact the defining property of the BSP-LE work. The claim of this section is therefore that *the vat model is a candidate architectural pattern for L2, with strong structural analogy to a single-stratum BSP propagator network, and with non-trivial residual differences that the L2 design tracks must address*. The engineering closure that the capability-async lineage has reached on distributed garbage collection (CapTP per-table reference counting), three-network introduction (signed handoff), persistent durable references (sturdy refs), revocable delegation (membranes), and re-entrancy hazards (no coroutines, only `when`-blocks) is *available* to the L2 architecture, but how much of it transfers directly versus needing adaptation depends on how the residual differences are resolved.

What the vat-model precedent does not provide, and what the quantale-enriched substrate adds, is *causal cost*. CapTP's wire model orders messages but does not carry quantitative weight; promise pipelining elides round-trips by execution at the receiver but does not bound latency or cost compositionally. The natural extension is *quantale-valued causal clocks*. Vector clocks are well-known to form a join-semilattice (§5.5; CRDT lineage); they are the canonical embedding of an event into the free `ℕ`-quantale-module on the process set, with the join (component-wise max) the lattice operation and the local-clock-tick the `ℕ`-action (§7.5.3). Lifting the quantale to a richer choice yields a richer clock. With Q = `T` (the Lawvere tropical quantale, [0,∞] under (min, +)), the clock value at each component is the minimum-cost causal path; the join is component-wise minimum. With Q = `[0,1]` under multiplication, the clock value is the probability mass of causal influence. With Q involutive, the clock distinguishes causally-from from causally-to.

The natural Prologos ambient is `ℕ ⊗ T`-valued clocks: round-number paired with tropical cost (§7.5.3). This unifies the BSP scheduler (round-counting) with the project's tropical-quantale cost addendum (cost-tracking) as a single quantale-clock structure. The component product `ℕ ⊗ T` is itself a quantale (a tensor product of quantales is a quantale), and the resulting causal clocks would in principle add quantitative-cost information to every cross-network message. The synthesis claim, *as a candidate L2 architecture rather than a settled design*, is that the L2 runtime could be a federation of vat-like single-stratum BSP propagator networks communicating via CapTP-style messages bearing `ℕ ⊗ T`-valued causal clocks, with cross-network topology requests as the protocol primitive and capability discipline as the security model. Engineering questions the synthesis chapter cannot answer — does this compose with CapTP's existing per-table reference counting? Does it pass the OCapN interoperability test suite? What is the wire-cost of the additional per-message metadata under a `T`-valued clock with O(P) component projections per process? — must be answered by L2 design tracks. "Generalising a structure the lineage has already validated" applies at the algebraic level; whether the engineering reduction works is a separate question.

Two further structural observations support the claim. *First*, the BloomL evidence (§5.5; Conway et al. 2012) demonstrates that a non-trivial lattice-based distributed-programming model already exists at industrial quality, with the CALM theorem providing the formal connection to coordination-freedom. A propagator-network with monotone merge is a BloomL program viewed through a different surface syntax; the L2 architecture inherits BloomL's CALM-monotonicity property within strata and therefore inherits its coordination-freedom guarantee for the monotone fragment. *Second*, the timely dataflow / differential dataflow line (§4.6; McSherry et al. 2013) is the closest existing system to a propagator network: cell ≈ operator state, propagator ≈ operator, lattice merge ≈ differential update, BSP round ≈ frontier advancement. Timely dataflow's frontier-advancement protocol is a candidate substrate for cross-network coordination, particularly for the CALM-monotone fragment. What timely dataflow lacks — and what the L2 architecture supplies — is the quantale-enriched cost model: timestamps order progress, but they do not carry quantitative weight.

The fairness and liveness obligations that frontier (c) does not address (per the user's framing — fairness and liveness checking is "tractably deployable at runtime via tropical structures and formal verification mechanisms") fall out at the substrate level via tropical-cost bounds: a fairness obligation is a quantale-valued threshold on the causal clock, and a liveness check is the assertion that this threshold is reached in bounded BSP rounds. The runtime verification is tractable because the threshold is a quantale value and the substrate already computes the necessary propagator-network quiescence. This is the connection §6.5.5 (resource-bounded type theory, graded modalities) opens but does not yet close in the propagator-network direction.

### §8.5 Decidability and Runtime Verification (Frontier (a), Light Treatment)

The asynchronous-session-subtyping decidability frontier (§5.3) settles negatively in the general case (Bravetti-Carbone-Lange-Yoshida-Zavattaro 2017) and admits decidable fragments characterised by structural restrictions on communication patterns (Lange-Yoshida 2019, k-multiparty compatibility at PSPACE). The k parameter is, in lattice terms, a height bound on the protocol-state Hasse diagram (§5.3); k-safety and k-exhaustivity are downward and upward closure conditions on a bounded sublattice. The structural form of these conditions is exactly what a quantale-graded substrate makes natively expressible.

The synthesis claim here is light, per the artifact's framing. A speculative reformulation: the buffer-bound k of Lange-Yoshida's k-MC framework is a structural constraint that may admit a quantale-graded reading — k as a tropical-cost budget at which subtyping checks become tractable. The chapter does not argue this identification (the slice does not establish it; the original Lange-Yoshida paper does not establish it); it merely observes that the k-bounded-fragment-decidability story is structurally compatible with a quantale-graded reading where the grading is the buffer-cost. The frontier-(a) hypothesis is therefore that *quantale-graded asynchronous session subtyping plausibly admits decidable fragments under tropical bounds*, with concrete construction left to design-track work. This is consistent with the project's broader stance (per the user) that tropical structures and runtime verification mechanisms together are tractable for boundedness, fairness, and liveness.

The synthesis does not commit to a stronger claim here. The decidable-fragment construction is a target for follow-on track work; the present chapter notes only that the algebraic substrate the artifact has surveyed is the natural setting for such a construction.

### §8.6 Cost / Order / Fairness / Resource as Projections (Frontier (b))

The fragmented quantitative-program-semantics literature surveyed in §6.4–§6.5 is the strongest evidence for the projection-claim. Each of cost, ordering, fairness, and resource has been formalised in published work as a quantale-graded computation; no published work unifies them. Cost: tropical lambda calculus (Barbarossa-Pistone CSL 2024 [BP24a], POPL 2025 [BP25]), network calculus (Le Boudec-Thiran 2001 [LBT01]), graded modalities (Orchard-Liepelt-Eades 2019 [OLE19], Atkey 2024 [Atk24]). Causal order: event structures for multiparty sessions (Castellani et al. 2024 [CDP+24]), happens-before in CRDTs (vector clocks). Fairness: graded-algebra type systems for fair termination (Bono-Castagna 2024 [BC24]). Resource: QTT multiplicities (Atkey-McBride), effect quantales (Gordon 2021 [Gor21]), resource theories as quantale modules (Gonda 2021 [Gon21]).

The synthesis claim here is that these are projections of one substrate. The Lawvere quantale `([0,∞], inf, +)` simultaneously encodes cost (tropical projection), distance (the metric structure on configurations), and approximation (the topology on cell-state space). Other quantales encode probability (`[0,1]` under multiplication), Boolean truth (`{⊥,⊤}` under conjunction), causality (vector-clock-style `ℕ^P` modules), or composite structures (the `ℕ ⊗ T` causal-clock quantale of §8.4). Each tradition treats its quantale separately; the unification is the recognition that a quantale-enriched substrate carries all of these as projections from one underlying object.

The Barbarossa-Pistone tropical-lambda-calculus papers (CSL 2024 / POPL 2025, §6.5.3) are the closest published instance of the unification. Part I establishes that "the tropical semiring as the weight-base of a relational model unifies (i) Lipschitz-condition-based program metrics and (ii) linear-logic-and-higher-order-differentiation-based resource analysis — two quantitative semantic traditions that had been developed independently. The general foundation is an abstract correspondence between tropical algebra and Lawvere's theory of generalised metric spaces" [BP24a]. Lawvere's framework is precisely the quantale-enrichment-of-Cat tradition (§3.5; Lawvere 1973/2002). The Barbarossa-Pistone papers establish that a tropical-quantale-weighted programming-language semantics is published, formal, and operational. What they do not extend to is the propagator-network substrate or the cc(D) operational form. That extension — the recognition that a propagator network with tropical-quantale-valued cells is the operational realisation of the relational model Barbarossa-Pistone treats — is the synthesis target of frontier (b).

The Xie-Johnson-Maclaurin-Paszke parallel-effect-handler paper (ICFP 2024 [XJM+24], §6.4.2) is the second relevant instance. The paper introduces λ_p, the first lambda calculus combining effect handlers with explicit parallel computations, and notes connections to *free applicative functors*. The free-applicative observation suggests a polynomial-functor view of parallel effect handlers (Joyal-Kock-Melliès lineage); polynomial functors are a candidate categorical structure for distinguishing parallel-handler from sequential-handler algebras. The connection to the project's substrate, if it can be made concrete, would be that the free-applicative structure on a quantale-enriched propagator network is the algebraic content of "all-at-once, all in parallel" — the parallel ⊗_∥ of the concurrent Kleene quantale (§8.3) potentially lifted to a polynomial-functor presentation. This is suggestive rather than established; the synthesis chapter flags the connection without committing.

The synthesis claim therefore is that cost, order, fairness, and resource are not separate concerns to be addressed by separate type systems and verification machinery; they are projections from a single quantale-enriched substrate, and the substrate's algebra produces compositional reasoning about them automatically. The propagator network that already runs the project's elaborator has the right structural shape for this; what it needs is the explicit quantale enrichment, the per-projection computation rules, and the verification machinery. These are design-track scope, not synthesis-chapter scope; the artifact's role is to surface that the algebraic substrate is the right one.

### §8.7 What's Missing from the Substrate Today

The Phase-1 frontier survey (§6.6) identified eight gaps in the published 2020–2026 literature. They are the agenda items for the project's research and design tracks; this section restates them with project-specific framing.

**Gap (a). Choreography projection as quantale morphism.** No published frontier paper explicitly formulates endpoint projection (Pirouette, Choral, Chorλ, ChoRus, λ_QC) as a quantale-enriched construction graded by communication cost, latency, fairness, or causal depth. The choreographic-programming community treats correctness exclusively. For the project, this gap is most immediately relevant if the L2 architecture (§8.4) admits a choreographic-programming surface syntax — a global protocol that compiles to per-vat propagator-network endpoint code, with deadlock-freedom as a structural-by-construction guarantee.

**Gap (b). Propagator network as quantale-enriched category.** No published 2020–2026 paper formulates the propagator-network operational substrate as a quantale-enriched category. The closest existing technology comes via quantale-valued coalgebraic logic (Beohar-Gurke-König-Messing-Forster-Schröder-Wild STACS 2024 [BGK+24]), the Lawvere-metric-space correspondence in tropical lambda-calculus (Barbarossa-Pistone CSL 2024 [BP24a]), and resource-theory-as-quantale-module formalism (Gonda 2021 [Gon21]). None of these papers connects the propagator-network operational substrate to the quantale-enriched-categorical algebra. This is the central original-substrate territory for the project.

**Gap (c). CCP-as-quantale-enriched-cc(D).** Saraswat's cc(D) [Saraswat 1989] + Panangaden-Saraswat-Scott-Seely 1995's hyperdoctrinal interpretation is the foundation. Soft CCP (semiring-graded constraint entailment, Bistarelli-Montanari-Rossi 1997 [Bistarelli-Montanari-Rossi 1997]) is the canonical instance. But the explicit equivalence "Saraswat's cc(D) is the operational form of computation in a quantale-enriched category over a propagator substrate" — which the working hypothesis holds — is unstated in published work. The project's contribution would be to make this equivalence explicit and to use the propagator-network operational realisation as evidence that the equivalence is consequential rather than merely structural.

**Gap (d). Unified treatment of cost + order + fairness + resource as quantale projections.** As §8.6 develops, the components exist independently in published work; no published work unifies them as different quantale projections of one substrate.

**Gap (e). Network-calculus min-plus on programming-language type substrate.** Network calculus is published exclusively in networking/performance venues; programming-language type systems for cost are published in POPL/ICFP/PLDI venues. The Prologos tropical-quantale cost layer (PPN 4C addendum) is, on available frontier evidence, the first crossover.

**Gap (f). Quantale-causal clocks (vs Lamport / vector clocks).** Lamport clocks and vector clocks are well-known to form monotone join-semilattices. The natural extension to quantale-graded clocks (§8.4) is not published. This is the most concrete missed frontier item: distributed-systems metadata is already quantale-shaped operationally (CRDT lattice merge is a join-semilattice operation), but the published formalisation stops at lattices and does not progress to quantales except in the network-calculus-on-data-paths reading.

**Gap (g). CapTP / OCapN in quantale formalism.** The Spritely / OCapN distributed-capability tradition (§4.5, §6.2) has not been formally connected to quantale or coalgebraic semantics. Capabilities are typically treated as resources in an unrestricted set-membership sense rather than as quantale-graded resources. The natural cross-pollination is: capability-passing async = quantale-valued message-cost-graded morphism between actor-cell-states.

**Gap (h). Function coloring at the substrate level.** §8.2 develops this gap explicitly. The algebraic-effect-handler lineage recognises the type-level dissolution of function coloring but does not advance the substrate-level claim that every "async" call is a propagator-fire-event under quantale-graded cost and ordering. The project's hypothesis is that the two dissolutions are layers of one phenomenon.

Two further gaps the artifact's preceding slices identified are project-specific rather than literature-survey gaps:

**Gap (i). Stratification on quantale modules.** §7.5.4 raised this as an open question. How do strata (S0 monotone, S1 NAF, S(-1) retraction, topology) carry over to the quantale-enriched substrate? Two candidate algebraic structures are identified — quantale-module retracts (closure operators per stratum) and iterated quantaloid layering (multi-typed generalisation per Stubbe). Both are speculative. Resolution requires new theoretical work.

**Gap (j). The propagator-network-as-fibred-hyperdoctrine formalisation.** §3.3 open question 2 surfaced this. Mendler-Panangaden-Scott-Seely 1995 establishes determinate cc as a hyperdoctrine; a propagator network with per-cell decomposition is, conjecturally, a *fibred* hyperdoctrine over the cell graph. This is not in the literature and is a candidate research output for the project.

The eight literature-survey gaps and two project-specific gaps together constitute the research program the working hypothesis implies. The artifact does not commit to any specific track ordering or design path; its role is to surface that the program exists, that its algebraic substrate is well-grounded, and that the gaps it identifies are tractable rather than speculative. The follow-on tracks the project will pursue from this foundation are catalogued in §9.

### §8.8 Reading the Hypothesis Against the Evidence

The working hypothesis admits five formulations of decreasing strength. (The original four-formulation framing of earlier drafts conflated parallel composition with asynchrony; the five-formulation version below distinguishes them.)

(H1') *Async composition involves a parallel-product operation in some quantale-enriched algebra.* **Strongly supported.** §3.3 (CCP closure operators), §3.4 (quantale theory), §7.3 (concurrent Kleene algebra of Hoare-Möller-Struth-Wehrman 2009 with quantale-completion), §5.5 (BloomL/CALM) jointly give this formulation strong evidential weight. The algebraic resources exist.

(H1) *Asynchrony IS a quantale operation* (specifically, the ⊗_∥ of a concurrent Kleene quantale). **Plausibly supported but not established.** The supported version is H1'; H1 makes the stronger identification claim that *async = parallel composition*, which is a category mistake — async involves *temporal decoupling* (the future-becomes-resolved-later structure) that ⊗_∥ does not by itself capture. The temporal-decoupling structure may admit a quantale-graded account (Q-valued causal clocks, §8.4), but identifying that account *with* ⊗_∥ requires further work.

(H2) *Propagator networks instantiate cc(D) for D = arbitrary lattice with monotone merge.* **Settled at the per-cell store level; with non-trivial extensions at the network level.** §3.3.7 makes the per-cell-store correspondence explicit; the closure-operator semantics carries through. Slice §3.3.8's three divergences (D1 dynamic topology, D2 stratification, D3 cell-locality) are extensions of the cc(D) framework that the project's design contributes, not features inherited from the cc literature.

(H3) *The quantale-enriched extension of cc(D) — soft cc(Q) — is the natural setting for asynchrony.* **Supported (with completion-conditions caveat).** Bistarelli-Montanari-Rossi's c-semirings (1997, soft CCP 2006) are commutative quantales *when the c-semiring's idempotent + is completed under arbitrary suprema and × is well-behaved*; the project's tropical-quantale work satisfies these conditions; the closure-operator semantics generalises to quantale-valued lattices. The qualifier matters because not every c-semiring is a quantale without completion, and design tracks should verify the completion holds in their specific instances.

(H4) *Cost, ordering, fairness, and resource are projections from a single quantale-enriched substrate.* **Plausibly supported but not demonstrated.** The components exist independently in published work; the unification is gap (d) and is not yet in the published literature. The Barbarossa-Pistone tropical-lambda papers (CSL 2024, POPL 2025) are the closest published instance and unify two of the four projections (cost, distance/approximation). The full unification awaits original work.

The synthesis chapter's lean — neutral with strong tilt toward the hypothesis, per the user's framing — is therefore well-calibrated. (H1') is well-supported; (H1) overreaches without further work; (H2) is settled at the per-cell store level with extensions the project contributes at the network level; (H3) is supported with the completion-conditions qualifier; (H4) is the genuine frontier hypothesis. The Phase-1 evidence supports the program of work that the hypothesis implies; what it does not do, and what no Phase-1 evidence could do, is *demonstrate* (H4). Demonstration is design-track work: the project's PReduce series, the L2 multi-network runtime track, the explicit quantale-enriched cc(D) realisation, and the unified projections framework will, severally, produce the evidence that (H4) needs.

A note on the chapter's posture. The strongest counter-arguments a hostile reader could mount are (1) that §8.2's "substrate-level relocation" of function coloring is a *renaming* of the problem (cost-bound budgeting) rather than a structural answer to it, and (2) that the L2 architecture sketch in §8.4 elides engineering questions about composition with the existing CapTP / OCapN protocols. Both objections are real; the chapter has been revised to acknowledge them rather than to elide them. Defending against (1) requires showing that the cost-projection on a propagator graph carries the *locus information* that async/await syntactically encodes — a concrete claim the design tracks must validate. Defending against (2) requires the L2 design tracks to engage the OCapN community and the existing implementation experience directly. Both are legitimate research-program items, not synthesis-chapter conclusions.

The genealogical claim of §8.1, the function-coloring dissolution of §8.2, the structured-concurrency-as-Concurrent-Kleene-quantale account of §8.3, the L2 architecture sketch of §8.4, the decidability-via-tropical-bounds remark of §8.5, the projection-claim of §8.6, and the gap inventory of §8.7 are seven facets of one synthesis: that the propagator-network substrate Prologos is building is, when its algebraic content is named, a quantale-enriched cc(D) realisation; that the asynchrony question dissolves at this substrate's algebra rather than at any user-facing syntax; and that the work the project has been doing in the AI/dataflow vocabulary instantiates a research programme — Saraswat's cc(D) family with its post-1991 quantale-graded extensions — that supplies the formal antecedents the project's design tracks can draw on. The remainder of this artifact (§9 for sub-questions, §10 for bibliography, §11 for out-of-scope declarations) treats the consequences.

## §9 Sub-Questions for Follow-On Tracks

This section converts the synthesis chapter's arguments into specific track-shaped research questions, each with cross-references to the artifact's evidence and to existing project tracking documents. The list is not a roadmap; it is a catalogue of design-track candidates that the working hypothesis suggests are tractable and worth pursuing. Track ordering, prioritisation, and resource allocation are decisions for the project's master roadmap (`docs/tracking/MASTER_ROADMAP.org`).

### §9.1 The L2 multi-network runtime track

**Working question.** What does a federation of propagator networks look like operationally, and does the vat-model design precedent compose with the project's existing CALM-monotone-within-strata discipline, capability hierarchy, and tropical-quantale cost layer?

**Evidence base.** §4.5 (Mark Miller's E and CapTP), §6.2 (Spritely / OCapN revival), §5.5 (BloomL and CALM), §4.6 (timely / differential dataflow as the closest existing system), §8.4 (the synthesis chapter's L2 architectural sketch).

**Sub-questions.**

- (a) *Vat-model adaptation.* Does the strict vat boundary (single-threaded turn-to-completion) compose with the project's parallel-firing-within-a-round BSP discipline? Two candidate adaptations: (i) make BSP rounds run-to-completion within a vat (loses intra-round parallelism); (ii) make the vat span multiple BSP rounds with cross-vat boundaries at round transitions (loses some of the engineering closure the lineage offers). Resolution requires concrete prototyping.
- (b) *CapTP and OCapN composition.* Can the CapTP wire protocol carry quantale-valued causal clocks (`ℕ ⊗ T`-valued or richer) without breaking the OCapN interoperability test suite? What is the per-message wire cost?
- (c) *Distributed garbage collection.* Does CapTP's per-table reference-counting plus `op:gc-exports` discipline transfer to cross-network propagator-cell references, or does the propagator-network's dynamic topology require a different discipline?
- (d) *Three-network introduction.* Can OCapN's signed-handoff protocol be cast as a small choreography in a propagator-substrate setting, with cross-network topology requests as the choreographic primitive?

**Prerequisites.** This track depends on Architecture A+D's effect-ordering infrastructure being available (which it is — AD-A through AD-F shipped). It is partially blocked on Racket-level concurrency primitives (the existing S8b deferral). Cross-references: DEFERRED.md "Session Types — Concurrent Runtime", DEFERRED.md "Capabilities — Phase 8d: Multi-Agent Cross-Network Reasoning".

### §9.2 The quantale-enriched cc(D) realisation track

**Working question.** Make the propagator-network-as-soft-cc(Q) identification explicit and operational. Specifically: cast the project's existing tropical-quantale cost layer (PPN 4C addendum) as a soft-CCP instance, demonstrate that the closure-operator semantics applies, and articulate which existing project results follow from inherited cc-quantale theorems versus which are project-original.

**Evidence base.** §3.3 (CCP deep-dive, particularly §3.3.6 and §3.3.10), §3.4–3.5 (quantale foundations), §7 (cross-cutting quantale deep-dive), §8.1.

**Sub-questions.**

- (a) *Formal identification.* Write down the c-semiring whose completion gives the tropical-quantale layer and verify the completion conditions. Confirm that Bistarelli-Montanari-Rossi 1997's results apply.
- (b) *Closure-operator semantics for tropical-cost propagators.* Verify that the project's tropical-cost propagator firings are closure operators on the quantale-valued lattice, and that the parallel composition theorem (§8.1's parallel-as-meet) holds in the quantale setting.
- (c) *Hyperdoctrinal extension.* Mendler-Panangaden-Scott-Seely 1995 establishes determinate cc as a hyperdoctrine. Conjecturally a propagator network with per-cell decomposition forms a *fibred* hyperdoctrine over the cell graph; the construction is open work (gap (j) in §8.7). Address it.
- (d) *Soft cc(Q) operational realisation.* Show that the project's existing propagator infrastructure already realises (or can be made to realise) the operational semantics of soft cc(Q), with bridge propagators corresponding to closure-operator composition.

**Prerequisites.** None beyond reading time. This is a *theoretical-foundations* track that produces formal results applicable to all subsequent design work.

### §9.3 The user-facing async syntax track (S8b extension)

**Working question.** Given a quantale-enriched substrate, what is the right user-facing syntax for asynchronous programming? S8a shipped type-level `sess-async-send`/`sess-async-recv` and reserved the `!!` / `??` / `@` operators; S8b (the runtime distinction, promise cells, multi-network execution) is deferred. The working hypothesis suggests that the substrate-level relocation of function coloring (§8.2) makes a substrate-aware syntax design tractable.

**Evidence base.** §4.2 (futures-and-async-await arc), §4.3 (function coloring diagnostic), §4.5 (E's `when`-blocks and promise pipelining), §5.1 (structured concurrency primitives), §5.2 (algebraic effect handlers), §6.2 (Goblins' `on`-form), §8.2.

**Sub-questions.**

- (a) *Function-coloring relocation.* In a quantale-enriched substrate, is the user-facing `async` marker even a meaningful distinction? If not, what replaces it? (The Lefkowitz position from §4.3 — that explicit suspension points are necessary for cognitive locality — argues that *some* syntactic marker is desirable. The Nystrom position argues that uniform concurrency obviates the marker. The substrate-level relocation suggests a third path: cost-aware suspension where the marker is the cost-bound rather than a binary async tag.)
- (b) *Promise pipelining.* The strong-form pipelining attributed to Miller-Tribble-Jellinghaus c. 1989 (§4.5.2) is operationally important for L2 latency. What does the user-facing syntax for strong-form pipelining look like?
- (c) *Structured concurrency.* Smith's nursery and level-triggered cancellation (§5.1) restore the black-box rule of structured programming. In a propagator-substrate world where cancellation is S(-1) retraction, what is the user-facing structured-concurrency primitive?
- (d) *`when`-blocks vs `await`-statements.* Goblins (and E before it) opted for `when`-blocks rather than `await`-statements, on re-entrancy grounds (§4.5.4, §6.2.2). The argument applies to any propagator-substrate design. Is the project's user-facing async syntax `when`-shaped?

**Prerequisites.** This track depends on the L2 multi-network runtime track (§9.1) for its operational substrate. Cross-references: DEFERRED.md "Session Types — Concurrent Runtime"; AD-F3 placeholder.

### §9.4 The unified projections track

**Working question.** Demonstrate that cost, ordering, fairness, and resource are projections from a single quantale-enriched substrate, by exhibiting a concrete substrate construction in which each projection is computable from the substrate's algebra alone.

**Evidence base.** §6.4 (effect-handler categorification), §6.5 (tropical / min-plus), §8.6, gap (d) in §8.7.

**Sub-questions.**

- (a) *Quantale schema.* Write down the quantale schema (the underlying lattice, the monoidal product, the residuations) under which cost, ordering, fairness, and resource are simultaneous projections.
- (b) *Projection morphisms.* For each of the four (cost, ordering, fairness, resource), exhibit a quantale morphism from the schema to a target quantale (tropical for cost, ℕ-modules for ordering, etc.).
- (c) *Compositional reasoning.* Show that the projection morphisms compose: the cost of a sequential composition is the sum of costs, the cost of a parallel composition is the max (in the right quantale), and so on for the other projections.
- (d) *Type-system implications.* What does a graded modal type system over the schema quantale look like? Connection to Atkey 2024 [Atk24], Orchard-Liepelt-Eades 2019 [OLE19], and the "Resource-Bounded Type Theory" preprint [RBT25].

**Prerequisites.** This track depends on the quantale-enriched cc(D) realisation track (§9.2) for its substrate.

### §9.5 The decidability-by-bounded-grading track

**Working question.** Asynchronous session subtyping is undecidable in general (Bravetti et al. 2017) and decidable on bounded fragments (Lange-Yoshida k-MC at PSPACE). Does quantale-graded subtyping admit decidable fragments via tropical bounds, with the bound determined by the quantale's choice?

**Evidence base.** §5.3 (asynchronous session types and the decidability frontier), §6.5 (tropical structures), §8.5.

**Sub-questions.**

- (a) *Tropical bound parameterisation.* Cast the k-MC framework's k parameter as a tropical-cost budget (latency, message count, or fairness deadline). Verify that bounded-k decidability transfers to the tropical-budget setting.
- (b) *Runtime verification.* Tropical thresholds at runtime (the project's existing fuel-budget cells) are tractably checkable in BSP rounds. Show that liveness and fairness obligations are quantale-valued thresholds checkable by the substrate's standard machinery.
- (c) *Boundedness as a quantale property.* Is "bounded" in this setting a property of the quantale (the budget is finite) or of the program (the program's quantale weight is finite)? Both can be made precise; the latter is more useful for static analysis.

**Prerequisites.** This track depends on §9.4 for its quantale schema.

### §9.6 The choreographic-projection-as-quantale-morphism track

**Working question.** Choreographic programming projects a global protocol onto per-party endpoint code. In a quantale-enriched substrate, is this projection a quantale morphism graded by communication cost? Gap (a) in §8.7.

**Evidence base.** §6.1 (choreographic programming), §6.2.3 (OCapN handoff as a small choreography), §8.4 (cross-network topology requests as choreographic-shaped).

**Sub-questions.**

- (a) *Projection as morphism.* Show that endpoint projection (Pirouette, Choral, ChoRus, λ_QC) admits a quantale-graded reading where the projection morphism's grading is the per-endpoint communication cost.
- (b) *OCapN handoff.* Cast the three-vat handoff protocol as a choreographic statement compiled to gifter, exporter, and receiver code. Verify that the compilation is a propagator-substrate cross-network-topology-request sequence.
- (c) *Choreographic L2.* Is the L2 architecture (§9.1) most naturally specified as a choreographic language, with vats as locations and topology requests as choreographic operations? Connection to Spritely / OCapN.

**Prerequisites.** This track depends on the L2 multi-network runtime track (§9.1) for its operational substrate.

### §9.7 The propagator-as-fibred-hyperdoctrine track

**Working question.** Mendler-Panangaden-Scott-Seely 1995 establishes that determinate cc is a hyperdoctrine. A propagator network with per-cell decomposition is conjecturally a *fibred* hyperdoctrine over the cell graph. The construction is gap (j) in §8.7. Address it.

**Evidence base.** §3.3 (especially §3.3.3 and §3.3.10), §3.6 (categorical concurrency, especially §3.6.2 event structures), §7.4 (quantale modules and closure operators).

**Sub-questions.**

- (a) *Fibration construction.* Given a propagator network, construct the hyperdoctrine fibration with the cell graph as base category and the per-cell closure-operator lattices as fibres.
- (b) *Substitution and existence.* Demonstrate that substitution along base-category morphisms (cell renaming, structural decomposition) and existential quantification (cylindrification / hiding) work as expected in the fibred setting.
- (c) *Quantale enrichment.* Lift the construction to soft cc(Q), with the fibres carrying quantale-module structure rather than plain closure-operator-lattice structure.

**Prerequisites.** This track depends on §9.2.

### §9.8 The non-track items

Two further observations that are not tracks but that the artifact should record.

*Validation against existing artifacts.* The genealogical claim (§8.1) implies that significant portions of existing project documentation should be rewritten in CCP terms — particularly DESIGN_PRINCIPLES.org, the `on-network.md` rule, and the `propagator-design.md` rule. This is not urgent, but the rewrite should happen at some point: the project's mantra, "all-at-once, all in parallel, structurally emergent information flow ON-NETWORK," is the operational form of CCP's tell-as-only-primitive discipline, and the documentation that does not say so understates the project's intellectual lineage. Sample rewrite: replace "propagator firing on a cell" with "cc agent on a constraint store" where the rewrite is more illuminating.

*Cross-references to existing principles.* The strongest connection between this artifact and the project's principles documents is the alignment between the working hypothesis and the *Hyperlattice Conjecture* (per `structural-thinking.md`). The conjecture says every computable function is expressible as a fixpoint computation on lattices, and the Hasse diagram of the lattice is the optimal parallel decomposition. The working hypothesis can be read as the *quantale-enriched* refinement: every computable function is a quantale-graded fixpoint computation, and the quantale's algebra is the optimal projections (cost, ordering, fairness, resource, distance, probability). The artifact is, in this reading, evidence for the conjecture's quantale-enriched form.

## §10 Bibliography

This artifact's bibliography is organised co-located with use: each era section (§2 through §6) and the cross-cutting quantale deep-dive (§7) carries its own *References* subsection at the end, with each citation annotated for its contribution to that section's argument. The synthesis chapter (§8) cites by reference to the era sections rather than carrying its own bibliography. Total citation count across the artifact is approximately 304 distinct references; major works (Saraswat 1989, Saraswat-Rinard-Panangaden 1991, Mulvey 1986, Lawvere 1973/2002, Conway et al. 2012, Hoare-Möller-Struth-Wehrman 2009, Bistarelli-Montanari-Rossi 1997, Miller 2006) are cited from multiple sections with each citation site supplying section-specific context.

### §10.1 Citation conventions used

Citations appear inline as `[Author Year]` (e.g., `[Saraswat 1989]`) or `[AuthorYear]` for short keys (e.g., `[SRP91]`) where the longer form would be ambiguous. The full annotated entry — author(s), title, venue, year, link, and 2–4 line annotation explaining contribution and relevance — appears in the *References* subsection of the era section that most centrally cites the work. Where a work is cited from multiple sections, the most central section is taken as canonical for the annotation; other sections cite by reference (e.g., "see [Author Year] in §3.3").

### §10.2 Bibliography by section

For navigation:

- **§2 References** — Era I foundational concurrency theory (~30 entries): CSP/CCS/π trinity, Hewitt actors and Erlang lineage, dataflow / I-structures / futures, CML events, Honda-Tokoro / Boudol asynchronous π-calculus.
- **§3.1, §3.2, §3.6 References** — Linear logic and Caires-Pfenning lineage, game semantics (HO, AJM, Melliès asynchronous games), event structures, Chu spaces, coalgebraic concurrency, Plotkin-Power algebraic effects (~38 entries).
- **§3.3 References** — Concurrent Constraint Programming (Saraswat-Rinard-Panangaden, Mendler-Panangaden-Scott-Seely, Bistarelli-Montanari-Rossi soft CCP, related cc-family languages and quantale-CCP work; ~48 entries).
- **§3.4, §3.5, §7 References** — Quantale theory (Mulvey, Joyal-Tierney, Rosenthal, Stubbe quantaloids, Eklund et al.), Lawvere metric, tropical mathematics (Maclagan-Sturmfels, Cuninghame-Green, Le Boudec-Thiran network calculus), concurrent Kleene quantales (Hoare-Möller-Struth-Wehrman 2009), recent quantale-coalgebraic logic (Wild-Schröder 2024), quantale modules and resource theories (~47 entries).
- **§4.1, §4.2, §4.3, §4.4, §4.6 References** — Erlang/Akka/Orleans actor lineage, F#/C#/JS/Python/Rust/Swift/Kotlin/Go async-await arc, function-coloring diagnostic (Nystrom 2015), FRP and reactive extensions, timely / differential dataflow / Materialize (~30 entries).
- **§4.5, §6.2 References** — Mark Miller and the capability-async lineage: KeyKOS, Joule, E (Miller 2006), CapTP, Spritely Institute, OCapN, Goblins (~14 entries).
- **§5 References** — Structured concurrency (Smith 2018, Trio, Kotlin coroutines, Swift SE-0304, Java JEP 444), algebraic effects (Plotkin-Power, Plotkin-Pretnar, Bauer-Pretnar Eff, Lindley-McBride-McLaughlin Frank, Leijen Koka, Sivaramakrishnan et al. OCaml 5, Ahman-Pretnar λ_ae), asynchronous session types and decidability (Honda-Yoshida-Carbone, Bravetti-Lange-Yoshida), capability-based effects (Brachthäuser-Schuster-Ostermann effekt), CALM/Bloom/BloomL (Hellerstein, Conway et al.) (~50 entries).
- **§6.1, §6.3, §6.4, §6.5, §6.6 References** — Choreographic programming (Pirouette, Choral, ChoRus, λ_QC), recent quantale-concurrent papers, frontier effect-handler categorification, tropical lambda calculus (Barbarossa-Pistone CSL 2024 / POPL 2025), explicit gap inventory (~47 entries).

### §10.3 Reading guide

For readers seeking the literature on a specific topic, the most direct paths through the bibliography are:

- *Algebraic foundations of concurrency*: §3.1, §3.2, §3.6, §3.3, §3.4, §3.5.
- *Operational concurrency systems and engineering*: §4.1, §4.2, §4.5, §4.6, §6.2.
- *Quantale theory and applications*: §3.4, §3.5, §7, §6.5.
- *Algebraic effects and structured concurrency*: §5.1, §5.2, §5.4, §6.4.
- *Distributed and capability-based async*: §4.5, §6.2, §5.5, §8.4.
- *Frontier (post-2020)*: §6.1, §6.3, §6.4, §6.5, §6.6.

For readers who want a single-author entry point: Saraswat (CCP foundations, §3.3), Miller (capability-async lineage, §4.5 / §6.2), Hellerstein and Conway (CALM / BloomL, §5.5), Plotkin and Pretnar (algebraic effects, §5.2), Lawvere (Q-enriched categories, §3.5), Mulvey (quantales, §3.4), Melliès (asynchronous games, §3.2), Smith (structured concurrency, §5.1), Nystrom (function-coloring diagnostic, §4.3).

## §11 Out-of-Scope Declarations

This section catalogues what the artifact deliberately does not cover, with reasons. Future readers can use the list to know where to look elsewhere and to avoid mistaking absence for omission.

### §11.1 Topics excluded by scope decision

**Formal verification of async (TLA+, Iris, Verdi, Coq libraries for concurrent reasoning).** The artifact treats verification only insofar as it informs the substrate question (§5.5 CALM, §8.5 decidability). The TLA+ tradition (Lamport's specifications), the Iris separation-logic-for-concurrency line (apart from the Tian-Madiot-Pottier OCaml-with-effect-handlers paper at §6.4.3), and Verdi's distributed-systems verification work are out of scope. The reason: these are verification *meta-techniques* for systems that have already settled their substrate; the artifact's question is upstream.

**GPU concurrency primitives (CUDA, OpenCL, SYCL, Metal, Vulkan compute).** The hardware-specific concurrency primitives of GPU programming are out of scope. The reason: GPU concurrency is a SIMT (single-instruction-multiple-thread) discipline operationally distinct from the propagator-substrate question; the connections — particularly through Naiad / timely dataflow's GPU-acceleration work — would warrant a separate research artifact.

**Hardware memory models (TSO, weak memory, Linux's memory model, the C++ memory model).** The artifact treats memory ordering only insofar as it informs causal ordering at the algebraic level (§4.6, §8.4). The hardware-level details — Adve-Hill's data-race-free models, Sewell's weak-memory work, the C++ atomic operations specification — are out of scope. The reason: these are substrate questions for the *machine* substrate, not the programming-language substrate; the artifact is two levels of abstraction up.

**JavaScript event-loop specifics, Promise A+ resolution procedure details, Node.js libuv internals.** The artifact treats JavaScript only insofar as it provides a cautionary tale about the loss of strong-form promise pipelining (§4.5.2). The libuv event-loop details, the precise Promise A+ resolution procedure, and the V8 engine's microtask queue are out of scope. The reason: these are implementation-engineering details of one platform; the artifact is concerned with the language-design question.

**Specific library APIs (asyncio, Twisted, Trio, Tokio, async-std, Akka APIs, etc.).** The artifact treats these libraries only at the conceptual / design-pattern level. API signatures, library-specific idioms, and engineering trade-offs in each platform are out of scope. The reason: the artifact is concerned with the algebraic substrate, not the wrapper library.

### §11.2 Topics deliberately treated lightly

**Decidability of asynchronous session subtyping (frontier (a)).** Per the user's framing, the decidability question is treated lightly (§5.3, §8.5). The reason: the project's stance is that tropical structures and runtime verification mechanisms together are tractable for boundedness, fairness, and liveness; the published decidability results are informational rather than load-bearing.

**Probabilistic concurrency (probabilistic process calculi, probabilistic session types, probabilistic CCP).** The artifact treats probabilistic frameworks only where they directly bear on the quantale-enrichment story (§3.3.5 Probabilistic CCP, §6.3.4 Probabilistic Quantale Semantics, §7.3 Probabilistic Quantale). The full probabilistic-concurrency literature is much larger; the artifact's coverage is selective.

**Quantum process calculi.** The artifact mentions quantum process calculi briefly (§6.3.2, citing Ceragioli-Gadducci-Lomurno-Tedeschi 2024 [CGL+24]) but does not develop the connection. The reason: quantum process calculi are an active area but not load-bearing for the project's design tracks.

### §11.3 Topics intentionally bounded

**Eras outside 1965–2026.** The artifact's historical coverage stops in 1965 (Dijkstra EWD 123) on the early end and at 2026 on the late end. Earlier work (Petri's nets, McCarthy's atomic operations, von Neumann's parallelism considerations) is not covered. Later work (post-2026) cannot be covered.

**Eras V coverage at the citation level.** The Era V section (§6) is a frontier scan, not an exhaustive survey. Citations are selected for their relevance to the working hypothesis and for their representation of major research programs. Many recent papers are not cited; this is intentional and reflects the artifact's scope. Future readers seeking a more exhaustive frontier survey should consult the dblp / arXiv / Google Scholar searches the agents performed (the citations §6 does include should serve as entry points).

### §11.4 Topics flagged for future research artifacts

These are topics the artifact identifies as worth covering in future research efforts, but that fall outside this artifact's scope.

**The relationship between Prologos's stratification and modal type theories.** Bak and Urschumzew's "Choreographic Programming in Modal Type Theory" (CP 2024) is mentioned briefly in §6.1.2; the broader connection between modal type theories and stratified concurrent semantics is worth its own artifact. The connection to the project's `structural-thinking.md` and its lattice-modal framing is suggestive.

**Engineering economics of distributed-propagator runtimes.** The L2 architecture sketch in §8.4 raises engineering questions (per-message wire cost, OCapN composition) that the artifact does not answer. A future engineering-economics artifact, comparing propagator-substrate L2 architectures against existing distributed-systems primitives at scale, would complement this synthesis.

**Cross-pollination between Prologos and the Spritely / Goblins community.** The artifact identifies the Miller-E-CapTP-OCapN lineage as the most thoroughly engineered design precedent for L2 (§4.5, §6.2). A direct collaboration with the Spritely Institute on whether the lineage's engineering closure transfers to a propagator-substrate setting would be a high-value follow-on; that cross-pollination is out of scope here but flagged for the project.
